- **Section 6A** (Assam) was upheld 4:1 by the Supreme Court on 17 October 2024 ✅ (repository note).
**Trap:** Single citizenship (like Canada); exceptions: Art 16(3), Art 19(5) tribal areas, Art 371 series, ex-Art 35A (abrogated 2019). CAA gives **no automatic citizenship** and the Art 14 challenge was pending at the repository's last check.
**Back:** *"11 = Parliament's pen: BeD RaIN to get, R-T-D to lose."*

### PART III — Fundamental Rights (Articles 12–35)

### Flashcard Art-12 — Article 12
**Front:** Article 12 — Definition of "State" (for Part III)
**Provision:** "State" includes (i) the Government and Parliament of India; (ii) the Government and the legislature of each State; (iii) all local or other authorities within the territory of India or under the control of the Government of India. Only State action is reachable through Part III; purely private bodies are outside this definition.
**Learn:**
- Three limbs: Union executive + Parliament; State executive + legislature; "local or other authorities" (municipalities, panchayats, district boards, statutory bodies).
- Judicial test for "other authorities": instrumentality or agency of the State, judged by deep and pervasive State control (*Ajay Hasia* 1981 ✅ owner-verified; the owner also cites LIC and ONGC as State bodies).
- Corporations, banks, statutory bodies, and government companies can be "State"; a purely private body cannot, even if it performs a public function. Article 226 can reach public duties beyond Art 12, so the Art 12 test and the Art 226 scope are not the same.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** *Rajasthan State Electricity Board v. Mohan Lal* (1967) and *Sukhdev Singh v. Bhagatram* (1975) developed the instrumentality idea ⚠️ (standard knowledge, not in the owner file; verify the holdings before quoting them). *Ajay Hasia v. Khalid Mujib* (1981) gave the test in the owner file. Art 12 feeds Art 13 (laws of the "State" can be void) and Art 32 (writs run against the "State").
**Trap:** The definition is for Part III only. Do not extend it to every statutory term "State" in other Parts without checking the context. Also "local authorities" and "other authorities" are separate limbs; a body need not be a local authority to be "State".
**Back:** *"G-L-L-O: Government, Legislature, Local bodies, Other instrumentalities — 'G-L-L-O' is the State."*

### Flashcard Art-13 — Article 13
**Front:** Article 13 — Laws inconsistent with or in derogation of Fundamental Rights
**Provision:** (1) All pre-Constitution laws in force in India, to the extent they are inconsistent with Part III, are void. (2) The State shall not make any law that takes away or abridges the rights in Part III; a law made in contravention is void to the extent of the contravention. (3) "Law" includes any ordinance, order, bye-law, rule, regulation, notification, and custom or usage having in the territory of India the force of law; "laws in force" includes pre-Constitution laws. (4) (inserted by the 24th Amendment, 1971) Nothing in Art 13 applies to an amendment made under Art 368.
**Learn:**
- Art 13 is the textual basis of **judicial review** of legislation against Part III; the Court can strike down a law but does not "amend" it.
- Art 13(1) (pre-Constitution law) and Art 13(2) (post-Constitution law) work differently. Post-Constitution inconsistent laws are void from the start; pre-Constitution inconsistent laws are dormant (see Eclipse below).
- Art 13(3)(a) covers custom or usage, which is why a custom that violates a fundamental right is void (2026 Prelims Q54, provisional key; no answer inferred).
- Art 13(4) keeps constitutional amendments out of "law"; the basic-structure doctrine then polices them (*Kesavananda* 1973).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** *Bhikaji Narain Dhakras v. State of M.P.* (1955): **Doctrine of Eclipse**: a pre-Constitution inconsistent law is dormant, not dead; it revives if the FR bar is removed. *Basheshar Nath v. CIT* (1959): fundamental rights cannot be waived ⚠️ (owner cites it; verify). *Kesavananda Bharati* (1973): amendments are not "law" under Art 13 but must respect the basic structure. Doctrinal consolidation: `Constitutional-Interpretation-Doctrines.md` (severability, eclipse, waiver).
**Trap:** Eclipse means "temporarily unenforceable", not "repealed"; "pre-Constitution law" is dormant, while "post-Constitution law" is void. Art 13(1) and 13(2) are not interchangeable. The 24th Amendment (1971) inserted 13(4); do not attribute it to the 42nd.
**Back:** *"O-O-B-R-R-N-C: Ordinance, Order, Bye-law, Rule, Regulation, Notification, Custom — every one is 'law' under 13(3)."*

### Flashcard Art-14 — Article 14
**Front:** Article 14 — Equality before law and equal protection of laws
**Provision:** The State shall not deny to any person equality before the law or the equal protection of the laws within the territory of India. Applies to **all persons**: citizens, foreigners and legal persons.
**Learn:**
- Two limbs: **equality before law** (negative; British/Dicey idea: no special privileges, no one above ordinary law) and **equal protection of laws** (positive; American idea: like treated alike, reasonable classification allowed).
- **Reasonable classification:** valid if (i) based on an intelligible differentia distinguishing the grouped from the rest and (ii) the differentia has a rational nexus to the object of the law. Class legislation is forbidden.
- **Arbitrariness test:** Art 14 forbids arbitrary State action; unguided and uncontrolled executive discretion violates Art 14 (*E.P. Royappa* 1974, *Maneka Gandhi* 1978).
- Dicey's rule of law: three elements. India takes only the first two (equality before law; supremacy of ordinary law, no arbitrary power), not the third, because the Constitution itself is the source of rights here.
- Exceptions: Art 361 (President/Governor immunity), Art 361A (fair reports of legislative proceedings), Art 105 (MPs) and Art 194 (state legislators). Art 31C was traditionally said to displace Art 14 for laws implementing DPSP 39(b)/(c), subject to the 1973 limits.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** *E.P. Royappa v. State of T.N.* (1974) (arbitrariness is the antithesis of equality). *Maneka Gandhi* (1978) (Arts 14, 19, 21 form the "golden triangle"). *Ram Krishna Dalmia* (1958) for the reasonable-classification test ⚠️ (standard knowledge; verify before quoting). Owner: 2021 GS-II Q13 (Right to Equality in India and USA; 15 marks).
**Trap:** Art 14 is for all persons, not citizens only; Art 15/16 are citizen-specific. Art 14 does not require absolute equality. Do not describe the arbitrariness test as an "American" import; the American side is the Equal Protection Clause.
**Back:** *"Before-law = Britain (negative); Protection = America (positive); Classify fairly, never arbitrarily."*

### Flashcard Art-15 — Article 15
**Front:** Article 15 — Prohibition of discrimination on grounds of religion, race, caste, sex or place of birth
**Provision:** (1) The State shall not discriminate against any citizen on grounds **only** of religion, race, caste, sex, place of birth or any of them. (2) No citizen shall be subjected to any disability, liability, restriction or condition on these grounds in access to shops, public restaurants, hotels, places of public entertainment, or in the use of wells, tanks, bathing ghats, roads and public resorts maintained wholly or partly from State funds or dedicated to public use. (3) Special provisions for **women and children**. (4) Special provisions for the advancement of **socially and educationally backward classes (SEBCs), SCs and STs**. (5) Special provisions for SEBC/SC/ST in **educational institutions**, including private unaided, except minority educational institutions under Art 30(1). (6) Special provisions for the **economically weaker sections (EWS)**, up to 10%, outside existing SC/ST/OBC reservations.
**Learn:**
- The keywords are "discrimination" and "only": the ground must be the sole basis.
- Art 15(2) binds **private persons** in access to shops, hotels, wells, roads, etc. (horizontal reach).
- Clauses (3)–(6) are enabling exceptions, not fundamental rights in their own right. 15(4) came by the First Amendment (1951) ✅ standard knowledge; 15(5) by the 93rd Amendment (2005) ✅ owner; 15(6) by the 103rd Amendment (2019) ✅ owner.
- OBC education reservation (27%) under the 93rd Amendment (2005), upheld with a creamy-layer exclusion in *Ashoka Kumar Thakur* (2008) ✅ owner.
- Creamy-layer exclusion for OBCs; the income ceiling was ₹8 lakh from 2017 ✅ owner. ✅ Wikipedia "Creamy layer" (fetched): ₹8 lakh set in 2017; no later revision recorded there; the NCBC's 2015 proposal of ₹15 lakh is not recorded as adopted. The threshold as of 30 Oct 2026 is still ⚠️ unverified; check the current notification before quoting.
- EWS reservation upheld by majority in *Janhit Abhiyan* (2022) ✅ owner.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** *Indra Sawhney* (1992) (creamy layer, 50% ceiling; cross-ref Art 16(4)). *Ashoka Kumar Thakur* (2008). *Janhit Abhiyan* (2022) (103rd upheld by majority). Art 46 (DPSP for weaker sections) is the directive counterpart; Art 335 (efficiency) is the counterweight for SC/ST claims in services (Art 16/335).
**Trap:** 15(4)/(5)/(6) are **permissive**, not compulsory. The 93rd Amendment's 15(5) excludes minority educational institutions covered by Art 30(1). Art 15 is citizen-only, unlike Art 14 (all persons). Art 15(2) horizontal reach is only for shops, wells, roads and similar places.
**Back:** *"Sole ground only: R-R-C-S-P (Religion, Race, Caste, Sex, Place of birth); women-children and EWS get enabling exceptions."*

### Flashcard Art-16 — Article 16
**Front:** Article 16 — Equality of opportunity in matters of public employment
**Provision:** (1) Equality of opportunity in matters relating to employment or appointment to any office under the State. (2) No citizen shall be discriminated against or made ineligible on grounds of religion, race, caste, sex, descent, place of birth, residence, or any of them. (3) Parliament may prescribe residence requirement for certain employment. (4) Reservation for any backward class not adequately represented in State service. (4A) Reservation in promotion for SCs/STs (if not adequately represented). (4B) Carry-forward of unfilled reserved vacancies. (5) Religious or denominational office exemption. (6) Reservation for EWS up to 10%.
**Learn:**
- Seven prohibited grounds: religion, race, caste, sex, descent, place of birth, residence. (Residence is in Art 16(2) only, not Art 15.)
- Art 16(3) residence requirement: Parliament's 1957 Act expired in 1974 ✅ owner. Residence-based reservation survives now only for AP and Telangana ✅ owner; the source is Art 371D(2)(c) (preference or reservation for candidates who resided or studied in the local area) ✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XXI.
- Mandal Commission (appointed 1979 under Art 340, reported 1980 ✅ owner) recommended 27% OBC reservation; implemented in 1990.
- *Indra Sawhney v. Union of India* (1992) ✅ owner: upheld OBC reservation; applied the creamy-layer exclusion; laid down that total reservations should ordinarily not exceed 50%; held that reservation is not extended to promotions (later reversed by 77th Amendment for SC/ST).
- Amendments overriding *Indra Sawhney*: 77th (1995) promotions for SC/ST ✅ owner; 81st (2000) carry-forward ✅ owner; 85th (2001) consequential seniority ✅ owner; 102nd (2018) NCBC constitutional status under Art 338B ✅ owner.
- *M. Nagaraj* (2006) ✅ owner: upheld Art 16(4A)/(4B) enabling amendments; required backwardness, inadequacy and efficiency (Art 335) data. *Jarnail Singh* (2018) ✅ owner: removed the backwardness-data requirement for SCs/STs but kept the quantifiable-inadequacy requirement; did **not** overrule *Nagaraj* wholesale. *Davinder Singh* (2024) ✅ owner: permits sub-classification within SCs (by majority). Art 16(4) is enabling, not a right.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** *Indra Sawhney* (1992); *M. Nagaraj* (2006); *Jarnail Singh* (2018); *Janhit Abhiyan* (2022, EWS); *Davinder Singh* (2024). Art 335 (efficiency of administration, SC/ST claims). Art 341 (SC list).
**Trap:** 16(4) is an enabling power, not a right (owner trap). Art 16(4) covers *backward* classes, while 16(4A) covers promotions for SCs/STs; do not mix them. The 50% ceiling is a court-made limit from *Indra Sawhney*, not text. Art 16 is citizen-only (Art 14 is for all persons).
**Back:** *"Seven grounds, R-R-C-S-D-P-R (Religion, Race, Caste, Sex, Descent, Place of birth, Residence); reservation is an enabling 16(4), not a right."*

### Flashcard Art-17 — Article 17
**Front:** Article 17 — Abolition of Untouchability
**Provision:** "Untouchability" is abolished and its practice in any form is forbidden. The enforcement of any disability arising out of untouchability is an offence punishable in accordance with law.
**Learn:**
- The term "untouchability" is not defined in the Constitution; courts read it as the historical caste-based practice, not literal touching (owner: Mysore HC view).
- Enforceable **against private individuals**, which makes Art 17 one of the horizontal rights (with 15(2), 23, 24).
- Statutory anchors: the Protection of Civil Rights Act, 1955 (originally the Untouchability (Offences) Act, 1955; renamed in 1976 ✅ owner). The Scheduled Castes and Scheduled Tribes (Prevention of Atrocities) Act, 1989 ✅ standard knowledge; verify before quoting section details.
- Art 17 is a Right to Equality provision, not a Right against Exploitation provision.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Owner: 2020 Prelims Q4 (untouchability protection belongs to the Right to Equality, Arts 14–18, not the Right against Exploitation). Art 46 (DPSP on weaker sections) is the directive counterpart.
**Trap:** The Right against Exploitation (Arts 23–24) is a different family; untouchability is in Arts 14–18. Art 17 does not abolish "caste" as a concept, only untouchability in practice. Do not state that "untouchability" has a statutory definition in the Constitution.
**Back:** *"Ban the barrier, bar the touch-taboo — 17 binds private hands too, and is punished by law."*

### Flashcard Art-18 — Article 18
**Front:** Article 18 — Abolition of titles
**Provision:** (1) No title may be conferred by the State, except a military or academic distinction. (2) No citizen shall accept any title from any foreign State. (3) A non-citizen holding any office of profit or trust under the State shall not accept any foreign title without the President's consent. (4) No person holding any office of profit or trust under the State shall accept any present, emolument or title from a foreign State without the President's consent.
**Learn:**
- Two levels: (a) State titles (only military or academic are allowed) and (b) foreign titles (citizens barred; office-holders need the President's consent).
- *National Awards case* (1996 ✅ owner): Bharat Ratna and Padma awards are **not "titles"** under Art 18; they are valid but may not be used as prefixes or suffixes (else forfeiture). Instituted 1954 ✅ owner; discontinued 1977 ✅ owner; revived 1980 ✅ owner.
- Military distinctions (e.g., PVC, MVC) and academic distinctions are permitted ✅ standard knowledge; verify the names before writing them in an answer.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** National Awards case (1996). Owner: 2021 Prelims Q96 (Bharat Ratna/Padma awards not barred by Art 18; no prefix/suffix).
**Trap:** Bharat Ratna and Padma awards are **not** titles under Art 18, so using them as "Dr. Bharat Ratna X" is the prohibited use. Art 18(2) bars citizens from accepting foreign titles, while Art 18(4) applies only to office-holders accepting foreign gifts or titles.
**Back:** *"M-A only: Military or Academic titles; foreign titles need the President's nod for office-holders."*

### Flashcard Art-19 — Article 19 (including omitted 19(1)(f))
**Front:** Article 19 — Six freedoms (citizens only; against the State)
**Provision:** (1) All citizens shall have the right: (a) to freedom of speech and expression; (b) to assemble peaceably and without arms; (c) to form associations or unions or co-operative societies; (d) to move freely throughout the territory of India; (e) to reside and settle in any part of India; (f) **[OMITTED by the 44th Amendment (1978) — formerly the right to acquire, hold and dispose of property; see Art 31 card]**; (g) to practise any profession, or to carry on any occupation, trade or business.
(2) Speech restrictions: sovereignty and integrity of India, security of the State, friendly relations with foreign States, public order, decency or morality, contempt of court, defamation, incitement to an offence. (3) Assembly restrictions: sovereignty and integrity of India, public order. (4) Association restrictions: sovereignty and integrity of India, public order, morality. (5) Movement and residence restrictions: general public interest, or protection of the interests of any Scheduled Tribe. (6) Profession restrictions: professional or technical qualifications; State trade, business, industry or service, monopolies, citizens' corporations.
**Learn:**
- Six freedoms now (not seven): 19(1)(a)–(e) and 19(1)(g). The 19(1)(f) number is vacant. The original property right (once Art 19(1)(f) and Art 31) went with the 44th Amendment and became a legal right under Art 300-A.
- **Eight speech grounds** (Art 19(2)): exhaustive. The "reasonable" word was added by the First Amendment (1951), and "sovereignty and integrity" came with the 16th Amendment (1963) ✅ owner.
- Key cases ✅ owner: *Romesh Thappar* (1950) and *Brij Bhushan* (1950) (prior restraint struck; both decided on pre-First-Amendment text); *Kedar Nath Singh* (1962) (upheld sedition s.124A by reading it down to incitement to violence/public disorder); *Shreya Singhal* (2015) (struck down s.66A IT Act for vagueness and overbreadth; upheld s.69A; read down s.79); *Anuradha Bhasin* (2020) (internet speech and trade protected under 19(1)(a) and (g); proportionality; indefinite shutdown impermissible; orders must be published and reviewed); *Subramanian Swamy* (2016) (upheld criminal defamation, reputation under Art 21 as a counterweight).
- Sedition status ✅ owner, dated 13 August 2026: IPC s.124A kept in abeyance by interim order of 11 May 2022 in *S.G. Vombatkere*; constitutional challenge still undecided; BNS 2023 s.152 is a successor with different ingredients and is itself under challenge.
- The owner's advanced file says the property right is "(g) deleted"; that is a labelling error. The omitted sub-clause is 19(1)(f) ✅ (Wikisource Const. 2020, Part III footnote 12: omitted by the 44th Amendment). The owner error is at `advanced/07_Fundamental-Rights.md` line 96 ("Property right (g) deleted"), which should read 19(1)(f) (not edited here).
- Art 19(1)(g) (profession) is the sub-clause used in the owner's Art 19(6) restrictions.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** First Amendment (1951), 16th Amendment (1963), 44th Amendment (1978). 2022 GS-II Q2 (movement and residence; Art 19(1)(d)/(e) and 19(5)). Art 21 protects foreign travel (*Maneka Gandhi*). No press Article exists; press freedom is derived from 19(1)(a).
**Trap:** Art 19 is citizen-only; Art 20/21/22 protect all persons. Art 19(1)(a) has **no** separate press Article. Foreign travel is Art 21, not 19(1)(d). The 2019 "internet" holding is about speech and trade through the internet, not a standalone right. Restrictions require "by law" and "reasonable" (the First Amendment inserted "reasonable"). "Offensiveness", "hurt sentiment", and "fake news" are not Art 19(2) grounds (*Shreya Singhal* and owner).
**Back:** *"Speak, Assemble, Associate, Move, Reside, Practise — (f) property is gone; eight speech grounds, all 'by law, reasonable'."*

### Flashcard Art-20 — Article 20
**Front:** Article 20 — Protection in respect of conviction for offences
**Provision:** (1) No person shall be convicted of any offence except for violation of a law in force at the time of the act (no ex-post-facto criminal law), nor subjected to a penalty greater than that which might have been inflicted under the law in force at the time. (2) No person shall be prosecuted and punished for the same offence more than once (double jeopardy). (3) No person accused of an offence shall be compelled to be a witness against himself (self-incrimination).
**Learn:**
- Applies to **all persons**, including foreigners and companies (where applicable).
- 20(1) bars **conviction**, not trial; it is criminal-only (not civil or tax); it does not cover preventive detention ✅ owner.
- 20(2) double jeopardy applies only to prosecution before a court or judicial tribunal; departmental proceedings are not barred ✅ owner.
- 20(3) covers oral and documentary testimony, but not thumb impressions, specimen signatures, blood samples or body exhibition ✅ owner. The Court has treated these as non-testimonial (*Kathi Kalu Oghad* 1961 ⚠️ standard knowledge; verify before quoting).
- Non-suspendable during Emergency: Art 359(1) as amended by the 44th Amendment (1978) protects Arts 20 and 21 ✅ owner.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 44th Amendment (1978) (non-suspendability). Art 22 (arrest safeguards; a separate regime). Art 21 (fair procedure; with 20 the core of criminal-process rights). Owner: 7.5 Bank B (Art 20 protections).
**Trap:** Art 20(1) forbids a **retrospective penalty**, not every retrospective legislation. Art 20(2) requires a prior **conviction or acquittal** and a court-based prosecution; departmental proceedings and civil cases are outside it. Specimen signature is not "testimony" under 20(3).
**Back:** *"Three No's: No retro law, No double jeopardy, No self-incrimination — 'Ex-Double-Self', non-suspendable."*

### Flashcard Art-21 — Article 21
**Front:** Article 21 — Protection of life and personal liberty
**Provision:** No person shall be deprived of his life or personal liberty except according to procedure established by law. Applies to **all persons**.
**Learn:**
- Narrow phase: *A.K. Gopalan* (1950): "procedure established by law" meant only that a valid statute was enough, and the protection was only against executive action ✅ owner.
- Wide phase: *Maneka Gandhi* (1978): procedure must be **fair, just and reasonable**; the protection now runs against executive and legislative action; life means life with human dignity ✅ owner.
- Judicially built rights (owner's list): dignity (*Francis Coralie Mullin* 1981), livelihood (*Olga Tellis* 1985), speedy trial (*Hussainara Khatoon* 1979), free legal aid (*M.H. Hoskot* 1978), prisoners' rights (*Sunil Batra* 1978), pollution-free environment (*Subhash Kumar* 1991), privacy (*K.S. Puttaswamy* 2017, nine judges, 24 August 2017), passive euthanasia and living will (*Common Cause* 2018, five judges).
- Art 21 anchors are remedial rather than distributive: fair procedure, safeguards and compensation, not a right to housing or jobs ✅ owner.
- Art 21 protects foreign travel (*Maneka*), and the right to marry a person of one's choice (owner: 2019 Prelims Q56).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** *A.K. Gopalan* (1950); *Maneka Gandhi* (1978) (golden triangle with Arts 14, 19); *Puttaswamy* (2017); *Olga Tellis* (1985); *D.K. Basu* (1997) (custodial safeguards). Art 21A (education, 86th Amendment). Art 39A (free legal aid, through Art 21 with Legal Services Authorities Act, 1987). 2023 GS-II Q11 (living instrument; 15 marks). 2024 GS-II Q12 (privacy and DNA testing; 15 marks).
**Trap:** Art 21 uses "person", not "citizen". Due process in the *Maneka* sense is from 1978, not 1950. Privacy is **not** a new Article; it is read into Art 21 (2024 Prelims Q76 and 2021 Q79, owner-verified routing; 2018 Q92). Art 21 is non-suspendable in Emergency. Do not say Art 21 gives an enforceable right to housing or general health services.
**Back:** *"Life and liberty by fair, just, reasonable procedure — Gopalan narrow, Maneka wide, Puttaswamy privacy."*

### Flashcard Art-21A — Article 21A
**Front:** Article 21A — Right to education
**Provision:** The State shall provide free and compulsory education to all children of the age of six to fourteen years in such manner as the State may, by law, determine. (Inserted by the 86th Amendment, 2002.)
**Learn:**
- Inserted by the 86th Amendment (2002) ✅ owner. The 86th also recast Art 45 to early childhood care (under six) and added a fundamental duty under Art 51A ✅ owner (the duty is parental: Art 51A(k) ✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part IV-A; footnote: inserted by the 86th Amendment, w.e.f. 1-4-2010).
- Implementing statute: Right of Children to Free and Compulsory Education Act, 2009 (RTE Act) ✅ owner.
- Judicial path: *Unni Krishnan v. State of A.P.* (1993) read the right to primary education into Art 21; *Mohini Jain* (1992) was overruled in part by *Unni Krishnan* ✅ owner. The full cycle: judicial interpretation → constitutional text → statute.
- Art 21A is a **fundamental right**, not a directive principle.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 86th Amendment (2002); RTE Act 2009; Art 45 (recast DPSP); Art 51A (parental duty). Owner: Art 21 anchors.
**Trap:** The 6–14 right is **Art 21A**, not Art 21. Art 45 is a directive principle, not a right; the age group for Art 21A is 6–14, not 6–18. Art 21A is not a "free education up to graduation" right.
**Back:** *"Six to fourteen, free and compulsory — 21A after 86th (2002), RTE 2009 implements."*

### Flashcard Art-22 — Article 22
**Front:** Article 22 — Protection against arrest and detention in certain cases
**Provision:** (1) Every arrested person must be informed of the grounds of arrest as soon as may be, and must not be denied the right to consult and be defended by a legal practitioner of choice. (2) Every arrested person must be produced before the nearest magistrate within 24 hours of arrest (excluding journey time), and no detention beyond that without the magistrate's authority. (3) Clauses (1) and (2) do not apply to an enemy alien or to a person detained under a preventive detention law. (4) Preventive detention may not exceed **three months** unless an Advisory Board, consisting of High Court judges or persons qualified to be such (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part III), reports sufficient cause. (5) Grounds of detention must be communicated and the detenu given the earliest opportunity to make a representation. (6) The authority need not disclose facts it considers against public interest. (7) Parliament prescribes the circumstances in which detention beyond three months may be made without Advisory Board review, the maximum period and the procedure of the Board.
**Learn:**
- Two regimes: **punitive** (ordinary arrest; Art 22(1)–(2)) and **preventive** (detention without trial; Art 22(4)–(7)).
- The 44th Amendment (1978) sought to cut the preventive-detention ceiling from three to two months, but this was **never brought into force**; the three-month ceiling stands ✅ owner; the un-commenced 44th text is flagged in the Wikisource Const. 2020, Part III editorial note ✅.
- Parliament has exclusive power over preventive detention on defence and foreign affairs grounds; concurrent with States for security and public order ✅ owner (Art 246 read with Seventh Schedule, List I entry 9 and List III entry 3 ✅ Wikipedia "Seventh Schedule to the Constitution of India").
- Preventive detention statutes: NSA 1980 ✅ owner; MISA (repealed) ✅ owner; COFEPOSA ✅ owner.
- Art 22 is a rights Article, but Art 22(3)–(7) is an exception that permits detention without trial. Owner notes this as a structural anomaly.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 44th Amendment (1978) (two-month text, not in force). Owner: 2021 Prelims Q82 (police custody vs judicial custody; both framed by Art 22's 24-hour rule). Art 21 (procedure); Art 20.
**Trap:** 24 hours excludes journey time; 3 months is the operative ceiling (not 2 months). Enemy aliens and preventive detainees are outside 22(1)–(2). The Advisory Board is composed of High Court judges or persons qualified to be such (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part III summary); the chairman rule is not in the fetched text, so do not quote it as text ⚠️. Police custody and judicial custody differ (police holds for interrogation; judicial custody needs permission to interrogate) ✅ owner.
**Back:** *"Twenty-four hours to a magistrate; three months' preventive ceiling, unless the Board says yes."*

### Flashcard Art-23 — Article 23
**Front:** Article 23 — Prohibition of traffic in human beings and forced labour
**Provision:** (1) Traffic in human beings, begar and other similar forms of forced labour are prohibited, and any contravention shall be an offence punishable in accordance with law. (2) Nothing in this Article prevents the State from imposing compulsory service for public purposes, but the State shall not make any discrimination on grounds only of religion, race, caste or class in imposing such service.
**Learn:**
- Applies to **all persons**, and binds private persons too (horizontal reach).
- "Traffic" covers trafficking in women and children, devadasis and slavery ✅ owner.
- "Force" includes economic compulsion; *PUDR v. Union of India* (1982, Asiad workers) is commonly cited for the view that paying less than the minimum wage can amount to forced labour ⚠️ (case not reachable; soften until verified; do not quote as a holding).
- Statutes: Immoral Traffic (Prevention) Act, 1956 ✅ owner; Bonded Labour System (Abolition) Act, 1976 ✅ owner; Minimum Wages Act, 1948 ✅ owner.
- Art 23(2) exception: compulsory service for public purposes (military, social service) ✅ owner.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 24 (child labour); Art 17 (horizontal; untouchability); Art 15(2). DPSP Art 39(e), (f) relevant to child welfare ✅ standard knowledge.
**Trap:** Art 23 does **not** ban all compulsory service; only compulsion without public purpose or with discrimination is barred. Art 23 is a Right against Exploitation, not a Right to Equality. Economic compulsion is argued to count as "force" (PUDR, unverified ⚠️).
**Back:** *"T-B-F: Traffic, Begar, Forced labour — public-purpose service is the only carve-out, and no caste or class bias."*

### Flashcard Art-24 — Article 24
**Front:** Article 24 — Prohibition of employment of children in factories, etc.
**Provision:** No child below the age of **fourteen years** shall be employed to work in any factory, mine or other hazardous employment.
**Learn:**
- Narrower than the common "child labour" ban: it covers factories, mines and hazardous employment only (not harmless work) ✅ owner.
- Statutes: Child Labour (Prohibition and Regulation) Act, 1986 ✅ owner; renamed Child and Adolescent Labour (Prohibition and Regulation) by a 2016 amendment ✅ owner. Commissions for Protection of Child Rights Act, 2005 ✅ owner.
- *M.C. Mehta v. State of Tamil Nadu* (1996) ✅ owner: ₹20,000 per child fine (on the employer) to a Child Labour Rehabilitation-cum-Welfare Fund.
- Horizontal reach: enforceable against private employers ✅ owner.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 23 (forced labour); Art 39(e), (f) DPSP; Art 45 (old text). Right against Exploitation (Arts 23–24).
**Trap:** The bar is under **14**, not 18; the Article covers only factories, mines and hazardous employment. Art 24 is a fundamental right, not a DPSP. Do not write "Art 24 bans all child work".
**Back:** *"Under fourteen, no factory, mine or hazard — the 14 bar is absolute."*

### Flashcard Art-25 — Article 25
**Front:** Article 25 — Freedom of conscience and free profession, practice and propagation of religion
**Provision:** (1) All persons are equally entitled to freedom of conscience and the right freely to profess, practise and propagate religion, subject to public order, morality and health and to the other Part III provisions. (2) Nothing affects: (a) regulation or restriction of economic, financial, political or other secular activity associated with religious practice; (b) social welfare and reform, or the throwing open of Hindu religious institutions of a public character to all classes and sections of Hindus. Explanation I: wearing and carrying a kirpan is included in the profession of the Sikh religion. Explanation II: references to Hindus include Sikhs, Jains and Buddhists.
**Learn:**
- Individual right; applies to **all persons**.
- "Practice" protects essential religious practices; the doctrine is judicially applied ✅ owner.
- State may regulate secular activity and reform religion (Art 25(2)(a), (b)) ✅ owner.
- Essential Religious Practices: origin in *Shirur Mutt* (1954) ✅ owner; applied in *Sabarimala* (2018 ✅ owner: majority held exclusion of women unconstitutional; reference to a larger bench heard April–May 2026 and reserved; no verdict as of 13 August 2026 per the owner file; ⚠️ current status NOT verified: the Wikipedia case pages were unreachable (404) and the 30 Oct 2026 status must be re-checked before writing any status).
- Propagation does **not** include a right to forcibly convert others (owner; *Stainislaus v. State of M.P.* (1977) ⚠️ standard knowledge, verify).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** *Shirur Mutt* (1954); *Indian Young Lawyers Association* (Sabarimala, 2018); Art 26 (denominations); Art 27–28; Art 25(2)(b) (social reform, throwing open Hindu institutions). 2019 GS-II Q5 (France and Indian secularism; 10 marks).
**Trap:** Propagation ≠ conversion. Art 25 is individual; Art 26 is denominational. Art 25(2)(b) makes religious freedom weaker in India than in a strict-separation system (owner). Do not say Sabarimala is "overruled" (owner: it is not).
**Back:** *"Conscience, Profess, Practise, Propagate — but not Convert by force; 25(2) lets reform in."*

### Flashcard Art-26 — Article 26
**Front:** Article 26 — Freedom to manage religious affairs
**Provision:** Subject to public order, morality and health, every religious denomination or section has the right: (a) to establish and maintain institutions for religious and charitable purposes; (b) to manage its own affairs in matters of religion; (c) to own and acquire movable and immovable property; (d) to administer such property in accordance with law.
**Learn:**
- Collective right of a denomination or section, not an individual right.
- Subject to public order, morality and health only (owner: not subject to other FRs).
- Denomination test (SC): common belief system, common organisation and a distinctive name ✅ owner. Ramakrishna Mission and Ananda Marga are denominations; Aurobindo Society is not ✅ owner.
- Art 26(b) and *Shirur Mutt* (1954): management of religious matters is protected; secular administration may be regulated.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** *Shirur Mutt* (1954); *Sabarimala* (2018). Art 25 (individual); Art 27 (no tax); Art 30 (minority institutions, an educational parallel). Places of Worship (Special Provisions) Act, 1991 ✅ owner (Arts 25/26 cross-link; Act's s.3–5).
**Trap:** Art 26 covers denominations; it is not an individual right to worship. Art 26 is limited to public order, morality and health; Art 25 has the same limits plus "other Part III provisions". Waqf (Amendment) Act, 2025 is under challenge under Arts 14, 15, 25 and 26; interim order of 15 September 2025 ✅ owner; no final judgment located as of 13 August 2026 ✅ owner.
**Back:** *"Found, Manage, Own, Administer — four denominational rights, bounded by order, morality, health."*

### Flashcard Art-27 — Article 27
**Front:** Article 27 — Freedom from taxation for promotion of any religion
**Provision:** No person shall be compelled to pay any taxes, the proceeds of which are specifically appropriated in payment of expenses for the promotion or maintenance of any particular religion or religious denomination.
**Learn:**
- Bars **taxes**, not **fees** ✅ owner. Regulatory fees for secular administration or pilgrim services are allowed ✅ owner.
- Protects the individual against a tax earmarked for a religion; does not bar general taxes that happen to benefit religious bodies.
- Secularism here is positive, not a strict wall (owner: principled-distance model, not French-style exclusion).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 25(2)(b) (reform); Art 28 (religious instruction). Owner: 2019 GS-II Q5 (India's secularism compared with France).
**Trap:** Art 27 bars **taxes** but **permits fees** (owner trap). The Article covers "any particular religion", so a general tax is outside it. Do not claim it bans all State association with religion.
**Back:** *"Tax NOT, Fee YES — Art 27 blocks only tax earmarked for one religion."*

### Flashcard Art-28 — Article 28
**Front:** Article 28 — Freedom from religious instruction in certain educational institutions
**Provision:** (1) No religious instruction shall be provided in any educational institution wholly maintained out of State funds. (2) Clause (1) does not apply to an institution administered by the State but established under an endowment or trust that requires religious instruction to be imparted there. (3) No person attending any educational institution recognised by the State or receiving aid out of State funds shall be required to take part in religious instruction or attend religious worship in that institution without his consent (or, if a minor, his guardian's consent).
**Learn:**
- Four categories (owner): (a) wholly State-maintained → instruction **prohibited**; (b) State-administered but trust-established → **permitted**; (c) State-recognised → permitted **only voluntarily** (with consent); (d) State-aided → permitted **only voluntarily** (with consent) ✅ owner.
- Consent principle: no compulsion in recognised or aided institutions (Art 28(3)).
- Religion-neutral State education is the default for State-funded schools.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 25 (freedom of conscience); Art 29–30 (minority education); Art 45 (early childhood, owner-referenced). 2019 GS-II Q5 (India's secularism).
**Trap:** Wholly State-maintained = prohibited; State-administered trust-established = permitted. Consent is needed in recognised and aided institutions, not in wholly State-run ones. Do not collapse (b) and (c).
**Back:** *"Wholly State: Nil; State-trust: Yes; Recognised or Aided: only with Consent."*

### Flashcard Art-29 — Article 29
**Front:** Article 29 — Protection of interests of minorities
**Provision:** (1) Any section of the citizens residing in the territory of India or any part thereof having a distinct language, script or culture of its own shall have the right to conserve the same. (2) No citizen shall be denied admission into any educational institution maintained by the State or receiving aid out of State funds on grounds only of religion, race, caste, language or any of them.
**Learn:**
- "Section of citizens" is wider than "minority": the Supreme Court held that it is not limited to minorities; a majority section may also invoke it ✅ owner.
- Citizen-only right (owner's citizen-specific list: Arts 15, 16, 19, 29, 30).
- Art 29(2) protects admission to State-maintained and State-aided institutions; the grounds are religion, race, caste, language, or any of them.
- Distinct from Art 30 (minorities' right to establish and run institutions).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 30 (minority institutions); Art 15(1) (similar grounds); Art 350A (mother-tongue instruction at the primary stage: "It shall be the endeavour of every State..." ✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XVII). Owner: Bank C.
**Trap:** Art 29(1) is not limited to religious or linguistic *minorities*; Art 29(2) concerns admission, not running institutions. Art 29 is citizen-only; Art 14 is for all persons.
**Back:** *"Language, script, culture: conserve it; no admission barred on R-R-C-L grounds."*

### Flashcard Art-30 — Article 30
**Front:** Article 30 — Right of minorities to establish and administer educational institutions
**Provision:** (1) All minorities, whether based on religion or language, shall have the right to establish and administer educational institutions of their choice. (1A) (inserted by the 44th Amendment, 1978) In making any law for compulsory acquisition of property of a minority educational institution, the State shall ensure that the amount fixed for it does not restrict or abrogate the right. (2) The State shall not discriminate against any educational institution in granting aid on the ground that it is managed by a minority, whether religion-based or language-based.
**Learn:**
- Only for **minorities** (religious or linguistic); the term "minority" is undefined in the Constitution ✅ owner.
- Rights: to establish and administer; compensation on acquisition (30(1A)); no discrimination in aid (30(2)) ✅ owner.
- *Malankara Syrian Catholic College* (2007) ✅ owner: the right covers choosing the governing body, staff, students and fees, but **no right to maladminister**; regulatory measures for standards are valid; aid does not change minority character.
- *T.M.A. Pai Foundation* (2002) ⚠️ standard knowledge: linguistic minorities are determined at the State level; verify before quoting.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** *Malankara Syrian Catholic College* (2007); *T.M.A. Pai* (2002) ⚠️; 44th Amendment (1978) (30(1A)). Art 29(2) (admission); Art 15(5) (excludes minority institutions from OBC reservation).
**Trap:** "Minority" is not defined; Art 30 is for minorities, not all citizens (that is Art 29). Art 30 does not protect maladministration. Art 15(5) excludes minority institutions under Art 30(1) from the SEBC/SC/ST quota.
**Back:** *"Minorities Found and Run schools; compensation under 30(1A); no bias in aid — no right to maladminister."*

### Flashcard Art-31 — Article 31 (omitted)
**Front:** Article 31 — Right to property (omitted)
**Provision:** **Omitted** by the 44th Amendment (1978; effective 20 June 1979 ✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part III note). Formerly: (1) No person shall be deprived of his property save by authority of law. (2) Property could be acquired or requisitioned only for a public purpose, on payment of compensation (✅ corrected: the original 1950 text left the adequacy of compensation open to judicial review; the amount was made non-justiciable by the 4th Amendment (1955), and the 25th Amendment (1971) changed the clause again) ⚠️ (paraphrase of the original clauses; exact clause text not fetched; verify before quoting).
**Learn:**
- Property was the 7th Fundamental Right; removal by the 44th Amendment (1978) ✅ owner.
- Replacement: Art 300-A (Part XII): "No person shall be deprived of his property save by authority of law" — a **constitutional/legal right**, not a fundamental right ✅ owner.
- Property is protected from arbitrary executive action through Art 300-A and the statutes on land acquisition ✅ owner.
- The original Art 19(1)(f) (right to acquire, hold and dispose of property) was omitted by the 44th Amendment at the same time ✅ (Wikisource Const. 2020, Part III footnote; see Art 19).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 44th Amendment (1978); Art 300-A; Art 31A–31C (saving provisions for acquisition laws; 31A/B/C also apply here). Owner: 2021 Prelims Q92 (Right to Property not a fundamental right; Art 300-A).
**Trap:** Right to property is **not** a fundamental right after 1978. The correct article for the property right is Art 300-A. Art 31 has a number but no operative text; do not cite it as live. Do not say the 44th Amendment "removed Art 300-A".
**Back:** *"31 out, 300-A in — property is a legal right, not a fundamental right, since 1978."*

### Flashcard Art-31A — Article 31A
**Front:** Article 31A — Saving of laws providing for acquisition of estates, etc.
**Provision:** Laws for acquisition of estates (agrarian reform), taking over of management of property, amalgamation of corporations, and extinguishment or modification of rights of managing agents, directors and shareholders (the categories as named in the card; exact sub-clause wording ⚠️ unverified) are not void for inconsistency with Art 14 or 19 ✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part III (the original text also cited Art 31). Presidential assent is required for State laws ✅ Wikisource Part III; the proviso protecting personally cultivated land within ceiling limits is also ✅ Wikisource Part III.
**Learn:**
- Inserted by the First Amendment (1951) ✅ standard knowledge; widened by later amendments.
- Protects specified **categories** of State land and enterprise laws from Art 14 and Art 19 challenges.
- Part of the saving-provision trio with Arts 31B and 31C ✅ owner.
- Land-reform protection and the Ninth Schedule (31B) together formed the 1950s–1970s "shield" for property legislation.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** First Amendment (1951) (31A, 31B); 4th, 17th and 25th amendments (widening) ⚠️ standard knowledge; Kesavananda (1973) on the basic structure limit; Art 31C (DPSP shield); Art 300-A (property now legal right). Owner: Bank D.
**Trap:** Art 31A is a **category** saving: it protects certain types of law only, not all laws. Protection is against Arts 14 and 19 (owner); it does not protect against Art 21 or Art 32 ✅ owner's Bank D frame. Do not treat it as an exemption from basic structure review.
**Back:** *"A for Acquisition — estates, enterprises and managing agents; saved from 14 and 19, not from basic structure."*

### Flashcard Art-31B — Article 31B
**Front:** Article 31B — Validation of certain Acts and Regulations (Ninth Schedule)
**Provision:** Without prejudice to Art 31A, none of the Acts and Regulations specified in the **Ninth Schedule** shall be deemed void or ever to have become void on the ground that they are inconsistent with, or take away or abridge, any of the fundamental rights; each shall have effect as if included in the Constitution.
**Learn:**
- Inserted by the First Amendment (1951) ✅ owner (Ninth Schedule inserted 1951).
- Protection is **retrospective** ("shall be deemed void or ever to have become void") and covers "any of the fundamental rights", not only Art 14/19 ✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part III (the text also says "notwithstanding any judgment, decree or order of any court").
- *I.R. Coelho v. State of T.N.* (2007) ✅ owner: laws placed in the Ninth Schedule **after 24 April 1973** (the Kesavananda date) are open to basic-structure review; earlier ones are protected.
- Laws originally in the Ninth Schedule in 1951 and many later ones remain protected from direct challenge.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** First Amendment (1951); *Kesavananda* (1973) (cut-off date for basic structure check); *I.R. Coelho* (2007) ✅ owner. Art 31A (categories). Art 13 (the Ninth Schedule avoids Art 13 challenges).
**Trap:** Ninth Schedule laws are **not automatically immune**: post-24 April 1973 additions are reviewable (*Coelho*). Do not say "everything in the Ninth Schedule is beyond challenge". Art 31B covers all FRs; Art 31A covers only categories.
**Back:** *"B = Bundled in the Ninth Schedule; post-1973 bundles can still be judged by basic structure (Coelho 2007)."*

### Flashcard Art-31C — Article 31C
**Front:** Article 31C — Saving of laws giving effect to certain DPSPs
**Provision:** Laws giving effect to the policy in **Art 39(b)** (distribution of material resources of the community) or **Art 39(c)** (prevention of concentration of wealth and means of production) shall not be void on the ground of inconsistency with Art 14, 19 or 31 (the protection cannot be questioned in court on that ground). Inserted by the 25th Amendment (1971) ✅ owner (via Property Owners framework and standard knowledge).
**Learn:**
- Narrower than the 42nd Amendment's version (which covered all DPSPs); the 42nd extension was struck down in *Minerva Mills* (1980) ✅ (Wikisource Const. 2020, Part III footnote: Minerva Mills invalidated s.4 of the 42nd Amendment; Wikipedia 42nd Amendment page: decided 31 July 1980).
- The second proviso (barring judicial review of such laws) was struck down in *Kesavananda* (1973) ✅ (Wikisource Const. 2020, Part III footnote: Kesavananda held the declaration-immunity words invalid). The first part survived.
- *Property Owners Association v. State of Maharashtra* (5 November 2024) ✅ owner: not every privately owned resource is a "material resource of the community" under Art 39(b); inquiry is contextual.
- Owner's phrasing: "where 31C comes in, Art 14 goes out" (Art 14 yields to a valid 31C law).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 25th Amendment (1971); *Kesavananda* (1973); *Minerva Mills* (1980) ✅; *Property Owners Association* (2024) ✅ owner. Art 39(b), (c) DPSP; Art 300-A.
**Trap:** Art 31C is limited to **Art 39(b) and (c)**. The 42nd Amendment's wider version was held invalid (✅ *Minerva Mills*). Do not claim that every private asset becomes a community resource.
**Back:** *"C = Community resource laws (39(b), 39(c)) — shielded from 14, 19 and 31, but not from basic structure."*

### Flashcard Art-31D — Article 31D (omitted)
**Front:** Article 31D — Saving of laws against anti-national activities (omitted)
**Provision:** **Omitted** by the 43rd Amendment (passed 1977; effective 13 April 1978 ✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part III note) ✅ owner (WebFetch-verified: the 43rd Amendment "repealed six articles — 31D, 32A, 131A, 144A, 226A and 228A"). Inserted by the 42nd Amendment (1976) ✅ owner. Formerly it enabled Parliament to legislate on "anti-national activities" and "anti-national associations" (scope and override language ⚠️ verify the original text before quoting).
**Learn:**
- A 42nd Amendment (1976) provision in the Emergency period; removed by the 43rd Amendment (1977) ✅ owner.
- It was part of the 1976–77 Emergency restructuring of rights and judicial review that the 43rd Amendment reversed.
- The 43rd Amendment also omitted Art 32A, 131A, 144A, 226A, 228A ✅ owner.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 42nd Amendment (1976); 43rd Amendment (1977). Art 32A (also omitted). Owner: the 44th Amendment (1978) reversed Emergency-era shielding of Arts 20/21 from suspension.
**Trap:** Art 31D is **not** the same as Art 31 (property). Do not write it as a live provision; it is omitted. Anti-national activity laws today are under ordinary statutes (for example the Unlawful Activities (Prevention) Act, 1967 ⚠️ standard knowledge), not Art 31D.
**Back:** *"31D: Dropped in 43rd (1977) — the 42nd's anti-national shield is gone."*

### Flashcard Art-32 — Article 32
**Front:** Article 32 — Right to constitutional remedies
**Provision:** (1) The right to move the Supreme Court by appropriate proceedings for the enforcement of the rights in Part III is guaranteed. (2) The Supreme Court has power to issue directions, orders or writs, including writs in the nature of habeas corpus, mandamus, prohibition, quo warranto and certiorari, as appropriate, for the enforcement of Part III rights. (3) Parliament may empower any other court to exercise the same powers, without prejudice to the Supreme Court's powers. (4) The right under this Article shall not be suspended except as otherwise provided by the Constitution (for example Art 359).
**Learn:**
- Dr Ambedkar: the "heart and soul" of the Constitution ✅ owner. A basic feature (owner).
- Art 32 is itself a fundamental right; the Supreme Court **cannot refuse** an Art 32 remedy for an FR violation ✅ owner.
- Supreme Court Art 32 jurisdiction: only for FR violations; High Court Art 226 jurisdiction is wider (FRs plus any other legal right) ✅ owner. Art 226 relief is discretionary ✅ owner.
- Five writs (owner): habeas corpus, mandamus, prohibition, certiorari (since 1991 also against administrative bodies ✅ owner), quo warranto.
- Owner: 2022 Prelims Q18 (mandamus and quo warranto may be issued by both the SC under Art 32 and HCs under Art 226).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 226 (High Court writs); Art 359 (Emergency suspension); Art 13; Art 12 (who is bound); Art 33 and 34 (armed forces, martial law). *Sunil Batra* (1978) (epistolary jurisdiction, owner). Owner: 2019 Prelims Q85 (liberty in governance), 2021 Q85 (unguided discretion).
**Trap:** SC writ power is only for **FR** enforcement; High Courts' is wider. Art 32 is a fundamental right, but Art 32 remedies can be suspended under Art 359 (owner). Certiorari since 1991 is available against administrative bodies (owner).
**Back:** *"H-M-P-C-Q (Hot Mangoes Preferably Chilled, Quickly): five writs; Art 32 is the remedy that is itself a right."*

### Flashcard Art-32A — Article 32A (omitted)
**Front:** Article 32A — Constitutional validity of State laws not to be considered in proceedings under Article 32 (omitted)
**Provision:** **Omitted** by the 43rd Amendment (1977) ✅ owner (WebFetch-verified: the 43rd Amendment omitted 32A; effective 13 April 1978 ✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part III note). Inserted by the 42nd Amendment (1976) ✅ owner. Formerly: the Supreme Court could not consider the constitutional validity of State laws in writ proceedings for the enforcement of Part III rights ✅ owner (WebFetch-verified description).
**Learn:**
- A 1976 Emergency-era bar on judicial review of State laws under Art 32, reversed by the 43rd Amendment (1977) ✅ owner.
- Its counterpart for High Courts was Art 226A (Central laws) ✅ owner.
- Restoring Art 32 meant the Supreme Court could again examine State-law challenges in FR writs.
- The 43rd Amendment also omitted Arts 131A, 144A, 226A, 228A ✅ owner.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 42nd Amendment (1976); 43rd Amendment (1977); Art 226A (also omitted); Art 131A (also omitted). Owner: Art 32 (basic feature, "heart and soul").
**Trap:** Art 32A is **not** a live provision; do not write it as current. The 43rd Amendment is the reversal, not the 44th.
**Back:** *"32A: Abolished (1977) — the 42nd's gag on FR writs against State laws is gone."*

### Flashcard Art-33 — Article 33
**Front:** Article 33 — Power to modify rights in their application to armed and police forces
**Provision:** Parliament may, by law, determine to what extent any of the rights in Part III shall be restricted or abrogated in their application to members of the Armed Forces, the Forces charged with maintenance of public order, persons employed in intelligence or counter-intelligence organisations, persons employed in telecommunication systems for those forces, and similar categories. (Only **Parliament**, not State legislatures.)
**Learn:**
- Covers armed, para-military, police and intelligence forces, and telecommunications employees ✅ owner.
- Art 33 is in its substituted form (50th Amendment) ✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part III. Examples of laws: Army Act, 1950; Air Force Act, 1950; Navy Act, 1957 (✅ corrected: was "Navy Act, 1950"; standard knowledge, not in the fetched text); Police Forces (Restriction of Rights) Act, 1966 ✅ owner.
- Can exclude court martial proceedings from writ jurisdiction ✅ owner. Covers non-combatants (cooks, barbers) ✅ owner.
- Owner's 12 features: restrictable for armed forces (Art 33) ✅ owner.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 34 (martial law); Art 35 (only Parliament); Art 359 (emergency suspension). Owner: Bank D.
**Trap:** The power is **Parliament's alone** (State legislatures cannot restrict FRs of armed forces under Art 33). It applies to the listed categories only, not to all civilians.
**Back:** *"Parliament alone may trim rights for forces — armed, police, intelligence and their telecom staff."*

### Flashcard Art-34 — Article 34
**Front:** Article 34 — Restriction on rights while martial law is in force
**Provision:** Notwithstanding anything in Part III, Parliament may by law indemnify any person in the service of the Union or a State, or any other person, for any act done in connection with the maintenance or restoration of order in an area where martial law was in force; and may validate sentences, forfeitures or punishments passed under martial law. (Martial law is not defined in the Constitution.)
**Learn:**
- Martial law = military rule in an area when civil order breaks down; "undefined", derived from English common law ✅ owner.
- Art 34 does not itself suspend Part III; it permits restriction and indemnity ✅ owner (implicit in Art 34).
- SC: martial law does **not** ipso facto suspend habeas corpus ✅ owner.
- Martial law vs National Emergency (owner Table 7.3): martial law is area-specific, affects only FRs, and its cause is law and order breakdown; National Emergency is national or part, is caused only by war, external aggression or armed rebellion, and affects Centre-State relations ✅ owner.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 33 (armed forces); Art 35 (Parliament); Art 352 (national Emergency). Owner: Art 33–35 section.
**Trap:** Martial law is not a National Emergency; it is area-specific and has no express constitutional provision. Art 34 covers indemnity and validation, not suspension of all rights. Do not say martial law suspends the Supreme Court or habeas corpus automatically (owner).
**Back:** *"Martial law: area-bound, undefined, rights restricted — Parliament may indemnify and validate."*

### Flashcard Art-35 — Article 35
**Front:** Article 35 — Legislation to give effect to the provisions of Part III
**Provision:** Parliament (and not the State legislatures) has exclusive power to make laws: (a) prescribing residence as a condition for certain employment or appointment under a State or Union Territory (or a local or other authority within it), as provided in Art 16(3) (✅ corrected: was "under the Union, a State or..."; Art 16(3) concerns State and UT posts); (b) punishing acts declared offences under Part III (for example untouchability under Art 17); and, in the same exclusive category, matters under Arts 32(3), 33 and 34 ✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part III summary. Existing laws on these matters continue in force until altered or repealed by Parliament.
**Learn:**
- Ensures pan-India uniformity on the specified FR matters ✅ owner (feature 12: "some need enabling law — only Parliament").
- Transition: existing laws (for example the Untouchability (Offences) Act, 1955) continue until Parliament replaces them ✅ owner-consistent (PCRA 1955).
- Parliament's power over residence requirements is linked to Art 16(3) ✅ owner.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 16(3) (residence); Art 17 (untouchability); Art 35A (J&K, separate). Owner: feature list (Art 35 only Parliament).
**Trap:** Art 35 is exclusive to **Parliament** on the listed matters; States cannot legislate on them. Art 35 does not make all FRs Parliament-only. Art 35A was **not** part of Art 35 (see separate card).
**Back:** *"35 = Parliament alone: uniform law on residence and Part III offences — existing laws stay until replaced."*

### Flashcard Art-35A — Article 35A (NOT part of the Constitution proper)
**Front:** Article 35A — Special rights for "permanent residents" of Jammu and Kashmir (not in the Constitution proper)
**Provision:** **Not a Constitutional Article in the original text.** Inserted into the Constitution by the **Constitution (Application to Jammu and Kashmir) Order, 1954** (issued under Art 370(1)(d)). It empowered the Jammu and Kashmir legislature to define "permanent residents" and confer special rights and privileges on them in State employment, acquisition of immovable property, settlement and State scholarships; such laws could not be void for inconsistency with other Part III rights ✅ (WebFetch-verified: the 1954 Order's legal basis and content).
**Learn:**
- The 1954 Order was issued by President Rajendra Prasad under Art 370 ✅ (WebFetch-verified). It was issued without Parliamentary debate ✅ (WebFetch-verified).
- On **5 August 2019** a new Presidential Order (C.O. 272) superseded the 1954 Order and applied the whole Constitution to J&K; Art 35A thereby ceased ✅ (WebFetch-verified: "stood deleted", via supersession). The J&K Reorganisation Act, 2019 (passed 5–6 August 2019) created the Union Territories of J&K and Ladakh, effective 31 October 2019 ✅ (Wikipedia "Article 370 of the Indian Constitution", fetched); C.O. 272 dated 5 August 2019 ✅ same page.
- **11 December 2023**: five-judge Constitution Bench **unanimously upheld** the abrogation of Art 370 ✅ (✅ Wikipedia "Article 370 of the Indian Constitution", fetched: five-judge bench, unanimous outcome; the page gives no case title and no Art 35A holding). ⚠️ The case name and the Court's express holding on Art 35A were **not** verified; verify the judgment before stating either.
- Art 35A was never part of the Constitution's text; its only legal basis was Art 370. The 2019 change removed the basis, which is why the card is marked "not in the Constitution proper".
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 370 (abrogated in effect by the 2019 Order); Art 35 (Parliament's power, but 35A was never part of it); 1954 Order; 2019 Order (C.O. 272); J&K Reorganisation Act, 2019. The Supreme Court's 2023 judgment on Art 370 ⚠️ (case name unverified).
**Trap:** "Art 35A is in the Constitution" is false; it came through the 1954 Order under Art 370. Do not say it is still in force. Do not conflate Art 35 (Parliament's power) with Art 35A (J&K permanent residents). Do not state the 2023 judgment's holding on 35A without verifying it.
**Back:** *"Arrived by 1954 Order under Art 370; ceased with the 2019 supersession — 35A is not in the Constitution proper."*

### PART IV — Directive Principles of State Policy (Articles 36–51)

### Flashcard Art-36 — Article 36
**Front:** Article 36 — Definition of "the State" in Part IV
**Provision:** Unless the context otherwise requires, "the State" in Part IV has the same meaning as in Part III (Art 12): the Government and Parliament of India, the Government and legislature of each State, and all local or other authorities within the territory of India or under the control of the Government of India.
**Learn:**
- Part IV's addressees are the same bodies that are bound by Fundamental Rights, so DPSP bind the legislature and executive at Union, State and local level alike.
- Article 36 is a definitional bridge only; it creates no duty by itself.
- Part III's definition is in Art 12, so the two Parts share one set of addressees.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 12 (Part III definition); Art 37 (duty to apply the principles in making laws); Art 13 is the Part III check that voids laws inconsistent with fundamental rights; DPSP-based laws are tested against Part III through Art 31C and the basic-structure doctrine.
**Trap:** Art 36 defines "State" for Part IV; it does not make the DPSP enforceable against private persons. A candidate who says "the State" in Part IV means only the Union Government has misread the cross-reference to Art 12.
**Back:** *"Same State as Part III: Art 36 borrows Art 12, so Union, States, local bodies all owe the directives."*

### Flashcard Art-37 — Article 37
**Front:** Article 37 — Application of the principles contained in this Part
**Provision:** The provisions of Part IV "shall not be enforceable by any court, but the principles therein laid down are nevertheless fundamental in the governance of the country and it shall be the duty of the State to apply these principles in making laws."
**Learn:**
- Two-part clause: (i) non-justiciable — no court can enforce a DPSP; (ii) "fundamental in governance" plus a duty on the State to apply them while legislating.
- Sanction is moral and political, mainly through electoral accountability and public opinion.
- Courts use DPSP to interpret ambiguous statutes and to test whether a restriction on a fundamental right is reasonable under Arts 14/19.
- Ambedkar's rationale for non-justiciability: limited resources, diversity and backwardness, and freedom for the new State to decide the order and mode of implementation.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Champakam Dorairajan (1951) — FR prevail over DPSP; Golaknath (1967); 24th and 25th Amendments (1971) — Art 31C; Kesavananda Bharati (1973); 42nd Amendment (1976); Minerva Mills (1980) — harmony and balance of Parts III and IV is basic structure; Art 31C (surviving shield for genuine Art 39(b)/(c) laws).
**Trap:** Art 37 says the principles are "not enforceable by any court", not that they are "not binding" or "irrelevant". Do not write that DPSP are justiciable or that courts can compel a Uniform Civil Code. Art 37 makes DPSP non-justiciable; Arts 32 and 226 do not reach them. Champakam gave FR primacy over DPSP, not the reverse.
**Back:** *"Not enforced, yet fundamental: Art 37 is a no-court, yes-governance clause."*

### Flashcard Art-38 — Article 38
**Front:** Article 38 — State to secure a social order for the promotion of welfare of the people
**Provision:** (1) The State shall strive to promote the welfare of the people by securing and protecting, as effectively as it may, a social order in which justice — social, economic and political — shall inform all the institutions of the national life. (2) (inserted by the 44th Amendment, 1978) The State shall strive to minimise the inequalities in income, and endeavour to eliminate inequalities in status, facilities and opportunities, not only amongst individuals but also amongst groups of people residing in different areas or engaged in different vocations.
**Learn:**
- Clause (1) is the general welfare-state mandate; it is the textual basis for the "welfare state" as opposed to a "police state".
- Clause (2) was added by the 44th Amendment, not the 42nd; it extends inequality-reduction to groups and regions as well as individuals.
- The verb differs by clause: "strive" in (1) and (2), "endeavour" in (2) for eliminating inequalities.
- Socialistic group in the textbook classification.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 44th Amendment (1978) inserted cl (2); Art 39 (economic directives); Art 46 (weaker sections); Art 335 and Art 16(4) for reservation-linked equality; Art 39(b)/(c) are the concrete economic follow-ups.
**Trap:** Cl (2) is from the 44th Amendment (1978), not the 42nd (1976). Examiners pair "minimise inequalities of income" (cl 2) with Art 39(c) "concentration of wealth". The inequality clause does not command equal incomes; it commands minimisation.
**Back:** *"Strive for justice, shrink the gap: Art 38(1) justice in all institutions, Art 38(2) inequality (44th)."*

### Flashcard Art-39 — Article 39
**Front:** Article 39 — Certain principles of policy to be followed by the State
**Provision:** The State shall, in particular, direct its policy towards securing: (a) that all citizens, men and women equally, have the right to an adequate means of livelihood; (b) that the ownership and control of the material resources of the community are so distributed as best to subserve the common good; (c) that the operation of the economic system does not result in the concentration of wealth and means of production to the common detriment; (d) equal pay for equal work for both men and women; (e) that the health and strength of workers, men and women, and the tender age of children are not abused and citizens are not forced by economic necessity to enter avocations unsuited to their age or strength; (f) (recast by the 42nd Amendment, 1976) that children are given opportunities and facilities to develop in a healthy manner and in conditions of freedom and dignity, and that childhood and youth are protected against exploitation and against moral and material abandonment.
**Learn:**
- Clauses (b) and (c) are the economic-justice core: (b) is about "material resources of the community"; (c) is about concentration of wealth.
- Clause (d) is the equal-pay principle; it is a DPSP for men and women, and it is linked to the equality mandate.
- Clause (f) was recast by the 42nd Amendment, which added "freedom and dignity" and protection against abandonment.
- Article 31C (25th Amendment, 1971) protected laws giving effect to Art 39(b) and (c) from Arts 14 and 19 challenge.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Ranganatha Reddy v State of Karnataka (1977) — broad reading of "material resources" ⚠️ (verify holding detail); Sanjeev Coke (1983) ⚠️; Property Owners Association v State of Maharashtra (5 Nov 2024; 9-judge Bench, 8:1 with Dhulia J dissenting; ✅ Wikipedia) — contextual inquiry; not every privately owned asset is a material resource of the community; Minerva Mills (1980) — the 42nd Amendment's extension of 31C to all DPSP struck, but 39(b)/(c) protection survived; Art 31C; Art 39(c) is the Prelims anchor for "wealth concentration" (2021 Q91).
**Trap:** 39(b) (material resources, common good) and 39(c) (concentration of wealth and means of production) are different. Trap question: "the 42nd Amendment recast clause (f)" — not clause (b) or (c). Also do not say Art 39(d) equal pay is a Fundamental Right; it is a DPSP.
**Back:** *"Livelihood, Resources, Wealth, Pay, Workers, Childhood: (a)–(f) in six words."*

### Flashcard Art-39A — Article 39A
**Front:** Article 39A — Equal justice and free legal aid
**Provision:** The State shall secure that the operation of the legal system promotes justice, on a basis of equal opportunity, and shall, in particular, provide free legal aid, by suitable legislation or schemes or in any other way, to ensure that opportunities for securing justice are not denied to any citizen by reason of economic or other disabilities.
**Learn:**
- Inserted by the 42nd Amendment (1976), a socialistic-category directive.
- Implemented through the Legal Services Authorities Act, 1987 — statutory, not constitutional; NALSA at the apex, State Legal Services Authorities, District Legal Services Authorities and Taluk committees.
- Lok Adalats under the 1987 Act have civil-court status and their awards are binding and non-appealable.
- Judicially read into Art 21 as part of fair procedure: Hussainara Khatoon (1979), M.H. Hoskot (1978).
- Eligibility categories include SC/ST, women and children, persons with disability, persons in custody and low-income litigants (income ceiling notified by the appropriate government, varying by State).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 42nd Amendment (1976); Legal Services Authorities Act, 1987; NALSA; Lok Adalats; Art 21 (fair procedure); Art 14 (equality before law); 2023 GS-II Q2 routed to this owner (10 marks, assess NALSA's role).
**Trap:** NALSA is statutory, not a constitutional body. Income ceiling for legal aid is not a single national figure. The Lok Adalat award is "binding and non-appealable" under the Act; a candidate who calls Art 39A "a Fundamental Right" is wrong.
**Back:** *"Justice not bought: Art 39A — free legal aid; statute 1987; Lok Adalat awards bind."*

### Flashcard Art-40 — Article 40
**Front:** Article 40 — Organisation of village panchayats
**Provision:** The State shall take steps to organise village panchayats and endow them with such powers and authority as may be necessary to enable them to function as units of self-government.
**Learn:**
- Gandhian-category directive (in the textbook grouping).
- Constitutionally implemented by the 73rd Amendment (1992) which inserted Part IX (Arts 243–243O) on Panchayati Raj.
- The 74th Amendment (1992) separately constitutionalised municipalities (Part IXA); it is not the textual implementation of Art 40.
- Village-level self-government: gram sabha, panchayats at three tiers, reservation for SC/ST and women (one-third of seats, Art 243D).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 73rd Amendment (1992) — Part IX, Art 243–243O, Eleventh Schedule (29 subjects); 74th (Part IXA, Twelfth Schedule); Art 243G (panchayat powers).
**Trap:** Art 40 is a DPSP (non-justiciable). The 73rd Amendment, not Art 40 itself, carries the enforceable Part IX mechanism. Do not say the 74th Amendment implemented Art 40; it is municipal (Part IXA). Eleventh Schedule lists panchayat subjects; Twelfth Schedule lists municipal subjects.
**Back:** *"Village is a unit of self-government: Art 40 directive, 73rd Amdt gives the machinery."*

### Flashcard Art-41 — Article 41
**Front:** Article 41 — Right to work, to education and to public assistance in certain cases
**Provision:** The State shall, within the limits of its economic capacity and development, make effective provision for securing the right to work, to education and to public assistance in cases of unemployment, old age, sickness and disablement, and in other cases of undeserved want.
**Learn:**
- Socialistic-category directive; the "limits of economic capacity and development" qualifier is the reason it is not enforceable against the State.
- Three-part content: right to work; right to education; public assistance in unemployment, old age, sickness, disablement and undeserved want.
- Linked to Art 21A (right to education, 86th Amendment) and to Art 45 (early childhood) as the education directives.
- Implemented through statutes and schemes, not Art 41 itself: MGNREGA (2005) was repealed in December 2025 and replaced by the Viksit Bharat–Guarantee for Rozgar and Ajeevika Mission (Gramin) Act, 2025 (VB-G RAM G) (✅ corrected: was "MGNREGA (2005)" presented as the current scheme; Wikipedia MGNREGA page, passage c. 18–19 Dec 2025; assent and commencement dates not on that page).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Unni Krishnan (1993) read the right to education into Art 21 in light of Arts 41, 45 and 46 (DPSP illuminating a FR); 86th Amendment (2002) gave textual effect via Art 21A.
**Trap:** "Within the limits of economic capacity" is the qualifier; do not write Art 41 as unlimited. The right to work in Art 41 is a directive, not the Art 21 FR. Art 41 does not itself make education free; Art 21A (FR, 6–14) does.
**Back:** *"Work, learn, get help when unemployed, old, sick or disabled — within the State's means."*

### Flashcard Art-42 — Article 42
**Front:** Article 42 — Provision for just and humane conditions of work and maternity relief
**Provision:** The State shall make provision for securing just and humane conditions of work and for maternity relief.
**Learn:**
- Socialistic-category directive for workers and women.
- Maternity relief is linked to the Maternity Benefit Act, 1961 (statutory implementation).
- Pairs with Art 39(d) equal pay and Art 43 living wage.
- Note the textual order: "just and humane conditions" first, then maternity relief.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Maternity Benefit Act, 1961; Art 39(e) (health and strength of workers); Art 43 (living wage); Art 43A (workers' participation).
**Trap:** The text says "maternity relief", not "maternity leave" as a fixed number of weeks. Do not claim Art 42 is a Fundamental Right; it is a directive.
**Back:** *"Humane work and maternity relief: Art 42, paired with Maternity Benefit Act 1961."*

### Flashcard Art-43 — Article 43
**Front:** Article 43 — Living wage, etc., for workers
**Provision:** The State shall endeavour to secure, by suitable legislation or economic organisation or in any other way, to all workers, agricultural, industrial or otherwise, work, a living wage, conditions of work ensuring a decent standard of life and full enjoyment of leisure and social and cultural opportunities, and, in particular, the State shall endeavour to promote cottage industries on an individual or co-operative basis in rural areas.
**Learn:**
- Two-part directive: (i) workers' living wage, decent life, leisure and cultural opportunities; (ii) promotion of cottage industries in rural areas.
- Textbook grouping puts cottage industry under Gandhian and living wage under socialistic.
- Statutory anchors: Minimum Wages Act, 1948; Payment of Wages Act, 1936; Bonus Act, 1965 (owner list).
- Art 43A (42nd Amendment) is the next article: workers' participation in management.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 39(d) equal pay; Art 42 humane conditions; Art 43A; Art 43B (co-operatives, 97th Amendment); Art 40 (panchayats).
**Trap:** Cottage industries are "on an individual or co-operative basis" in "rural areas"; do not say "urban small industries". Living wage is an aim, not a fixed statutory number. Art 43 is not Art 43A.
**Back:** *"Living wage and cottage craft: Art 43 serves workers and rural homes."*

### Flashcard Art-43A — Article 43A
**Front:** Article 43A — Participation of workers in management of industries
**Provision:** The State shall take steps, by suitable legislation or in any other way, to secure the participation of workers in the management of undertakings, establishments or other organisations engaged in any industry.
**Learn:**
- Inserted by the 42nd Amendment (1976), in the socialistic category.
- A directive on industrial democracy; implementation is by statute and institutional schemes, not by Art 43A itself.
- Parallels the Industrial Disputes Act, 1947 mechanisms (works committees, joint management councils) ⚠️ (verify statutory form before using in an answer).
- Sits between living wage (Art 43) and co-operatives (Art 43B).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 42nd Amendment (1976); Art 43; Art 43B (97th, co-operatives); Industrial Disputes Act, 1947 ⚠️.
**Trap:** It is a directive, not a Fundamental Right to participate in management. Do not assign it to "Gandhian" just because it is later in the article sequence; the owner classifies it as socialistic.
**Back:** *"Workers in the boardroom: Art 43A, 42nd Amdt (1976), by law or otherwise."*

### Flashcard Art-43B — Article 43B
**Front:** Article 43B — Promotion of co-operative societies
**Provision:** The State shall endeavour to promote voluntary formation, autonomous functioning, democratic control and professional management of co-operative societies.
**Learn:**
- Inserted by the 97th Amendment (2011); Gandhian-category directive in the textbook grouping.
- Four keywords: voluntary, autonomous, democratic control, professional management.
- The 97th Amendment also inserted Part IXB (Arts 243ZH–243ZT) on co-operatives; the Supreme Court read it down in Union of India v Rajendra Shah (2021) — is reported to have held Part IXB invalid as applied to co-operatives of State-level character for want of State ratification ⚠️ (primary judgment not reached; do not quote as settled).
- Co-operatives are chiefly a State subject (State List Entry 32 ✅ Wikipedia State List: "co-operative societies" named in the incorporation entry); multi-State co-operatives fall under Union competence ⚠️ (entry not verified).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 97th Amendment (2011); Part IXB; Union of India v Rajendra Shah (2021) ⚠️; Art 43 (cottage industries on co-operative basis); Art 19(1)(c) (right to form associations).
**Trap:** Art 43B is about co-operatives, not "co-operative banking" alone. The 97th Amendment added the Part IXB mechanism (Art 243ZH–ZT), and the Court read Part IXB down in 2021 — do not write that Part IXB was struck entirely. Art 43B is a directive, not a FR.
**Back:** *"Four keywords for co-ops: voluntary, autonomous, democratic, professional — 97th Amdt (2011)."*

### Flashcard Art-44 — Article 44
**Front:** Article 44 — Uniform civil code for the citizens
**Provision:** The State shall endeavour to secure for the citizens a uniform civil code throughout the territory of India.
**Learn:**
- Liberal-intellectual category; non-justiciable under Art 37.
- Civil code covers personal law: marriage, divorce, maintenance, succession, adoption and guardianship. Criminal and contract law are already uniform.
- Personal laws sit in Concurrent List Entry 5, so both Parliament and State legislatures may legislate.
- Goa has a common family-law regime with exceptions; Uttarakhand's UCC Act commenced on 27 January 2025 (owner record; ⚠️ primary source not reached).
- Hindu Code statutes (mid-1950s) are community-specific codification, not a uniform code.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Mohd Ahmed Khan v Shah Bano Begum (1985), Sarla Mudgal (1995), John Vallamattom (2003) — courts observed Art 44 remained unimplemented (observations, not directions); Shayara Bano v Union of India (2017) set aside instant triple talaq; Muslim Women (Protection of Rights on Marriage) Act, 2019; Special Marriage Act, 1954; Law Commission (21st, 2018 consultation paper) favoured reform within personal laws.
