Uttarakhand amendment (downgraded, ⚠️ still-unverified): the owner file reports a January 2026 Amendment Ordinance and a "Uniform Civil Code, Uttarakhand (Amendment) Act, 2026" (GO 7 Apr 2026); the official UCC portal fetched on 2026-10-30 lists UCC Rules 2025 and four Rules amendments but no such Act or undated Ordinance. Do not cite as settled law.
**Trap:** Courts have urged, not ordered, a UCC. Do not write that the Supreme Court "directed" a UCC. The Special Marriage Act is opt-in, not a route for "any two citizens" automatically. Art 44 is a DPSP, not a Fundamental Right; the religious-freedom side is Arts 25–28.
**Back:** *"One civil code, not yet: Art 44, personal law only, urged by courts, never ordered."*

### Flashcard Art-45 — Article 45
**Front:** Article 45 — Provision for early childhood care and education to children below the age of six years (as amended by the 86th Amendment)
**Provision:** The State shall endeavour to provide early childhood care and education for all children until they complete the age of six years.
**Learn:**
- Original text (1950) directed free and compulsory education for all children up to 14 years within ten years; the 86th Amendment (2002) recast it to early childhood care and education up to six.
- The 86th Amendment simultaneously inserted Art 21A (FR: free and compulsory education for children aged 6–14) and Art 51A(k) (parental duty to provide education for children aged 6–14).
- RTE Act, 2009 implements Art 21A; Art 45 covers the under-six stage.
- Liberal-intellectual category (textbook grouping).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 86th Amendment (2002); Art 21A; Art 51A(k); RTE Act, 2009; Unni Krishnan (1993) read education into Art 21 illuminated by Arts 41, 45, 46.
**Trap:** Art 45 is a DPSP covering children under six; Art 21A is a Fundamental Right covering ages 6–14. Do not swap them. The 2025 Q55 provision-to-Part matching: Art 21A is Part III, Art 45 is Part IV. The 86th did not "make education a DPSP".
**Back:** *"Under six is Art 45 (directive); six to fourteen is Art 21A (right)."*

### Flashcard Art-46 — Article 46
**Front:** Article 46 — Promotion of educational and economic interests of Scheduled Castes, Scheduled Tribes and other weaker sections
**Provision:** The State shall promote with special care the educational and economic interests of the weaker sections of the people, and, in particular, of the Scheduled Castes and the Scheduled Tribes, and shall protect them from social injustice and all forms of exploitation.
**Learn:**
- Gandhian-category directive in the textbook grouping.
- "Weaker sections" is not defined; SC/ST are named expressly.
- Two duties: promote educational and economic interests; protect from social injustice and all forms of exploitation.
- Backed by reservation provisions (Art 15(4), 16(4), 335 and 330/332 for legislatures) and the Protection of Civil Rights Act, 1955 and SC/ST (Prevention of Atrocities) Act, 1989 ⚠️ (verify the 1989 Act's name and year before citing).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 15(4) (1st Amdt, 1951); Art 16(4); Art 335 (SC/ST claims to services consistent with efficiency); Art 350-A; Art 39(e).
**Trap:** Art 46 names "weaker sections" generally, with SC/ST specifically; do not claim it lists OBCs by name. It is a directive; the reservation is in FR Arts 15(4)/16(4).
**Back:** *"Care for the weakest first: Art 46, SC/ST by name, against social injustice and exploitation."*

### Flashcard Art-47 — Article 47
**Front:** Article 47 — Duty of the State to raise the level of nutrition, the standard of living and to improve public health
**Provision:** The State shall regard the raising of the level of nutrition and the standard of living of its people and the improvement of public health as among its primary duties, and, in particular, the State shall endeavour to bring about prohibition of the consumption, except for medicinal purposes, of intoxicating drinks and of drugs which are injurious to health.
**Learn:**
- Gandhian-category directive; its prohibition clause is the best-known "Gandhian" plank.
- Three duties: nutrition and living standard; public health; prohibition (except medicinal use) of intoxicating drinks and drugs injurious to health.
- Prohibition is State policy; a State may adopt total or partial prohibition under its own competence (State List, Entry 8 intoxicating liquors and Entry 6 public health; ✅ Wikipedia State List).
- Linked to food-security and public-health programmes (National Food Security Act, 2013 ⚠️ verify name and year).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 47 (primary duty language); Art 39(e) and (f); Art 21 (health as part of life); Art 51A(h) and (j) (scientific temper and excellence).
**Trap:** "Except for medicinal purposes" — the prohibition is not a total ban on all consumption; the duty is "endeavour", not an immediate ban. Do not write it as a Fundamental Duty. "Raising nutrition" is a primary duty, which is a higher textual signal than the other directives, but still non-justiciable.
**Back:** *"Nutrition, health, and prohibition except medicine: Art 47 — primary duty, endeavour to ban."*

### Flashcard Art-48 — Article 48
**Front:** Article 48 — Organisation of agriculture and animal husbandry
**Provision:** The State shall endeavour to organise agriculture and animal husbandry on modern and scientific lines and shall, in particular, take steps for preserving and improving the breeds, and prohibiting the slaughter, of cows and calves and other milch and draught cattle.
**Learn:**
- Gandhian-category directive in the textbook grouping (Liberal-intellectual per some texts; follow owner).
- Three elements: modern-scientific agriculture and husbandry; preserving and improving breeds; prohibiting slaughter of cows, calves and other milch and draught cattle.
- Prohibition covers cows, calves and milch and draught cattle only, not all cattle.
- State cattle-slaughter laws are State subject (State List, Entry 15: "preservation, protection and improvement of stock and prevention of animal diseases"; ✅ Wikipedia State List) ⚠️ (application of Entry 15 specifically to slaughter laws not separately verified).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Mohd Hanif Quareshi v State of Bihar (1958) ⚠️ (upheld a partial ban and struck a total ban on bulls/bullocks beyond useful age — verify holding); Art 19(1)(g) and Art 25 challenges; Art 48A (environment, forests, wildlife) is the next article.
**Trap:** Art 48 covers cows, calves and milch and draught cattle; it does not say "all cattle". Do not confuse Art 48 (cattle and agriculture) with Art 48A (environment). The owner's textbook classification puts Art 48 under Gandhian and Art 48A under liberal-intellectual.
**Back:** *"Modern farms, protected milch: Art 48 — science in agriculture, cows and milch cattle spared."*

### Flashcard Art-48A — Article 48A
**Front:** Article 48A — Protection and improvement of environment and safeguarding of forests and wildlife
**Provision:** The State shall endeavour to protect and improve the environment and to safeguard the forests and wildlife of the country.
**Learn:**
- Inserted by the 42nd Amendment (1976); liberal-intellectual category in the textbook grouping.
- Read with Art 51A(g) (citizen duty to protect and improve the natural environment including forests, lakes, rivers, wildlife and to have compassion for living creatures) and Art 21 (right to life includes a clean environment).
- Statutes: Environment (Protection) Act, 1986; Wildlife (Protection) Act, 1972; Forest (Conservation) Act, 1980 ⚠️ (verify the exact years before exam citing).
- Judicial anchor: Subhash Kumar v State of Bihar (1991) — right to pollution-free water and air under Art 21 (✅ owner Supreme Court basic file: "Article 21 includes pollution-free environment"; primary judgment not reached).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 42nd Amendment (1976); Art 51A(g); Art 21; M.C. Mehta line of cases ⚠️.
**Trap:** Art 48A is a directive; Art 51A(g) is the citizen's duty; Art 21 is the right that courts use to enforce environmental protection. Do not say Art 48A is a FR. The 42nd Amendment inserted it, not the 44th.
**Back:** *"Green State, green citizen: Art 48A directs the State; Art 51A(g) binds the citizen."*

### Flashcard Art-49 — Article 49
**Front:** Article 49 — Protection of monuments and places and objects of national importance
**Provision:** It shall be the obligation of the State to protect every monument or place or object of artistic or historic interest, declared by or under law made by Parliament to be of national importance, from spoliation, disfigurement, destruction, removal, disposal or export, as the case may be.
**Learn:**
- Liberal-intellectual category.
- Coverage is limited to monuments and objects declared of national importance by or under a law made by Parliament.
- Implemented by the Ancient Monuments and Archaeological Sites and Remains Act, 1958 (✅ name and year as given in owner Directive-Principles file; primary statute not reached).
- Pairs with Art 51A(f) (cherish composite culture) and the Union List entries on archaeology.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** AMASR Act, 1958 (✅ owner); Art 51A(f) (composite culture); the "declared by or under law made by Parliament" trigger.
**Trap:** The trigger is "national importance" declared by Parliamentary law, not any monument. Art 49 protects against spoliation, disfigurement, destruction, removal, disposal or export — five heads, not "conservation" alone.
**Back:** *"Parliament declares, State protects: Art 49 monuments of national importance."*

### Flashcard Art-50 — Article 50
**Front:** Article 50 — Separation of judiciary from executive
**Provision:** The State shall take steps to separate the judiciary from the executive in the public services of the State.
**Learn:**
- Liberal-intellectual category; a Prelims favourite (2020 Q5).
- Implemented partly by the Code of Criminal Procedure, 1973 (separate executive and judicial magistracy at district level) ⚠️ (verify the effective year and provisions).
- Art 50 covers the State's public services; Arts 233–237 (subordinate judiciary) and Arts 124 and 217 are the wider judicial-independence architecture.
- Independence of the judiciary is also reinforced in Art 121, Art 124 (removal of SC judges) and Art 211.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** CrPC 1973 ⚠️; Arts 233–237; Art 124(4)–(5) (removal of SC judges by address of two-thirds present and voting plus majority of total membership); Art 121 bars discussion of judges' conduct.
**Trap:** Art 50 is a DPSP, not a Fundamental Right; the Constitution does not set a single date for full separation. Do not confuse Art 50 with Art 121 (Parliament may not discuss SC judges' conduct) or Art 124 (appointment and removal of SC judges). 2020 Q5 answer: Art 50.
**Back:** *"Judges apart from officers: Art 50 — separation of judiciary from executive, a DPSP."*

### Flashcard Art-51 — Article 51
**Front:** Article 51 — Promotion of international peace and security
**Provision:** The State shall endeavour to: (a) promote international peace and security; (b) maintain just and honourable relations between nations; (c) foster respect for international law and treaty obligations in the dealings of organised peoples with one another; and (d) encourage settlement of international disputes by arbitration.
**Learn:**
- Liberal-intellectual category; last of the Part IV directives.
- Four clauses: peace and security; just relations; respect for international law and treaties; arbitration of disputes.
- Art 253 empowers Parliament to make laws implementing treaties and international decisions.
- Arbitration is implemented by the Arbitration and Conciliation Act, 1996 (✅ owner cross-reference; primary text not reached).
- Foreign-policy practice: Panchsheel, UN role, Non-Aligned Movement — stated as examples only.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 253 (treaty implementation by Parliament); Union List Entry 14 (treaties, agreements, and implementing them) and Art 246 place foreign affairs with the Union; Art 51 is the Part IV reference point for foreign policy.
**Trap:** Art 51 is a directive: the State "endeavours" and the duty does not give a citizen a claim. "Encourage settlement by arbitration" is one clause among four; it is not the whole article. Do not call Art 51 a "Fundamental Duty" (that is Art 51A).
**Back:** *"Peace, law, treaties, arbitration: Art 51 — four endeavours for the world."*

### PART IV-A — Fundamental Duties (Article 51A)

### Flashcard Art-51A — Article 51A
**Front:** Article 51A — Fundamental Duties of every citizen of India (eleven duties, (a)–(k))
**Provision:** It shall be the duty of every citizen of India — (a) to abide by the Constitution and respect its ideals and institutions, the National Flag and the National Anthem; (b) to cherish and follow the noble ideals which inspired our national struggle for freedom; (c) to uphold and protect the sovereignty, unity and integrity of India; (d) to defend the country and render national service when called upon to do so; (e) to promote harmony and the spirit of common brotherhood amongst all the people of India transcending religious, linguistic, regional or sectional diversities, to renounce practices derogatory to the dignity of women; (f) to value and preserve the rich heritage of our composite culture; (g) to protect and improve the natural environment including forests, lakes, rivers and wild life, and to have compassion for living creatures; (h) to develop the scientific temper, humanism and the spirit of inquiry and reform; (i) to safeguard public property and to abjure violence; (j) to strive towards excellence in all spheres of individual and collective activity so that the nation constantly rises to higher levels of endeavour and achievement; (k) who is a parent or guardian, to provide opportunities for education to his child or, as the case may be, ward between the age of six and fourteen years.
**Learn:**
- Part IV-A (single Article); borrowed in spirit from the erstwhile USSR; added by the 42nd Amendment (1976) with 10 duties (a)–(j).
- Swaran Singh Committee (1976) recommended 8 duties and also suggested penalties for non-compliance and a tax duty — those were not accepted.
- The 86th Amendment (2002) added clause (k) — education for children aged 6–14 — making the total eleven.
- Duties apply to citizens only, are non-justiciable, and cannot be enforced by a writ on their own; Parliament may legislate to enforce them.
- J.S. Verma Committee (1999) mapped existing laws that enforce some duties — e.g. Prevention of Insults to National Honour Act, 1971; Protection of Civil Rights Act, 1955; Wildlife (Protection) Act, 1972; Representation of the People Act, 1951 — without adding new duties.
- Durga Dutt v Union of India (W.P.(C) 67/2022) is pending on the owner's record; the 11 September 2024 order sought a synopsis of enactments and relisted; do not treat it as a final holding.
- 📰 ⚠️ Current-status check: the owner Fundamental-Duties file (control date 28 Aug 2026) says Durga Dutt is still pending; not re-verified here, so confirm before quoting.
**Mnemonic letter-map:** A (a) Cheerful (b) Unit (c) Defends (d) Harmony (e) Preserves (f) Greenery (g) Humanity (h) Safeguards (i) Excellence (j) Educates (k).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 42nd Amendment (1976) inserted Part IV-A; 86th Amendment (2002) added (k); Swaran Singh Committee (1976); Verma Committee (1999); Durga Dutt (pending); SC (1992) — a law giving effect to a FD may be reasonable under Art 14/19 (owner record ⚠️ verify case name). Art 51A(g) with Art 21 and 48A.
**Trap:** There are 11, not 10 (the 42nd gave 10). Swaran Singh recommended 8 (not 10). The duty to pay tax and to vote are not Fundamental Duties. The FD list is for citizens only, and it is not justiciable. Not 'Part IV' — it is Part IV-A.
**Back:** *"A Cheerful Unit Defends Harmony, Preserves Greenery, Humanity Safeguards Excellence, Educates — (a) to (k), 11 duties."*


### PART V — The Union (Articles 52–78)

### Flashcard Art-52 — Article 52
**Front:** Article 52 — The President of India
**Provision:** There shall be a President of India.
**Learn:**
- Head of the Union and the first constitutional office in Part V; the President is the nominal (constitutional) head, while the Council of Ministers headed by the PM is the real executive (Art 74).
- Art 52 is only the creating clause; powers and qualifications are in Arts 53–62.
- The President is the "head of State, not of the executive" (Ambedkar's formulation, owner record).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Arts 53–62 (office, election, term, qualifications, impeachment); Art 74 (binding Council advice); Art 79 (Parliament includes the President).
**Trap:** Art 52 does not give powers; a candidate who cites it for executive power has cited the wrong clause. The Vice-President is Art 63, not Art 52.
**Back:** *"Art 52 creates the head; Arts 53–62 say what the head may do."*

### Flashcard Art-53 — Article 53
**Front:** Article 53 — Executive power of the Union
**Provision:** (1) The executive power of the Union shall be vested in the President and shall be exercised by him either directly or through officers subordinate to him in accordance with this Constitution. (2) Without prejudice to the generality of the foregoing, the supreme command of the Defence Forces of the Union shall be vested in the President and the exercise thereof shall be regulated by law. (3) Nothing in this article shall (a) prevent Parliament from conferring by law functions on authorities other than the President, or (b) be deemed to transfer to the President any functions conferred by any existing law on any government, authority or person other than the President.
**Learn:**
- Executive power is formally vested in the President; in practice exercised by the Council of Ministers under Art 74.
- Supreme command of the Defence Forces (cl 2) is a formal, nominal command; the exercise is regulated by law.
- Cl (3) preserves Parliament's power to confer functions on other authorities.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 74 (Council aid and advice); Art 77 (business rules); Art 73 (extent of Union executive); Art 352 (President's emergency power acts on the Cabinet's advice).
**Trap:** "Supreme command" of the armed forces is nominal; the actual operational control is through the Defence Minister and the civil-military structure. Do not confuse Art 53 with Art 74 (advice binding) or Art 73 (extent).
**Back:** *"Vested in the President, exercised through ministers; supreme command is nominal."*

### Flashcard Art-54 — Article 54
**Front:** Article 54 — Election of President
**Provision:** The President shall be elected by the members of an electoral college consisting of (a) the elected members of both Houses of Parliament; and (b) the elected members of the Legislative Assemblies of the States (and, after the 70th Amendment, the elected members of the Legislative Assemblies of the Union territories of Delhi and Puducherry, as provided in the Article's explanation).
**Learn:**
- Electoral college: elected MPs (LS + RS) and elected MLAs of States; nominated members of Parliament and members of Legislative Councils do not vote.
- Delhi and Puducherry elected legislators vote (70th Amendment, 1992).
- Indirect election keeps the office symbolic; direct election was rejected as costly for a nominal head.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 55 (manner of election and vote value); Art 71 (SC decides disputes); 70th Amendment (1992); Presidential and Vice-Presidential Elections Act, 1952.
**Trap:** Art 54 (President) includes MLAs; Art 66 (VP) excludes MLAs. Nominated MPs don't vote for the President. Do not write "elected by the people directly".
**Back:** *"MPs and MLAs elect the President; nominated and Council members don't."*

### Flashcard Art-55 — Article 55
**Front:** Article 55 — Manner of election of President
**Provision:** (1) As far as practicable, the election shall be held in accordance with the system of proportional representation by means of the single transferable vote and the voting at such election shall be by secret ballot. (2) (Formula) The vote value of each elected MLA is determined by the population of the State ÷ total number of elected MLAs, divided by 1000 (with a rounding rule), and the vote value of each MP is determined from the total MLA vote value ÷ total elected MPs of both Houses. (3) Uniformity of scale: as far as practicable, uniform scale of representation between the States as a whole and the Union, to be fixed on the basis of the 1971 census until the first census after 2026 is published.
**Learn:**
- Method: proportional representation by single transferable vote (PR-STV), secret ballot; the winner needs the quota (majority of valid votes).
- MLA value = (State population ÷ elected MLAs) ÷ 1000, frozen at the 1971 census by the 84th Amendment (2001) until the first census after 2026.
- MP value = total MLA votes ÷ total elected MPs of both Houses.
- Disputes go to the Supreme Court (Art 71).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 84th Amendment (2001); Art 71 (SC jurisdiction); Presidential and Vice-Presidential Elections Act, 1952 and Rules, 1974.
**Trap:** Vote values use the 1971 census, not the 1951 or 2011 census; MP value is based on total MLA votes, not on population directly. 2018 Q32 and 2023 Q80 are routed to this owner (key not in repository record — do not infer the answer).
**Back:** *"Quota by single transfer, values by 1971: MLA = pop ÷ MLAs ÷ 1000."*

### Flashcard Art-56 — Article 56
**Front:** Article 56 — Term of office of President
**Provision:** (1) The President shall hold office for a term of five years from the date on which he enters upon his office, subject to: (a) he may, by writing under his hand addressed to the Vice-President, resign; (b) he may be removed by impeachment under Art 61 for violation of the Constitution. (2) A person who holds office as President (whether by reason of his election or otherwise) shall, notwithstanding the expiry of his term, continue to hold office until his successor enters upon his office.
**Learn:**
- Five-year term from the date he enters office; no fixed limit on re-election (Art 57).
- Resignation letter is addressed to the Vice-President (not the Prime Minister or the Speaker).
- Continuation clause (cl 2): the outgoing President holds office until the successor enters.
- Removal only by impeachment (Art 61); no President has been impeached.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 57 (eligibility for re-election); Art 61 (impeachment); Art 62 (vacancy filling); Rajendra Prasad served two full terms (1950–62), the only one to do so (owner record).
**Trap:** Resignation is to the Vice-President, not the President's Council or the Chief Justice. Do not say the term ends on expiry "automatically without a successor"; cl (2) keeps him in office. Art 56 is not Art 56(1)(b) impeachment with a separate procedure from Art 61.
**Back:** *"Five years, resign to the VP, impeach for the Constitution, and stay till successor enters."*

### Flashcard Art-57 — Article 57
**Front:** Article 57 — Eligibility for re-election
**Provision:** A person who holds, or who has held, office as President shall, subject to the other provisions of this Constitution, be eligible for re-election to that office.
**Learn:**
- No limit on the number of re-elections; contrast with the US two-term limit.
- The President is re-electable subject to the qualifications in Art 58 and the other conditions of office.
- Owner record: only Dr Rajendra Prasad served two full presidential terms.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 56 (term); Art 58 (qualifications); Art 59 (conditions of office).
**Trap:** Indian Presidents have no two-term cap; the US President has. Do not assert a limit.
**Back:** *"No cap on re-election: unlike the US, India sets no term-limit for the President."*

### Flashcard Art-58 — Article 58
**Front:** Article 58 — Qualifications for election as President
**Provision:** (1) No person shall be eligible for election as President unless he (a) is a citizen of India; (b) has completed the age of thirty-five years; and (c) is qualified for election as a member of the House of the People. (2) A person shall not be eligible for election as President if he holds any office of profit under the Government of India or the Government of any State or under any local or other authority subject to the control of any of the said Governments. (Explanation: the office of President, VP, Governor of a State, or Minister of the Union or a State is not an office of profit for this purpose.)
**Learn:**
- Three qualifications: citizen, 35 years, qualified for Lok Sabha.
- No office of profit under the Union, State, or any authority controlled by them, with a listed exception for President, VP, Governor and ministers.
- Age 35 contrasts with 25 for Lok Sabha (Art 84) and 30 for Rajya Sabha (Art 84).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 84 (LS/RS qualifications); Art 102 (disqualifications for membership); Art 59 (conditions of office).
**Trap:** Age 35 for President and Vice-President alike; LS is 25, RS is 30. Art 58 uses "office of profit"; a Governor is exempt under the Explanation. Do not confuse Art 58 with Art 84.
**Back:** *"Citizen, thirty-five, LS-qualified, no profit office: Art 58 ("35" is the number to remember)."*

### Flashcard Art-59 — Article 59
**Front:** Article 59 — Conditions of President's office
**Provision:** (1) The President shall not be a member of either House of Parliament or of a House of the Legislature of any State, and if a member of such a House is elected President, he shall be deemed to vacate his seat on the date on which he enters upon his office as President. (2) The President shall not hold any other office of profit. (3) The President shall be entitled without payment of rent to the use of his official residences and to such emoluments, allowances and privileges as may be determined by Parliament by law, and until so determined, as specified in the Second Schedule. (4) The emoluments and allowances of the President shall not be diminished during his term of office.
**Learn:**
- Cl (1): no membership of Parliament or a State legislature; sitting member's seat is vacated on taking office.
- Cl (2): no office of profit during term.
- Cl (3): emoluments and privileges determined by Parliament; the Second Schedule applies until so determined.
- Cl (4): emoluments cannot be diminished during the term.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 58(2) (eligibility); Art 102 (disqualification); Second Schedule (President's emoluments); Art 59(4) no reduction during term.
**Trap:** Cl (1) vacates the seat on the date he enters office — not on election; cl (4) protects emoluments during the term, not after. Do not write that the President may hold any office of profit with leave.
**Back:** *"No seat, no profit office, pay fixed by Parliament but not reduced."*

### Flashcard Art-60 — Article 60
**Front:** Article 60 — Oath or affirmation by the President
**Provision:** Every President and every person discharging the functions of the President shall, before entering upon his office, make and subscribe an oath or affirmation in the presence of the Chief Justice of India or, in his absence, the senior-most Judge of the Supreme Court available, to (a) faithfully execute the office of President, (b) preserve, protect and defend the Constitution and the law, and (c) devote himself to the service and well-being of the people of India.
**Learn:**
- Oath administered by the Chief Justice of India (or senior-most available SC judge).
- Three limbs: faithful execution of office; preserve, protect and defend the Constitution and the law; devote to service and well-being of the people.
- Applies to persons discharging the President's functions too (acting President).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 69 (VP oath administered by the President); Art 75(4) (ministers' oath before the President); Art 124 (SC judges' oath, with the CJI).
**Trap:** President's oath is by the CJI — the reverse of the VP's oath, which is by the President (Art 69). Do not write "Speaker" or "Vice-President" as the administering officer.
**Back:** *"CJI swears the President: protect the Constitution and the law, serve the people."*

### Flashcard Art-61 — Article 61
**Front:** Article 61 — Procedure for impeachment of the President
**Provision:** (1) When a President is to be impeached for violation of the Constitution, the charge shall be preferred by either House of Parliament. (2) No such charge shall be preferred unless (a) the proposal is contained in a resolution which has been moved after at least fourteen days' notice in writing signed by not less than one-fourth of the total number of members of the House has been given of their intention to move the resolution, and (b) such resolution has been passed by a majority of not less than two-thirds of the total membership of the House. (3) When a charge has been so preferred by one House, the other House shall investigate the charge, and the President shall have the right to appear and to be represented at the investigation. (4) If, as a result of the investigation, a resolution is passed by a majority of not less than two-thirds of the total membership of the House, the President shall, on such resolution being passed, cease to hold office from the date on which the resolution is so passed.
**Learn:**
- Only ground: "violation of the Constitution" (undefined in the text).
- Charge may be preferred by either House; 14 days' notice signed by at least one-fourth of the House; passed by two-thirds of the total membership of that House.
- The other House investigates; the President may appear and be represented; a two-thirds majority in that House removes him.
- Nominated MPs may take part in impeachment (but not in the presidential election); state MLAs do not.
- No President has been impeached.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 56(1)(b); Art 54 (electoral college); Art 67(b) (VP removal, contrast); Art 124 (SC judges' removal, for comparison).
**Trap:** The charge is "preferred" (not "initiated"); either House may initiate; two-thirds of the total membership, not of present and voting, in both stages. Impeachment is not the same as removal of the VP, which needs an RS resolution and LS agreement, with no ground.
**Back:** *"One ground, two houses, two-thirds twice: Art 61 — violation of the Constitution."*

### Flashcard Art-62 — Article 62
**Front:** Article 62 — Election to fill vacancy in the office of President and term of office of person so elected
**Provision:** (1) An election to fill a vacancy caused by expiry of a President's term shall be completed before the expiry of the term, and in any other case as soon as possible and in any case within six months from the occurrence of the vacancy. (2) A person elected to fill a vacancy shall be entitled to hold office for a full term of five years from the date on which he enters upon his office.
**Learn:**
- Two timings: before expiry, for normal term-end; within six months, for a casual vacancy (death, resignation, removal).
- A successor elected to a casual vacancy gets a fresh full five-year term.
- Art 65 covers the Vice-President acting as President in the interim.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 65 (VP acts as President); Art 56 (term); Art 68 (VP's vacancy, different schedule).
**Trap:** Presidential casual vacancy: within six months (Art 62(1)); Vice-Presidential casual vacancy: "as soon as possible" (Art 68), with no six-month deadline. A candidate who cites six months for the VP has merged the two.
**Back:** *"Expiry: before the end. Casual: within six months. Successor gets a full five years."*

### Flashcard Art-63 — Article 63
**Front:** Article 63 — The Vice-President of India
**Provision:** There shall be a Vice-President of India.
**Learn:**
- Creates the second constitutional office; the VP is the ex officio Chairman of the Rajya Sabha (Art 64) and succeeds to President's functions in a vacancy (Art 65).
- Only the creating clause; qualifications and electoral college are in Arts 66–68.
- Owner record (checked 28 Aug 2026, re-verify before use): C. P. Radhakrishnan, elected 9 September 2025 and in office from 12 September 2025. (✅ Wikipedia "Vice President of India" lists him as incumbent since 12 Sep 2025; that page is undated, so re-verify before use; the 9 Sep 2025 election date is not shown on it.)
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 64 (Chairman of RS); Art 65 (acting President); Art 66 (election); Art 67 (term and removal); Art 68 (vacancy); Art 69 (oath).
**Trap:** The VP is not a member of the Rajya Sabha (Art 64 is "ex officio Chairman" only); the Chairman does not vote except to break a tie (Art 100). Do not confuse Art 63 with Art 64.
**Back:** *"Art 63 creates the VP; Art 64 makes him the Rajya Sabha's chair."*

### Flashcard Art-64 — Article 64
**Front:** Article 64 — The Vice-President to be ex officio Chairman of the Council of States
**Provision:** The Vice-President shall be ex officio Chairman of the Council of States (Rajya Sabha) and shall not hold any other office of profit. (Owner note: he is not a member of the House.)
**Learn:**
- Ex officio Chairman: the VP presides over the Rajya Sabha by virtue of office.
- He is not a member of the Rajya Sabha; he cannot vote except in the event of a tie (Art 100).
- Deputy Chairman presides in his absence (Art 89).
- While a resolution for his own removal is under consideration he does not preside (Art 92).
- Acts as Tenth Schedule adjudicator for RS members' anti-defection disqualifications (para 6); subject to judicial review (Kihoto Hollohan, 1992).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 89 (Deputy Chairman); Art 92 (no presiding during own removal); Art 100 (casting vote); Tenth Schedule para 6; Kihoto Hollohan v Zachillhu (1992).
**Trap:** The Chairman is not a member and does not certify Money Bills (Art 110 — Lok Sabha Speaker's role). The Speaker, by contrast, is a member of the Lok Sabha. Art 64 bars "any other office of profit".
**Back:** *"Ex officio, not a member: the VP chairs the Rajya Sabha; casts a vote only to break a tie."*

### Flashcard Art-65 — Article 65
**Front:** Article 65 — The Vice-President to act as President or to discharge his functions during casual vacancies in the office, or during the absence, of President
**Provision:** (1) In the event of the occurrence of any vacancy in the office of the President by reason of his death, resignation or removal, or otherwise, the Vice-President shall act as President until the date on which a new President, elected to fill such vacancy, enters upon his office. (2) When the President is unable to discharge his functions owing to absence, illness or any other cause, the Vice-President shall discharge his functions until the date on which the President resumes his functions. (3) The Vice-President acting as, or discharging the functions of, President shall have all the powers and immunities of the President and be entitled to such emoluments, allowances and privileges as are determined by Parliament.
**Learn:**
- Two situations: vacancy (acts as President until successor enters) and absence or inability (discharges functions until the President resumes).
- No six-month cap appears in Art 65 itself: the VP acts until the successor enters office. The six-month limit governs the presidential election (Art 62(1)); the owner line calling the VP's acting term "maximum six months" conflates the two (✅ corrected).
- Acting VP does not hold the full unexpired term (contrast the US VP succession).
- Cl (3): full powers and immunities of the President.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 62(1) (election within six months); Art 70 (Parliament's law for other contingencies); Art 68 (VP's vacancy, not 6 months).
**Trap:** The VP "acts" as President; he does not "succeed" for the unexpired term. Do not say the six-month cap applies to the VP's own vacancy (that is "as soon as possible", Art 68).
**Back:** *"Vacancy: VP acts. Absence: VP discharges. All powers, no full term."*

### Flashcard Art-66 — Article 66
**Front:** Article 66 — Election of Vice-President
**Provision:** (1) The Vice-President shall be elected by the members of an electoral college consisting of the members of both Houses of Parliament in accordance with the system of proportional representation by means of the single transferable vote and the voting at such election shall be by secret ballot. (2) The Vice-President shall not be a member of either House of Parliament or of a House of the Legislature of any State, and if a member of either House of Parliament is elected Vice-President he shall be deemed to have vacated his seat in that House on the date he enters upon his office. (3) No person shall be eligible for election as Vice-President unless he (a) is a citizen of India; (b) has completed the age of thirty-five years; and (c) is qualified for election as a member of the Council of States. (✅ corrected: the earlier sub-clause (2) text was a paraphrase and omitted the eligibility clause; checked against Wikisource Part V.)
**Learn:**
- Electoral college: members of both Houses of Parliament — elected and nominated — and no State legislative members (owner record).
- Method: PR-STV, secret ballot; no MLA vote values (contrast Art 55).
- Narrower base than the President's electoral college.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 54 (President's college includes MLAs); Art 55 (vote values for President only); Art 71 (SC decides disputes); Presidential and Vice-Presidential Elections Act, 1952.
**Trap:** Nominated MPs vote in the VP election but not in the presidential election; State MLAs vote in the presidential election but not the VP election. A candidate who lists "MLAs" for the VP has reversed the two colleges.
**Back:** *"VP: MPs only (elected and nominated); President: MPs plus MLAs."*

### Flashcard Art-67 — Article 67
**Front:** Article 67 — Term of office of Vice-President
**Provision:** The Vice-President shall hold office for a term of five years from the date on which he enters upon his office: provided that — (a) he may, by writing under his hand addressed to the President, resign his office; (b) he may be removed from his office by a resolution of the Council of States passed by a majority of all the then members of the Council and agreed to by the House of the People; provided that no resolution for the purpose of clause (b) shall be moved unless at least fourteen days' notice has been given of the intention to move it; and (c) he shall, notwithstanding the expiration of his term, continue to hold office until his successor enters upon his office. (✅ corrected: "effective majority" was a gloss not in the text, and the 14-day notice is a proviso; checked against Wikisource Part V.)
**Learn:**
- Five-year term; resignation addressed to the President.
- Removal: by a resolution of the Rajya Sabha passed by a majority of all its then members and agreed to by the House of the People (Lok Sabha); 14 days' notice of the motion.
- No ground is required for removal (contrast impeachment in Art 61).
- Continues until successor takes office.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 61 (presidential impeachment); Art 68 (vacancy); Art 64 (Chairman's removal interplay with Art 92).
**Trap:** The VP is removed by a Rajya Sabha resolution, not by impeachment; the initiation is only in the RS, and no ground is required. Do not write "two-thirds" for the VP.
**Back:** *"Five years; resign to the President; removed by RS first, LS agreeing, no ground."*

### Flashcard Art-68 — Article 68
**Front:** Article 68 — Time of holding election to fill vacancy in the office of Vice-President and term of office of person so elected
**Provision:** (1) An election to fill a vacancy caused by expiry of the term of office of Vice-President shall be completed before the expiry of the term. (2) An election to fill a vacancy caused by any other cause shall be held as soon as possible after the occurrence of the vacancy, and the person elected shall be entitled to hold office for a full term of five years from the date on which he enters upon his office.
**Learn:**
- Expiry: complete before term ends.
- Casual vacancy: "as soon as possible" — no six-month deadline (contrast Art 62(1) for the President).
- Successor gets a fresh full five years.
- Owner record, partly confirmed (✅ Wikipedia "Vice President of India": Dhankhar's term ended 21 July 2025 with the footnote "Resigned"; Radhakrishnan incumbent since 12 Sept 2025). The election date of 9 Sept 2025 is ⚠️ still unverified.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 62 (President's schedule); Art 63; Art 67 (resignation and term).
**Trap:** Six months is the President's rule (Art 62); the VP's casual vacancy is "as soon as possible". Do not invent a six-month deadline for the VP.
**Back:** *"VP casual vacancy: as soon as possible, not six months; a fresh five-year term."*

### Flashcard Art-69 — Article 69
**Front:** Article 69 — Oath or affirmation by the Vice-President
**Provision:** Every Vice-President shall, before entering upon his office, make and subscribe an oath or affirmation before the President, or some person appointed in that behalf by the President, in the following form, which is set out in the article itself (✅ corrected: the earlier text said "the Third Schedule"; Art 69 does not cite it; Wikisource Part V).
**Learn:**
- Oath administered by the President (or person appointed by him).
- The oath wording is set out inline in Art 69 ("I, A.B., do swear in the name of God..."); Art 69 does not cite the Third Schedule, and it is not "the same form" as Art 75(4). Art 75(4) is the clause that cites the Third Schedule (✅ corrected; Wikisource Part V).
- Parallel to Art 60 (President's oath by CJI).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 60 (President's oath by CJI); Art 75(4) (minister's oath); Third Schedule (forms of oath).
**Trap:** The VP's oath is by the President; the President's oath is by the CJI. Candidates who reverse these lose the marks.
**Back:** *"President swears the VP; CJI swears the President."*

### Flashcard Art-70 — Article 70
**Front:** Article 70 — Discharge of President's functions in other contingencies
**Provision:** Parliament may by law make such provision as it thinks fit for the discharge of the functions of the President in any contingency not provided for in this Chapter.
**Learn:**
- Residual power: Parliament may fill any contingency not covered by Arts 54–69 (e.g. where both President and VP are absent).
- Requires a parliamentary law; the Constitution itself does not name a fallback person beyond Art 65.
- Cross-check against Art 65 (VP acts in vacancy) and Art 70 (contingency law).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 65 (VP's acting role); any enabling law under Art 70 is a parliamentary statute; verify the name before citing.
**Trap:** Art 70 is a residual enabling clause, not a fixed succession order; do not cite it as creating a succession list.
**Back:** *"Parliament fills what the Chapter misses: Art 70 is the residual clause."*

### Flashcard Art-71 — Article 71
**Front:** Article 71 — Matters relating to, or connected with, the election of a President or Vice-President
**Provision:** (1) All doubts and disputes arising out of or in connection with the election of a President or Vice-President shall be inquired into and decided by the Supreme Court, whose decision shall be final. (2) If the election of a person as President or VP is declared void by the Supreme Court, acts previously done by him in the exercise of the office, including acts done before the declaration, are not invalidated by reason of the declaration. (3) Subject to the provisions of this Constitution, Parliament may by law regulate any matter relating to or connected with the election of a President or Vice-President (✅ wording checked, Wikisource Part V; sub-clause numbering ⚠️ still-unverified because the page's own footnotes on a clause (4) do not agree with the summary of it).
**Learn:**
- Exclusive, final jurisdiction of the Supreme Court over presidential and VP election disputes.
- Acts done before a void declaration are not invalidated (validity protected).
- Parliament may regulate related matters by law.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 54–55 (electoral college, vote values); Art 66 (VP electoral college); Presidential and Vice-Presidential Elections Act, 1952.
**Trap:** The SC, not the Election Commission, decides presidential and VP election disputes. Do not say acts before a void declaration are invalid. 2023 Q36 (owner record) turns on this protection.
**Back:** *"Supreme Court decides presidential disputes; acts before a voided election stand."*

### Flashcard Art-72 — Article 72
**Front:** Article 72 — Power of President to grant pardons, etc., and to suspend, remit or commute sentences in certain cases
**Provision:** (1) The President shall have the power to grant pardons, reprieves, respites or remissions of punishment or to suspend, remit or commute the sentence of any person convicted of any offence (a) in all cases where the punishment or sentence is by a court martial; (b) in all cases where the punishment or sentence is for an offence against any law relating to a matter to which the executive power of the Union extends; and (c) in all cases where the sentence is a sentence of death. (2) Nothing in sub-clause (a) of clause (1) affects the power conferred by law on any officer of the armed forces to suspend, remit or commute a sentence of a court martial. (3) Nothing in sub-clause (c) of clause (1) shall affect the power to suspend, remit or commute a death sentence exercisable by the Governor of a State under any law for the time being in force (✅ corrected: was "by any officer or authority"; checked against Wikisource Part V).
**Learn:**
- Five powers: pardon, reprieve, respite, remission, commutation (and suspension).
- Reaches court-martial sentences and death sentences — the President's distinctive reach over the Governor's Art 161.
- Advice is binding in practice: Maru Ram (1980) — exercised on the Cabinet's advice.
- Limited judicial review (Epuru Sudhakar, 2006) for mala fides, non-application of mind or irrelevant material; the President may re-appraise (Kehar Singh, 1989).
- 2025 GS-II Q3 (compare President's pardon power, India vs USA; pre-emptive pardon) and 2025 Prelims Q86 (pardon review and advice) are routed here (owner record).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Maru Ram v Union of India (1980); Kehar Singh v Union of India (AIR 1989 SC; judgment dated 1988 per owner file); Epuru Sudhakar v Govt of A.P. (2006); Art 161 (Governor); Art 74 (binding advice).
**Trap:** Art 72 vs Art 161 — only the President may pardon a death sentence or a court-martial sentence. Pardon is not the President's personal will. Pre-emptive pardon is a US concept, not an Indian constitutional term.
**Back:** *"Pardon, reprieve, respite, remission, commute — and death, court-martial: Art 72 is wider than Art 161."*

### Flashcard Art-73 — Article 73
**Front:** Article 73 — Extent of executive power of the Union
**Provision:** (1) Subject to the other provisions of this Constitution, the executive power of the Union shall extend (a) to the matters with respect to which Parliament has power to make laws; and (b) to the exercise of such rights, authority and jurisdiction as are exercisable by the Government of India by virtue of any treaty or agreement. (2) Until otherwise provided by Parliament, a State and its officers may continue to exercise executive functions in matters where the executive power is concurrent under the Constitution with the Union.
**Learn:**
- Two spheres: (a) matters on which Parliament can legislate; (b) rights under treaties and agreements.
- Parallel to Art 162 (States' executive power) and Art 246 (legislative lists).
- Cl (2): concurrent matters — States continue to exercise functions until Parliament provides otherwise.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 162 (State executive); Art 246 (Union/Concurrent/State Lists); Art 253 (treaty implementation); Art 51 (international peace).
**Trap:** Union executive power follows Parliament's legislative competence (Art 73(1)(a)), and treaty-based power is added as a separate limb (Art 73(1)(b)). Do not treat Art 73 as a grant of power beyond the lists.
**Back:** *"Union executive follows Union law and treaties: Art 73."*

### Flashcard Art-74 — Article 74
**Front:** Article 74 — Council of Ministers to aid and advise President
**Provision:** (1) There shall be a Council of Ministers with the Prime Minister at the head to aid and advise the President in the exercise of his functions, except in so far as he is by or under this Constitution required to exercise his functions or any of them in his discretion. (Proviso, inserted by the 42nd Amendment, 1976 and amended by the 44th, 1978:) The President shall act in accordance with such advice, but may require the Council of Ministers to reconsider such advice, either generally or otherwise, and the President shall act in accordance with the advice tendered after such reconsideration. (✅ corrected: the word "once" is not in the text; checked against Wikisource Part V.) (2) The question whether any, and if so what, advice was tendered by Ministers to the President shall not be inquired into in any court.
**Learn:**
- Advice binds the President; the 42nd Amendment made it binding, the 44th added the reconsideration proviso ("either generally or otherwise"); the Wikipedia Art 74 page reads it as once-only, which is an interpretation, not express text.
- Limited discretion exists "in so far as" the Constitution requires the President to act in his discretion (e.g. appointment of PM in a hung House — situational).
- Cl (2): courts cannot inquire into whether advice was tendered or what it was — but the act itself remains reviewable on other grounds.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 42nd (1976), 44th (1978); Shamsher Singh v State of Punjab (1974) (✅ owner Governor-and-CM basic: seven-judge Bench; constitutional heads act on aid and advice save exceptional situations; primary judgment not reached); Maru Ram (1980) (Cabinet advice on pardon binding); Art 75 (PM and ministers); Art 163 (Governor's advice and discretion, contrast).
**Trap:** The reconsideration power is textually "either generally or otherwise"; it is read as once-only (Wikipedia Art 74 page), and the President must act on the reconsidered advice. Art 74(2) bars inquiry into advice; it does not immunise the executive act from review. "Discretion" is limited to what the Constitution itself names.
**Back:** *"Council advises, President acts: binding after one reconsideration; the advice itself is not for the court."*

### Flashcard Art-75 — Article 75
**Front:** Article 75 — Other provisions as to Ministers
**Provision:** (1) The Prime Minister shall be appointed by the President and the other Ministers shall be appointed by the President on the advice of the Prime Minister. (1A) (91st Amendment, 2003) The total number of Ministers, including the Prime Minister, in the Council of Ministers shall not exceed fifteen per cent of the total number of members of the House of the People. (1B) A member of either House belonging to any political party who is disqualified for being a member of that House under the Tenth Schedule shall also be disqualified to be appointed as a Minister. (2) The Ministers shall hold office during the pleasure of the President. (3) The Council of Ministers shall be collectively responsible to the House of the People. (4) Before a Minister enters upon his office, the President shall administer to him the oaths of office and of secrecy according to the forms set out in the Third Schedule. (5) A Minister who for any period of six consecutive months is not a member of either House of Parliament shall at the expiration of that period cease to be a Minister. (6) The salaries and allowances of Ministers shall be such as Parliament may from time to time by law determine.
**Learn:**
- Cl (1): PM appointed by President (no procedure); other ministers on PM's advice.
- Cl (1A): cap of 15% of the Lok Sabha's strength (not both Houses), including PM.
- Cl (1B): defector disqualified under Tenth Schedule cannot be a minister.
- Cl (2): individual pleasure of the President (exercised on PM's advice).
- Cl (3): collective responsibility to the Lok Sabha only.
- Cl (5): non-member can be minister for six consecutive months.
- Cl (4): oath by President; Third Schedule.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 91st Amendment (2003); S.P. Anand v H.D. Deve Gowda (1996) (non-member PM subject to Art 75(5)) per owner record; Art 88 (ministers' rights in House); Art 78 (PM's duty to inform).
**Trap:** Collective responsibility (cl 3) is to the Lok Sabha, not the Rajya Sabha; individual responsibility is cl 2 (pleasure). The 15% cap is on Lok Sabha strength. 2020 Q19 and 2022 Q14 are routed to this owner (owner record). Cabinet is not a constitutional term in Art 75; it entered via Art 352 (44th Amendment).
**Back:** *"PM appointed; ministers pleasure; LS responsibility; 15% cap; six months for non-members."*

### Flashcard Art-76 — Article 76
**Front:** Article 76 — Attorney-General for India
**Provision:** (1) The President shall appoint a person who is qualified to be appointed a Judge of the Supreme Court to be Attorney-General for India. (2) It shall be the duty of the Attorney-General to give advice to the Government of India upon such legal matters, and to perform such other duties of a legal character, as may from time to time be referred or assigned to him by the President. (3) The Attorney-General shall have the right to audience in all courts in the territory of India. (4) The Attorney-General shall hold office during the pleasure of the President, and shall receive such remuneration as the President may determine.
**Learn:**
- Highest law officer of India; qualification = Supreme Court judge; appointed by the President.
- Advice on referred legal matters and such other legal duties as assigned by the President.
- Right of audience in all courts; speaks but does not vote in Parliament (Art 88).
- Pleasure tenure; remuneration determined by the President; no fixed term or removal procedure.
- Appearances for the Government of India in the Supreme Court and in Art 143 references are carried by the Attorney-General's assigned duties and practice ⚠️ (the appearance duties are not spelled out in the clause text).
- Solicitor General and Additional Solicitors General are non-constitutional executive posts.
- Contempt of Courts Act, 1971 (s.15): written consent of the Advocate-General for private criminal-contempt motions, though the Court may act suo motu (⚠️ whether the Solicitor-General is also named is unverified; Act text not reached).
- 2019 GS-II Q14 (15 marks) and 2025 GS-II Q5 (10 marks) are routed here (owner record).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 88 (speaking rights); Art 105 (privileges); Art 143 (reference); Art 165 (Advocate-General analogue); Contempt of Courts Act 1971 s.15.
**Trap:** Only the AG and Advocate-General are constitutional law officers (Arts 76 and 165); the SG/ASG are not. The AG cannot vote; cannot advise or appear against the Government of India. Qualification is Supreme Court judge (Art 76(1)); for the Advocate-General it is High Court judge (Art 165).
**Back:** *"Supreme Court qualified, pleasure tenure, every court, no vote: Art 76 AG."*

### Flashcard Art-77 — Article 77
**Front:** Article 77 — Conduct of business of the Government of India
**Provision:** (1) All executive action of the Government of India shall be expressed to be taken in the name of the President. (2) Orders and other instruments made and executed in the name of the President shall be authenticated in such manner as may be specified in rules to be made by the President, and the validity of an order or instrument which is so authenticated shall not be called in question on the ground that it is not an order or instrument made or executed by the President. (3) The President shall make rules for the more convenient transaction of the business of the Government of India, and for the allocation among Ministers of the said business.
**Learn:**
- Executive action expressed in the President's name (formal act).
- Authentication rules; validity cannot be challenged merely on the ground of authentication (cl 2).
- The President makes the Transaction of Business Rules and Allocation of Business Rules (1961 sets are the owner record).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Government of India (Allocation of Business) Rules, 1961; Transaction of Business Rules, 1961; Art 74 (advice); Art 53 (executive power); Art 78 (PM's information duty).
**Trap:** Authentication under Art 77(2) does not erase ministerial responsibility or judicial review of legality (owner record). Do not say the President personally decides allocation of portfolios.
**Back:** *"Acts in the President's name; rules allocate business; authenticated acts stand."*

### Flashcard Art-78 — Article 78
**Front:** Article 78 — Duty of Prime Minister to furnish information to President, etc.
**Provision:** It shall be the duty of the Prime Minister (a) to communicate to the President all decisions of the Council of Ministers relating to the administration of the affairs of the Union and proposals for legislation; (b) to furnish such information relating to the administration of the affairs of the Union and proposals for legislation as the President may call for; and (c) if the President so requires, to submit for the consideration of the Council of Ministers any matter on which a decision has been taken by a Minister but which has not been considered by the Council.
**Learn:**
- Three duties: communicate Council decisions; furnish information on request; submit for Council consideration any ministerial decision the President wants reconsidered by the Council.
- Makes the PM the constitutional channel between the Council and the President.
- Supports the PM-centred concentration of power debate (owner record).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 74 (Council); Art 75 (PM and collective responsibility); Art 77 (business rules).
**Trap:** The duty runs from the PM to the President; the President cannot demand information from ministers directly under Art 78. Art 78(c) applies only to matters decided by a single Minister and not yet considered by the Council.
**Back:** *"PM is the President's channel: decisions, information, and Council reconsideration on request."*

### PART V — The Union (Articles 79–151)

### Flashcard Art-79 — Article 79
**Front:** Article 79 — Constitution of Parliament
**Provision:** There shall be a Parliament for the Union, which consists of the President and two Houses: the Council of States (Rajya Sabha) and the House of the People (Lok Sabha).
**Learn:**
- Parliament = President + RS + LS. The President is not a member of either House but summons, prorogues, addresses Parliament and assents to Bills.
- LS is the popular House (direct election); RS is the permanent, federal, revising House (indirect election by State MLAs).
- The Union legislature is bicameral; State legislatures are unicameral or bicameral under Arts 168–169.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Arts 80–81 (composition), 85 (sessions), 111 (assent), 368 (amendment; the joint sitting is not available for constitutional amendments).
**Trap:** The President is part of Parliament but not a *member* of either House. Never describe the President as a member of the RS or LS.
**Back:** *"President plus two Houses makes Parliament: the head is in the Sansad, but holds no seat."*

### Flashcard Art-80 — Article 80
**Front:** Article 80 — Composition of the Council of States
**Provision:** RS may have not more than 250 members: not more than 238 representatives of the States and UTs (Art 80(1)(a)–(b)) and 12 members nominated by the President (Art 80(1)(c)) who have special knowledge or practical experience in literature, science, art and social service (Art 80(3)). State seats are allotted under the Fourth Schedule (Art 80(2)). State representatives are elected by the elected members of the State Legislative Assembly (Art 80(4)).
**Learn:**
- Maximum 250 = 238 elected + 12 nominated. Sanctioned working figure is 245 (owner file); the constitutional maximum is 250.
- Elected by indirect election, single transferable vote (proportional representation), open ballot.
- Nominated members are not elected and do not count against any State's allocation.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Representation of the People Act, 1951 governs RS elections. Kuldip Nayar v. Union of India (2006) upheld the statutory RP (Amendment) Act, 2003 change to open ballot and the removal of the domicile bar (owner file ✅). Those changes are statutory, not constitutional.
**Trap:** The open ballot and the domicile change are statutory (RP Act), not constitutional amendments. The 12 nominated members are not "elected by States" and are a presidential nomination.
**Back:** *"Two-thirty-eight elected, twelve nominated: the house of States, not the house of the crowd."*

### Flashcard Art-81 — Article 81
**Front:** Article 81 — Composition of the House of the People
**Provision:** LS may have not more than 530 members chosen by direct election from territorial constituencies in the States and not more than 20 members to represent the Union Territories (Art 81(1)(a)–(b)). Art 81(2)(a): the ratio between the seats allotted to a State and its population shall, so far as practicable, be the same for all States.
**Learn:**
- Maximum strength 550 (530 + 20). Stable elected seat count is 543 (owner file).
- Anglo-Indian nomination (old Art 331) was abolished by the 104th Amendment (2019, effective 2020); LS has no nominated members now.
- Elected by universal adult suffrage through FPTP in single-member constituencies; minimum age 25 (Art 84).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 42nd and 84th Amendments freeze seat allocation (Art 82). 104th Amendment (2019) also extended SC/ST reservation. 106th Amendment (2023; Arts 330A, 332A, 334A) reserves one-third of seats for women: enacted 2023; the owner file reports commencement on 16 April 2026 (⚠️ not confirmed: Wikipedia Act page not reachable this pass), but the reservation is still not operational until delimitation after the relevant census figures are published (owner file; ⚠️ 📰 verify). Owner file also records that the Constitution (131st Amendment) Bill, 2026, which proposed a 850 ceiling, was defeated on 17 April 2026 (298 : 230, short of the Art 368(2) two-thirds limb) (⚠️ 📰 verify). It is a defeated Bill, not law.
**Trap:** 550 is the maximum; 543 is the elected seat count. The 850 figure is a defeated Bill's proposal, never law.
**Back:** *"Five-thirty plus two-twenty: five hundred fifty at most, and no nominations from the President."*

### Flashcard Art-82 — Article 82
**Front:** Article 82 — Readjustment after each census
**Provision:** On the completion of each census, the allocation of seats in the House of the People to the States and the division of each State into territorial constituencies shall be readjusted by such authority and in such manner as Parliament may by law determine. The proviso provides that such readjustment does not affect representation in the LS until the dissolution of the then-existing House.
**Learn:**
- Delimitation is done by a statutory commission under Delimitation Acts (1952, 1962, 1972, 2002 — owner file ✅).
- Population freeze chain: 42nd Amendment (1976) froze seat allocation on the 1971 census until after the first census after 2000; 84th Amendment (2001) extended the freeze until after the first census taken after 2026, while allowing boundary readjustment on the 1991 census; 87th Amendment (2003) shifted boundary readjustment to the 2001 census.
- Art 329(a) bars courts from questioning the validity of a delimitation law or allotment of seats.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Census 2027 is the first census after 2026 (owner file), and so the census that releases the 84th freeze and that Art 334A(1) keys to. The owner file records that publication of its figures is not notified, so no delimitation date may be stated (⚠️ 📰 verify). Art 170 is the State Assembly analogue.
**Trap:** Enumeration date is not the date figures are published. The freeze applies to seat allocation, not to boundary readjustment; the 2001 census basis came from the 87th Amendment.
**Back:** *"The census counts, the commission carves, and the 84th freeze keeps seats still."*

### Flashcard Art-83 — Article 83
**Front:** Article 83 — Duration of Houses of Parliament
**Provision:** (1) The RS is not subject to dissolution; one-third of its members retire every second year (six-year term per member). (2) The LS, unless sooner dissolved, continues for five years from the date of its first sitting. While a Proclamation of Emergency is in operation, Parliament may by law extend this period for one year at a time, but not beyond six months after the Emergency ceases to operate.
**Learn:**
- RS is a continuing body; LS is dissolved at the end of five years or earlier by the President.
- The 42nd Amendment (1976) extended the LS term during Emergency to six years; the 44th Amendment (1978) restored the five-year limit (⚠️ verify history before quoting).
- Dissolution of the LS does not dissolve the RS.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 85 (prorogation and dissolution); Art 352 (Emergency).
**Trap:** The RS is never dissolved; the LS is five years, not six; any Emergency extension is by Parliament's law in one-year blocks, not by the President.
**Back:** *"Five years for the Lok, six for each Raj member, one-third turning every two."*

### Flashcard Art-84 — Article 84
**Front:** Article 84 — Qualification for membership of Parliament
**Provision:** A person is qualified to be chosen as a member of Parliament only if he (a) is a citizen of India and makes and subscribes before the Election Commission or a person authorised by it an oath or affirmation; (b) is not less than 30 years of age for the RS and not less than 25 years for the LS; and (c) possesses such other qualifications as may be prescribed by or under any law of Parliament.
**Learn:**
- Minimum age: RS 30; LS 25.
- Other qualifications are prescribed by Parliament (for example, enrolment as an elector under the Representation of the People Act, 1951).
- Oath or affirmation is taken before a person authorised by the ECI for candidature; the Third Schedule sets the form.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 102 (disqualifications); Art 101 (vacation); Art 99 (oath before taking seat); Art 173 (State legislature qualifications, 25 for Assembly, 30 for Council).
**Trap:** Do not mix the age limits: President and Vice-President 35 (Arts 58, 66), Governor 35 (Art 157), voting age 18 (Art 326). RS 30 and LS 25 are the two Parliament limits.
**Back:** *"Thirty for the Raj, twenty-five for the Lok: the upper house asks for more years of life."*

### Flashcard Art-85 — Article 85
**Front:** Article 85 — Sessions of Parliament; prorogation and dissolution
**Provision:** (1) The President shall summon each House at such time and place as he thinks fit, but six months shall not intervene between its last sitting in one session and its first sitting in the next session. (2) The President may prorogue the Houses and may dissolve the House of the People.
**Learn:**
- The six-month rule is the only hard floor. The Constitution sets no minimum number of sitting days.
- Budget, Monsoon and Winter sessions are conventions, not constitutional session names.
- Prorogation ends a session (pending business survives; pending notices lapse). Dissolution ends the life of the LS.
- Adjournment and adjournment sine die are by the presiding officer, not the President.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 107 (lapse on prorogation/dissolution); Art 87 (special address); Art 83 (LS term).
**Trap:** Prorogation and dissolution are the President's acts; adjournment is the presiding officer's. The RS cannot be dissolved, so only the LS is dissolved.
**Back:** *"The President summons, prorogues and dissolves; the Speaker only adjourns."*

### Flashcard Art-86 — Article 86
**Front:** Article 86 — Right of the President to address Houses and send messages
**Provision:** The President may address either House or both Houses assembled together, and may for that purpose require the attendance of members. He may also send messages to either House, whether with respect to a Bill then pending in Parliament or otherwise.
**Learn:**
- The general address and message power is discretionary and is the formal executive-legislative link.
- Messages may concern pending Bills or any other business.
- Contrast the mandatory special address in Art 87.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 79 (President as constituent of Parliament); Art 87 (special address); Art 111 (assent).
**Trap:** Art 86 is a general, discretionary power; Art 87 is a mandatory address on two occasions. Do not confuse them.
**Back:** *"Address in the hall, message by letter: the President speaks, the Houses listen."*

### Flashcard Art-87 — Article 87
**Front:** Article 87 — Special address by the President
**Provision:** At the commencement of the first session after each general election to the House of the People, and at the commencement of the first session of each year, the President shall address both Houses assembled together and inform Parliament of the causes of its summons.
**Learn:**
- Two mandatory occasions: the first session after each general election to the LS, and the first session of every year.
- The address is delivered in a joint sitting and sets out the government's policy agenda.
- No vote is taken on the address; it is a constitutional statement of the causes of summons.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 86 (general address); Art 85 (summoning); Art 112 (Budget).
**Trap:** Art 87 is mandatory on two occasions; Art 86 is discretionary. Do not confuse with the Governor's address to a State legislature (Art 176).
**Back:** *"Election-year and year-start, the President addresses both Houses together."*

### Flashcard Art-88 — Article 88
**Front:** Article 88 — Rights of ministers and Attorney-General as respects Houses
**Provision:** Every minister and the Attorney-General of India shall have the right to speak in, and otherwise take part in the proceedings of, either House, any joint sitting and any committee of Parliament of which he is a member, but shall not by virtue of this article be entitled to vote.
**Learn:**
- A non-member minister may speak and take part in either House, without voting rights.
- The Attorney-General is the government's chief legal adviser; the speaking right enables legal input on Bills.
- The vote belongs only to members of the House.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 75(5) (minister must be a member of either House within six months); Art 76 (Attorney-General); Art 177 (State analogue: Advocate-General).
**Trap:** Speaking right is not a voting right. A minister who is a member votes in the House of membership.
**Back:** *"Speak but never vote: the minister and the AG may talk, not tally."*

### Flashcard Art-89 — Article 89
**Front:** Article 89 — Chairman and Deputy Chairman of the Council of States
**Provision:** (1) The Vice-President of India shall be ex officio Chairman of the Council of States. (2) The Council shall choose a member to be its Deputy Chairman.
**Learn:**
- The VP is ex officio Chairman of the RS. He is not a member of the House (Art 66(1) bars a VP from being a member of Parliament), but presides over it.
- The Deputy Chairman is elected by the RS from among its own members.
- The Chairman has no vote except a casting vote in case of equality (Art 100).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 63 (VP office); Art 67 (VP's tenure; removal by RS resolution with LS concurrence); Art 90 (Deputy Chairman's office); Art 91 (panel).
**Trap:** The RS Chairman is the Vice-President, not the Speaker. The Deputy Chairman of the RS is elected by the RS; the Deputy Speaker of the LS is elected by the LS.
**Back:** *"The Vice-President takes the RS chair; the Deputy is chosen from within the House."*

### Flashcard Art-90 — Article 90
**Front:** Article 90 — Vacation, resignation and removal of Deputy Chairman
**Provision:** The Deputy Chairman of the Council of States shall vacate his office (a) if he ceases to be a member of the Council; (b) by resigning in writing addressed to the Chairman; or (c) if he is removed from office by a resolution of the Council passed by a majority of all the then members of the Council, of which at least fourteen days' notice has been given.
**Learn:**
- Removal requires a majority of all members (effective majority), not of those present and voting.
- Fourteen days' notice of the removal resolution is mandatory.
- Ceasing to be an RS member ends the office automatically.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 94 (Speaker's parallel rule); Art 67(b) (VP's removal); Art 91 (panel).
**Trap:** The effective-majority rule (majority of all members) is the same as for the Speaker (Art 94(c)). Majority of those present and voting is the wrong test.
**Back:** *"Fourteen days' notice and a majority of all: the whole House must count."*

### Flashcard Art-91 — Article 91
**Front:** Article 91 — Panel of Vice-Chairmen
**Provision:** There shall be a panel of Vice-Chairmen nominated by the Chairman from among the members of the Council of States. When the Chairman and the Deputy Chairman are both absent, any member of this panel may preside, and while so presiding he has the same powers as the Chairman (⚠️ conflict: the printed Art 91 text, as summarised by Wikisource, sets out the Deputy Chairman's acting role and the order for absence from a sitting, with no panel clause visible in that summary; verify before quoting).
**Learn:**
- The panel is a continuity device for the RS chair when both the Chairman and Deputy Chairman are absent.
- Panel members hold no constitutional office; they preside only in the Chairman's absence.
- The LS analogue is the panel of chairpersons under its Rules of Procedure.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 89 (Chairman and Deputy Chairman); Art 95 (Speaker's Deputy); Art 100 (casting vote).
**Trap:** Panel members are not "Deputy Chairman" in constitutional terms; the panel is a continuity device, not an office.
**Back:** *"When both chairs are empty, the panel sits in the middle."*

### Flashcard Art-92 — Article 92
**Front:** Article 92 — Chairman or Deputy Chairman not to preside while a resolution for his removal is under consideration (✅ heading: Wikisource Const. 2020, Part V)
**Provision:** While a resolution for the removal of the Vice-President (Chairman of the RS) from office is under consideration, the Chairman does not preside, though he is present; he may speak and take part in the proceedings. Under cl. (2) he has no vote on that resolution at all, not even a first-instance vote, and no casting vote (✅ corrected: the card said the first-instance vote is allowed as in Art 96; Wikisource Const. 2020, Part V, Art 92(1)–(2), checked via summaries). The same bar on presiding applies to the Deputy Chairman during a resolution for his own removal (✅ heading and summary).
**Learn:**
- Structural analogue to Art 96 (Speaker). The adjudicator under attack may defend himself but not preside.
- The Deputy Chairman presides during the proceedings.
- Keeps the chair out of the presiding officer's own removal.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 67(b) (VP removal); Art 90 (Deputy Chairman removal); Art 96 (Speaker's analogue).
**Trap:** Do not conflate Art 92 (RS) with Art 96 (LS). Art 92(2) gives the RS Chairman no vote at all (not a first-instance vote as in Art 96); quote the clause only after checking the printed text.
**Back:** *"No chair while you stand in the dock: speak in your defence, but do not preside."*

### Flashcard Art-93 — Article 93
**Front:** Article 93 — Speaker and Deputy Speaker of the House of the People
**Provision:** The House of the People shall, as soon as may be, choose two members to be respectively Speaker and Deputy Speaker thereof, and whenever the office of Speaker or Deputy Speaker becomes vacant, the House shall choose another member to fill it.
**Learn:**
- Both offices are elected by the LS from among its members, by majority of members present and voting.
- The Speaker is the presiding authority of the LS and the final interpreter of its Rules of Procedure within the House.
- The Speaker's casting vote (Art 100), Money Bill certificate (Art 110(3)) and Tenth Schedule para 6 powers are decisive.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 94 (vacation); Art 95 (Deputy Speaker's duties); Art 96 (removal); Art 108 (joint sitting, presided by the Speaker); Art 110 (Money Bill certificate); Tenth Schedule para 6.
**Trap:** Art 93 says "as soon as may be" and sets no deadline. The owner file records that the Deputy Speaker's post was vacant through the 17th LS (2019–2024): a propriety debate, not a breach of an express deadline.
**Back:** *"Two chairs of the Lok, chosen by the House, answerable to the House."*

### Flashcard Art-94 — Article 94
**Front:** Article 94 — Vacation, resignation and removal of Speaker and Deputy Speaker
**Provision:** The Speaker or Deputy Speaker of the House of the People shall vacate his office (a) if he ceases to be a member of the House; (b) by resigning in writing (the Speaker to the Deputy Speaker, and the Deputy Speaker to the Speaker); or (c) if he is removed by a resolution of the House passed by a majority of all the then members, of which at least fourteen days' notice has been given. The Speaker does not vacate his office on dissolution of the LS and continues until immediately before the first meeting of the next House (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part V, Art 94, dissolution sentence).
**Learn:**
- Removal requires an effective majority of all members with 14 days' notice.
- The Constitution does not require the Speaker to resign from his party; Indian practice differs from the British convention.
- The Speaker continues across dissolution, a continuity safeguard.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 96 (no presiding during own removal); Art 90 (RS equivalent); Art 93 (election).
**Trap:** The Speaker does not vacate on dissolution. Removal needs an effective majority (not present-and-voting) plus 14 days' notice.
**Back:** *"Fourteen days, all members, in writing: the Speaker leaves only by the House's full count."*

### Flashcard Art-95 — Article 95
**Front:** Article 95 — Power of the Deputy Speaker or other person to perform duties of Speaker
**Provision:** While the office of Speaker is vacant, the duties of the office shall be performed by the Deputy Speaker; if the office of Deputy Speaker is also vacant, by such member of the House as the President may appoint for the purpose. The Deputy Speaker, when performing the Speaker's duties or presiding during the Speaker's absence, has the same powers as the Speaker.
**Learn:**
- Triggers: vacancy of the Speaker's office (Art 95(1)) and the Speaker's absence from a sitting.
- The Deputy Speaker's own office is independent; he is answerable to the House, not to the Speaker.
- If both posts are vacant, the President appoints a member to act.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 93 (election); Art 96 (removal bar); Art 91 (RS panel analogue); Art 100 (casting vote).
**Trap:** Deputy Speaker has the Speaker's full powers while presiding, and is answerable to the House; the President's appointment power arises only if both posts are vacant.
