**Back:** *"When the chair is empty, the second chair sits with the same power, answerable to the House."*

### Flashcard Art-96 — Article 96
**Front:** Article 96 — Speaker or Deputy Speaker not to preside while resolution for his removal is under consideration
**Provision:** While any resolution for the removal of the Speaker from his office is under consideration in the House, the Speaker shall not preside, although he may be present. He has the right to speak in and otherwise take part in the proceedings of the House, and is entitled to vote only in the first instance on such resolution (or any other matter during such proceedings), but not in case of an equality of votes (no casting vote).
**Learn:**
- The Speaker defends himself from the floor but cannot preside over his own removal.
- The Deputy Speaker presides during the proceedings.
- Speaker's first-instance vote is allowed; casting vote is barred.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 94 (removal procedure); Art 100 (casting vote); Art 92 (RS analogue; its cl. (2) gives the Chairman no vote at all, see Art-92 card).
**Trap:** Speaker "may vote in the first instance but not exercise the casting vote". He is not barred from the House, only from presiding.
**Back:** *"Voice yes, chair no, tie-breaker no: no one judges his own removal."*

### Flashcard Art-97 — Article 97
**Front:** Article 97 — Salaries and allowances of Speaker, Deputy Speaker, Chairman and Deputy Chairman
**Provision:** There shall be paid to the Speaker and the Deputy Speaker of the House of the People, and to the Chairman and the Deputy Chairman of the Council of States, such salaries and allowances as are respectively fixed by Parliament by law and, until so fixed, as are specified in the Second Schedule.
**Learn:**
- Salaries are fixed by Parliament by law (Second Schedule specifies the initial figures).
- Presiding officers' pay is not set by the presiding officer or the House's own ruling; it is set by statute.
- Protects the chair from executive pressure over remuneration.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 106 (members' salaries); Art 112(3) (charged expenditure) and Art 112 generally; Second Schedule.
**Trap:** Presiding officers' pay is fixed by Parliament's law, not by the Budget vote. Do not state that the Speaker's salary is votable.
**Back:** *"The chair's pay is set by law and kept off the vote."*

### Flashcard Art-98 — Article 98
**Front:** Article 98 — Secretariat of each House of Parliament
**Provision:** Each House of Parliament shall have a separate secretarial staff. Parliament may by law regulate the recruitment and conditions of service of persons appointed to the secretarial staff of either House; until so regulated, the President, after consultation with the Speaker or Chairman, may make rules for the purpose.
**Learn:**
- Separate secretariats for the LS and the RS (for example, the Lok Sabha Secretariat and the Rajya Sabha Secretariat).
- Until Parliament legislates, the President frames the rules after consultation with the presiding officer; the presiding officer then controls day-to-day staff matters.
- Keeps the staff of Parliament out of executive control.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** DRSC secretariat split (16 LS-serviced, 8 RS-serviced; owner file).
**Trap:** Two separate secretariats, not one joint secretariat for Parliament.
**Back:** *"Two houses, two staffs: each chamber keeps its own clerks."*

### Flashcard Art-99 — Article 99
**Front:** Article 99 — Oath or affirmation by members
**Provision:** Every member of either House shall, before taking his seat, make and subscribe before the President, or some person appointed in that behalf by him, an oath or affirmation according to the form set out for the purpose in the Third Schedule.
**Learn:**
- The oath is taken before taking the seat, before the President or a person he appoints.
- The Third Schedule prescribes the form; ministers' and members' forms are both set out there.
- Sitting or voting without the oath is penalised under Art 104.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 104 (penalty Rs 500 per day); Art 84 (qualifications); Art 188 (State analogue).
**Trap:** The oath is taken before the President or his appointee, not before the Election Commission (the ECI is for the candidature oath under Art 84).
**Back:** *"Oath before the seat, before the President's man: the Third Schedule is the script."*

### Flashcard Art-100 — Article 100
**Front:** Article 100 — Voting in Houses, power of Houses to act notwithstanding vacancies and quorum
**Provision:** (1) All questions at any sitting are determined by a majority of votes of members present and voting, other than the presiding officer, who shall not vote except in case of an equality of votes (casting vote). (2) A House may act notwithstanding any vacancy in its membership. (3) The quorum to constitute a meeting of either House is one-tenth of the total number of members (✅ corrected: quorum is cl. (3), not (2); Wikisource Const. 2020, Part V, Art 100).
**Learn:**
- Quorum = one-tenth: 55 in the LS, 25 in the RS.
- Ordinary business: majority of members present and voting. Amendments and removal of judges need special majorities (Arts 368(2), 124(4)).
- The presiding officer does not vote normally; the casting vote breaks ties.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 96 (Speaker's casting-vote bar during own removal); Art 368(2) (amendment); Art 124(4) (judges' removal).
**Trap:** Quorum (1/10) is not a majority needed to pass a motion. The casting vote is used only on an equality.
**Back:** *"One-tenth to sit, majority to pass, the chair votes only to break a tie."*

### Flashcard Art-101 — Article 101
**Front:** Article 101 — Vacation of seats
**Provision:** (1)–(2) No person shall be a member of both Houses of Parliament; Parliament shall by law provide for vacation of the seat in one House by a person chosen for both. (3) A person shall vacate his seat in Parliament if he becomes subject to a disqualification mentioned in Art 102(1) or (2), which includes the Tenth Schedule ground (✅ corrected: clause numbering; Wikisource Const. 2020, Part V, Art 101(3)). (4) A member vacates his seat if he is absent from all meetings for sixty days without the House's permission; prorogation or adjournment for more than four consecutive days is excluded (✅ Wikisource Art 101(4) summary).
**Learn:**
- Dual membership is barred; the RP Act, 1951 supplies the mechanics.
- Sixty days' unauthorised absence vacates the seat, a ground distinct from disqualification.
- Disqualification vacates the seat automatically once it arises.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 102 (disqualifications); Art 102(2) and the Tenth Schedule (52nd Amendment, 1985); Art 191 (State analogue).
**Trap:** Art 101 vacates seats; Art 102 lists disqualifications; Art 103 decides Art 102(1) questions. The 60-day rule is a separate vacating ground.
**Back:** *"Two seats or sixty days' silence: the chair goes empty."*

### Flashcard Art-102 — Article 102
**Front:** Article 102 — Disqualifications for membership
**Provision:** A person shall be disqualified for being chosen as, and for being, a member of either House: (a) if he holds any office of profit under the Government of India or a State Government, other than an office exempted by law of Parliament; (b) if he is of unsound mind and so declared by a competent court; (c) if he is an undischarged insolvent; (d) if he is not a citizen of India, or has voluntarily acquired citizenship of a foreign State, or is under acknowledgement of allegiance to a foreign State; (e) if he is so disqualified by or under any law made by Parliament. (2) A person is disqualified for being a member if he is so disqualified under the Tenth Schedule.
**Learn:**
- Five grounds (a)–(e), plus the Tenth Schedule ground under Art 102(2).
- Office of profit exemptions are listed in the Parliament (Prevention of Disqualification) Act, 1959 (owner file).
- Tenth Schedule grounds: voluntarily giving up party membership, or voting or abstaining contrary to the whip without condonation within 15 days (para 2(1)).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 52nd Amendment (1985) inserted the Tenth Schedule and amended Arts 101, 102, 190, 191 (owner file: Anti-Defection-Law.md). 91st Amendment (2003) deleted the one-third split defence.
**Trap:** The Tenth Schedule disqualification is decided by the Speaker or Chairman under para 6 (Kihoto Hollohan, 1992: a reviewable tribunal). Art 103 decides only Art 102(1) questions, through the President and the ECI.
**Back:** *"Office, sanity, solvency, citizenship, law: and the whip, which the Tenth Schedule adds."*

### Flashcard Art-103 — Article 103
**Front:** Article 103 — Decision on questions as to disqualifications
**Provision:** (1) If any question arises whether a member of either House has become subject to any disqualification mentioned in Art 102(1), the question shall be referred to the President for decision. (2) Before giving any decision, the President shall obtain the opinion of the Election Commission and shall act according to such opinion.
**Learn:**
- Art 102(1) questions (office of profit, unsound mind, insolvency, citizenship, Parliament law) go to the President.
- The President must obtain and act on the ECI's opinion.
- Anti-defection questions under Art 102(2) and the Tenth Schedule are decided by the Speaker or Chairman under para 6, not by the President.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Tenth Schedule para 6; Kihoto Hollohan v. Zachillhu (1992, five-judge Bench: Speaker acts as a tribunal, reviewable for mala fides, perversity or violation of natural justice; owner file ✅).
**Trap:** The President and the ECI decide Art 102(1) questions; the Speaker decides defection questions. Do not swap the two routes.
**Back:** *"Office and citizenship go to the President on the ECI's view; the whip goes to the Speaker."*

### Flashcard Art-104 — Article 104
**Front:** Article 104 — Penalty for sitting and voting when not qualified or disqualified
**Provision:** If a person sits or votes as a member of either House when he is not qualified, or when he is disqualified, or when he is prohibited from doing so under Art 99 (no oath or affirmation), he shall be liable to a penalty of five hundred rupees for each day on which he so sits or votes, recoverable as a debt due to the Union.
**Learn:**
- Penalty: Rs 500 per day of unlawful sitting or voting.
- Recoverable as a debt due to the Union.
- Covers unqualified and disqualified persons, and those who have not taken the oath.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 99 (oath); Art 102 (disqualification); Art 101 (vacation).
**Trap:** The penalty is Rs 500 per day; the seat is vacated only under Art 101, not by Art 104 itself.
**Back:** *"Five hundred a day for sitting without the oath or the qualification."*

### Flashcard Art-105 — Article 105
**Front:** Article 105 — Powers, privileges and immunities of Parliament and its members
**Provision:** (1) Freedom of speech in Parliament, subject to the Constitution and the rules and standing orders. (2) No member shall be liable to any proceedings in any court in respect of anything said or any vote given by him in Parliament or any committee thereof. (3) Until Parliament defines them, the powers, privileges and immunities are those the Houses had immediately before s.15 of the Constitution (44th Amendment) Act, 1978 came into force (✅ corrected: the card said UK Commons; Wikisource Const. 2020, Part V, Art 105(3)). (4) Persons with a right to speak in a House (ministers, AG) enjoy the same protection as members (Art 105(4)).
**Learn:**
- Freedom of speech and immunity from court proceedings for speech and vote (Art 105(1)–(2)).
- Until Parliament defines them, privileges are those the Houses had immediately before s.15 of the 44th Amendment (1978) came into force (✅ corrected: the card said the UK Commons; Wikisource Const. 2020, Part V, Art 105(3)).
- Art 194 is the State analogue.
- Sita Soren v. Union of India (2024; seven-judge Bench, 4 March 2024) overruled P.V. Narasimha Rao (1998): a legislator who takes a bribe for a vote or speech is not immune (owner file ✅).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 194 (State legislatures); Art 122 (courts not to inquire); Art 121 (judges' conduct); Committee of Privileges (distinct from the Ethics Committee; owner file).
**Trap:** Bribery is not protected after Sita Soren (2024). Privilege still protects bona fide speech and vote. Do not write that the privilege is unlimited.
**Back:** *"Speak freely, vote freely, but take no bribe for either."*

### Flashcard Art-106 — Article 106
**Front:** Article 106 — Salaries and allowances of members of Parliament
**Provision:** Members of each House shall receive such salaries and allowances as Parliament may from time to time by law determine. Until so determined, they receive the allowances that applied to members of the Constituent Assembly of the Dominion of India (✅ corrected: was 'specified in the Second Schedule'; Wikisource Const. 2020, Part V, Art 106 summary).
**Learn:**
- Member pay is fixed by Parliament by law, not by the Speaker's ruling.
- Interim figures are the Constituent Assembly rates (see Provision), not the Second Schedule.
- Salary and allowances are statutory; MPLADS is a scheme with its own rules (owner file; verify current amount before quoting).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 97 (presiding officers); Art 112 (Budget); Art 106 is distinct from MPLADS funds.
**Trap:** Do not conflate members' salaries with MPLADS funds or with the presiding officers' pay (Art 97).
**Back:** *"Parliament sets its own pay by law, and the law is public."*

### Flashcard Art-107 — Article 107
**Front:** Article 107 — Provisions as to introduction and passing of Bills
**Provision:** (1) Subject to Arts 109 and 117, a Bill other than a Money Bill may originate in either House. (2) Subject to Arts 108 and 109, a Bill is not deemed passed by Parliament unless both Houses agree (✅ corrected: prorogation was misnumbered as (2)). (3) A Bill pending in Parliament does not lapse by reason of prorogation. (4) A Bill pending in the RS that has not been passed by the LS does not lapse on dissolution of the LS. (5) A Bill pending in the LS, or passed by the LS and pending in the RS, lapses on dissolution (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part V, Art 107(1)–(5)). The point that a Bill passed by both Houses and pending assent does not lapse is not in cl. (1)–(5); it is an inference (⚠️ unverified as a clause).
**Learn:**
- Non-money Bills can start in either House.
- Prorogation does not lapse pending Bills; dissolution does lapse some.
- The RS's permanence protects only Bills the RS holds that the LS has not passed.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 108 (joint sitting); Art 109 (Money Bills); Art 117 (financial Bills); Art 111 (assent).
**Trap:** Bills lapse on dissolution, not on prorogation. A Bill passed by the LS and pending in the RS lapses.
**Back:** *"Prorogation keeps the Bill; dissolution lapses the Lok's Bill, and the Lok-passed Bill still pending in the Raj."*

### Flashcard Art-108 — Article 108
**Front:** Article 108 — Joint sitting of both Houses in certain cases
**Provision:** If after a Bill has been passed by one House and transmitted to the other: (a) it is rejected by the other House; (b) the Houses have finally disagreed as to amendments; or (c) more than six months elapse from its receipt by the other House without its being passed — the President may summon both Houses to meet in a joint sitting. The joint sitting is presided over by the Speaker of the LS or, in his absence, by a person determined under the joint-sitting rules (Art 118(4); ✅ corrected: Art 108 itself names no presiding officer, and the Deputy Speaker is not named in the printed text).
**Learn:**
- Joint sitting resolves deadlock on ordinary Bills; the larger LS usually prevails.
- Used three times: Dowry Prohibition Bill (1961), Banking Service Commission (Repeal) Bill (1978) and POTA (2002) (owner file ✅).
- No joint sitting for a Money Bill (the RS can only recommend; deemed passage under Art 109(4)), and none for a constitutional amendment (Art 368(2) requires separate House votes).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 109 (Money Bill); Art 368(2) (amendment); Art 79 (Parliament structure).
**Trap:** The joint sitting is presided over by the Speaker, not the Vice-President. There is no joint sitting for constitutional amendments, a common special-majority trap.
**Back:** *"Deadlock to the joint table: the Lok's numbers decide, the Speaker holds the gavel."*

### Flashcard Art-109 — Article 109
**Front:** Article 109 — Special procedure in respect of Money Bills
**Provision:** (1) A Money Bill shall not be introduced in the RS. (2) After a Money Bill is passed by the LS, it is transmitted to the RS for its recommendations; the RS must return it within fourteen days, with or without recommendations. (3) If the RS recommends amendments, the LS may accept or reject them. (4) If the RS does not return the Bill within fourteen days, it is deemed passed by both Houses at the expiry of that period in the form in which the LS passed it.
**Learn:**
- The RS can only recommend, not reject or amend, a Money Bill; it must return within 14 days.
- The LS may accept or reject RS recommendations.
- Deemed passage after 14 days if the RS does not return it.
- The LS's final say on money matters is the "power of the purse".
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 110 (definition); Art 117 (financial Bills); Aadhaar Act (certified as Money Bill: introduced 3 March 2016 under the Art 110(1)(c)–(e) heads, passed by the LS on 11 March 2016 after RS amendments were rejected; ✅ Wikipedia Aadhaar Act page; K.S. Puttaswamy (2018) upheld the Act, but the owner file's 4:1 split and Chandrachud J's dissent are ⚠️ unverified); Rojer Mathew v. South Indian Bank (2019) doubted that reasoning and referred the money-bill question to a larger Bench (owner file; ⚠️ 📰 verify status before dating).
**Trap:** The RS "recommends" but cannot "reject". A Money Bill is never introduced in the RS. The 14-day clock is for returning, not for enactment.
**Back:** *"Fourteen days to recommend, never to reject: the Raj advises, the Lok decides."*

### Flashcard Art-110 — Article 110
**Front:** Article 110 — Definition of "Money Bill"
**Provision:** (1) A Bill is a Money Bill if it contains only provisions dealing with: (a) imposition, abolition, remission, alteration or regulation of any tax; (b) regulation of borrowing by the Government of India, or giving of guarantees; (c) custody of the Consolidated Fund or Contingency Fund, and payments into or withdrawals from them; (d) appropriation of money out of the CFI; (e) declaring any expenditure to be charged on the CFI, or increasing any such amount; (f) receipt of money on account of the CFI or the Public Account, or the audit of the accounts of the Union or a State; (g) any matter incidental to these. (2) A Bill is not a Money Bill merely because it imposes fines or fees for licences or services, or demands payment for local purposes. (3) If a question arises whether a Bill is a Money Bill, the Speaker of the LS decides, and his decision is final.
**Learn:**
- The seven heads (a)–(g) are the exhaustive test.
- Fines and licence fees alone do not make a Money Bill (Art 110(2)).
- The Speaker's certificate is final (Art 110(3)).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Puttaswamy (2018); Rojer Mathew (2019; ⚠️ verify status). Art 117 (financial Bills); Art 199 (State analogue).
**Trap:** "Final" is not absolute: constitutional courts retain narrow review for illegality or constitutional misuse (owner file ✅). Do not confuse Money Bills (Art 110) with Financial Bills (Art 117).
**Back:** *"Tax, borrow, fund, spend, charge, receive, audit: seven doors, and the Speaker holds the key."*

### Flashcard Art-111 — Article 111
**Front:** Article 111 — Assent to Bills
**Provision:** When a Bill is passed by both Houses it is presented to the President, who declares that he assents, or withholds assent, or returns the Bill for reconsideration. A Bill other than a Money Bill may be returned for reconsideration; if the Houses re-pass it, with or without amendment, the President shall not withhold assent from it.
**Learn:**
- Three options: assent, withhold assent, or return for reconsideration.
- Return for reconsideration is confined to non-money Bills.
- Once the Houses re-pass a returned Bill, assent is mandatory.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 111 is the President's qualified (suspensive) veto; Art 200 (Governor analogue); Art 123 (ordinance).
**Trap:** The reconsideration route does not apply to a Money Bill; after re-passage the President cannot withhold assent again.
**Back:** *"Assent, withhold, or send back once; a re-passed Bill must be signed."*

### Flashcard Art-112 — Article 112
**Front:** Article 112 — Annual financial statement (Budget)
**Provision:** The President shall in respect of every financial year cause to be laid before both Houses a statement of the estimated receipts and expenditure of the Government of India for that year (the Annual Financial Statement). Expenditure is classified as (a) expenditure charged on the CFI and (b) other expenditure, which is votable. Charged expenditure (Art 112(3)) includes (a) the President's emoluments and office expenses; (b) the salaries and allowances of the Chairman and Deputy Chairman of the RS and the Speaker and Deputy Speaker of the LS; (c) debt charges; and (d) the pay and pensions of Supreme Court Judges and the pensions of Federal and High Court Judges (✅ corrected: the card listed the CAG's salary here and omitted (b); Wikisource Const. 2020, Part V, Art 112(3) summary. The CAG's charged status rests on Art 148(3), not checked here).
**Learn:**
- Annual Financial Statement = the Budget.
- Charged expenditure is discussed but not voted by Parliament.
- Votable expenditure goes to the demands for grants (Art 113).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 113 (demands); Art 114 (Appropriation); Art 115 (supplementary grants); Art 116 (votes on account); Art 266 (Consolidated Fund); Art 146(3) (SC expenses charged).
**Trap:** Do not say all Budget items are votable. Charged items include judges' salaries (Art 112(3)(d)) and presiding officers' salaries (Art 112(3)(b)); the Speaker's pay is charged, though its rate is fixed by Parliament under Art 97 (✅ corrected: the card said the Speaker's pay was not charged).
**Back:** *"The Budget is the statement; the votable half goes to the vote, the charged half is only noted."*

### Flashcard Art-113 — Article 113
**Front:** Article 113 — Procedure in Parliament with respect to estimates
**Provision:** The estimates of expenditure from the CFI for the year, other than charged expenditure, shall be submitted to the House of the People in the form of demands for grants. No demand for a grant shall be made except on the recommendation of the President. The LS may assent or refuse to assent to a demand, or assent subject to a reduction, but cannot increase it. Charged expenditure is not submitted to the vote.
**Learn:**
- Demands for grants are voted by the LS alone; the RS discusses the budget but does not vote demands.
- Cut motions: Policy Cut (grant reduced to Re 1), Economy Cut, Token Cut (Rs 100) (owner file).
- Guillotine: undiscussed demands are clubbed and voted at the deadline, so large sums pass without debate.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 112 (budget); Art 114 (Appropriation Bill); DRSCs scrutinise demands (24 since 2004; 17 in 1993 per owner file); Estimates Committee (30 members, LS only).
**Trap:** The RS cannot vote demands for grants. The Estimates Committee is LS-only. The LS cannot increase a demand.
**Back:** *"Demands are the Lok's alone; the Raj only discusses."*

### Flashcard Art-114 — Article 114
**Front:** Article 114 — Appropriation Bills
**Provision:** (1) The Appropriation Bill, after the grants are made, covers the demands voted by the LS and the charged expenditure (the latter not exceeding the amount shown). (2) No amendment may be proposed to such a Bill in either House which would vary the amount of any expenditure or alter the destination of any grant; the ruling of the presiding person is final. (3) Subject to Arts 115 and 116, no money may be withdrawn from the Consolidated Fund of India except under an appropriation made by law (✅ corrected: clause numbers were misassigned; Wikisource Const. 2020, Part V, Art 114(1)–(3)).
**Learn:**
- The Appropriation Bill legalises withdrawal from the Consolidated Fund; no withdrawal without an Appropriation Act.
- The Finance Bill enacts taxation; the Appropriation Bill enacts spending.
- Amendments to an Appropriation Bill cannot vary amount or purpose.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 266(3) (CFI withdrawal); Art 113 (demands); Art 115 (supplementary, additional and excess grants); Art 116 (votes on account).
**Trap:** The Appropriation Bill cannot be amended to alter the amount or purpose. The Finance Bill is not an appropriation law.
**Back:** *"No rupee leaves the Fund without a law: the Appropriation Act is the key."*

### Flashcard Art-115 — Article 115
**Front:** Article 115 — Supplementary, additional or excess grants
**Provision:** The President shall cause to be laid before both Houses, in respect of the year, (a) a supplementary, or additional, estimate for expenditure not included in the budget (insufficient provision or a new service); or (b) an excess grant where money has been spent on any service in excess of the amount granted for that year (✅ corrected: Art 115(1) has two limbs, (a) and (b), not three; Wikisource Const. 2020, Part V). Each is voted by the LS in the manner of Arts 113–114.
**Learn:**
- Supplementary: top-up for unforeseen expenditure within the year, voted before the money is spent.
- Additional: expenditure not provided for in the budget.
- Excess: regularising overspend after the money has been spent (post-facto).
- The excess grant is regularised typically on PAC's recommendation (a practice; owner file).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 114 (appropriation); Art 116 (votes on account); PAC (22 members, Opposition chair, reviews CAG reports; owner file).
**Trap:** Supplementary is in-year top-up before spending; excess is post-facto regularisation after overspend. Do not equate them.
**Back:** *"Top-up before, regularise after: supplementary anticipates, excess confesses."*

### Flashcard Art-116 — Article 116
**Front:** Article 116 — Votes on account, votes of credit and exceptional grants
**Provision:** Notwithstanding that the procedure of Arts 113–115 is not complete, the LS may (a) make a grant in advance (vote on account) in respect of the estimated expenditure for a part of the year; (b) make a vote of credit to meet an unexpected demand for which the amount cannot be stated in the budget; (c) make an exceptional grant for a special purpose not in the normal budget.
**Learn:**
- Vote on account: an advance grant pending full appropriation, so government keeps running (the Article fixes no duration; the practice of a few months is ⚠️ unverified, as the Wikipedia 'Vote on account' page was not reachable this pass, so do not quote a number).
- Vote of credit: lump-sum grant for an emergency of uncertain amount, itemised later.
- Exceptional grant: special-purpose grant outside the normal budget.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 115 (supplementary); Art 114 (appropriation); Art 113 (demands).
**Trap:** Vote on account is interim pending full appropriation; vote of credit is a lump sum for an emergency of uncertain amount; exceptional grant is special-purpose. Three distinct devices.
**Back:** *"Advance, lump-sum, special: three bridges across the budget gap."*

### Flashcard Art-117 — Article 117
**Front:** Article 117 — Special provisions as to financial Bills
**Provision:** (1) A Bill or amendment making provision for any matter in Art 110(1)(a)–(f) may not be introduced or moved except on the President's recommendation, and such a Bill may not be passed by a House unless the President has recommended its consideration (Financial Bill I, which may contain other matters too). (3) A Bill which, if enacted, would involve expenditure from the CFI may not be passed by a House unless the President has recommended consideration of the Bill (Financial Bill II). In both cases the RS has full powers of amendment and rejection, unlike a Money Bill.
**Learn:**
- Financial Bill I (Art 117(1)): contains Money Bill matters plus other matters; needs the President's recommendation for introduction; the RS has full powers.
- Financial Bill II (Art 117(3)): involves CFI expenditure; may originate in either House; the RS has full powers; the President's recommendation is needed for consideration.
- Every Money Bill is a Financial Bill, but not every Financial Bill is a Money Bill.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 110 (Money Bill); Art 109 (RS 14-day limit); Art 199 (State analogue).
**Trap:** Both categories of Financial Bills give the RS full powers; only a Money Bill confines the RS to a 14-day recommendation. "All Financial Bills are Money Bills" is false.
**Back:** *"Money bills are the Lok's alone; financial bills let the Raj vote, once the President nods."*

### Flashcard Art-118 — Article 118
**Front:** Article 118 — Rules of procedure
**Provision:** (1) Each House of Parliament may make rules for regulating its procedure and the conduct of its business, subject to the Constitution. (2) Until such rules are made, the rules of procedure and standing orders in force immediately before the commencement of the Constitution, adapted as necessary, apply. (3) The President, after consultation with the Chairman of the Council of States and the Speaker of the House of the People, may make rules for the procedure of joint sittings. (4) At a joint sitting the Speaker of the LS presides, or in his absence the person determined under the rules made under (3) (✅ corrected: the card assigned joint-sitting rule-making to cl. (2) and a Speaker/Chairman power to cl. (3), which the printed text does not contain; Wikisource Const. 2020, Part V, Art 118(1)–(4)).
**Learn:**
- Each House makes its own Rules of Procedure: the LS Rules and the RS Rules are separate documents.
- Question Hour and Zero Hour are governed differently: Question Hour is in the Rules; Zero Hour is an Indian innovation (since 1962) that is not in the Rules (owner file ✅).
- Procedural rules are internal, but they must comply with the Constitution.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 105 (privileges); Art 122 (courts not to inquire into procedure); Art 108 (joint sitting).
**Trap:** Zero Hour is not in the Rules; Question Hour is. Art 118 gives each House autonomy over its procedure, subject to the Constitution.
**Back:** *"Each House writes its own rules, and Zero Hour is an improvised guest that never got into the rulebook."*

### Flashcard Art-119 — Article 119
**Front:** Article 119 — Regulation by law of procedure in Parliament in relation to financial business
**Provision:** Parliament may, for the purpose of the timely completion of financial business, regulate by law the procedure of, and the conduct of business in, each House in relation to financial matters and appropriation Bills; such a law prevails over inconsistent rules or standing orders made under Art 118(1)–(2) (✅ corrected: the card's 'notwithstanding Art 118' was not the printed purpose clause; Wikisource Const. 2020, Part V, Art 119).
**Learn:**
- Art 119 is the statutory counterpart of Art 118 for financial business.
- Gives Parliament a legislative route to regulate budgetary procedure beyond the Rules of each House.
- Used to frame the rules of financial business; not as frequently invoked as Art 118.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 118 (rules); Arts 112–117 (budget procedure); Art 110 (Money Bill).
**Trap:** Art 119 is a *law* of Parliament over financial procedure; Art 118 is each House's *rules*. Do not conflate the two.
**Back:** *"Rules for each House, a law for the money: Art 118 writes the rules, Art 119 makes the law."*

### Flashcard Art-120 — Article 120
**Front:** Article 120 — Language to be used in Parliament
**Provision:** (1) Business in Parliament shall be transacted in Hindi or in English; the presiding officer may permit a member who cannot adequately express himself in either language to address the House in his mother tongue. (2) Unless Parliament by law otherwise provides, English ceases to be used for Parliament business after fifteen years from the commencement of the Constitution, i.e. after 1965 (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part V, Art 120 summary; '1965' is the card's gloss); the Official Languages Act, 1963 continues English (⚠️ statute wording not verified).
**Learn:**
- Hindi and English are the languages of Parliament business.
- The presiding officer may allow a member to speak in his mother tongue.
- The 1965 limit was made inoperative by statute (Official Languages Act, 1963).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 343–344 (Official Languages); Art 348 (SC language); Eighth Schedule languages.
**Trap:** Parliament business is in Hindi or English, not in all Eighth Schedule languages; the mother-tongue permission is at the presiding officer's discretion.
**Back:** *"Hindi and English in the chamber; mother tongue by the chair's leave."*

### Flashcard Art-121 — Article 121
**Front:** Article 121 — Restriction on discussion in Parliament
**Provision:** No discussion shall take place in Parliament with respect to the conduct of any Judge of the Supreme Court or of a High Court in the discharge of his duties, except upon a motion for presenting an address to the President praying for the removal of the Judge under Art 124(4).
**Learn:**
- Protects judicial independence by preventing routine parliamentary attacks on judges' conduct.
- The only door is a removal motion under Art 124(4).
- Applies to SC and HC judges.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 124(4) (removal); Art 211 (State analogue restricts legislative discussion of High Court judges' conduct); Judges (Inquiry) Act, 1968 (motion signed by 100 LS or 50 RS members; owner file).
**Trap:** Art 121 bars discussion of judicial conduct except on a removal motion; it does not bar legislative criticism of judgments as such (⚠️ scope to be read carefully).
**Back:** *"No talk of a judge's conduct on the floor, unless the motion is for his removal."*

### Flashcard Art-122 — Article 122
**Front:** Article 122 — Courts not to inquire into proceedings of Parliament
**Provision:** (1) The validity of any proceedings in Parliament shall not be called in question on the ground of any alleged irregularity of procedure. (2) No officer or member of Parliament in whom powers are vested for regulating procedure, or the conduct of business, or for maintaining order, shall be subject to the jurisdiction of any court in respect of the exercise of those powers.
**Learn:**
- Irregularity of procedure does not invalidate proceedings; courts cannot inquire into it.
- Presiding officers are protected from court jurisdiction in exercising order-keeping powers.
- Mirrors Art 212 for State legislatures.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 105 (privileges); Art 212 (State analogue); Art 118 (rules).
**Trap:** Procedural irregularity is not justiciable, but a constitutional violation is. Do not claim that courts never review Parliament; they can review constitutional questions on Bills.
**Back:** *"Irregular procedure stays valid: the chair is shielded, not the Constitution."*

### Flashcard Art-123 — Article 123
**Front:** Article 123 — Power of the President to promulgate Ordinances during recess of Parliament
**Provision:** If at any time, except when both Houses are in session, the President is satisfied that circumstances exist which render it necessary to take immediate action, he may promulgate such Ordinances as the circumstances appear to him to require. An Ordinance has the same force and effect as an Act of Parliament, but (a) must be laid before both Houses when they reassemble; and (b) ceases to operate at the expiration of six weeks from reassembly, or earlier if disapproved by resolutions of both Houses. It may be withdrawn at any time by the President.
**Learn:**
- Ordinance = executive law-making when Parliament is not in session; same force as an Act.
- Six weeks from reassembly is the life of the ordinance unless replaced by an Act or disapproved.
- Governor's analogue: Art 213 (State).
- Judicial review applies; D.C. Wadhwa v. State of Bihar (1987) held repromulgation a fraud on the Constitution; Krishna Kumar Singh v. State of Bihar (2017) reaffirmed that ordinances are reviewable (owner file ✅).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 213 (Governor's ordinance power); Art 13 (judicial review); Art 368 (no ordinance can amend the Constitution in practice).
**Trap:** The "six weeks" runs from reassembly of Parliament, not from promulgation; an ordinance is not a permanent substitute for a Bill.
**Back:** *"Six weeks from the reassembly, then the ordinance expires unless the Act replaces it."*

### Flashcard Art-124 — Article 124
**Front:** Article 124 — Establishment and constitution of the Supreme Court
**Provision:** (1) There shall be a Supreme Court of India, consisting of the Chief Justice and, until Parliament by law prescribes a larger number, not more than seven other judges. (2) Every judge is appointed by the President after consultation with the CJI and such other judges as necessary; the CJI must be consulted for any appointment other than his own; a judge holds office until age 65. (3) Qualifications: (a) five years as a High Court judge; (b) ten years as a High Court advocate; or (c) a distinguished jurist in the President's opinion. (4) A judge may be removed by the President on an address by each House supported by a majority of its total membership and a majority of not less than two-thirds of the members present and voting, on proved misbehaviour or incapacity. (5) Parliament may regulate investigation and proof of misbehaviour. (7) No retired judge may plead or act in any court or before any authority in India.
**Learn:**
- Appointment: President appoints on the collegium's recommendation (judge-made practice; not text).
- Removal: effective-special majority of both Houses; never successfully used against a SC judge (owner file: V. Ramaswami, 1993).
- Judges (Inquiry) Act, 1968: motion signed by 100 LS or 50 RS members; three-member inquiry committee.
- Sanctioned strength: Wikipedia's 'Supreme Court of India' page gives 38 judges including the CJI and says Parliament raised the number to 38 'in 2026' (✅ partly). The owner file's 'Act 14 of 2026' and '16 May 2026' commencement are NOT confirmed by the page read (⚠️ downgraded; treat the Act number and date as unverified).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 217 (High Court analogue; retirement 62); Art 124A–124C (NJAC, struck down 2015); Art 121 (no discussion of conduct); Art 125 (salaries); Art 128 (retired judges sitting).
**Trap:** Retirement is 65 for SC judges and 62 for HC judges (Art 217). Removal needs the Art 124(4) special majority in both Houses; "proved misbehaviour or incapacity" is the only ground.
**Back:** *"Sixty-five to retire, two-thirds to remove, five-ten-jurist to qualify: the Court keeps its own."*

### Flashcard Art-124A — Article 124A
**Front:** Article 124A — National Judicial Appointments Commission (NJAC) — composition
**Provision:** Inserted by the 99th Amendment Act (2014) and struck down by the Supreme Court in *Supreme Court Advocates-on-Record Association v. Union of India* (16 October 2015, 4:1). The NJAC was to consist of: (a) the Chief Justice of India, as ex officio chairperson; (b) two senior-most Supreme Court judges after the CJI; (c) the Union Minister in charge of law and justice; and (d) two eminent persons nominated by a committee of the CJI, the Prime Minister and the Leader of Opposition (or the leader of the single largest opposition party in the LS).
**Learn:**
- NJAC = six-member commission, with the CJI, two senior SC judges, the Law Minister and two eminent persons.
- Struck down for impairing the independence of the judiciary, which the Court held part of the basic structure (4:1).
- Revived the collegium system (per owner file, collegium restored in 2015).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 124B (functions); Art 124C (power of Parliament to regulate); 99th Amendment (2014); Second and Third Judges Cases (1993, 1998) (collegium origins).
**Trap:** The NJAC was struck down (2015) — it was not "rejected by the President" or "defeated in Parliament". The Law Minister's presence and the eminent persons' veto were the core defects.
**Back:** *"Six seats, one law, struck down: the collegium stayed, the Minister left."*

### Flashcard Art-124B — Article 124B
**Front:** Article 124B — Functions of the National Judicial Appointments Commission
**Provision:** It shall be the duty of the Commission to (a) recommend persons for appointment as Chief Justice of India, other judges of the Supreme Court, Chief Justices and judges of High Courts; (b) recommend transfers of Chief Justices and other judges of High Courts from one High Court to another; and (c) ensure that the person recommended is of ability and integrity. Parliament may by law regulate the procedure.
**Learn:**
- The NJAC was meant to replace the collegium's recommendation role.
- Functions: appointment recommendation, transfer recommendation, ability-integrity check.
- Never came into operation after the 2015 judgment.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 124A (composition); Art 124C (law-making power); Art 217 (HC appointment); Art 222 (transfers); SCAORA (2015).
**Trap:** The functions (Art 124B) were also struck down with the Commission; the collegium, not the NJAC, now makes recommendations.
**Back:** *"Recommend, transfer, screen for ability and integrity: the duties of a commission that never sat."*

### Flashcard Art-124C — Article 124C
**Front:** Article 124C — Power of Parliament to make law
**Provision:** Parliament may by law make provision for regulating the procedure of the Commission and for matters connected therewith or incidental thereto.
**Learn:**
- Power to regulate the NJAC's procedure by statute (the NJAC Act, 2014).
- The power to create the Commission rested on the 99th Amendment; the Act regulated its working.
- The entire NJAC framework (Arts 124A–124C plus the NJAC Act, 2014) fell in 2015.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 99th Amendment (2014); NJAC Act, 2014; SCAORA (2015, basic structure).
**Trap:** Art 124C is a power, not a surviving institution. Do not state that the NJAC currently exists.
**Back:** *"Parliament may regulate the commission it created; the Court then removed the commission."*

### Flashcard Art-125 — Article 125
**Front:** Article 125 — Salaries, etc., of Judges
**Provision:** (1) The salaries of the judges of the Supreme Court shall be such as Parliament may by law determine; until so determined, they are as specified in the Second Schedule. (2) Every judge is entitled to such allowances, and to leave and pension, as Parliament may determine, and their rights in respect of leave, pension and allowances shall not be varied to his disadvantage after his appointment.
**Learn:**
- Salaries are fixed by Parliament by law (Second Schedule for initial figures).
- Post-appointment variation to disadvantage is barred (financial autonomy).
- Judges' salaries are charged on the Consolidated Fund (Art 112(3)(d) in the printed text; the earlier cross-reference to (a) was corrected).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 112(3)(d) (charged expenditure); Art 146(3) (SC administrative expenses charged on the CFI); Art 50 (separation of judiciary and executive).
**Trap:** Salaries are charged on the CFI (non-votable), and cannot be cut to a judge's disadvantage after appointment, except in a financial emergency (Art 360 — ⚠️ verify exceptions).
**Back:** *"Set by law, charged on the Fund, never cut after appointment: money as a shield."*

### Flashcard Art-126 — Article 126
**Front:** Article 126 — Appointment of acting Chief Justice
**Provision:** When the office of Chief Justice of India is vacant, or when the CJI is unable to perform his duties or is absent, the duties of the office shall be performed by such one of the other judges of the Court as the President may appoint for the purpose.
**Learn:**
- The President appoints one of the other judges as acting CJI.
- By convention the senior-most judge is appointed, though the text gives the President a choice.
- Acting CJI has the full duties of the CJI for the period.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 124 (judges); Art 127 (ad hoc judges); Art 128 (retired judges sitting).
**Trap:** The text gives the President the choice; the seniority convention is not in the Constitution (owner file). Do not state that the senior-most judge is a constitutional requirement.
**Back:** *"When the CJI is away, the President names the stand-in, usually the senior-most by convention."*

### Flashcard Art-127 — Article 127
**Front:** Article 127 — Appointment of ad hoc judges
**Provision:** If at any time there is a lack of quorum of the judges of the Supreme Court to hold or continue any session, the Chief Justice may, with the previous consent of the President, request in writing the attendance at the Court of a judge of a High Court qualified for appointment as a Supreme Court judge, to sit and act as a judge of the Supreme Court (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part V, Art 127(1) summary: CJI request with the President's previous consent, after consultation with the HC Chief Justice. The printed text shows the 99th Amendment's NJAC wording, which was struck down in 2015, so the CJI wording governs; the HC consultation clause is ⚠️ unverified).
**Learn:**
- Ad hoc judges are High Court judges, not retired judges (retired judges fall under Art 128).
- Requires the CJI's request and the President's prior consent.
- Used to fill quorum gaps, not to add judges permanently.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 128 (retired judges sitting); Art 224A (retired HC judges sitting in a HC); Art 145 (rules of procedure, quorum).
**Trap:** Ad hoc judges are serving HC judges; retired judges sit under Art 128. Do not mix the two routes.
**Back:** *"Quorum short? Call a qualified High Court judge: the President's nod, the CJI's letter."*

### Flashcard Art-128 — Article 128
**Front:** Article 128 — Attendance of retired judges at sittings of the Supreme Court
**Provision:** Notwithstanding anything in this Chapter, the Chief Justice of India may at any time, with the previous consent of the President, request any person who has held the office of a judge of the Supreme Court or who is a retired judge of a High Court qualified for appointment as a Supreme Court judge, to sit and act as a judge of the Supreme Court. Such a person shall be paid such allowances as the President may determine and shall have the jurisdiction, powers and privileges of a judge of the Supreme Court (but not otherwise be a judge). (The printed Wikisource text shows NJAC wording for the requesting authority from the struck-down 99th Amendment; the operative request is by the CJI after SCAORA (2015). ⚠️ check against the printed text.)
**Learn:**
- The CJI requests; the President consents; the retired judge consents to sit.
- Retired SC judges and eligible retired HC judges can be requested.
- Allowances are set by the President; the person is not a serving judge.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 124(7) (no pleading or acting after retirement, except as Art 128 permits); Art 224A (retired HC judges); Art 127 (ad hoc judges).
**Trap:** Art 128 permits sitting as a judge of the SC; Art 124(7) bars pleading or acting before any court. Art 128 sitters are judges of the Supreme Court while sitting, which is different from acting as an advocate.
**Back:** *"Retired judges may sit on the bench, but never plead at the bar."*

### Flashcard Art-129 — Article 129
**Front:** Article 129 — Supreme Court to be a court of record
**Provision:** The Supreme Court shall be a court of record and shall have all the powers of such a court including the power to punish for contempt of itself.
**Learn:**
- Court of record: its records are evidence and cannot be questioned, and it has inherent contempt power.
- Contempt power is constitutional; the Contempt of Courts Act, 1971 defines civil and criminal contempt but does not curtail the constitutional power (owner file ✅).
- The High Courts are courts of record under Art 215.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 215 (High Court analogue); Contempt of Courts Act, 1971 (truth a defence if bona fide and in public interest, since 2006; punishment up to six months and/or Rs 2,000 fine — owner file).
**Trap:** Contempt power is inherent and constitutional, but the Contempt of Courts Act defines the offence; do not say the Act is the source of the power.
**Back:** *"Court of record, power to punish its own contempt: the record speaks and the bench enforces."*

### Flashcard Art-130 — Article 130
**Front:** Article 130 — Seat of the Supreme Court
**Provision:** The Supreme Court shall sit ordinarily in Delhi or in such other place or places as the Chief Justice of India may, with the approval of the President, from time to time appoint.
**Learn:**
- Seat: Delhi by default; the CJI may appoint other places with the President's approval.
- The Court sits in Delhi in its ordinary course.
- The seat is administrative, not a constitutional restriction on jurisdiction.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 214 (High Court seat analogue); Art 130 is a convenience, not a limit on jurisdiction.
**Trap:** The seat is Delhi by default, and the CJI with the President's approval can add others; it is not fixed by Parliament's law.
**Back:** *"Delhi by default, other seats by the CJI's pen and the President's nod."*

### Flashcard Art-131 — Article 131
**Front:** Article 131 — Original jurisdiction of the Supreme Court
**Provision:** Subject to the Constitution, the SC has exclusive original jurisdiction in any dispute (a) between the Government of India and one or more States; (b) between the Government of India and any State or States on one side and any other State or States on the other; or (c) between two or more States, if and in so far as the dispute involves any question (whether of law or fact) on which the existence or extent of a legal right depends. The proviso excludes certain disputes, including those arising from pre-Constitution treaties and agreements, and disputes under Art 262 (inter-State water disputes; owner file ✅).
**Learn:**
- Federal disputes only: Centre-State and State-State, where a legal right is at stake.
- Excludes inter-State water disputes (Art 262: tribunals), pre-Constitution treaty matters and other excluded categories listed in the proviso.
- Political questions are not justiciable under this jurisdiction (⚠️ verify limit).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 131A (omitted; see below); Art 32 (writ); Art 262 (water disputes); Art 263 (inter-governmental councils).
**Trap:** Art 131 covers only federal disputes where a legal right is in question; inter-State water disputes are excluded (Art 262). The Finance Commission matters are not Art 131 disputes (owner file).
**Back:** *"Only federal fights with a legal right at stake; water goes to the tribunals."*

### Flashcard Art-131A — Article 131A
**Front:** Article 131A — Exclusive jurisdiction in regard to questions as to constitutional validity of Central laws (omitted)
**Provision:** **Omitted** by the 43rd Amendment (1977; in force 1978). The 42nd Amendment (1976) had inserted this Article, giving the Supreme Court exclusive jurisdiction over questions of the constitutional validity of Central laws, so that such questions could not be raised before High Courts.
**Learn:**
- The 42nd Amendment created an exclusive SC jurisdiction over Central law validity.
- The 43rd Amendment removed it, restoring the High Courts' concurrent power to strike down Central laws.
- Removal reflected the 1977–78 reversal of the Emergency-era constitutional changes.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 42nd Amendment (1976) inserted Arts 131A, 139A, 144A and others; 43rd Amendment (1977; in force 1978) omitted 131A and 144A (✅ Wikipedia 43rd Amendment page: passed 1977, in force 13 April 1978; omits 131A and 144A).
**Trap:** 131A is omitted, not "in force"; the High Courts retain jurisdiction under Art 226 and the SC under Art 132–136 for constitutional questions.
**Back:** *"The 42nd gave the Court a monopoly on Central laws; the 43rd gave it back to the High Courts."*

### Flashcard Art-132 — Article 132
**Front:** Article 132 — Appellate jurisdiction of the Supreme Court in appeals from High Courts in certain cases
**Provision:** (1) An appeal lies to the Supreme Court from any judgment, decree or final order of a High Court in civil, criminal or other proceedings, if the High Court certifies under Art 134A that the case involves a substantial question of law as to the interpretation of the Constitution. (2) [Omitted: shown as asterisks in the printed text, Wikisource Const. 2020, Part V, Art 132(2). ✅ corrected: the card's Parliament-power clause is Art 134(2).] (3) Where such a certificate is given, any party may appeal on the ground that any question of law as to the interpretation of the Constitution has been wrongly decided (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part V, Art 132(3); wording partly elided in the printed text).
**Learn:**
- Constitutional-question appeal: the HC must certify a substantial question of law as to the interpretation of the Constitution.
- Certificate route is supplemented by Art 134A (the HC must consider the certificate).
- The SC is the final interpreter of the Constitution on appeal.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 134A (certificate); Art 133 (civil appeals); Art 134 (criminal appeals); Art 136 (SLP); 44th Amendment (1978) amended Art 132.
**Trap:** Art 132 is the constitutional-question appeal, which needs an HC certificate; Art 136 SLP is discretionary and does not need a certificate.
**Back:** *"Constitutional question, High Court certificate: the appeal climbs only on a constitutional rung."*

### Flashcard Art-133 — Article 133
**Front:** Article 133 — Appellate jurisdiction of the Supreme Court in appeals from High Courts in regard to civil matters
**Provision:** (1) An appeal lies to the SC from any judgment, decree or final order of a High Court in a civil proceeding, if the High Court certifies that the case involves a substantial question of law of general importance and that in the opinion of the High Court the question needs to be decided by the SC (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part V, Art 133(1)). (2) Any party appealing under (1) may urge as one ground that a substantial question of law as to the interpretation of the Constitution has been wrongly decided (✅ Wikisource Art 133(2) summary). (3) Notwithstanding anything in the article, unless Parliament by law otherwise provides, no appeal lies to the SC from a judgment of one Judge of a High Court (✅ corrected: the card described cl. (3) as a certificate rule; Wikisource Art 133(3)). The 30th Amendment (1972) amended Art 133 by replacing the value test with a substantial-question-of-law test (✅ Wikipedia 30th Amendment page; in force 9 June 1972).
**Learn:**
- The civil-appeal route needs an HC certificate of general importance.
- Before the 30th Amendment (1972), the civil-appeal route had a value limit; the Amendment replaced it with the legal-question test (✅ Wikipedia 30th Amendment page).
- SLP under Art 136 remains the wider discretionary door.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 132 (constitutional appeals); Art 134 (criminal appeals); Art 134A (certificate); Art 136 (SLP).
**Trap:** Civil appeals need a "substantial question of law of general importance" certificate, not just any question of law; SLP is different.
**Back:** *"Civil appeals need a general-importance certificate from the High Court: a gate, not a free road."*


### Flashcard Art-134 — Article 134
**Front:** Article 134 — Appellate jurisdiction of the Supreme Court in regard to criminal matters
**Provision:** (1) An appeal lies to the SC from any judgment, final order or sentence in a criminal proceeding of a High Court, if the High Court (a) has on appeal reversed an order of acquittal and sentenced the accused to death; (b) has withdrawn for trial before itself any case from a subordinate court and convicted the accused and sentenced him to death; or (c) certifies under Art 134A that the case is a fit one for appeal to the SC. (2) Parliament may by law confer further powers on the SC in criminal appeals.
**Learn:**
- Death-sentence appeals are the core route: acquittal reversed to death, or withdrawn-and-convicted-to-death.
- A certificate under Art 134A extends the route.
- Parliament may confer further criminal-appeal powers by law.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 134A (certificate); Art 132 (constitutional); Art 136 (SLP).
**Trap:** Criminal appeals as of right are limited to death-sentence cases (Art 134(1)(a)–(b)); other criminal appeals need a certificate under 134A or SLP under Art 136.
**Back:** *"Death sentences climb as of right; other convictions need a certificate or special leave."*

### Flashcard Art-134A — Article 134A
**Front:** Article 134A — Certificate for appeal to the Supreme Court
**Provision:** Every High Court, whether on its own motion or on the application of a party, shall immediately after the passing of its judgment, decree or final order in a case, consider whether it is a fit case for appeal to the Supreme Court under Art 132(1), 133(1) or 134(1)(c), and if it so determines, shall give and grant a certificate accordingly.
**Learn:**
- Inserted by the 44th Amendment (1978, owner-cited via Wikipedia amendment table).
- The HC must consider the certificate suo motu or on application.
- Makes the certificate process a mandatory consideration rather than an optional request.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 44th Amendment (1978); Arts 132, 133 and 134 (certificate routes); Art 136 (SLP as the discretionary alternative).
**Trap:** The 134A certificate is for Arts 132(1), 133(1) and 134(1)(c) routes; it is not the Art 136 special leave.
**Back:** *"The High Court must consider the certificate: it cannot simply forget it."*

### Flashcard Art-135 — Article 135
**Front:** Article 135 — Jurisdiction and powers of the Federal Court under existing law to be exercisable by the Supreme Court
**Provision:** Until Parliament by law otherwise provides, the Supreme Court shall have jurisdiction and powers with respect to any matter for which the Federal Court had jurisdiction immediately before the commencement of the Constitution, and the law in force relating to such jurisdiction continues.
**Learn:**
- Continuity provision: the pre-1950 Federal Court's jurisdiction passes to the SC.
- Federal Court existed from 1937 to 1950 (the SC replaced it on 28 January 1950).
- The Court's jurisdiction is thus both constitutional and continuing from earlier law.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 124 (SC establishment); Art 374 (continuity of Federal Court proceedings); Privy Council appeals ended by Art 374(4) (the owner file records the replacement).
**Trap:** Art 135 is a continuity clause, not a new head of jurisdiction; the SC replaced the Federal Court and the Privy Council.
**Back:** *"The Federal Court's keys passed to the new Court on 28 January 1950."*

### Flashcard Art-136 — Article 136
**Front:** Article 136 — Special leave to appeal by the Supreme Court
**Provision:** (1) Notwithstanding anything in this Chapter, the Supreme Court may, in its discretion, grant special leave to appeal from any judgment, decree, determination, sentence or order in any cause or matter passed or made by any court or tribunal in the territory of India. (2) Clause (1) does not apply to any judgment, determination, sentence or order passed or made by any court or tribunal constituted by or under any law relating to the armed forces.
**Learn:**
- Special Leave Petition (SLP) is the Court's discretionary, residual gateway to correct injustice.
- Available against any court or tribunal's order, except military tribunals.
- Not a regular right of appeal; exercised sparingly.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 132–134A (certificate-based routes); Art 142 (complete justice); Art 137 (review).
**Trap:** SLP is discretionary and not a regular right; the military-tribunal exclusion is the explicit exception in Art 136(2).
**Back:** *"Special leave is the Court's discretion: any court, any tribunal, except the military ones."*

### Flashcard Art-137 — Article 137
**Front:** Article 137 — Review of judgments or orders by the Supreme Court
**Provision:** Subject to the provisions of any law made by Parliament or any rules made under Art 145, the Supreme Court shall have power to review any judgment pronounced or order made by it.
**Learn:**
- Review is the Court's own power to revisit its judgments, subject to law and rules.
- Review is on limited grounds (error apparent on the face of the record, or new evidence, under the rules).
- Review is distinct from the judge-made curative petition (*Rupa Ashok Hurra v. Ashok Hurra*, 2002; owner file).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 145 (rules); Art 142 (complete justice); Art 129 (court of record); Rupa Ashok Hurra (2002) curative petition.
**Trap:** Review (Art 137) and curative petition (2002, judge-made) are separate correction routes. The curative petition is an extraordinary last remedy.
**Back:** *"The Court may review its own judgments within the rules, and curative petitions are the last door."*

### Flashcard Art-138 — Article 138
**Front:** Article 138 — Enlargement of the jurisdiction of the Supreme Court
**Provision:** (1) The Supreme Court shall have such further jurisdiction and powers with respect to any of the matters in the Union List as Parliament may by law confer. (2) The Supreme Court shall have such further jurisdiction and powers as the Government of India and the Government of any State may by special agreement confer, if Parliament by law provides for it.
**Learn:**
- Parliament may confer further jurisdiction on the SC by law (Union List matters).
- Special agreement between the Centre and a State can confer jurisdiction if Parliament provides for it.
- Enlargement is by statute or by agreement, not by the Court's own choice.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 138 (enlargement); Art 139 (writs for other purposes); Art 140 (ancillary powers); Art 131 (original jurisdiction).
**Trap:** The Court cannot enlarge its own jurisdiction; Parliament confers it by law (or the Centre and a State by agreement under a Parliament law).
**Back:** *"Parliament confers the Court's reach by law, never the Court itself."*

### Flashcard Art-139 — Article 139
**Front:** Article 139 — Conferment on the Supreme Court of powers to issue certain writs
**Provision:** Parliament may by law confer on the Supreme Court power to issue directions, orders or writs for any purposes other than those mentioned in Art 32(2) (the five writs are named in Art 32, not in this Article).
**Learn:**
- Writ power under Art 32 is for enforcing Fundamental Rights; Art 139 extends the same writ power to other purposes if Parliament so provides.
- Parliament must enact the extension by law.
- The writs themselves (habeas corpus, mandamus, prohibition, quo warranto, certiorari) are the Art 32 set.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 32 (FR writs); Art 226 (HC writs); Art 139A (transfer of cases); Art 140 (ancillary powers).
**Trap:** Art 139 requires Parliament's law; the Court does not extend itself to other writs. Art 32 writs are FR-only; Art 226 is wider in purpose.
**Back:** *"Writs beyond Fundamental Rights need Parliament's law; the Court does not write its own mandate."*

### Flashcard Art-139A — Article 139A
**Front:** Article 139A — Transfer of certain cases
**Provision:** (1) Where cases involving the same or substantially the same questions of law are pending before the Supreme Court and one or more High Courts, or before two or more High Courts, the Supreme Court may, on its own motion or on the application of the Attorney-General of India or a party, withdraw the cases to itself and dispose of them. (2) The SC may, if it deems it expedient for the ends of justice, transfer any case, appeal or other proceeding pending before one High Court to any other High Court (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part V, Art 139A(2)). The proviso to (1) also lets the Court return withdrawn cases to the High Court with its judgment on the legal questions (✅ Wikisource summary).
**Learn:**
- Inserted by the 42nd Amendment (1976; ✅ Wikipedia 42nd Amendment page); the 44th Amendment (1978) is listed among the articles it sought to amend (✅ Wikipedia 44th Amendment page).
- Consolidation device: the SC can take over cases raising the same questions of law.
- Avoids conflicting High Court rulings on the same question.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 139 (writs); Art 141 (binding precedent); Art 131 (original jurisdiction).
**Trap:** The power exists to avoid inconsistency; it is not an appeal route. Do not confuse with Art 136 SLP.
**Back:** *"Same question in many courts: the Supreme Court may gather them all to itself."*

### Flashcard Art-140 — Article 140
**Front:** Article 140 — Ancillary powers of the Supreme Court
**Provision:** Parliament may by law make provision for conferring on the Supreme Court such supplemental powers not inconsistent with the provisions of this Constitution as may appear to be necessary or desirable for the purpose of enabling the Court more effectively to exercise the jurisdiction conferred on it by or under the Constitution.
**Learn:**
- Parliament supplies supplementary powers by law to make SC jurisdiction effective.
- Limit: such powers must not be inconsistent with the Constitution.
- Complements Art 142 (the Court's own complete-justice power).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 142 (complete justice); Art 138 (enlargement); Art 145 (rules).
**Trap:** Art 140 powers come from Parliament's law; Art 142 powers come from the Constitution itself. Do not confuse them.
**Back:** *"Parliament may add tools to the Court's kit, provided they fit the Constitution."*

### Flashcard Art-141 — Article 141
**Front:** Article 141 — Law declared by Supreme Court to be binding on all courts
**Provision:** The law declared by the Supreme Court shall be binding on all courts within the territory of India.
**Learn:**
- The binding part is the ratio decidendi (the reasoning necessary to the decision), not obiter dicta.
- Binds all courts and tribunals in India; the Supreme Court is not strictly bound by its own decisions and may overrule them (by larger bench, per practice).
- Judge-made norms operate only until Parliament legislates (Vishaka guidelines, 1997; owner file).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Vishaka v. State of Rajasthan (1997): guidelines binding under Art 141 "until Parliament legislates"; superseded by the 2013 Act (owner file ✅). Art 142 (enforcement); Art 145(3) (Constitution Bench).
**Trap:** Art 141 binds courts, not Parliament; Parliament can legislate over a judge-made rule. Obiter dicta are not binding in the same way as ratio.
**Back:** *"What the Court decides binds every court, until Parliament speaks."*

### Flashcard Art-142 — Article 142
**Front:** Article 142 — Enforcement of decrees and orders of Supreme Court and unless as to discovery, etc.
**Provision:** (1) The Supreme Court in the exercise of its jurisdiction may pass such decree or make such order as is necessary for doing complete justice in any cause or matter pending before it; any decree so passed or order so made is enforceable throughout the territory of India in such manner as may be prescribed by law, and until so prescribed, in such manner as the President may by order direct. (2) Subject to any law made by Parliament, the Supreme Court has all and every power to make any order for securing the attendance of any person, the discovery or production of documents, or the investigation or punishment of any contempt of itself.
**Learn:**
- "Complete justice" power, exercised in a cause or matter pending before the Court.
- Limits (owner file ✅): Prem Chand Garg v. Excise Commissioner (1963): cannot override a fundamental right; Supreme Court Bar Association v. Union of India (1998): "supplement, not supplant", so Art 142 cannot override substantive statutory law.
- Used for the Bhopal (Union Carbide) settlement (owner file; ✅ Wikipedia 'Bhopal disaster': the Supreme Court approved a US$470 million settlement in February 1989; the Art 142 basis is not on the page read).
- The owner file cites the Art 143 opinion of 20 November 2025 as holding that Art 142 cannot grant "deemed assent" to Bills (📰 ⚠️ verify).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 141 (binding precedent); Art 143 (advisory opinion); Art 129 (contempt); Art 140 (ancillary powers).
**Trap:** Art 142 is supplementary, not supplanting. It cannot override a fundamental right (1963) or statutory law applicable to a case (1998). It is not unlimited legislative power.
**Back:** *"Complete justice, but supplement not supplant, and never against a fundamental right."*

### Flashcard Art-143 — Article 143
**Front:** Article 143 — Power of the President to consult the Supreme Court
**Provision:** (1) If at any time it appears to the President that a question of law or fact has arisen, or is likely to arise, which is of such a nature and of such public importance that it is expedient to obtain the opinion of the Supreme Court, he may refer the question to the Court, which may, after such hearing as it thinks fit, report its opinion. (2) The President may, notwithstanding the proviso to Art 131, refer to the Supreme Court a dispute of the kind excluded by that proviso (pre-Constitution treaties, agreements, sanads and similar instruments), and the Court shall then report its opinion (✅ corrected: the reference is permissive ('may'), not a mandatory route; Wikisource Const. 2020, Part V, Art 143(2)).
**Learn:**
- Advisory jurisdiction: under Art 143(1) the Court may decline; under Art 143(2) the reference is the President's choice, but once made the Court must report its opinion (✅ Wikisource summary).
- Advisory opinions do not bind as a judgment (owner file ✅).
- The owner file records Special Reference No. 1 of 2025 (opinion of 20 November 2025, cited as 2025 INSC 1333): no rigid timelines for Governor or President on Bills; no deemed assent under Art 142; prolonged unexplained inaction remains reviewable (📰 ⚠️ still unverified: the Wikipedia reference page was not reachable this pass; verify the text and citation before quoting).
- Art 145(3) requires a Constitution Bench (at least five judges) for an Art 143 reference on a substantial question of constitutional interpretation.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 145(3) (Constitution Bench); Art 142 (complete justice); Arts 200–201 (Governor and President on Bills).
**Trap:** An advisory opinion does not "overrule" a judgment. Describe the 2025 opinion as later and controlling, not as overruling State of Tamil Nadu v. Governor (8 April 2025; owner file). The Court may decline Art 143(1) references.
**Back:** *"The President may ask, the Court may answer; the answer advises but does not bind."*

### Flashcard Art-144 — Article 144
**Front:** Article 144 — Civil and judicial authorities to act in aid of the Supreme Court
**Provision:** All authorities, civil and judicial, in the territory of India shall act in aid of the Supreme Court.
**Learn:**
- All civil and judicial authorities must assist the SC: enforcement, production of records, compliance with orders.
- Executive compliance is essential because the Court has "neither purse nor sword" (owner file).
- Foundation of the Court's enforcement power, alongside Art 142.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 142 (enforcement); Art 129 (contempt); Art 32 (FR writs); Art 144A (omitted).
**Trap:** Art 144 is an obligation on authorities; the Court still depends on executive compliance in practice.
**Back:** *"Every authority in India acts in aid of the Supreme Court: the Court's sword is borrowed."*

### Flashcard Art-144A — Article 144A
**Front:** Article 144A — Special provisions as to disposal of questions relating to constitutional validity of laws (omitted)
**Provision:** **Omitted** by the 43rd Amendment (1977; in force 1978). The 42nd Amendment (1976) had inserted this Article, prescribing special rules (including a special bench rule: per Wikipedia's 43rd Amendment page, a law could be struck down only by a bench of at least seven judges, with a two-thirds majority; ✅) for disposing of questions about the constitutional validity of laws.
**Learn:**
- The 42nd Amendment created the special regime; the 43rd Amendment removed it.
- Paired with the omitted Art 131A (exclusive SC jurisdiction over Central laws).
- Current rule for constitutional questions: Art 145(3) Constitution Bench (minimum five judges).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 42nd Amendment (1976); 43rd Amendment (1977; in force 1978); Art 131A (omitted); Art 145(3).
**Trap:** Do not cite Art 144A as current law. For constitutional questions today, use Art 145(3).
**Back:** *"The 42nd made a special bench rule; the 43rd took it back: today the five-judge Constitution Bench governs."*

### Flashcard Art-145 — Article 145
**Front:** Article 145 — Rules of Court, etc.
**Provision:** (1) Subject to any law made by Parliament, the Supreme Court may from time to time, with the approval of the President, make rules for regulating generally the practice and procedure of the Court. (2) Rules may fix the number of judges sitting for any purpose. (3) A case involving a substantial question of law as to the interpretation of the Constitution, or a reference under Art 143, must be heard by a Bench of not less than five judges (a Constitution Bench). (4) No judgment is delivered save in open court (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part V, Art 145(1)–(4) summary).
**Learn:**
- The Court makes its own rules with the President's approval.
- Constitution Bench = minimum five judges for constitutional interpretation or an Art 143 reference.
- Most cases are heard by two- or three-judge benches; the five-judge floor applies to constitutional questions.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 143 (advisory reference); Art 137 (review under rules); Art 131 (original jurisdiction); the 42nd and 43rd Amendments amended Art 145 in 1976–78 (amendment table).
**Trap:** A Constitution Bench is a five-judge bench for a substantial constitutional question, not "the whole Court" and not any "larger bench" of arbitrary size.
**Back:** *"Five judges for the Constitution; the Court writes its rules with the President's approval."*

### Flashcard Art-146 — Article 146
**Front:** Article 146 — Officers and servants and the expenses of the Supreme Court
**Provision:** (1) Appointments of officers and servants of the Supreme Court shall be made by the Chief Justice of India or by another judge or officer he authorises (the President may by rule require UPSC consultation; ✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part V, Art 146(1)). (2) Parliament may by law regulate the conditions of service of such officers and servants; until so provided, rules are made by the CJI, with the President's approval as to pay, leave and pensions (✅ corrected: the card gave the President the general rule-making role; Wikisource Art 146(2)). (3) The administrative expenses of the Supreme Court, including all salaries, allowances and pensions payable to officers and servants, shall be charged on the Consolidated Fund of India.
**Learn:**
- The CJI controls appointments of court staff, subject to law.
- Administrative expenses, including staff pay, are charged on the CFI (non-votable).
- Financial autonomy supports judicial independence.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 112(3) (charged expenditure); Art 125 (judges' salaries); Art 50 (separation of judiciary and executive); Art 98 (parallel for Parliament's secretariat).
**Trap:** Staff and administrative expenses are charged on the CFI; judges' own salaries are charged under Art 112(3)(d) and Art 125.
**Back:** *"The CJI hires the staff, the Fund pays the bills, and the vote never reaches it."*

### Flashcard Art-147 — Article 147
**Front:** Article 147 — Interpretation
**Provision:** In Chapter IV of Part V and Chapter V of Part VI, references to a substantial question of law as to the interpretation of the Constitution include a substantial question of law as to the interpretation of the Government of India Act, 1935 (including any enactment amending or supplementing it), of the Indian Independence Act, 1947, or of orders made under them, or of the Constitution (✅ corrected: the 1947 Act and orders are also covered; Wikisource Const. 2020, Part V, Art 147 summary).
**Learn:**
- Extends the constitutional-question certificate and Constitution Bench rules to questions on the 1935 Act.
- Bridges the pre-1950 constitutional order and the present Constitution.
- Interpretive clause only; it confers no new jurisdiction.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 132 (constitutional appeals); Art 145(3) (Constitution Bench); Government of India Act, 1935 (pre-Constitution law).
**Trap:** Art 147 is an interpretation clause, not a grant of power; do not treat it as an independent head of jurisdiction.
**Back:** *"Questions on the 1935 Act count as constitutional questions for the Court's purposes."*

### Flashcard Art-148 — Article 148
**Front:** Article 148 — Comptroller and Auditor-General of India
**Provision:** (1) There shall be a CAG of India, appointed by the President by warrant under his hand and seal, and removable only in the like manner and on the like grounds as a judge of the Supreme Court (address of each House by special majority on proved misbehaviour or incapacity) (✅ corrected: removal is in cl. (1); Wikisource Const. 2020, Part V, Art 148(1)). (2) The CAG takes an oath or affirmation before the President or his appointee before entering office (✅ Wikisource Art 148(2) summary). (3) The salary and conditions of service of the CAG are determined by Parliament and cannot be varied to his disadvantage after appointment; his salary and administrative expenses are charged on the Consolidated Fund of India. (4) The CAG is ineligible for further office under the Government of India or any State after ceasing to hold office. The six-year-or-65 term is fixed by the DPC Act, 1971 (s.4, owner file).
**Learn:**
- Appointment: President alone, by warrant under hand and seal (no collegium).
- Removal: like a SC judge (Art 124(4)), not at the President's pleasure.
- Post-office bar: no further office under the Union or a State.
- Head of the Indian Audit and Accounts Department; "guardian of the public purse" (Ambedkar: "the most important officer under the Constitution", owner file).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 124(4) (judges' removal); Art 149 (duties); CAG's (DPC) Act, 1971 (term and duties; 1976 amendment separated accounts from audit, owner file ✅); Art 279 (net proceeds certified by CAG).
**Trap:** The CAG is removable like a SC judge, not at the President's pleasure. The six-year-or-65 term is statutory (DPC Act s.4); the removal rule and the post-office bar are constitutional.
**Back:** *"Appointed by warrant, removed like a judge, barred from further office: the guardian of the purse."*

### Flashcard Art-149 — Article 149
