**Front:** Article 149 — Duties and powers of the Comptroller and Auditor-General
**Provision:** The CAG shall perform such duties and exercise such powers in relation to the accounts of the Union and of the States, and of any other authority or body, as may be prescribed by or under any law made by Parliament, and until Parliament so provides, the duties of the pre-Constitution Auditor-General (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part V, Art 149 summary).
**Learn:**
- Duties are statutory in detail: the CAG's (Duties, Powers and Conditions of Service) Act, 1971 (DPC Act).
- Audit covers the Consolidated Fund, Contingency Fund and Public Account; receipts and expenditure; stores; government companies; bodies substantially financed from public funds (owner file ✅).
- Audit is ex post: it reports, it does not reverse or disallow.
- Three kinds of audit: legal/regulatory (obligatory), propriety (discretionary), performance (owner file).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 148 (appointment); Art 150 (form of accounts); Art 151 (reports); Art 279 (net proceeds); PAC and COPU (scrutiny of reports; owner file).
**Trap:** Only legal/regulatory audit is obligatory; propriety audit is discretionary. The CAG audits after the spend; he cannot stop money leaving the Fund, which is why India's CAG is a "comptroller in name only".
**Back:** *"Duties by statute, audit after the spend: the CAG reports, Parliament decides."*

### Flashcard Art-150 — Article 150
**Front:** Article 150 — Form of accounts of the Union and the States
**Provision:** The accounts of the Union and of the States shall be kept in such form as the President may, on the advice of the Comptroller and Auditor-General of India, prescribe.
**Learn:**
- The President prescribes the form of accounts on the CAG's advice.
- The text covers the accounts of both the Union and the States.
- Gives the CAG a technical role in financial reporting standards.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 148 (CAG); Art 149 (duties); Art 151 (audit reports); Art 266 (Consolidated Fund accounts).
**Trap:** Art 150 is the form of accounts (on the CAG's advice); Art 151 is the laying of audit reports. Do not swap them.
**Back:** *"The CAG advises on the form; the President prescribes it."*

### Flashcard Art-151 — Article 151
**Front:** Article 151 — Audit reports
**Provision:** (1) The reports of the CAG relating to the accounts of the Union shall be submitted to the President, who shall cause them to be laid before each House of Parliament. (2) The reports of the CAG relating to the accounts of a State shall be submitted to the Governor, who shall cause them to be laid before the Legislature of the State.
**Learn:**
- Union reports: CAG to President, President lays before Parliament.
- State reports: CAG to Governor, Governor lays before the State legislature.
- Reports are then examined by the PAC (Union) and COPU (public undertakings) (owner file).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Arts 148–150; PAC (22 members, Opposition chair); COPU (22 members: 15 LS + 7 RS); Art 279 (net proceeds).
**Trap:** Union reports go to Parliament through the President; State reports go through the Governor to the State legislature. Do not send State reports to the President.
**Back:** *"Union reports travel via the President to Parliament; State reports via the Governor to the Assembly."*

### PART VI — The States (Articles 152–237)

### Flashcard Art-152 — Article 152
**Front:** Article 152 — Definition of "State" in Part VI
**Provision:** Original text: "In this Part, unless the context otherwise requires, the expression 'State' does not include the State of Jammu and Kashmir." ⚠️ Its present operation after Article 370 was abrogated (5 Aug 2019) and J&K was reorganised into Union territories must be checked against the current official text before quoting.
**Learn:**
- Narrow definition: "State" means a State only for Part VI (Arts 152–237), not for the whole Constitution.
- Part VI covers the State executive (Governor, CM, Advocate-General), State legislature, High Courts and subordinate judiciary.
- "Unless the context otherwise requires" — the definition yields to context.
- Mains hook: the definition is a reminder that Part VI is State-architecture; J&K's post-2019 status is a live question for any "federal" answer.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 370 (abrogated 2019) ⚠️; Art 12 defines "State" for Part III (wider: includes local authorities); UT legislatures: Art 239A (Puducherry), Art 239AA (Delhi).
**Trap:** Do not use Art 12's wide "State" (includes local authorities) for Part VI; and do not assume the old J&K exclusion operates unchanged post-2019 — ⚠️ verify.
**Back:** *"152: 'State' in Part VI is the narrow term — Art 12's 'State' is wide."*

### Flashcard Art-153 — Article 153
**Front:** Article 153 — Governors of States
**Provision:** "There shall be a Governor for each State." Proviso (inserted by the Seventh Amendment, 1956): "nothing in this article shall prevent the appointment of the same person as Governor for two or more States."
**Learn:**
- One Governor per State (28 States today); one person may hold Governorship of two or more States — a structural fact, not a discretionary power.
- The Governor is the constitutional head of the State and the nominal executive (Art 154).
- Union Territories have Lieutenant Governors or administrators under Art 239/239AA, not Governors — do not conflate.
- Mains hook: the dual-role design (State head and Union appointee) starts here.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Seventh Amendment (1956) added the proviso; Art 155 (appointment), 156 (tenure), 157–158 (qualifications, conditions).
**Trap:** "A Governor for each State" does not mean each Governor is a State's own nominee; and a UT's head is not a "Governor" under Art 153.
**Back:** *"153: One Governor per State, but one person may serve several (1956 proviso)."*

### Flashcard Art-154 — Article 154
**Front:** Article 154 — Executive power of the State
**Provision:** "The executive power of the State shall be vested in the Governor and shall be exercised by him either directly or through officers subordinate to him in accordance with this Constitution."
**Learn:**
- Formal vesting in the Governor; real direction through the Council of Ministers headed by the CM (Art 163).
- All executive action is expressed in the Governor's name (Art 166(1)).
- Mains hook: formal vesting vs real executive is the core of the "nominal head, real executive" design (parallel to Art 53/74 for the Union).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 162 (extent), Art 163 (aid and advice), Art 166 (conduct of business), Art 53 (Union parallel).
**Trap:** "Executive power vested in the Governor" does not make the Governor the decision-maker; the CM and CoM exercise it on advice.
**Back:** *"154: The Governor holds the pen; the Council of Ministers holds the power."*

### Flashcard Art-155 — Article 155
**Front:** Article 155 — Appointment of Governor
**Provision:** "The Governor of a State shall be appointed by the President by warrant under his hand and seal."
**Learn:**
- The Governor is a Union appointee, not elected, and not appointed by the State legislature or the CM.
- Appointment by the President (in practice, the Union government) is the root of the "agent of the Centre" critique.
- The Sarkaria (1988), Punchhi (2010) and NCRWC (2002) reports proposed consultation with the CM and an eminent-outsider criterion — these are proposals, not law.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 156 (tenure at pleasure), Art 157 (qualification), Art 72 (President's warrant parallel); B.P. Singhal v. Union of India (2010) on the limits of removal.
**Trap:** Art 155 (Governor appointed by the President) vs Art 164 (CM appointed by the Governor) — the chain of appointment runs Union → Governor → CM.
**Back:** *"155: President's warrant, hand and seal — the Union's hand on the State's head."*

### Flashcard Art-156 — Article 156
**Front:** Article 156 — Term of office of Governor
**Provision:** (1) The Governor holds office during the pleasure of the President. (2) The Governor may resign by writing under his hand addressed to the President. (3) Subject to (1), he holds office for five years from the date he enters upon office, but continues until a successor enters office.
**Learn:**
- The five-year term is nominal; removal can come at any time without stated grounds (the pleasure doctrine).
- B.P. Singhal v. Union of India (2010): pleasure is not unfettered; removal on capricious, unreasonable or mala fide grounds is open to review, though reasons need not be disclosed as routine.
- Reform recommendations (Sarkaria, Punchhi, NCRWC): security of tenure and a removal process — proposals only.
- Mains hook: "pleasure doctrine" = dependence on the Centre, the root of Governor-politics friction.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 155, Art 157, Art 361 (personal immunity while in office).
**Trap:** "Five-year term" is not security of tenure; the Governor also continues until a successor takes office (Art 156(3)).
**Back:** *"156: Five years on paper, the President's pleasure in practice."*

### Flashcard Art-157 — Article 157
**Front:** Article 157 — Qualifications for appointment as Governor
**Provision:** "No person shall be eligible for appointment as Governor unless he is a citizen of India and has completed the age of thirty-five years."
**Learn:**
- Two qualifications only: Indian citizenship and age 35.
- No constitutional bar on appointing a person from outside the State (no domicile rule).
- Convention, not text, favours an outsider and prior consultation with the CM — conventions are often breached.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 158 (conditions of office), Art 58 (President also 35); Art 173 (MLA age 25, MLC 30).
**Trap:** 35 years (Governor and President) — not 30 (Rajya Sabha) or 25 (Lok Sabha/Assembly); "outsider" is a convention, not a qualification.
**Back:** *"157: Citizen and 35 — no domicile rule, only convention favours outsiders."*

### Flashcard Art-158 — Article 158
**Front:** Article 158 — Conditions of Governor's office
**Provision:** (1) The Governor shall not be a member of either House of Parliament or of a House of a State legislature; if he is, he is deemed to vacate that seat on entering office. (2) He shall not hold any other office of profit. (3) His emoluments, allowances and privileges are as Parliament determines, and they shall not be diminished during his term. (4) He is entitled to free official residence and the privileges and allowances Parliament fixes.
**Learn:**
- Governor is outside the legislature; he is not a member of any House.
- Pay cannot be reduced during the term — a protected feature of the office.
- Mains hook: the Governor is a constitutional head whose independence is protected by pay and office rules, not by party tenure.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 361 (personal immunity: no criminal proceedings during term); Art 194 (members' privilege, not the Governor's — owner-flagged distinction).
**Trap:** Art 158 emoluments protection vs Art 361 immunity — PYQ 2018 Q41 routed (key not held locally; treat as routed, do not state an answer).
**Back:** *"158: Not in a House, no office of profit, pay cannot be cut."*

### Flashcard Art-159 — Article 159
**Front:** Article 159 — Oath or affirmation by the Governor
**Provision:** Every Governor, and every person discharging the Governor's functions, makes and subscribes an oath or affirmation before the Chief Justice of the State's High Court or, in his absence, the senior-most available judge of that High Court (form in the Third Schedule).
**Learn:**
- Oath is before the High Court, not the Supreme Court or the President.
- Governor's oath form is in the Third Schedule.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 60 (President's oath before the CJI or senior-most SC judge); Art 219 (HC judge's oath before the Governor).
**Trap:** Governor swears before the HC Chief Justice; the HC judge swears before the Governor; the President swears before the CJI — three different oath-takers.
**Back:** *"159: Governor swears before the High Court; President before the Supreme Court."*

### Flashcard Art-160 — Article 160
**Front:** Article 160 — Discharge of the Governor's functions in contingencies
**Provision:** "The President may make such provision as he thinks fit for the discharge of the functions of the Governor of a State in any contingency not provided for in this Chapter."
**Learn:**
- Residuary power: the President fills a gap in the Governor's functions in an unforeseen situation.
- Used for temporary arrangements when a Governor is absent or the office is vacant (the usual practice is an acting Governor — ⚠️ verify wording on specific appointments).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 161 (pardon), Art 167/166 (business); Art 214 (HC) analogy not needed.
**Trap:** Art 160 is the President's power, not the Governor's; it covers contingencies not provided for, not ordinary acting arrangements already covered by the Constitution.
**Back:** *"160: Unplanned gap → the President writes the rule."*

### Flashcard Art-161 — Article 161
**Front:** Article 161 — Power of Governor to grant pardons, etc.
**Provision:** The Governor may grant pardons, reprieves, respites or remissions of punishment, or suspend, remit or commute the sentence of any person convicted of any offence against a law relating to a matter to which the executive power of the State extends.
**Learn:**
- Scope is tied to State executive power (State List and concurrent matters within State power), not to all offences.
- Court-martial sentences lie outside Art 161 — only the President (Art 72) covers court-martial.
- Death sentence: Art 161's text is silent on death sentences — it speaks only of "the sentence of any person convicted" (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part VI). The textbook position that the Governor cannot pardon a death sentence but may suspend, remit or commute it is not checked against the judgments in this build; the owner files state it differently in places (one says "may pardon … including a death sentence"). ⚠️ Do not state either answer as settled.
- Clemency is exercised on the Council of Ministers' advice; Maru Ram v. Union of India (1980) (advice on Arts 72/161) and A.G. Perarivalan (2022) (delay reviewable) — ⚠️ verify holdings (Wikipedia case pages returned 404 in this build; holdings unverified).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 72 (President's wider pardon power); Art 163 (advice); Maru Ram (1980); A.G. Perarivalan (2022).
**Trap:** Art 161 (Governor, State-law offences) vs Art 72 (President, incl. court-martial and every death sentence). Do not merge the two.
**Back:** *"161: Governor's mercy covers State-law offences — not court-martial, the President's domain."*

### Flashcard Art-162 — Article 162
**Front:** Article 162 — Extent of executive power of the State
**Provision:** "Subject to the provisions of this Constitution, the executive power of a State shall extend to the matters with respect to which the Legislature of the State has power to make laws." Proviso: in a matter on which Parliament and the State Legislature both have power (Concurrent List), the State's executive power is subject to and limited by the executive power of the Union under the Constitution or a Parliamentary law.
**Learn:**
- Executive power mirrors legislative power: State List matters (full), Concurrent List matters (subject to Union law).
- Parallel to Art 73 for the Union.
- Mains hook: Concurrent List federalism — Union law prevails in the executive field.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 73 (Union), Art 246 (legislative lists), Art 254 (repugnancy in the concurrent list).
**Trap:** Executive extent (Art 162) is co-extensive with legislative competence, not wider; the Union limit applies on the Concurrent List.
**Back:** *"162: State executive = State legislative field, with the Union limit on the Concurrent List."*

### Flashcard Art-163 — Article 163
**Front:** Article 163 — Council of Ministers to aid and advise the Governor
**Provision:** (1) There shall be a Council of Ministers, headed by the CM, to aid and advise the Governor in exercising his functions, except where he is required by or under the Constitution to act in his discretion. (2) If a question arises whether a matter is required to be done in discretion, the Governor's decision is final, and the validity of anything he does cannot be questioned on the ground that he ought not to have acted in his discretion. (3) Courts cannot inquire into the advice tendered by ministers. (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part VI, Art 163(1)–(3))
**Learn:**
- Advice is the rule (Shamsher Singh v. State of Punjab, 1974, seven-judge Bench).
- Express discretion: reserving a Bill for the President (Art 200), recommending President's Rule (Art 356), certain tribal-area and special-responsibility functions (Art 371A(1)(b) Nagaland; Art 371H(a) Arunachal, owner-flagged; Sixth Schedule).
- Situational discretion: appointing a CM in a hung House, dismissing a ministry that has lost majority, dissolving the Assembly.
- Mains hook: the text uses "aid and advise" without Art 74's express "shall act in accordance with advice" — an argument for discretion, but Shamsher Singh keeps advice the default.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Shamsher Singh (1974); S.R. Bommai v. Union of India (1994) (Art 356 reviewable; floor test); Nabam Rebia v. Deputy Speaker (2016) (discretion narrow; ⚠️ verify holding wording); Art 74 (President's parallel).
**Trap:** Discretion is narrow; the finality clause in 163(2) does not create unlimited discretion. The Governor is not "bound" by text in the way the President is under Art 74 — but advice remains the default.
**Back:** *"163: Advice is the rule; discretion is the narrow exception — Shamsher Singh."*

### Flashcard Art-164 — Article 164
**Front:** Article 164 — Other provisions as to Ministers
**Provision:** (1) The CM is appointed by the Governor; other Ministers are appointed on the CM's advice; Ministers hold office during the Governor's pleasure. Proviso: Ministers in charge of tribal welfare in Bihar, Chhattisgarh, Jharkhand, Madhya Pradesh and Odisha (94th Amendment, 2006 — ⚠️ the fetched summary listed only Chhattisgarh, Jharkhand, Madhya Pradesh and Odisha; Bihar and the 94th attribution are unconfirmed). (1A) Total Ministers, including CM, cannot exceed 15% of the Assembly's strength, and not less than 12 (91st Amendment, 2003 — ✅ 15%/12 per Wikisource Const. 2020, Part VI; amendment year from memory). (1B) A member disqualified under paragraph 2 of the Tenth Schedule on defection is barred from ministerial office until the end of his term or until he is elected in a contest before then, whichever is earlier (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part VI). (2) Council of Ministers is collectively responsible to the Legislative Assembly. (3) Oath as per Third Schedule. (4) A Minister who is not a member of the State legislature for six consecutive months ceases to be a Minister. (5) Salaries as the legislature fixes.
**Learn:**
- The Governor picks the leader of the majority; in a hung House the Governor uses situational discretion.
- A non-MLA may be CM for six months (Art 164(4)); a CM may come from either House.
- Responsibility runs only to the Legislative Assembly — the Council cannot remove the ministry.
- Mains hook: 91st Amendment cap is the anti-defection "reward" check.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** S.R. Bommai (1994); Art 75 (Union parallel: 15% cap); Art 167 (CM's duties to Governor); Tenth Schedule (91st Amendment).
**Trap:** 15% of the Assembly (not of both Houses; not 10%); minimum 12; the Governor appoints the CM, the CM appoints other ministers.
**Back:** *"164: Governor names, Assembly judges — 15% cap (minimum 12), defectors barred, six-month outsider rule."*

### Flashcard Art-165 — Article 165
**Front:** Article 165 — Advocate-General for the State
**Provision:** The Governor appoints a person qualified to be a High Court judge as Advocate-General. The AG gives legal advice to the State government on matters referred and performs other legal duties the Governor assigns; holds office during the Governor's pleasure; receives remuneration as the Governor fixes.
**Learn:**
- The AG is the State's chief law officer, not a member of the CoM (no vote, no executive office by this article alone).
- Rights in the House under Art 177 (speak, take part, no vote).
- Parallel to Art 76 (Union Attorney-General).
- Mains hook: the AG is a Governor-appointed office, so Centre-State politics can reach legal advice.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 76 (Union AG); Art 177 (House rights); Art 165's "during pleasure" is a parallel to Art 156.
**Trap:** Advocate-General (Art 165, State) vs Attorney-General (Art 76, Union). Do not describe the AG as a Minister or as a voting member.
**Back:** *"165: The State's lawyer, HC-judge qualified, serves at the Governor's pleasure — the Union's twin is Art 76."*

### Flashcard Art-166 — Article 166
**Front:** Article 166 — Conduct of business of the Government of a State
**Provision:** (1) All executive action of the State government is expressed to be taken in the Governor's name. (2) Orders and instruments made in the Governor's name are authenticated as specified in rules made by the Governor, and their validity cannot be challenged merely because they were not made by the Governor. (3) The Governor makes rules for the more convenient transaction of business and the allocation of business among Ministers.
**Learn:**
- Governor's name on every order; authentication protects validity.
- Governor makes the business rules (not the CM alone).
- Parallel to Art 77 (Union).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 77 (Union), Art 154 (executive), Art 163.
**Trap:** Art 166(2) protects validity against a challenge that the order was not personally made by the Governor — not a power to make orders without advice.
**Back:** *"166: Every order in the Governor's name; the Governor makes the business rules."*

### Flashcard Art-167 — Article 167
**Front:** Article 167 — Duties of the CM as respects furnishing information to Governor, etc.
**Provision:** It is the CM's duty to (a) communicate to the Governor all decisions of the CoM relating to administration and proposals for legislation; (b) furnish information on administration and legislative proposals as the Governor calls for; (c) if the Governor requires, submit to the CoM any matter decided by a single Minister but not considered by the CoM.
**Learn:**
- The CM is the Governor's information channel and the CoM's central link.
- Clause (c) allows the Governor to push a minister's solo decision to the full CoM.
- Parallel to Art 78 (PM and President).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 78 (Union parallel); Art 163 (advice); Art 166 (business).
**Trap:** The duty is to inform and to refer on request — it is not an executive veto for the Governor, and the Governor cannot compel a particular decision under Art 167.
**Back:** *"167: CM briefs the Governor — decisions, information, and any minister's solo decision on request."*

### Flashcard Art-168 — Article 168
**Front:** Article 168 — Constitution of Legislatures in States
**Provision:** For every State there shall be a Legislature consisting of the Governor and (a) where there is one House, the Legislative Assembly; or (b) where there are two Houses, the Legislative Council and the Legislative Assembly.
**Learn:**
- The Governor is part of the legislature but is not a member of either House.
- Only six States have a Legislative Council: Andhra Pradesh, Telangana, Uttar Pradesh, Bihar, Maharashtra and Karnataka (mnemonic: KATBUM).
- J&K's Legislative Council was abolished in 2019 (owner-flagged); do not list it.
- Unicameral is the default; bicameralism is optional.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 169 (creation/abolition); Art 171 (composition); Art 79 (Union: Parliament is two Houses plus President).
**Trap:** The Governor is part of the State legislature but not a House; do not make him a member of either House.
**Back:** *"168: Governor plus one or two Houses — six States keep the upper House (KATBUM)."*

### Flashcard Art-169 — Article 169
**Front:** Article 169 — Abolition or creation of Legislative Councils in States
**Provision:** Notwithstanding Art 168, Parliament may by law provide for the abolition of a State's Legislative Council, or the creation of one, if the Legislative Assembly passes a resolution to that effect by a majority of its total membership and by a majority of not less than two-thirds of the members present and voting. Such a law is not an amendment of the Constitution for Art 368 purposes.
**Learn:**
- Two-step: Assembly special-majority resolution, then Parliament's ordinary Act (simple majority).
- The Council's existence depends on the Assembly's will, not on a constitutional amendment.
- Live debates in several States about creating or abolishing Councils (📰 — verify any specific proposal before dating).
- Mains hook: a Council can be removed by a hostile majority, so it is fragile by design.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 368 (amendment not needed); Art 171 (composition); Art 197 (Council's delaying power).
**Trap:** Art 169 law is not a constitutional amendment (no Art 368 two-thirds of Parliament, no State ratification); the resolution threshold is two-thirds of those present and voting, plus a majority of total membership.
**Back:** *"169: Assembly two-thirds resolves, Parliament legislates simply — no amendment needed."*

### Flashcard Art-170 — Article 170
**Front:** Article 170 — Composition of the Legislative Assemblies
**Provision:** The Legislative Assembly consists of not more than 500 and not less than 60 members chosen by direct election from territorial constituencies, subject to Art 333 (✅ corrected: the card said 'Art 332'; Wikisource Const. 2020, Part VI, Art 170(1) cites Art 333 — the Anglo-Indian seat provision; SC/ST reserved seats are under Art 332) and special provisions for smaller States; each State is divided into territorial constituencies with seat allocation as the Constitution provides.
**Learn:**
- Normal range 60–500 directly elected members.
- Art 170 itself sets 60–500 only; the owner's special minima for smaller States (Sikkim 32, Goa 40, Mizoram 40) sit in other special provisions, not in Art 170 — ⚠️ unverified here.
- Seat-allocation freeze (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part VI, Art 170(2)–(3) and third proviso): until figures of the first census after 2026 are published, total seats stay on the 1971 census and constituency divisions on the 2001 census. The 84th (2001) and 87th (2003) attribution is from memory, not checked against the amendment texts.
- 106th Amendment (women's reservation in State Assemblies via Art 332A) (✅ Wikipedia, Nari Shakti Vandan Adhiniyam page): a gazette notification of 16 April 2026 brought the Act into force; the reservation stays inoperative until a new census and delimitation (Art 334A). The page does not say the Act changes Art 170.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 332 (SC/ST seats; Art 334 reservation duration); Art 332A (106th, women's seats); 84th, 87th, 106th Amendments; Art 81/82 (Union parallels).
**Trap:** Assembly 60–500 (not 40); Council maximum is one-third of the Assembly (Art 171), minimum 40.
**Back:** *"170: Assembly 60 to 500, directly elected, territorial — the Council starts at 40."*

### Flashcard Art-171 — Article 171
**Front:** Article 171 — Composition of the Legislative Councils
**Provision:** (1) Max one-third of the Assembly's total strength; in no case less than forty. (2) Until Parliament provides otherwise, composition follows clause (3) (✅ CORRECTED: the "permanent body" point belongs to Art 172(2), not 171(2)). (3) Composition: (a) one-third elected by members of municipalities, district boards and other local authorities; (b) one-twelfth elected by graduates of at least three years' standing; (c) one-twelfth elected by teachers of at least three years' standing in institutions not lower than secondary school; (d) one-third elected by MLAs from among non-members; (e) the remainder (one-sixth) nominated by the Governor from persons with special knowledge or practical experience in literature, science, art, co-operative movement and social service. (4) Elections under (a)–(d) use single transferable vote (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part VI, Art 171(1)–(5)).
**Learn:**
- 5/6 indirectly elected + 1/6 nominated; elections under (a)–(d) by single transferable vote (✅ Art 171(4)).
- Mnemonic: 3-12-12-3-6 = local bodies, graduates, teachers, MLAs, Governor (✅ corrected: the earlier order put MLAs first; the numbers are the fractions in clause order (a)–(e)).
- Mains hook: the Council's composition is heterogeneous and non-federal — hence the "ornamental chamber" critique.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 169, 172, 197–198; Art 80 (Rajya Sabha parallel, not in range).
**Trap:** Council is not directly elected; the Governor nominates only to the Council (never to the Assembly); the 1/12 seats are for graduates and teachers, not for MLAs.
**Back:** *"171: 3-12-12-3-6 — local bodies, graduates, teachers, MLAs, Governor; cap one-third, floor forty."*

### Flashcard Art-172 — Article 172
**Front:** Article 172 — Duration of State Legislatures
**Provision:** (1) Every Legislative Assembly, unless sooner dissolved, continues for five years from the date appointed for its first meeting; its expiry operates as dissolution (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part VI, Art 172(1)–(2)). Proviso: while a Proclamation of Emergency is in force, Parliament may by law extend the period by one year at a time, but not beyond six months after the Emergency ceases. (2) The Legislative Council is a permanent body not subject to dissolution; one-third of its members retire every second year.
**Learn:**
- Assembly: five years, dissolvable; Council: permanent (like the Rajya Sabha).
- Emergency extension is by Parliament's law, in one-year steps, capped at six months after Emergency ends.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 352 (Emergency); Art 83 (Union duration parallel).
**Trap:** "Permanent" Council does not mean no member retires — one-third retire every two years; the Assembly is not permanent.
**Back:** *"172: Assembly five years and dissolvable; Council forever, one-third renewed every two years."*

### Flashcard Art-173 — Article 173
**Front:** Article 173 — Qualification for membership of the State Legislature
**Provision:** A person is qualified to be chosen to fill a seat in a State legislature only if he (a) is a citizen of India and makes and subscribes the oath or affirmation before the person authorised by the Election Commission (Third Schedule form); (b) is at least 25 years old for the Legislative Assembly and at least 30 for the Legislative Council; and (c) has other qualifications Parliament prescribes by law (Representation of the People Act, 1951).
**Learn:**
- Age: 25 for Assembly, 30 for Council.
- Oath before the ECI-authorised person.
- Mains hook: qualifications are set by Parliament's electoral law, not by the State.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** RP Act 1951; Art 84 (LS/RS qualifications); Art 102 (disqualifications for Parliament).
**Trap:** Age figures: Assembly 25 and Council 30; Governor and President 35; Rajya Sabha 30; Lok Sabha 25.
**Back:** *"173: 25 for the Assembly, 30 for the Council — citizen, oath, and RP Act rules."*

### Flashcard Art-174 — Article 174
**Front:** Article 174 — Sessions of the State Legislature, prorogation and dissolution
**Provision:** (1) The Governor summons the House or each House at such times and places as he thinks fit, but six months shall not intervene between its last sitting in one session and the first sitting in the next session. (2) The Governor may prorogue the House or Houses and may dissolve the Legislative Assembly.
**Learn:**
- Summons, prorogation and dissolution are normally on the CM's advice; the Governor's discretion is situational (see Nabam Rebia, 2016 — ⚠️ verify wording).
- The Council cannot be dissolved (Art 172); only the Assembly can.
- Mains hook: the dissolution power is a key site of Governor-CM friction.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Rameshwar Prasad v. Union of India (2006) (Bihar Assembly dissolution struck as mala fide); S.R. Bommai (1994) (floor test); Art 85 (Union parallel).
**Trap:** Six-month maximum gap between sessions; the Governor may dissolve the Assembly, never the Council.
**Back:** *"174: Summon, but not six months silent; prorogue; dissolve the Assembly, never the Council."*

### Flashcard Art-175 — Article 175
**Front:** Article 175 — Right of the Governor to address and send messages to the House or Houses
**Provision:** (1) The Governor may address the Legislative Assembly or either House, and may send messages to them, in respect of a Bill pending or otherwise. (2) Each House must consider any message the Governor sends as soon as possible.
**Learn:**
- Address and messages are the Governor's outward link to the legislature.
- Nabam Rebia (2016) treats these powers as normally bound by aid and advice (⚠️ verify wording).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 176 (special address), Art 163 (advice), Art 86 (Union parallel).
**Trap:** The Governor's messages are not a veto; they trigger consideration, not automatic action.
**Back:** *"175: The Governor speaks to the House and sends messages on pending Bills."*

### Flashcard Art-176 — Article 176
**Front:** Article 176 — Special address by the Governor
**Provision:** (1) The Governor addresses the Legislative Assembly at the first session after each general election and at the first session of each year, explaining the causes of its summons. (2) Each House makes rules for regulating procedure for this address; the Governor may address on other occasions and send messages.
**Learn:**
- Two mandatory addresses: after each general election and at the first session of each year.
- The address is followed by a Motion of Thanks (owner-flagged practice).
- Mains hook: the address is the government's policy statement delivered by the Governor's voice.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 87 (President's address, parallel); Art 175.
**Trap:** The address is mandatory at two points only; the "special" address is not a general discretionary speech.
**Back:** *"176: Election session and yearly session — the Governor's address, then the Motion of Thanks."*

### Flashcard Art-177 — Article 177
**Front:** Article 177 — Rights of Ministers and Advocate-General as respects the Houses
**Provision:** Every Minister and the Advocate-General have the right to speak in and take part in the proceedings of the Legislative Assembly (and of the Council, where one exists), and of any committee of the legislature of which they are named members, but are not entitled to vote unless they are members of that House.
**Learn:**
- Non-member ministers and the AG may speak and participate but cannot vote.
- Parallel to Art 88 for the Union.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 88 (Union); Art 165 (AG); Art 164(4) (six-month rule for non-members).
**Trap:** Speaking right is not a voting right; a non-member minister still needs membership within six months (Art 164(4)).
**Back:** *"177: Minister and AG may speak, not vote — even when they are not members."*

### Flashcard Art-178 — Article 178
**Front:** Article 178 — Speaker and Deputy Speaker of the Legislative Assembly
**Provision:** Every Legislative Assembly shall, as soon as may be, choose two members to be respectively Speaker and Deputy Speaker; when either office falls vacant, another member is chosen.
**Learn:**
- Elected by the Assembly from among its members; not appointed by the Governor or the CM.
- The Speaker presides over the Assembly; the Deputy stands in when needed (Art 180).
- Mains hook: impartiality of the presiding officer is a recurring concern (see Art 179–181 and the Tenth Schedule).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 93 (Union: Speaker of Lok Sabha); Art 182 (Council's Chairman); Tenth Schedule para 6 (Speaker decides defection).
**Trap:** Speaker of the Assembly (Art 178) vs Chairman of the Council (Art 182) — both are presiding officers but of different Houses.
**Back:** *"178: The Assembly picks its own Speaker and Deputy from within."*

### Flashcard Art-179 — Article 179
**Front:** Article 179 — Vacation and resignation of, and removal from, the offices of Speaker and Deputy Speaker
**Provision:** A Speaker or Deputy Speaker vacates office (a) on ceasing to be a member of the Assembly; (b) by resignation in writing to the Deputy Speaker (or to the Speaker, if he is the Deputy); (c) by removal by a resolution passed by a majority of all then members of the Assembly, after at least 14 days' notice. Dissolution does not end office: the Speaker continues until immediately before the first meeting of the new Assembly.
**Learn:**
- Removal needs a majority of all members (not just those present) and 14 days' notice.
- The Speaker does not vacate on dissolution.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 181 (presides no more during own removal); Art 183 (Council parallel); PYQ 2018 Q39 routed (key not held locally — do not state answer).
**Trap:** Removal needs an all-members majority and 14 days' notice; dissolution does not end the Speaker's office.
**Back:** *"179: Membership, resignation, or 14-day notice removal by all members — dissolution does not end the Speaker."*

### Flashcard Art-180 — Article 180
**Front:** Article 180 — Power of Deputy Speaker or other person to perform duties of Speaker
**Provision:** While the office of Speaker is vacant, the duties are performed by the Deputy Speaker, or, if that office is also vacant, by such member of the Assembly as the Governor appoints for the purpose.
**Learn:**
- Order of succession: Deputy Speaker first; then a member the Governor appoints.
- The Governor does not appoint the Speaker himself — the House elects the Speaker (Art 178).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 184 (Council equivalent); Art 178.
**Trap:** Governor appoints a stand-in member only when both offices are vacant; the Governor does not choose the Speaker.
**Back:** *"180: Deputy first; if both are vacant, the Governor names a member — never the CM."*

### Flashcard Art-181 — Article 181
**Front:** Article 181 — Speaker or Deputy Speaker not to preside while a resolution for his removal is under consideration
**Provision:** The Speaker does not preside at any sitting while a resolution for his removal is under consideration (he may speak and take part in the proceedings, and may vote on that resolution only in the first instance, "but not in the case of an equality of votes" — so he has no casting vote; ✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part VI, Art 181(1)–(2)).
**Learn:**
- Self-protection rule: the presiding officer steps aside during his own removal motion.
- Nabam Rebia (2016) raised whether a Speaker facing removal notice can adjudicate disqualification; the Court referred the question to a larger Bench, and it remains undecided (owner-flagged).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 179; Art 185 (Council equivalent); Nabam Rebia (2016).
**Trap:** He does not preside, but he is not silenced; do not state the outcome of the referred Nabam Rebia question.
**Back:** *"181: The Speaker steps aside while his own removal is debated — he speaks, but does not preside."*

### Flashcard Art-182 — Article 182
**Front:** Article 182 — Chairman and Deputy Chairman of the Legislative Council
**Provision:** The Legislative Council shall, as soon as may be, choose two members to be respectively Chairman and Deputy Chairman; when either office falls vacant, another member is chosen.
**Learn:**
- The Council's presiding officer is the Chairman, elected by the Council's members.
- The Chairman has the casting vote in a tie (Art 189(2)), like the Speaker.
- The Council cannot pass a no-confidence motion against the ministry (Art 164(2)).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 178; Art 183; Art 189.
**Trap:** Chairman (Council) vs Speaker (Assembly); the Council's Chairman does not decide Money Bill certification — that is the Speaker (Art 199(3)).
**Back:** *"182: The Council picks its own Chairman and Deputy, like the Assembly does."*

### Flashcard Art-183 — Article 183
**Front:** Article 183 — Vacation and resignation of, and removal from, the offices of Chairman and Deputy Chairman
**Provision:** The Chairman or Deputy Chairman of the Council vacates office (a) on ceasing to be a member of the Council; (b) by resignation in writing to the Deputy Chairman (or Chairman, if he is the Deputy); (c) by removal by a resolution passed by a majority of all then members of the Council, after at least 14 days' notice.
**Learn:**
- Parallel to Art 179 for the Council.
- No dissolution clause, because the Council is permanent.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 179; Art 172 (permanent Council); Art 185.
**Trap:** Do not import Art 179's dissolution rule into the Council — it has no dissolution.
**Back:** *"183: Council's presiding officers: same fourteen days and all-members majority as the Speaker."*

### Flashcard Art-184 — Article 184
**Front:** Article 184 — Power of Deputy Chairman or other person to perform duties of Chairman
**Provision:** While the office of Chairman is vacant, the Deputy Chairman performs the duties; if that office is also vacant, the duties are performed by such member of the Council as the Governor appoints for the purpose.
**Learn:**
- Same structure as Art 180 for the Council.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 180; Art 182.
**Trap:** Council's stand-in is also a Governor's appointee, not a Council election — the Chairman's vacancy is filled by the Governor's nominee only as a fallback.
**Back:** *"184: Council's Deputy first; if both are vacant, the Governor names a member."*

### Flashcard Art-185 — Article 185
**Front:** Article 185 — Chairman or Deputy Chairman not to preside while a resolution for his removal is under consideration
**Provision:** The Chairman does not preside at any sitting while a resolution for his removal is under consideration, though he may speak and take part, and may vote on that resolution only in the first instance, "but not in the case of an equality of votes" (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part VI, Art 185(1)–(2)).
**Learn:**
- Parallel to Art 181 for the Council.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 181; Art 183.
**Trap:** Stepping aside is not removal; the Chairman keeps the right to speak.
**Back:** *"185: The Council's Chairman also steps aside during his own removal motion."*

### Flashcard Art-186 — Article 186
**Front:** Article 186 — Salaries and allowances of the Speaker, Deputy Speaker, Chairman and Deputy Chairman
**Provision:** The Speaker and Deputy Speaker (and the Chairman and Deputy Chairman) receive such salaries and allowances as the legislature fixes by law; until fixed, as the Second Schedule specifies. ✅ CORRECTED: Art 186 has no charge on the Consolidated Fund and no Governor rule-making power (was: "Their pay is charged on the State's Consolidated Fund"; Wikisource Const. 2020, Part VI).
**Learn:**
- Legislature sets the presiding officers' pay by its own law; the Second Schedule is the default until then.
- ✅ Corrected: the "charge on the Consolidated Fund" point is removed; Art 186 is silent on it.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 97 (Union parallel); Art 195 (members' pay).
**Trap:** Presiding officers' pay is set by the legislature, not the Governor or CM.
**Back:** *"186: Presiding officers' pay — legislature sets it; the Second Schedule until then."*

### Flashcard Art-187 — Article 187
**Front:** Article 187 — House or Houses of the Legislature to have separate secretarial staff
**Provision:** Each House has a separate secretarial staff (a proviso allows posts common to both Houses); the legislature may regulate recruitment and service conditions by law (cl 2). Until it does, the Governor may make rules after consulting the Speaker or Chairman, and those rules are subject to any later law (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part VI, Art 187(1)–(3)).
**Learn:**
- Staff autonomy of the House; the presiding officer controls secretarial staff.
- The Governor's rules are an interim measure only.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 98 (Union parallel: separate secretariat for each House).
**Trap:** The Governor's rule-making is interim; once the legislature legislates, its law governs.
**Back:** *"187: Each House runs its own staff; the Governor's rules only until the legislature legislates."*

### Flashcard Art-188 — Article 188
**Front:** Article 188 — Oath or affirmation by members
**Provision:** Every member of a State legislature, before taking his seat, makes and subscribes an oath or affirmation before the Governor or a person appointed by him, in the form in the Third Schedule.
**Learn:**
- Oath is before the Governor (or his appointee), not the Speaker or Chairman.
- Parallel to Art 99 for Parliament.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 99 (Union parallel); Art 193 (penalty for sitting without oath).
**Trap:** Member's oath before the Governor; the HC judge's oath is also before the Governor (Art 219), but the Governor's own oath is before the HC Chief Justice (Art 159).
**Back:** *"188: Member swears before the Governor — not the Speaker."*

### Flashcard Art-189 — Article 189
**Front:** Article 189 — Voting in Houses, power of Houses to act notwithstanding vacancies and quorum
**Provision:** (1) Questions are decided by a majority of votes of members present and voting, other than the presiding officer. (2) The Speaker or Chairman does not vote except in a tie, when he has a casting vote. (3) Quorum: until the legislature provides otherwise by law, ten members or one-tenth of the total membership, whichever is greater. (4) A House may act notwithstanding any vacancy.
**Learn:**
- Majority of those present and voting; presiding officer votes only to break a tie.
- State quorum is "10 or one-tenth, whichever is greater" — different from the Union's Art 100 (one-tenth of total).
- Mains hook: quorum and casting-vote rules are the presiding officer's main tools of order.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 100 (Union quorum); Art 182 (Council's presiding officer); Art 189(4) (no invalidity from vacancies).
**Trap:** Speaker's casting vote arises only in a tie — not a second ordinary vote; the State quorum is the greater of 10 or one-tenth.
**Back:** *"189: Majority of those present; the chair votes only to break ties; quorum is ten or one-tenth, whichever is greater."*

### Flashcard Art-190 — Article 190
**Front:** Article 190 — Vacation of seats
**Provision:** (1) No person may be a member of both Houses of a State legislature; the State must provide by law for vacating one seat if he is chosen to both. (2) No person may sit in the legislatures of two or more States in the First Schedule; the seats fall vacant after the period fixed in Presidential rules, unless he has resigned from all but one. (3) A seat becomes vacant if the member (a) incurs a disqualification under Art 191(1) or (2), or (b) resigns in writing to the Speaker or Chairman and the resignation is accepted (the presiding officer must refuse a resignation found not voluntary or genuine). (4) If a member is absent for sixty days without the House's permission, the House may declare the seat vacant; prorogation and adjournments of more than four consecutive days are not counted (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part VI, Art 190(1)–(4)). ✅ CORRECTED: the earlier text's "14-day mechanism", "Parliament–State dual membership" clause and "other vacancies per law" are not in Art 190. The 14-day period appears in the removal provisos of Arts 179/183; state–Parliament dual membership is governed elsewhere (⚠️ not verified in this build).
**Learn:**
- Sixty days' unauthorised absence is the key rule — the House may declare the seat vacant.
- ✅ Corrected: Art 190(3)(a) links to defection disqualification — a seat falls vacant when the member is disqualified under Art 191(2) (Tenth Schedule) — so Art 190 does touch the anti-defection route.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 101 (Union parallel: 60 days); Art 191 (disqualification); Tenth Schedule (defection).
**Trap:** 60 days (not 30 or 90); Art 190 concerns absence and dual seats; defection disqualification is Art 191(2) and the Tenth Schedule.
**Back:** *"190: Two seats cannot be kept; sixty days' silence — the seat may be declared vacant."*

### Flashcard Art-191 — Article 191
**Front:** Article 191 — Disqualifications for membership (with the anti-defection link)
**Provision:** A person is disqualified for being chosen as, or being, a member of the Assembly or Council (a) if he holds an office of profit under the Union or the State government, other than one exempted by State law; (b) if of unsound mind and so declared by a competent court; (c) if an undischarged insolvent; (d) if not a citizen of India, or has voluntarily acquired foreign citizenship or allegiance, or is under acknowledgement of allegiance to a foreign State; (e) if disqualified under any Parliamentary law. (2) A person is disqualified if disqualified under the Tenth Schedule.
**Learn:**
- Five Art 191(1) grounds: profit, unsound mind, insolvency, nationality, law.
- Tenth Schedule (inserted by the 52nd Amendment, 1985): party members are disqualified for (a) voluntarily giving up membership, or (b) voting or abstaining against the whip without permission and not condoned within 15 days (para 2(1)).
- Independents: disqualified on joining any party (para 2(2)); nominated members: disqualified only if they join a party after six months (para 2(3)).
- Only the merger defence survives: at least two-thirds of the legislature party agree and the original party merges (para 4); the one-third split exception was deleted by the 91st Amendment, 2003.
- Speaker/Chairman decides under para 6 (final on its face); Kihoto Hollohan v. Zachillhu (1992): the Speaker acts as a tribunal and his decision is reviewable (mala fides, perversity, natural justice).
- Keisham Meghachandra Singh (2020): ordinarily decide within three months; independent tribunal suggested.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 192 (Governor decides Art 191 questions on ECI's opinion); Art 102 (Union parallel); 52nd Amendment (1985); 91st Amendment (2003); Ravi S. Naik (1994) (conduct can show voluntary giving up membership); Subhash Desai (2023) (whip belongs to the party; ECI symbol track separate).
**Trap:** Art 191 questions go to the Governor (on ECI's opinion); Tenth Schedule questions go to the Speaker/Chairman. The "split" defence no longer exists. PYQ 2025 Q88 and 2022 Q16 are routed (keys — do not state answers here; see owner).
**Back:** *"P-U-I-N-L: Profit, Unsound mind, Insolvent, Non-citizen, Law — then the Tenth Schedule (191(2))."*

### Flashcard Art-192 — Article 192
**Front:** Article 192 — Decision on questions as to disqualifications of members
**Provision:** (1) If a question arises whether a member has become subject to any disqualification under Art 191, it is referred to the Governor, whose decision is final. (2) Before deciding, the Governor obtains the opinion of the Election Commission and acts according to that opinion.
**Learn:**
- Governor, not Speaker, decides Art 191 disqualification questions; ECI's opinion binds in practice (Governor "shall act according to" it).
- Speaker, not Governor, decides Tenth Schedule defection questions (para 6).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 103 (President decides for Parliament, with ECI opinion); Tenth Schedule para 6.
**Trap:** Art 192 (Governor + ECI) vs Tenth Schedule para 6 (Speaker/Chairman) — the two channels must not be merged in an answer.
**Back:** *"192: Art 191 questions → the Governor, after the ECI's opinion; defection → the Speaker."*

### Flashcard Art-193 — Article 193
**Front:** Article 193 — Penalty for sitting and voting before making oath or affirmation or when not qualified or when disqualified
**Provision:** If a person sits or votes as a member of either House when he is not qualified or is disqualified, or before he has complied with Art 188, he is liable to a penalty of five hundred rupees for each day on which he so sits or votes, recoverable as a debt.
**Learn:**
- Penalty is Rs 500 per day of sitting or voting.
- Applies both to unsworn members and to disqualified members.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 104 (Union parallel: Rs 500 per day); Art 188.
**Trap:** The penalty is per day of sitting or voting — not per Bill or per year; the figure is Rs 500.
**Back:** *"193: Sitting before oath or while disqualified costs Rs 500 a day."*

### Flashcard Art-194 — Article 194
**Front:** Article 194 — Powers, privileges and immunities of the Houses and members
**Provision:** (1) Freedom of speech in the legislature, subject to the Constitution and the rules. (2) No member is liable to any court proceeding for anything said or any vote given in the House or its committees; the same applies to any person entitled to speak in the House. (3) Other powers and privileges are as the legislature defines by law; until so defined, as those of the House of Commons (UK) at the Constitution's commencement. (4) The same protections apply to persons entitled to speak (e.g., Ministers and the Advocate-General).
**Learn:**
- Immunity for words spoken and votes given in the House belongs to members and to those entitled to speak.
- Governor is not a member: the Governor's personal immunity is Art 361 (owner-flagged).
- Mains hook: privileges are a legislature's autonomy and sometimes a shield against courts.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 105 (Union parallel); Art 361 (Governor's immunity); Art 212 (procedural irregularity).
**Trap:** Art 194 protects members speaking in the House, not the Governor (who is not a member); the Governor's protection is Art 361.
**Back:** *"194: Speech in the House is free and immune — privileges mirror UK Commons until law says otherwise."*

### Flashcard Art-195 — Article 195
**Front:** Article 195 — Salaries and allowances of members
**Provision:** Members of a State legislature receive such salaries and allowances as the legislature may determine by law; until so determined, as the Second Schedule specifies.
**Learn:**
- Members' pay is set by the legislature by its own law; the Second Schedule is the default.
- Parallel to Art 106 (Union).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 106 (Union parallel); Art 186 (presiding officers' pay).
**Trap:** Members' pay (Art 195) and presiding officers' pay (Art 186) are separate provisions.
**Back:** *"195: Members' pay — the legislature decides; the Second Schedule until then."*

### Flashcard Art-196 — Article 196
**Front:** Article 196 — Provisions as to introduction and passing of Bills
**Provision:** Bills may be introduced in either House (subject to Arts 198 and 207, per 196(1)); the lapse rules for Bills on prorogation and dissolution are in Art 196(3)–(5) (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part VI).
**Learn:**
- Prorogation does not lapse Bills.
- Dissolution lapses Bills pending in the Assembly and Bills passed by the Assembly but pending in the Council.
- A Council-originated Bill pending in the Council does not lapse on Assembly dissolution.
- ✅ Lapse rules verified: 196(3) prorogation does not lapse; 196(4) a Bill pending in the Council and not passed by the Assembly does not lapse on dissolution; 196(5) a Bill pending in the Assembly, or passed by it and pending in the Council, lapses on dissolution.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 107 (Union parallel); Art 197–198 (Council's powers).
**Trap:** Prorogation (no lapse) vs dissolution (lapse) — the pending-in-Council rule is a distinct category.
**Back:** *"196: Prorogation never kills a Bill; dissolution kills Assembly-side Bills, not Council-originated ones."*

### Flashcard Art-197 — Article 197
**Front:** Article 197 — Restriction on powers of Legislative Council as to Bills other than Money Bills
**Provision:** A Bill (other than a Money Bill) passed by the Assembly and sent to the Council: if the Council rejects it, or makes amendments the Assembly does not accept, or does not pass it within three months, the Assembly may pass it again; if the Council does not pass the re-passed Bill within one month, it is deemed passed by both Houses in the form the Assembly passed it.
**Learn:**
- Council's delaying power is at most four months (three + one).
- There is no joint sitting at State level (unlike Art 108 for the Union).
- Council is a dilatory chamber, not a revising body in the Rajya Sabha sense.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 108 (Union joint sitting); Art 169 (Council's existence); Art 171.
**Trap:** The four-month cap is three plus one; a State has no joint sitting to resolve deadlock; the Council cannot veto the Assembly.
**Back:** *"197: The Council may delay a Bill three months, then one more — four in all; the Assembly wins."*

### Flashcard Art-198 — Article 198
**Front:** Article 198 — Special procedure in respect of Money Bills
**Provision:** A Money Bill passed by the Assembly is transmitted to the Council, which must return it within fourteen days with recommendations; the Assembly may accept or reject all or any of the recommendations. If the Council does not return it within fourteen days, it is deemed passed by both Houses at the end of that period.
**Learn:**
- Council's role over Money Bills is recommendatory only.
- Fourteen days is the outer limit.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 109 (Union parallel: Rajya Sabha 14 days); Art 199.
**Trap:** The Council cannot reject a Money Bill — it can only recommend within 14 days.
**Back:** *"198: Money Bill — the Council has fourteen days to advise; it cannot veto."*

### Flashcard Art-199 — Article 199
**Front:** Article 199 — Definition of "Money Bills"
**Provision:** (1) A Bill is a Money Bill if it contains only provisions dealing with all or any of: (a) imposition, abolition, remission, alteration or regulation of any tax; (b) regulation of borrowing by the State or giving of guarantees; (c) custody of the Consolidated Fund or Contingency Fund of the State, payments into or withdrawals from them; (d) appropriation of moneys from the Consolidated Fund; (e) declaring expenditure to be charged on the Consolidated Fund or increasing such charge; (f) receipt of money on account of the Consolidated Fund or audit of accounts. (3) The Speaker's certificate that a Bill is a Money Bill is final. (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part VI, Art 199(1)(a)–(f), (3))
**Learn:**
- Six fund-related heads — mnemonic in the Back.
- Speaker certifies; the certificate is final.
- Mains hook: financial control rests with the Assembly.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 110 (Union parallel); Art 109; Art 207 (financial Bills).
**Trap:** A Money Bill is defined by content, not by label; the Speaker's certificate is final (no court review of the certificate in the owner's account, ⚠️ verify the leading authority).
**Back:** *"199: Six fund heads — tax, borrowing, funds, appropriation, charge, audit — the Speaker certifies."*

### Flashcard Art-200 — Article 200
**Front:** Article 200 — Assent to Bills
**Provision:** When a Bill is passed by the Assembly (or both Houses), it is presented to the Governor, who declares either that he assents, or that he withholds assent, or that he reserves the Bill for the President's consideration. Proviso 1: the Governor may return a Bill other than a Money Bill with a message for reconsideration; if the House passes it again, with or without amendment, the Governor shall not withhold assent. Proviso 2: the Governor shall reserve a Bill that in his opinion would so derogate from the powers of the High Court as to endanger its position under the Constitution.
**Learn:**
- Four options: assent, withhold, return (non-Money Bills only), reserve for the President.
- Reservation is mandatory where a Bill endangers the High Court's position (second proviso).
- Special Reference No. 1 of 2025 — owner file records an opinion of 20 Nov 2025 ("no court-created rigid timelines or deemed assent, but prolonged, unexplained inaction is open to limited mandamus"). ⚠️ NOT VERIFIED in this build: the Wikipedia TN judgment page does not mention the reference, and the TN judgment itself set time limits. Do not quote the reference's holding as fact.
- State of Tamil Nadu v. Governor of Tamil Nadu (8 Apr 2025; 2025 INSC 481; Pardiwala and Mahadevan JJ) — ✅ Wikipedia case page: the three options are mutually exclusive; no absolute or pocket veto; withholding is a temporary deferral; a re-passed Bill must be assented to; the Court set time limits (reserving or withholding on ministerial advice within one month at most; withholding against advice or reserving within three months; assent to re-passed Bills within one month at most); delay is reviewable by mandamus; deemed assent only "exceptionally under Article 142".
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 201; Art 213; Art 163 (advice); Art 111 (Union assent); Special Reference No. 1 of 2025 ⚠️.
**Trap:** A Money Bill cannot be returned for reconsideration under the first proviso; "pocket veto" is not a constitutional power — inaction is not a veto, but the TN judgment (2025) treats withholding and delay as reviewable (✅ Wikipedia summary).
**Back:** *"200: Assent, withhold, return (not Money Bills), or reserve — the Governor's four doors."*

### Flashcard Art-201 — Article 201
**Front:** Article 201 — Bills reserved for consideration
**Provision:** When a Bill is reserved for the President, the President declares either that he assents or that he withholds assent; or directs the Governor to return the Bill to the House(s) for reconsideration. If returned and the House(s) reconsider within six months of receiving the message and pass it again (with or without amendment), it is presented again to the President, who then considers it.
**Learn:**
- President's options mirror Art 200 but he is not bound to assent even after re-passage (owner-flagged).
- ✅ CORRECTED: the owner statement that presidential assent/withholding is not merits-justiciable with no court-created deadline is contradicted in part by the Wikipedia summary of the TN judgment (8 Apr 2025), which reports that a presidential withholding is reviewable on stated grounds and that a delayed decision can be compelled by mandamus. Scope to be confirmed against the judgment (⚠️).
- Mains hook: the reservation route is a channel of Union oversight over State law.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 200; Art 72 (President's pardon, unrelated); Art 111 (Union parallel); Special Reference No. 1 of 2025 ⚠️.
**Trap:** Six months is the House's reconsideration window after the return message; Art 201 itself sets no deadline for the President's own decision (⚠️ see Learn on the 2025 judgment's review point).
**Back:** *"201: President: assent, withhold, or send back — reconsider within six months."*

### Flashcard Art-202 — Article 202
**Front:** Article 202 — Annual financial statement
**Provision:** The Governor shall in respect of every financial year cause to be laid before the House or each House of the legislature a statement of the estimated receipts and expenditure of the State for that year (the Annual Financial Statement, i.e., the budget).
**Learn:**
- Budget is laid by the Governor on the government's advice.
- Parallel to Art 112 for the Union.
- Mains hook: budget is the Assembly's first power of the purse.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 112 (Union parallel); Art 203–206 (estimates, grants, appropriation).
**Trap:** Laying the budget is not voting it; grants are voted by the Assembly alone.
**Back:** *"202: The Governor lays the State budget — the annual financial statement — before the House(s)."*

### Flashcard Art-203 — Article 203
**Front:** Article 203 — Procedure in the Legislature with respect to estimates
**Provision:** Charged expenditure is not submitted to a vote but may be discussed (203(1)); other estimates go to the Legislative Assembly as demands for grants, which it may approve, reject or reduce (203(2)); no demand may be made except on the Governor's recommendation (203(3)) (✅ corrected numbering; Wikisource Const. 2020, Part VI).
**Learn:**
- Governor's recommendation is required for any demand for grants.
- Only the Assembly votes grants (Art 203 refers to the Assembly alone). ✅ Corrected: Art 203 does not say the Council "can only discuss" grants.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 113 (Union parallel); Art 202; Art 204.
**Trap:** Art 203 gives the vote on demands to the Assembly; do not attribute a Council discussion or vote role to this article.
**Back:** *"203: Grants need the Governor's nod; only the Assembly votes them."*

### Flashcard Art-204 — Article 204
**Front:** Article 204 — Appropriation Bills
**Provision:** As soon as a demand for grant is passed, a Bill is introduced to provide for the appropriation out of the Consolidated Fund of the State of the moneys required to meet the expenditure and the grants so voted; no money may be withdrawn from the Consolidated Fund except under an appropriation law.
**Learn:**
- No withdrawal from the State's Consolidated Fund without an appropriation law.
- Appropriation Bills are Money Bills (Art 199(1)(d)).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 114 (Union parallel); Art 199(1)(d); Art 266.
**Trap:** Appropriation Bill is a Money Bill; the Council has only the 14-day recommendation role.
**Back:** *"204: No money leaves the State fund without an appropriation law."*

### Flashcard Art-205 — Article 205
**Front:** Article 205 — Supplementary, additional or excess grants
**Provision:** Where additional or supplementary expenditure is needed, or money has been spent in excess of the grant voted for a year, the Governor causes the excess or additional amount to be presented to the legislature as supplementary, additional or excess grants; each requires its own appropriation law.
**Learn:**
- Supplementary, additional and excess grants each need legislative approval through an appropriation law.
- Parallel to Art 115 (Union).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 115 (Union); Art 204; Art 206.
**Trap:** Excess grant means overspending the authorised amount — not a new scheme; it still needs approval.
**Back:** *"205: Supplementary, additional, excess — each needs its own appropriation law."*

### Flashcard Art-206 — Article 206
**Front:** Article 206 — Votes on account, votes of credit and exceptional grants
**Provision:** The legislature may make provision for: votes on account (to cover expenditure for part of a financial year pending full appropriation); votes of credit (to meet unforeseen expenditure); and exceptional grants (for specific unforeseen purposes). Each is authorised by law as the Constitution provides.
**Learn:**
- Three interim routes to money before full appropriation.
- Parallel to Art 116 (Union).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 116 (Union); Art 204–205.
**Trap:** Vote on account covers part of the year; vote of credit covers unforeseen expenditure; exceptional grant is a specific extraordinary item — not the same thing.
**Back:** *"206: Advance, credit, exceptional — the three shortcuts before the full budget."*

### Flashcard Art-207 — Article 207
**Front:** Article 207 — Special provisions as to financial Bills
**Provision:** (1) A Bill or amendment that makes provision for any matter in Art 199(1)(a)–(f) may not be introduced or moved except on the Governor's recommendation; such a Bill may not be introduced in the Council (✅ Art 207(1), whose proviso exempts amendments that only reduce or abolish a tax). (2) Fines, licence or service fees and local-body taxes do not trigger clause (1). (3) A Bill entailing expenditure from the Consolidated Fund cannot be passed by a House unless the Governor has recommended its consideration; it is not a Money Bill merely because it involves expenditure. ✅ CORRECTED: the earlier sub-clause (2) (a "financial Bill" rule) is not in the text; the fees rule is (2) (Wikisource Const. 2020, Part VI, Art 207(1)–(3)).
**Learn:**
- Two categories: (a) financial Bills on Art 199 matters (need Governor's recommendation to introduce); (b) expenditure Bills (need recommendation to pass). Neither becomes a Money Bill by label.
- Mains hook: finance firewall keeps money in the Assembly.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 117 (Union parallel); Art 199; Art 198.
**Trap:** An expenditure Bill is not automatically a Money Bill; the Governor's recommendation is needed at different stages for different categories.
**Back:** *"207: Money-heads need the Governor to introduce; expenditure-heads need him to pass — neither is automatically a Money Bill."*

### Flashcard Art-208 — Article 208
**Front:** Article 208 — Rules of procedure
**Provision:** A House of the State legislature may make rules to regulate its procedure and the conduct of its business, subject to the Constitution. Until the House makes such rules, the pre-Constitution rules of the corresponding Province apply, with modifications the Speaker or Chairman makes (208(2)); the Governor may make rules on communications between the two Houses after consulting both presiding officers (208(3)) (✅ CORRECTED: the Governor's consultation duty attaches to communication rules, not to interim procedure rules; Wikisource Const. 2020, Part VI).
**Learn:**
- Each House controls its own procedure; the presiding officer enforces the rules.
- Procedure is protected from court review except for the Art 212 rule (procedural irregularity).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 118 (Union parallel); Art 212.
**Trap:** Rules are a House's own; they are not made by the Governor or the courts, apart from interim arrangements.
**Back:** *"208: Each House writes its own rulebook; the Governor's consultation fills gaps."*

### Flashcard Art-209 — Article 209
**Front:** Article 209 — Regulation by law of procedure in the legislature in relation to financial business
**Provision:** The legislature of a State may by law regulate the procedure and conduct of its business in relation to financial matters, and such a law prevails over inconsistent House rules (✅ CORRECTED: the Wikisource Const. 2020 text has one paragraph and no interim Governor's rule-making power; the earlier claim of Governor's interim rules is removed).
**Learn:**
- Financial procedure is governed by statute; Art 209 has no interim Governor's rules (✅ corrected).
- Parallel to Art 119 (Union).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 119 (Union); Art 208.
**Trap:** Financial procedure law is by the legislature; Art 209 gives the Governor no interim rule-making power.
**Back:** *"209: Money procedure is the legislature's law — no Governor's interim rules."*

### Flashcard Art-210 — Article 210
**Front:** Article 210 — Language to be used in the Legislature
**Provision:** (1) Business in the legislature is transacted in the official language or languages of the State, or in Hindi, or in English. (2) The presiding officer may permit a member who cannot adequately express himself in any of these languages to address the House in his mother tongue.
**Learn:**
- Official language of the State, Hindi or English are the default business languages.
- Mother-tongue address is at the presiding officer's discretion.
- Parallel to Art 120 (Union).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 120 (Union); Part XVII (official language).
**Trap:** The mother-tongue exception is discretionary, not a right; the three default options are State language, Hindi or English.
**Back:** *"210: Business in the State language, Hindi or English; the chair may allow the mother tongue."*

### Flashcard Art-211 — Article 211
**Front:** Article 211 — Restriction on discussion in the Legislature
**Provision:** No discussion may take place in the legislature with respect to the conduct of any Judge of the Supreme Court or of a High Court in the discharge of his duties, except when a motion is moved for presenting an address to the Governor praying for the removal of the Judge under Art 124.
**Learn:**
- Judges' conduct is off-limits in the House except for a removal motion.
- Parallel to Art 121 (Union).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 121 (Union); Art 124(4) (removal procedure); Art 218.
**Trap:** Removal motion is the only exception; ordinary criticism of a judge's conduct is barred in the House.
**Back:** *"211: Judges' conduct is off-limits in the House — except a removal motion."*

### Flashcard Art-212 — Article 212
**Front:** Article 212 — Courts not to inquire into proceedings of the Legislature
**Provision:** (1) The validity of any proceedings in the legislature cannot be called in question on the ground of an alleged irregularity of procedure. (2) No officer or member in whom powers for regulating procedure, conducting business or maintaining order are vested is subject to the jurisdiction of any court for exercise of those powers.
**Learn:**
- Procedural irregularity is protected; substantive unconstitutionality is not.
- Presiding officer's order-maintaining powers are immune from court jurisdiction.
- Mains hook: legislative autonomy vs judicial review — this is the balance point.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 122 (Union parallel); Art 194; Kihoto Hollohan (1992) (Speaker's tribunal decision reviewable for Tenth Schedule purposes, ⚠️ distinct question).
**Trap:** Art 212 bars review of procedural irregularity, not substantive illegality or unconstitutionality; Tenth Schedule decisions remain reviewable on Kihoto grounds.
**Back:** *"212: Courts won't review procedure; they still review illegality — Art 212 is procedural, not substantive."*

### Flashcard Art-213 — Article 213
**Front:** Article 213 — Power of the Governor to promulgate Ordinances during recess of the legislature
**Provision:** (1) When the Assembly (or, in a bicameral State, both Houses) is not in session, the Governor may promulgate ordinances if satisfied that circumstances exist requiring immediate action. An ordinance has the same force as a State Act, subject to provisos that restrict it where prior instructions from the President are needed. (2)(a) An ordinance must be laid before the Assembly (or both Houses) and ceases to operate at the expiry of six weeks from reassembly, or earlier if a resolution disapproving it is passed; (b) the Governor may withdraw any ordinance at any time (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part VI, Art 213(1)–(2)).
**Learn:**
- Ordinance is a recess-time substitute for legislation; six weeks from reassembly is the limit.
- Laying before the House is mandatory (Krishna Kumar Singh, 2017 — seven-judge Bench, 5:2 per owner).
- D.C. Wadhwa v. State of Bihar (1986, reported 1987): serial re-promulgation is a fraud on the Constitution.
- The 38th Amendment (1975) made the Governor's satisfaction final; the 44th Amendment (1978) restored judicial review of satisfaction. ✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part VI: Art 213(4) is shown omitted, footnote: inserted by the 38th and omitted by the 44th. The "satisfaction" effect itself is from memory.
- Mains hook: the ordinance route as a substitute for accountable law-making.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 123 (President's ordinance parallel); D.C. Wadhwa (1986/87); Krishna Kumar Singh v. State of Bihar (2017); Art 200 (Bill vs ordinance interplay).
**Trap:** Six weeks from reassembly (not six months); re-promulgation is not allowed as a substitute for legislation; ordinances need the House not to be in session.
**Back:** *"213: Recess ordinance — laid before the House, dies six weeks after it reassembles; never re-promulgated."*

### Flashcard Art-214 — Article 214
**Front:** Article 214 — High Courts for States
**Provision:** "There shall be a High Court for each State."
**Learn:**
- Default rule: one High Court per State; India has 25 High Courts today (owner-sourced; some are common to two or more States or UTs under Art 231).
- Part of the integrated judiciary: SC at the top, then High Courts, then subordinate courts.
- Mains hook: the HC is the State's constitutional court of rights and administration.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 231 (common HC); Art 215–231; Art 124 (SC parallel).
**Trap:** "One HC per State" is a default, not a rule for every State — Art 231 allows common High Courts.
**Back:** *"214: One High Court per State — the rule; Art 231 is the exception."*

### Flashcard Art-215 — Article 215
**Front:** Article 215 — High Courts to be courts of record
**Provision:** Every High Court is a court of record and has all the powers of such a court, including the power to punish for contempt of itself.
**Learn:**
- Court of record: its proceedings are recorded and carry evidentiary weight.
- Contempt power is the HC's own (contempt of itself) — separate from the Contempt of Courts Act's general framework.
- Parallel to Art 129 (SC).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 129 (SC court of record); Contempt of Courts Act, 1971.
**Trap:** The HC's contempt power covers itself and subordinate courts under the 1971 Act; the constitutional phrase in Art 215 is "contempt of itself".
**Back:** *"215: The High Court keeps its own record and punishes contempt of itself."*

### Flashcard Art-216 — Article 216
**Front:** Article 216 — Constitution of High Courts
**Provision:** Every High Court consists of a Chief Justice and such other Judges as the President may from time to time deem necessary to appoint.
**Learn:**
- Strength is not fixed in the text; the President decides the number of judges.
- Parallel to Art 124(1) for the SC.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 124 (SC); Art 217 (appointment); Art 224 (additional judges).
**Trap:** Strength is set by the President (on need), not by statute; sanctioned strength and working strength differ.
**Back:** *"216: CJ plus as many judges as the President deems necessary."*

### Flashcard Art-217 — Article 217
**Front:** Article 217 — Appointment and conditions of the office of a Judge of a High Court
**Provision:** (1) Every HC judge is appointed by the President by warrant under hand and seal, after consultation with the Chief Justice of India, the Governor of the State, and (for any judge other than the CJ) the Chief Justice of that High Court (⚠️ the fetched 2020 text shows only the 99th-Amendment NJAC wording, struck down 16 Oct 2015, and does not display the original consultation words; the list is unconfirmed here). Retirement at 62 years (after the 15th Amendment, 1963). Resignation to the President; removal by the President per Art 124(4). (2) Qualification: citizen of India and (a) held a judicial office in India for ten years, or (b) been an advocate of a High Court (or two or more HCs in succession) for ten years. (3) Disputes about age are decided by the President after consulting the CJI (✅ CORRECTED: was "decided by the CJI"; Wikisource Const. 2020, Part VI). Retirement at 62 replacing sixty is ✅ (footnote).
**Learn:**
- Consultation chain: CJI, Governor, HC CJ (except for the CJ's own appointment).
- Qualifications: 10 years judicial office or 10 years at the Bar — there is no "distinguished jurist" route (that is SC-only).
- Retirement at 62; SC judge at 65.
- Cases: S.P. Gupta v. Union of India (1981) (consultation, not concurrence); Second Judges Case (1993) (CJI's opinion has primacy; collegium); Third Judges Case (1998, Presidential Reference) (collegium: CJI and two senior-most judges for HCs); NJAC (99th Amendment, 2014) struck down in SCARA v. Union of India (2015).
- Mains hook: appointment-independence debate — collegium vs executive.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 124 (SC); 99th Amendment; Art 222 (transfer); Judges (Inquiry) Act 1968 (removal).
