**Trap:** Distinguished-jurist route is SC-only; the Governor is consulted (not the State CM); HC CJ is consulted for all but the CJ's appointment.
**Back:** *"217: Three advisers — CJI, Governor, HC Chief Justice; retire at 62; ten years at the bench or Bar."*

### Flashcard Art-218 — Article 218
**Front:** Article 218 — Application of certain provisions relating to Supreme Court to High Courts
**Provision:** The provisions of clauses (4) and (5) of Art 124 (removal of SC judges by impeachment; Parliament's regulation of the procedure) apply to a High Court as they apply to the Supreme Court.
**Learn:**
- HC judges can be removed by the same impeachment route as SC judges (address by both Houses; proved misbehaviour or incapacity).
- Owner-flagged: no lower standard for HC removal.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 124(4)–(5); Judges (Inquiry) Act 1968; Art 217(1)(c).
**Trap:** Art 218 applies the SC removal clauses to HCs; it does not give the Governor or CM a removal role.
**Back:** *"218: High Court judges fall to the same impeachment route as Supreme Court judges."*

### Flashcard Art-219 — Article 219
**Front:** Article 219 — Oath or affirmation by the Judges of High Courts
**Provision:** Every person appointed a Judge of a High Court shall, before entering office, make and subscribe an oath or affirmation before the Governor of the State, or some person appointed by him, in the form in the Third Schedule.
**Learn:**
- Oath before the Governor.
- Parallel to Art 124(6) (SC judges before the President or person appointed).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 124(6); Art 159 (Governor's oath before HC CJ).
**Trap:** HC judge's oath: before the Governor; Governor's oath: before HC CJ — mirror-image.
**Back:** *"219: The High Court judge swears before the Governor."*

### Flashcard Art-220 — Article 220
**Front:** Article 220 — Restriction on practice after being a permanent Judge
**Provision:** No person who, after the commencement of the Constitution, has held office as a permanent Judge of a High Court shall plead or act in any court or before any authority in India, except the Supreme Court and the other High Courts.
**Learn:**
- Ex-permanent HC judges may practise only in the SC and other HCs.
- Parallel to Art 124(7) for SC judges (which bars practice before any court except the SC — ⚠️ verify the exact SC rule).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 124(7); Art 217.
**Trap:** The exception is "the Supreme Court and the other High Courts" — not the same High Court.
**Back:** *"220: Ex-permanent HC judges may plead only in the Supreme Court or other High Courts."*

### Flashcard Art-221 — Article 221
**Front:** Article 221 — Salaries, etc. of Judges
**Provision:** HC judges receive salaries as Parliament determines by law and, until so determined, as the Second Schedule provides; allowances, leave and pension are as Parliament fixes; a judge's terms cannot be varied to his disadvantage after appointment.
**Learn:**
- Parliament sets HC judges' pay (not the State legislature).
- Pay cannot be reduced after appointment.
- Parallel to Art 125 (SC).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 125 (SC); Art 158 (Governor's pay protection).
**Trap:** HC judges' pay is set by Parliament, not the State; the protection against reduction is a post-appointment guarantee.
**Back:** *"221: Judges' pay is Parliament's to set — and it cannot be cut after appointment."*

### Flashcard Art-222 — Article 222
**Front:** Article 222 — Transfer of a Judge from one High Court to another
**Provision:** (1) The President may, after consultation with the Chief Justice of India, transfer a Judge from one High Court to any other High Court. (2) A transferred judge is entitled to such compensatory allowance as Parliament determines.
**Learn:**
- Consultation with the CJI; the judge's consent is not constitutionally required.
- The transfer process runs through the collegium-based Memorandum of Procedure (owner-flagged; the MoP is not in the text).
- Transfers must serve public interest, not punishment (Sankalchand Himatlal Sheth, 1977 — owner-flagged).
- Mains hook: transfer is both a safeguard and a pressure point.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Sankalchand Himatlal Sheth (1977); Second Judges Case (1993); Art 217 (appointment); Art 124 (SC transfer context).
**Trap:** Transfer is by the President (through the CJI consultation); no consent is needed; it is not a Governor's power.
**Back:** *"222: The President moves a judge, after the CJI's advice — consent not needed."*


### Flashcard Art-223 — Article 223
**Front:** Article 223 — Appointment of acting Chief Justice
**Provision:** When the office of Chief Justice of a High Court is vacant, or when the CJ is absent or unable to perform his duties, the President appoints one of the other judges of that Court to act as Chief Justice.
**Learn:**
- President appoints the acting CJ (not the Governor).
- Stop-gap only; usually the senior-most judge in practice.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 126 (SC acting CJI); Art 217.
**Trap:** The acting CJ is a President's appointment; the Governor takes the HC CJ's oath only in the Art 159 sense (not for this).
**Back:** *"223: Chief's chair vacant — the President names an acting CJ from the judges."*

### Flashcard Art-224 — Article 224
**Front:** Article 224 — Appointment of additional and acting Judges
**Provision:** (1) If by reason of an increase in business or arrears, the President may appoint duly qualified persons as additional judges for a period not exceeding two years. (2) When a judge is absent, or unable to perform, or appointed to act as CJ, the President may appoint a duly qualified person as an acting judge until the permanent judge resumes. (3) No additional or acting judge shall hold office after attaining 62 years of age.
**Learn:**
- Additional judges: temporary, up to two years, for workload.
- Acting judges: cover for an absent judge.
- Both stop at 62.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 217 (retirement at 62); Art 224A (retired judges); Art 216.
**Trap:** Additional judges are capped at two years; acting judges last until the permanent judge returns (not a fixed two years).
**Back:** *"224: Additional for two years, acting for the absent — both stop at 62."*

### Flashcard Art-224A — Article 224A
**Front:** Article 224A — Appointment of retired Judges at sittings of High Courts
**Provision:** Notwithstanding Art 217, the Chief Justice of a High Court may at any time, with the previous consent of the President, request any person who has held office as a judge of that High Court, or of any other High Court, to sit and act as a judge of that High Court; that person is entitled to allowances as the President determines and, while so sitting, has the jurisdiction, powers and privileges of a judge.
**Learn:**
- Inserted by the 15th Amendment (1963).
- Retired judges may be invited back to sit on a temporary basis, with the President's prior consent.
- Owner-flagged: a retired judge sitting under Art 224A is not a serving judge in the ordinary sense.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 15th Amendment (1963); Art 224; Art 217.
**Trap:** Art 224A (retired judge invited back, President's prior consent) vs Art 224 (additional judges, appointed by the President for up to two years) — three distinct routes.
**Back:** *"224A: The CJ may invite a retired judge back to sit — with the President's prior consent."*

### Flashcard Art-225 — Article 225
**Front:** Article 225 — Jurisdiction of existing High Courts
**Provision:** Subject to the provisions of the Constitution and to any law made by the legislature, the jurisdiction of, and the law administered in, any existing High Court, and the powers of the judges of that Court in relation to the administration of justice (including powers to make rules), are the same as immediately before the commencement of the Constitution.
**Learn:**
- Preserves inherited jurisdiction of pre-1950 High Courts.
- Subject to the Constitution and to legislation — preservation is not immunity.
- Mains hook: continuity of the colonial High Court system into the Republic.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 214; Art 226; Art 231 (common HC).
**Trap:** Art 225 preserves jurisdiction; it does not confer new jurisdiction (that comes from Arts 226, 227, 228 or statute).
**Back:** *"225: Old jurisdiction preserved — subject to the Constitution and the law."*

### Flashcard Art-226 — Article 226
**Front:** Article 226 — Power of High Courts to issue certain writs
**Provision:** (1) Notwithstanding anything in Art 32, every High Court may issue to any person or authority, including any Government within its territories, directions, orders or writs — including habeas corpus, mandamus, prohibition, quo warranto and certiorari, or any of them — for the enforcement of any rights conferred by Part III and for any other purpose. (2) The High Court may issue writs to any Government, authority or person outside its territory if the cause of action arises wholly or in part within its territory. (3) An interim order made against a party without copies of the petition and supporting documents, and without a hearing, may be vacated on that party's application; the High Court must dispose of the application within two weeks of receipt or of the copy being furnished, whichever is later, and otherwise the order "stands vacated" (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part VI, Art 226(3); insertion date and amendment ⚠️ unverified). (4) Art 226 does not derogate from Art 32.
**Learn:**
- Wider in purpose: FRs and any other legal right; narrower in territorial reach than Art 32.
- Five writs — mnemonic in the Back.
- Territorial extension via cause of action (Art 226(2) text ✅ Wikisource; the 15th Amendment, 1963 attribution is owner-flagged and unverified).
- L. Chandra Kumar v. Union of India (1997): judicial review under Arts 226/227 and Art 32 is part of the basic structure; tribunal decisions are reviewable by the High Court.
- Whirlpool Corp. v. Registrar of Trade Marks (1998): exceptions to the alternative-remedy rule.
- Radhey Shyam v. Chhabi Nath (2015): civil-court judicial orders are reviewable under Art 227 rather than Art 226 (owner-flagged; verify).
- Mains hook: the HC is the citizen's wider constitutional court.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 32 (SC writ); Art 227 (superintendence); 226A (omitted); L. Chandra Kumar (1997); Whirlpool (1998); Radhey Shyam (2015).
**Trap:** Art 226 requires a petition (issue writs); Art 227 is superintendence and can be suo motu. Do not describe Art 226 as an all-India writ — territorial limits apply.
**Back:** *"H-M-Q-P-C — Hot Mango Queen Prefers Chocolate: habeas, mandamus, quo warranto, prohibition, certiorari."*

### Flashcard Art-226A — Article 226A
**Front:** Article 226A — **Omitted** by the Forty-third Amendment (1977) (✅ omission confirmed: Wikisource Const. 2020, Part VI shows the heading only; the 43rd Amendment's effective date is 13 Apr 1978 per the 228A footnote)
**Provision:** **Omitted** by the Forty-third Amendment (1977). Inserted by the Forty-second Amendment (1976), it barred High Courts from considering the constitutional validity of Central laws in proceedings under Art 226 (repository live-session material records it among the 42nd's judicial-review restrictions, together with Arts 32A, 131A, 144A and 228A).
**Learn:**
- Purpose of the 42nd: restrict judicial review of Central laws in HC writ proceedings.
- The 43rd Amendment repealed it, restoring High Court review.
- Minerva Mills v. Union of India (1980): struck down other 42nd restrictions on judicial review as violating the basic structure (owner/repository-flagged; verify the scope).
- Mains hook: Emergency-era attack on judicial review reversed by 1977–80.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 42nd Amendment (1976); 43rd Amendment (1977); Art 226; Minerva Mills (1980).
**Trap:** 226A is not a current article; do not cite it as a live limit on High Court review.
**Back:** *"226A: The 42nd's gag on High Court validity review — gone by 1977."*

### Flashcard Art-227 — Article 227
**Front:** Article 227 — Power of superintendence over all courts by the High Court
**Provision:** (1) Every High Court has superintendence over all courts and tribunals throughout its territories in relation to which it exercises jurisdiction. (2) It may call for returns, make and issue general rules and prescribe forms for regulating the practice and proceedings of such courts. (3) It may settle tables of fees payable to officers and pleaders. (4) This article does not apply to any court or tribunal constituted under a law relating to the Armed Forces.
**Learn:**
- Superintendence is administrative and judicial, and can be exercised suo motu.
- Supervisory, not appellate: Radhey Shyam (2015) — owner-flagged; verify.
- Armed Forces tribunals are excluded.
- Mains hook: supervision keeps subordinate forums within jurisdiction without replacing the appeal ladder.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 226; Art 235 (control over subordinate courts); Art 228; L. Chandra Kumar (1997).
**Trap:** Art 226 (writ, petition needed) vs Art 227 (superintendence, suo motu allowed); Armed Forces tribunals are outside Art 227.
**Back:** *"227: The High Court supervises every court below it — except Armed Forces tribunals."*

### Flashcard Art-228 — Article 228
**Front:** Article 228 — Transfer of certain cases to High Court
**Provision:** If a High Court is satisfied that a case pending in a court subordinate to it involves a substantial question of law as to the interpretation of the Constitution, the determination of which is necessary for disposing of the case, it shall withdraw the case and (a) dispose of it itself, or (b) determine the question of law and return the case to the subordinate court with a copy of its judgment.
**Learn:**
- The HC can pull a constitutional interpretation question from a lower court.
- Only for substantial questions of constitutional interpretation.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 227; Art 228A (omitted); Art 139 (SC analogue).
**Trap:** Art 228 is not a general appeal route; it applies to constitutional-interpretation questions only.
**Back:** *"228: The High Court pulls a constitutional question up from below — to decide it or send it back."*

### Flashcard Art-228A — Article 228A
**Front:** Article 228A — **Omitted** by the Forty-third Amendment (1977) (✅ omission confirmed, Wikisource Const. 2020, Part VI: 43rd Amendment s.10, w.e.f. 13-4-1978; exact wording still ⚠️)
**Provision:** **Omitted** by the Forty-third Amendment (1977). Inserted by the Forty-second Amendment (1976) as a special provision for disposing of questions of constitutional validity of State laws; the Forty-second Amendment's version required such questions in High Court proceedings to be decided by the Supreme Court (⚠️ exact wording not verified in this build; repository live-session material records 228A among the 42nd's judicial-review restrictions).
**Learn:**
- Purpose: channel validity questions on State laws to the Supreme Court.
- Repealed in 1977 by the 43rd Amendment; the High Courts' ordinary jurisdiction was restored.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 42nd Amendment (1976); 43rd Amendment (1977); Art 226A (omitted); Art 228 (in force).
**Trap:** 228A is not a current article; Art 228 (withdrawal of constitutional questions) is in force and is a different rule.
**Back:** *"228A: The 42nd's route for State-law validity — erased in 1977."*

### Flashcard Art-229 — Article 229
**Front:** Article 229 — Officers and servants and the expenses of High Courts
**Provision:** (1) Appointments of officers and servants of a High Court are made by the Chief Justice (or another judge or officer he authorises); the Governor may by rule require consultation with the State Public Service Commission for certain posts (⚠️ verify). (2) Subject to any law made by the legislature, conditions of service are as the CJ (or authorised judge) prescribes. (3) Administrative expenses of the High Court, including salaries of officers and servants, are charged on the State's Consolidated Fund.
**Learn:**
- The CJ controls the court's staff; the State pays the court's administrative costs.
- Financial autonomy is limited by State funding.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 229 (HC staff); Art 235 (subordinate courts control); Art 229 parallel to Art 146 (SC).
**Trap:** HC staff are appointed by the CJ, not by the Governor or the PSC — the Governor's rule-making is an exception for certain posts only.
**Back:** *"229: The Chief Justice hires the court's staff; the State fund pays."*

### Flashcard Art-230 — Article 230
**Front:** Article 230 — Extension or exclusion of jurisdiction of High Courts to Union territories
**Provision:** (1) Parliament may by law extend or exclude the jurisdiction of a High Court to, or from, any Union territory. (2) Where the High Court's jurisdiction is extended or excluded, its territorial reach is modified accordingly (⚠️ verify the precise sub-clause).
**Learn:**
- Parliament decides which HC covers a UT (e.g., Delhi, Chandigarh, Ladakh arrangements — verify current position).
- Parallel to Art 231's common HC idea but applied to UTs.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 231; Art 239–239AA (UT administration); 7th Amendment (1956, UT reorganisation).
**Trap:** Art 230 is a Parliamentary power, not the President's; it extends or excludes jurisdiction, not the court itself.
**Back:** *"230: Parliament decides which High Court covers a Union territory."*

### Flashcard Art-231 — Article 231
**Front:** Article 231 — Establishment of a common High Court for two or more States
**Provision:** Notwithstanding Art 214, Parliament may by law establish a common High Court for two or more States, or for two or more States and a Union territory.
**Learn:**
- Inserted by the Seventh Amendment (1956).
- Examples in the owners: Punjab and Haryana High Court (Chandigarh is a UT); Bombay High Court (Maharashtra, Goa and UTs).
- Mains hook: the common HC is a federal-design compromise, not a federal right.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 214; Art 230; Seventh Amendment (1956).
**Trap:** Common HC requires a Parliamentary law; a State cannot create a common HC by its own act.
**Back:** *"231: Parliament may merge High Courts — Punjab and Haryana is the example."*

### Flashcard Art-232 — Article 232
**Front:** Article 232 — **Omitted** by the Seventh Amendment (1956)
**Provision:** **Omitted** by the Seventh Amendment (1956) (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part VI editorial note: 230–232 replaced by 230 and 231 through the Seventh Amendment, s.16, w.e.f. 1 Nov 1956). It was an interpretation clause in Part VII (the former Part B States' judicial arrangements); Part VII was removed when the Part A/B/C structure was abolished ⚠️ verify the exact original wording.
**Learn:**
- The omitted provision belonged to the old Part B States' arrangements.
- Its subject matter is now handled by Art 214 (one HC per State) and Art 231 (common HC).
- Repository material records Art 232 as "repealed".
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Seventh Amendment (1956); Art 214; Art 231.
**Trap:** There is no Article 232 circuit-bench provision — a repository MCQ option stating "Article 232 currently creates circuit benches" is a deliberate false option (owner material).
**Back:** *"232: Repealed in 1956 — no circuit-bench article in the Constitution."*

### Flashcard Art-233 — Article 233
**Front:** Article 233 — Appointment of district judges
**Provision:** (1) Appointments, postings and promotions of district judges in any State are made by the Governor in consultation with the High Court exercising jurisdiction over the State. (2) A person not already in the service of the Union or the State is eligible for appointment as district judge only if he has been for not less than seven years an advocate or pleader and is recommended by the High Court for appointment (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part VI, Art 233(1)–(2)).
**Learn:**
- Governor appoints; HC consultation is mandatory; the SPSC is not the decision-maker.
- Outsiders need seven years at the Bar and the HC's recommendation.
- Owner-flagged: Rejanish K.V. v. K. Deepa (2025) prospectively allowed qualifying serving judicial officers to compete in direct District-Judge recruitment — ⚠️ verify.
- Mains hook: the district-judge door is where State executive appointment meets judicial autonomy.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 234 (other judicial posts); Art 235 (control); Art 236 (definitions); Rejanish K.V. (2025) ⚠️.
**Trap:** Art 233 (Governor in consultation with HC) vs Art 234 (Governor under rules after PSC and HC consultation) — PSC is not in Art 233.
**Back:** *"233: The Governor appoints district judges with the High Court; outsiders need seven years at the Bar and the HC's nod."*

### Flashcard Art-233A — Article 233A
**Front:** Article 233A — Validation of certain appointments of, and judgments and orders by, district judges (inserted by the Twentieth Amendment, 1966) ⚠️ title and exact wording to verify
**Provision:** Inserted by the Twentieth Amendment (1966) (insertion ⚠️ unverified). ✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part VI: it validates appointments, postings, promotions and transfers of district judges made before the Twentieth Amendment (cl (a)(i)–(ii)), and the jurisdiction exercised and judgments, decrees, sentences, orders and other acts (cl (b)). The "defect under Art 233 or 234 as then understood" wording is not shown in the summary (⚠️).
**Learn:**
- Retrospective safety net for appointments that were challenged after the Chandra Mohan line of litigation (⚠️ verify the case context).
- It is a one-time validation, not a standing rule for future appointments.
- Repository ledger records Art 233A as validating past district-judge appointments and judgments.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Twentieth Amendment (1966); Art 233; Art 234; Art 236.
**Trap:** Art 233A is a validation provision for past appointments and acts — not a current appointment rule.
**Back:** *"233A: 1966 safety net — past district-judge appointments and judgments validated."*


### Flashcard Art-234 — Article 234
**Front:** Article 234 — Recruitment of persons other than district judges to the judicial service
**Provision:** Appointments of persons (other than district judges) to the judicial service of a State are made by the Governor in accordance with rules made by him, after consultation with the State Public Service Commission and with the High Court exercising jurisdiction.
**Learn:**
- Two consultations: State PSC and the HC.
- Governor makes the rules; appointments follow those rules.
- Owner-flagged: All India Judges Association (2025) prospectively restored a three-year Bar-practice condition for entry-level civil judges — ⚠️ verify; it is judicially prescribed, not Article text.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 233 (district judges); Art 235 (control); All India Judges Association (2025) ⚠️.
**Trap:** Art 234 requires both PSC and HC consultation; Art 233 requires only HC consultation for district judges.
**Back:** *"234: Below district judge — the Governor's rules, after both the PSC and the High Court."*

### Flashcard Art-235 — Article 235
**Front:** Article 235 — Control over subordinate courts
**Provision:** The control over district courts and courts subordinate thereto, including the posting and promotion of, and the grant of leave to, persons belonging to the judicial service of a State (and holding any post inferior to that of district judge), is vested in the High Court; but nothing in this article takes away any right of appeal the person has under the service law or to the Governor.
**Learn:**
- Control of the district and subordinate judiciary sits with the High Court, not with the executive.
- Art 50 directs separation of the judiciary from the executive.
- Mains hook: this is the structural core of judicial independence at the grassroots.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 50 (separation); Art 233; Art 234; Art 227.
**Trap:** Control vests in the HC, not in the Governor or State government; the appeal-to-Governor right is preserved for certain service matters.
**Back:** *"235: The High Court runs the lower courts — posting, promotion, leave; the executive stays out."*

### Flashcard Art-236 — Article 236
**Front:** Article 236 — Interpretation (for Arts 233–237)
**Provision:** In Arts 233–237: (a) "district judge" includes a judge of a city civil court, additional district judge, joint district judge, assistant district judge, chief judge of a small cause court, chief presidency magistrate, additional chief presidency magistrate, sessions judge, additional sessions judge and assistant sessions judge; (b) "judicial service" means a service consisting exclusively of persons intended to fill the post of district judge and other civil judicial posts inferior to the post of district judge.
**Learn:**
- "District judge" is a wide family, not just the title.
- Judicial service is the lower-judiciary service of the State.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 233–237; Art 235 (control).
**Trap:** Do not read "district judge" narrowly: additional, joint, assistant district judges, sessions judges and chief presidency magistrates are all included.
**Back:** *"236: 'District judge' is a family — additional, joint, assistant, sessions — all in."*

### Flashcard Art-237 — Article 237
**Front:** Article 237 — Application of the provisions of this Chapter to certain classes of magistrates
**Provision:** The Governor may by public notification direct that the foregoing provisions of this Chapter (Arts 233–236) and any rules made under them apply to any class or classes of magistrates in the State as they apply to persons appointed to the State's judicial service, with exceptions and modifications the notification specifies.
**Learn:**
- Governor's notification brings magistrate classes under HC control and the Chapter's appointment rules.
- Not automatic; requires the Governor's public notification.
- Owner-flagged: the Governor's notification power is the mechanism.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 233–236; Art 235 (HC control); Art 236 (definitions).
**Trap:** It is a Governor's notification, not a Parliamentary or HC act, and it applies only to the classes named.
**Back:** *"237: The Governor's notification can bring classes of magistrates under the judicial-service chapter."*

### PART VII — States in Part B of the First Schedule (repealed; Article 238)

### Flashcard Art-238 — Article 238 (omitted)
**Front:** Article 238 — Application of provisions of Part VI to States in Part B of the First Schedule (✅ corrected: was "Administration of States in Part B of the First Schedule"; heading per Wikisource Const. 2020, Part VII)
**Provision:** **Omitted** by the 7th Amendment (1956). It applied the provisions of Part VI to the Part B States (Hyderabad, Jammu & Kashmir (special position), Madhya Bharat, Mysore, PEPSU, Rajasthan, Saurashtra, Travancore-Cochin) as they stood in the original Constitution. (✅ omission by the 7th Amendment, s.29 and Sch., w.e.f. 1-11-1956, Wikisource Const. 2020, Part VII) ⚠️ verify the exact original list and wording.
**Learn:**
- The 7th Amendment (States Reorganisation Act 1956) abolished the Part A/B/C/D classification, so Part VII (Art 238) lapsed; States became one category, and Part C/D territories became Union Territories.
- Part VII is therefore the only whole Part of the Constitution that was repealed.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 7th Amendment 1956; States Reorganisation Act 1956 (14 States, 6 UTs); Art 239 (UTs, Part VIII).
**Trap:** Part VII is repealed, not "reserved". Original States were Part A (9), Part B (8), Part C (10) and Part D (Andaman & Nicobar).
**Back:** *"238 = the Part-B ghost: omitted 1956."*

### PART VIII — Union Territories (Articles 239–242)

### Flashcard Art-239 — Article 239
**Front:** Article 239 — Administration of Union territories
**Provision:** Save as otherwise provided by Parliament, every Union territory shall be administered by the **President** acting, to such extent as he thinks fit, through an **Administrator** to be appointed by him with such designation as he may specify. Art 239(1) lets the President appoint the Administrator (Lieutenant Governor, Chief Commissioner or Administrator, by designation). Art 239(2) allows the President to appoint the **Governor of an adjoining State** as Administrator of a UT, in which case the Governor acts **independently of his Council of Ministers** in that capacity.
**Learn:**
- The default constitutional model for UTs is **central administration**, not a State government.
- The Administrator is the **President's agent**, not a Governor and not a head of state; he is not bound to the State-style Council of Ministers in the same way (Art 239 is the base; Art 239A/AA add elected bodies only where so provided).
- Eight UTs today (📰 as of 2026): Andaman & Nicobar Islands, Chandigarh, Dadra & Nagar Haveli and Daman & Diu (merged 26 Jan 2020), Delhi (NCT), Jammu & Kashmir, Ladakh, Lakshadweep, Puducherry.
- Parliament may make a different provision by law (the "save as otherwise provided" clause).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 239A (local legislatures, for certain UTs); Art 239AA (Delhi, 69th Amendment 1991); Art 240 (President's Regulations); Art 241 (High Courts for UTs); Art 246(4) (Parliament's plenary power over UT territory). *Government of NCT of Delhi v Union of India* (2018) — LG bound by aid and advice except on reserved entries, LG is not a Governor and has no independent decision-making power.
**Trap:** A UT Administrator/LG is **not** a Governor under Part VI and not a head of state; "Administrator" is a designation, not a separate office. Do not say every UT has an elected assembly — Ladakh, A&N, Chandigarh, DNH-DD and Lakshadweep have none.
**Back:** *"Two-three-nine: the President's Agent runs the UTs by proxy."* (239 = President acts through his Administrator.)

### Flashcard Art-239A — Article 239A
**Front:** Article 239A — Creation of local Legislatures or Council of Ministers or both for certain Union territories
**Provision:** Parliament may by law create for **Puducherry** (Pondicherry) (✅ corrected: was "certain UTs"; Wikisource Const. 2020, Part VIII, Art 239A(1)) (a) a body, elected or partly elected and partly nominated, to function as a **Legislature**, or (b) a **Council of Ministers**, or (c) both, with such constitution, powers and functions as the law provides. Such a law may amend the Constitution for the purposes of the UT (Art 239A(2)), and the law is not treated as an amendment for Art 368 purposes.
**Learn:**
- It is an **enabling** provision for *statutory* UT legislatures; the Puducherry model was created under it.
- The statute used is the **Government of Union Territories Act, 1963** (Puducherry; Pondicherry's assembly and CoM).
- The Legislature's competence is conferred by the statute; Parliament keeps overriding power (Art 246(4)).
- Art 239A's scope is Puducherry only in the 2020 text, not all UTs (✅ corrected: was "specified UTs").
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Inserted by the **14th Amendment, 1962** (✅ year: Wikipedia, 14th Amendment, in force 28 Dec 1962); Puducherry's assembly under the **Government of Union Territories Act, 1963** (📰 owner); Art 239B supplies the ordinance route for such a body; Art 239AA is the constitutionalised Delhi scheme (not Art 239A).
**Trap:** Puducherry's assembly is **statutory** (Art 239A + 1963 Act); Delhi's is **constitutional** (Art 239AA, 69th Amendment 1991). Do not credit Puducherry to the 69th Amendment. Art 239A is for "certain" UTs, not Delhi.
**Back:** *"A for Assembly-by-Act: 239A gives a Puducherry-type body by law, not by the Constitution."*

### Flashcard Art-239AA — Article 239AA
**Front:** Article 239AA — Special provisions with respect to Delhi
**Provision:** Delhi is the **National Capital Territory of Delhi (NCT)**. Its Administrator is designated the **Lieutenant Governor** (Art 239AA(2)(a)). The Legislative Assembly's size is fixed by a law made by Parliament under Art 239AA(2)(b) (✅ clause confirmed, Wikisource Const. 2020, Part VIII; the figure **70** is the statutory number ⚠️ not verified against the statute). The Assembly is competent over State List and Concurrent List matters **except** entries **1, 2 and 18** of the State List (public order, police, land) and entries **64, 65 and 66** of the State List insofar as they relate to those entries (Art 239AA(3)(a)). The Council of Ministers, headed by the **Chief Minister**, aids and advises the LG; the **CM is appointed by the President** and the CoM is not more than **10% of total members** (i.e. 7 including the CM) (Art 239AA(4)). The LG acts on the aid and advice of the CoM **except** where he must act in his discretion, and a difference of opinion may be referred to the **President** (proviso to Art 239AA(4)). Art 239AA(8) applies Art 239B (ordinance power) to Delhi.
**Learn:**
- Created by the **69th Amendment, 1991**, which also inserted Art 239AB.
- Three excluded fields (public order, police, land) remain with the Centre through the LG.
- The 2018 Constitution Bench: LG bound by aid and advice except the reserved entries; presidential reference is an **exceptional** route, not a routine one.
- 2023 services case: elected government got control over services outside reserved fields; **Act 19 of 2023 (GNCTD Amendment)** ⚠️ (act number unverified) created the National Capital Civil Services Authority and gave the LG the final say on disagreement — 📰 the Act remains operative; its constitutional challenge is unresolved as of 5 September 2026 (owner note; ⚠️ status not verified; Wikipedia pages unreachable).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** *Government of NCT of Delhi v Union of India* (2018; pronounced 4 July 2018 by a five-judge Bench, ✅ Wikipedia) — representative government within its allotted field; (11 May 2023 — date ✅ Supreme Court listing "Govt. of NCT of Delhi vs Union of India", 11 May 2023; the services holding ⚠️ not verified); Art 239AB (failure of constitutional machinery); Art 246(4).
**Trap:** Delhi excludes **three** subjects (public order, police, land); J&K excludes **two** (public order, police) — land is not excluded for J&K. The CM is appointed by the **President**, not the LG. The Assembly's size is set by Parliament's law under Art 239AA(2)(b), not fixed in the Constitution (✅ corrected: was "The 70-member assembly is constitutional, not statutory"). Delhi is the only UT with its own High Court (Art 241).
**Back:** *"70 seats, 7 ministers, 3 locked (Order, Police, Land)."*

### Flashcard Art-239AB — Article 239AB
**Front:** Article 239AB — Provision in case of failure of constitutional machinery in Delhi
**Provision:** If the President, on receiving a report from the Lieutenant Governor or otherwise, is satisfied that a situation has arisen in which the admin of the NCT cannot be carried on in accordance with the provisions of Art 239AA or any law made under it, he may by order suspend the operation of any provision of Art 239AA, and make such incidental and consequential provisions as he thinks fit for administering the NCT, including the **suspension of the Legislative Assembly** in full or part (⚠️ the Assembly-suspension wording is not in the fetched summary; suspension of provisions of Art 239AA is confirmed, Wikisource Part VIII).
**Learn:**
- A President's-Rule-type safety valve specific to Delhi (its own "Art 356" for the NCT).
- Available only where the constitutional machinery of Art 239AA fails; not a routine tool.
- Parallels the President's Rule of Art 356 in form, but the trigger and scope are NCT-specific.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Inserted by the **69th Amendment, 1991** (✅ Act of 1991, in force 1 Feb 1992, Wikipedia 69th Am.); Art 356 (State President's Rule); Art 239AA (Delhi scheme); Art 239B (ordinance power in the NCT via Art 239AA(8)).
**Trap:** Art 239AB (Delhi failure-of-machinery) is **not** Art 239B (Puducherry/ordinance route) and not Art 356 (States). Do not mix the three.
**Back:** *"AB = Assembly Breakdown: Delhi's own President's Rule, with 'AB' to remember 'Art 356-lite'."* (mnemonic only; no statutory name)

### Flashcard Art-239B — Article 239B
**Front:** Article 239B — Power of Administrator to promulgate Ordinances during recess of Legislature
**Provision:** Where the Legislature of a UT referred to in Art 239A (and by extension Delhi under Art 239AA(8)) is not in session, the Administrator may, if he is satisfied that circumstances exist that render it necessary to take immediate action, promulgate Ordinances with the **force of law**, subject to the same limitations and restrictions as an Ordinance of a State Governor under Art 213. The power requires **prior instructions from the President** and cannot be exercised while the legislature is dissolved or suspended (✅ corrected/added; Wikisource Part VIII, Art 239B(1)); an Ordinance made on those instructions is deemed an Act of the UT legislature (✅ Art 239B(2)). An ordinance lapses after six weeks from reassembly of the Legislature, unless disapproved earlier.
**Learn:**
- Mirrors Art 213 (State Governor's ordinance power) for UTs with a legislature.
- The **Administrator** is the ordinance-making authority; the President's instructions can restrict him.
- The six-week lapse rule parallels Art 123/213 — this is the critical practice rule.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Inserted by the **14th Amendment, 1962** ⚠️ (the 1962 Act is confirmed by Wikipedia, but that page mentions only Art 239A; the 239B insertion is unconfirmed); Art 213 (State Governor); Art 123 (President); Art 239AA(8) (Delhi applies Art 239B); Puducherry ordinance route per owner (📰).
**Trap:** The ordinance-power route is Art 239B; the **regulation** power is Art 240 (President's Regulations for UTs without legislatures); the **failure-of-machinery** power is Art 239AB. Art 239B is an **ordinance**, not a regulation.
**Back:** *"B for Break-time: 239B = ordinance when the UT house sleeps, lapsing six weeks after reassembly."*

### Flashcard Art-240 — Article 240
**Front:** Article 240 — Power of President to make regulations for certain Union territories
**Provision:** The President may make regulations for the **peace, progress and good government** of: (a) the Andaman and Nicobar Islands; (b) Lakshadweep; (c) Dadra and Nagar Haveli and Daman and Diu (merged 2020); (d) Ladakh (added by the **Jammu and Kashmir Reorganisation Act, 2019**) ⚠️ (Wikisource 2020 text shows entry (d) as omitted "***"; Ladakh entry unverified). Puducherry is listed in cl (1)(e) (✅ Wikisource Part VIII); per Wikipedia (14th Amendment page), regulations for Puducherry stop once its Legislature is created and meets (✅ corrected: was "covered only when its Legislature is suspended or dissolved"; exact clause wording ⚠️ verify). Regulations have the **force of an Act of Parliament** and may repeal or amend any Act of Parliament applicable to the UT. Regulations must be laid before Parliament.
**Learn:**
- Governs UTs **without** a legislature (plus Puducherry while its legislature does not exist).
- Ladakh has **no** legislature; A&N, Lakshadweep, DNH-DD and Ladakh are "President's Regulation" territories.
- The regulations can amend Acts of Parliament applied to these UTs — a sharp executive power.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 240 amended by the **J&K Reorganisation Act 2019** (Ladakh); Art 239 (administration); Art 241 (High Courts for these UTs).
**Trap:** Art 240 regulations are **not** Art 239B ordinances; they are a President's law-making power with Act-of-Parliament force. The regulation list is for UTs **without** legislature — do not assume Ladakh's assembly exists (it does not).
**Back:** *"240 = President's Rule-Book: four UTs, Act-level force, no assembly."*

### Flashcard Art-241 — Article 241
**Front:** Article 241 — High Courts for Union territories
**Provision:** Parliament may by law constitute a High Court for a Union territory or declare any court in a UT to be a High Court for all or any of the purposes of the Constitution. Subject to Parliament's law, the **jurisdiction** of an existing High Court extends to a UT as the Constitution and statutes provide, (✅ Wikisource Part VIII, cl (3)–(4): continuance of pre-7th-Amendment jurisdiction and Parliament's power to extend or exclude State HC jurisdiction over UTs. ✅ corrected: the card's sentence "the President may make orders as to the high court's jurisdiction over UT territory" does not appear in cl (1)–(4), so it is removed.)
**Learn:**
- Only the **Delhi High Court** is a UT's own High Court (📰 owner: exclusive UT High Court).
- J&K and Ladakh share the **High Court of Jammu & Kashmir and Ladakh**.
- A&N → Calcutta High Court; Chandigarh → Punjab & Haryana HC; DNH-DD → Bombay HC; Lakshadweep → Kerala HC; Puducherry → Madras HC (per owner; ⚠️ verify each assignment — not in the Wikisource text).
- Art 241 is the UT counterpart to Art 214 (High Courts for States).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 214 (State High Courts); Art 226 (writ jurisdiction); Delhi High Court is a constitutionally recognised High Court under Art 241 (📰).
**Trap:** Do not say "every UT has its own High Court"; only Delhi does. Ladakh does not have a separate High Court (it shares with J&K).
**Back:** *"241 = One Delhi, the rest borrow: Delhi owns its High Court; others borrow neighbours."*

### Flashcard Art-242 — Article 242 (omitted)
**Front:** Article 242 — Coorg (omitted)
**Provision:** **Omitted** by the 7th Amendment (1956) (✅ heading "[Coorg]" and omission note, s.29 and Sch., w.e.f. 1-11-1956, Wikisource Const. 2020, Part VIII).
**Learn:**
- Article 242 provided the constitutional scheme for the Part C State of **Coorg** (Kodagu), a Chief Commissioner's Province of the original Constitution, including a legislative council and council of ministers for that unit ⚠️ (verify exact original wording).
- Coorg was merged into the State of Mysore (Karnataka) in 1956; the special scheme became unnecessary.
- The 7th Amendment (1956) abolished Part A/B/C State categories and reorganised States; this is why Art 242 has no current operation.
- Numbering gap: 239–241 are live; 242 is blank in the current text.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 7th Amendment 1956 (States Reorganisation Act 1956); Part VIII is now Art 239–241 (📰 owner).
**Trap:** A "Part C State" is not a UT; the Constitution's old Part C categories ended in 1956. Art 242 is **omitted**, not "repealed by the 42nd Amendment" — the 7th Amendment is the one.
**Back:** *"Coorg's gone: 242 left in 1956, when Part C went."*

### PART IX — Panchayats (Articles 243–243O)

### Flashcard Art-243 — Article 243
**Front:** Article 243 — Definitions (Part IX)
**Provision:** Defines key terms for Part IX: "district", "Gram Sabha", "intermediate level", "Panchayat", "Panchayat area", "population" (as per the last preceding census of which relevant figures have been published) and "village" (✅ lettering (a)–(g) confirmed: district (a), Gram Sabha (b), intermediate level (c), Panchayat (d), Panchayat area (e), population (f), village (g); Wikisource Const. 2020, Part IX). "Panchayat" means an institution (by whatever name called) of self-government for rural areas.
**Learn:**
- "Panchayat" is defined as a **rural** self-government institution — the urban equivalent is a Municipality (Part IXA).
- "Population" is fixed by the **last preceding census** for which published figures exist — relevant to the 20-lakh intermediate-tier exemption (Art 243B).
- "Gram Sabha" is defined as a body of registered electoral-roll members of a village within the Panchayat area (✅ Art 243(b), Wikisource Part IX).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 243A–243O (Part IX); 73rd Amendment 1992 (in force 24 April 1993 📰); Art 40 (DPSP — organise village panchayats).
**Trap:** Panchayat ≠ Municipality (Nagar Panchayat is urban, Part IXA). "Population" for Art 243B is the last census, not a current estimate.
**Back:** *"243: the Panchayat is rural; the Municipality is urban; the census decides the size."*

### Flashcard Art-243A — Article 243A
**Front:** Article 243A — Gram Sabha
**Provision:** A Gram Sabha may exercise such powers and perform such functions **at the village level** as the **State Legislature** may by law provide. The Constitution does not fix the Gram Sabha's powers; it leaves them to the State.
**Learn:**
- The Gram Sabha is the only direct-democracy body in Part IX: the assembly of all registered voters of a village.
- Its powers are whatever State law confers — the Constitution is silent on specific functions.
- PESA 1996 (Fifth Schedule areas) gives Gram Sabhas much stronger powers than the general Part IX scheme.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** PESA 1996 (Fifth Schedule areas, Art 244(1)); Forest Rights Act 2006 (Gram Sabha initiates forest-rights claims); Art 40 (DPSP).
**Trap:** The Gram Sabha's powers are **not** constitutionally fixed; a State can keep it weak. A "Gram Sabha" is not a panchayat — it is the village assembly, not an elected council.
**Back:** *"Gram Sabha = village assembly; State law gives it its wings."*

### Flashcard Art-243B — Article 243B
**Front:** Article 243B — Constitution of Panchayats
**Provision:** There shall be Panchayats at **village, intermediate and district** levels in every State. A State whose **population does not exceed twenty lakh** need not constitute Panchayats at the **intermediate** level.
**Learn:**
- Three-tier system: village (Gram Panchayat), intermediate (Panchayat Samiti / block / taluka), district (Zila Parishad).
- The **only** tier that may be skipped is the intermediate tier, and only for population **≤ 20 lakh** (owner: "not exceeding").
- Population is read against the last preceding census (Art 243 definition).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 73rd Amendment 1992 (in force 24 April 1993); Balwant Rai Mehta Committee (1957) — 3-tier origin; 2025 Prelims routing (Q91, "panchayats at the intermediate level") — key not recorded here.
**Trap:** Only the intermediate tier is optional. A State with population above 20 lakh **must** have the intermediate tier. The village and district tiers are compulsory everywhere.
**Back:** *"Three tiers, but the middle may hide if a State is small (≤ 20 lakh)."*

### Flashcard Art-243C — Article 243C
**Front:** Article 243C — Composition of Panchayats
**Provision:** All **seats** in a Panchayat at each level are filled by **direct election** from territorial constituencies; the village-level chairperson is elected as State law provides; the chairpersons of intermediate and district Panchayats are **elected indirectly** (from among the members of the Panchayats of the level below, as State law provides). The State legislature may make provision for representation of MPs and MLAs in intermediate or district bodies, and for representation of other persons with voting rights as State law provides (✅ MPs/MLAs representation at cl (3)(c), Council of States/Legislative Council members at cl (3)(d); village chairperson per cl (4); higher-tier chairpersons indirect per cl (5); Wikisource Part IX).
**Learn:**
- Territorial members are elected **directly**; the chairpersons of higher tiers are indirectly elected.
- The village chairperson method is left to State law (direct in some, indirect in others).
- MPs/MLAs of the area may be given membership by State law; they are not automatic members under the Constitution.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 243D (reservation for SC/ST, women); Art 243K (SEC conducts elections); Balwant Rai Mehta (3-tier); Ashok Mehta (2-tier) — do not swap.
**Trap:** "Direct election at all three levels" is true only for **territorial members**; the intermediate and district chairpersons are indirect. The village chairperson's method is not fixed by the Constitution.
**Back:** *"All seats direct; the top-chairs indirect; MPs/MLAs only if State lets them."*

### Flashcard Art-243D — Article 243D
**Front:** Article 243D — Reservation of seats
**Provision:** (1) Seats reserved for SCs and STs in each Panchayat in proportion to their population (✅ corrected: chairperson offices sit in cl (4), not cl (1), per Wikisource Part IX). (3) **Not less than one-third** of total seats (including SC/ST women seats) are reserved for women. (4) Offices of chairpersons at each level are reserved for SCs, STs and women as State law provides (✅ cl (4) structure); ⚠️ the "one-third of chairperson offices" figure is not in the fetched text. (6) State legislatures may make provision for reservation for **backward classes** (enabling).
**Learn:**
- The one-third is a **floor**, not a ceiling. Many States have legislated **50 per cent** women's reservation (State-specific, not constitutional).
- SC/ST reservation is population-linked, and the women quota cuts across it.
- Backward-class reservation is enabling, not mandatory.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 73rd Amendment 1992; Art 243T (municipal parallel); Art 243D(4) chairperson reservation; 2019 Mains routing (women and patriarchy) owned here.
**Trap:** "One-third" is a minimum floor; "50 per cent" is a State law, not the Constitution. Do not say the one-third applies only to members, not chairpersons. Backward-class reservation is enabling, not compulsory.
**Back:** *"One-third floor for women; States may go to half; SC/ST by population."*

### Flashcard Art-243E — Article 243E
**Front:** Article 243E — Duration of Panchayats
**Provision:** Every Panchayat continues for **five years** from the date of its first meeting unless dissolved earlier. A Panchayat dissolved before expiry must have fresh elections **before the expiry of its duration** or **within six months** from the date of dissolution. A Panchayat reconstituted after premature dissolution continues only for the **unexpired remainder** of the five-year term. No election is required where the remainder is less than six months (owner note).
**Learn:**
- Fixed five-year tenure; no indefinite supersession.
- The "six months" rule runs from dissolution; the "remainder" rule shortens the reconstituted body's life.
- Ended the pre-1993 practice of letting State governments hold or postpone panchayat polls indefinitely.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 73rd Amendment 1992; Art 243K (SEC conducts polls); Art 243U (municipal parallel); Kishansing Tomar (2006) — timely local elections are a constitutional mandate (owner, ⚖️).
**Trap:** The reconstituted body does **not** get a fresh five-year term; it serves only the unexpired period. The six-month rule is **within** six months of dissolution, not after.
**Back:** *"Five years, not six; a dissolved panchayat's successor serves only the remainder."*

### Flashcard Art-243F — Article 243F
**Front:** Article 243F — Disqualifications for membership
**Provision:** A person shall be disqualified for being chosen as, or being, a member of a Panchayat if he is so disqualified under any law for the time being in force for elections to the Legislature of the State, or under any law made by the State legislature, subject to the proviso to cl (1)(a): no person is disqualified on the ground that he is less than **twenty-five** years of age if he has attained **twenty-one** (✅ corrected: was "a person is disqualified if he is less than twenty-one years of age"; Wikisource Part IX, Art 243F). Any question as to disqualification is determined by such authority as the State legislature may prescribe.
**Learn:**
- The minimum candidate age for panchayats is **21**, not the 25 for State assemblies.
- Disqualification follows State-legislature election law plus State-made grounds.
- The Constitution fixes the age floor; it leaves the disqualification machinery to the State.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 173 (State legislature candidate age 25); Art 243V (municipal parallel); Art 102 and 191 (MP/MLA disqualification).
**Trap:** Candidate age for panchayats is **21** (Art 243F), not 25 (Art 173). Do not confuse the Art 243F disqualification scheme with the Art 102/191 scheme for legislators.
**Back:** *"21 for panchayat, 25 for assembly: 243F says the younger age."*

### Flashcard Art-243G — Article 243G
**Front:** Article 243G — Powers, authority and responsibilities of Panchayats
**Provision:** Subject to the Constitution, the State legislature may by law endow Panchayats with such powers and authority as may be necessary to enable them to function as **institutions of self-government**, and such law may contain provisions for the devolution of powers and responsibilities upon Panchayats, at the appropriate level, in relation to matters listed in the **Eleventh Schedule** (**29 subjects**), including economic development and social justice plans.
**Learn:**
- Eleventh Schedule = **29 subjects** (planning, agriculture, drinking water, rural housing, poverty alleviation, education, health, etc.).
- The language is **enabling** ("may"), so devolution of the 29 subjects is not automatic.
- The Eleventh Schedule was added by the 73rd Amendment; Art 243W/Twelfth Schedule (18 subjects) is for municipalities.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 73rd Amendment 1992; Eleventh Schedule; Twelfth Schedule (Art 243W, municipalities); "three Fs" (functions, functionaries, funds — 2020 Mains routing).
**Trap:** Eleventh = **29** panchayat subjects; Twelfth = **18** municipal subjects. Art 243G is enabling; States have not completed "activity mapping" in most places (owner).
**Back:** *"Eleven-twenty-nine: the eleventh schedule has 29 for panchayats; Art 243G says 'may'."*

### Flashcard Art-243H — Article 243H
**Front:** Article 243H — Power to impose taxes by, and Funds of, the Panchayats
**Provision:** The State legislature may by law (a) authorise a Panchayat to levy, collect and appropriate taxes, duties, tolls and fees; (b) assign to a Panchayat taxes, duties, tolls and fees levied and collected by the State; (c) provide for **grants-in-aid** from the Consolidated Fund of the State; (d) provide for constitution of **Funds** for Panchayats and for payment of moneys into them.
**Learn:**
- Four channels: own taxes, assigned State taxes, State grants-in-aid, and Panchayat Funds.
- Enabling: the Panchayat taxes only what the State authorises.
- Own-source revenue (property/house tax, user charges, tolls, rents from common property resources) is the key non-grant route; Finance-Commission grants are **not** own-source.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 73rd Amendment 1992; Art 243I (SFC reviews); 2018 Mains routing (non-grant financing); 15th/16th Finance Commission local-body grants (📰).
**Trap:** Finance Commission grants (Art 280 / 243I) are **grants**, not own revenue. The Panchayat cannot tax what the State does not authorise. Art 243H is "may", not "shall".
**Back:** *"H = Here are four money channels: own tax, assigned tax, State grant, Panchayat Fund."*

### Flashcard Art-243I — Article 243I
**Front:** Article 243I — Constitution of Finance Commission to review financial position
**Provision:** The **Governor** constitutes a **State Finance Commission** within one year of the commencement of the 73rd Amendment and thereafter at the expiry of **every fifth year**, to review the financial position of Panchayats and make recommendations to the Governor on (a) distribution of net proceeds of State taxes, duties, tolls and fees between the State and Panchayats and between Panchayats at all levels; (b) allocation, assignment or appropriation of grants-in-aid; (c) measures to improve Panchayat finances; (d) any other matter. The Governor lays the recommendations, with an action-taken memorandum, before the State legislature.
**Learn:**
- Constituted by the **Governor**, not the Chief Minister.
- Meets **every five years**; recommendations must be laid before the Legislature with an action memorandum.
- In practice, delayed or ignored SFC reports are a key implementation gap.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 73rd Amendment 1992; Art 243Y (municipal SFC, same body rules); Art 280 (Union Finance Commission, the national counterpart); L.M. Singhvi Committee (1986) (constitutional status).
**Trap:** The **State** Finance Commission (Art 243I) is not the **Union** Finance Commission (Art 280). The SFC recommends; the Legislature decides. Do not say the SFC is "every three years".
**Back:** *"I for Income: the Governor sets the SFC every five years to share money."*

### Flashcard Art-243J — Article 243J
**Front:** Article 243J — Audit of accounts of Panchayats
**Provision:** The State legislature may by law make provision with respect to the **maintenance of accounts** by Panchayats and the **auditing** of such accounts (✅ wording confirmed, Wikisource Part IX; a State-law enabling provision).
**Learn:**
- The audit of panchayat accounts is State-law territory, subject to the State's audit machinery.
- Accountability structure: accounts → audit → legislature.
- Companion to Art 243H/243I on financial accountability.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 73rd Amendment 1992; Art 149/151 (CAG's role for the Union and States — not Panchayats directly); Art 243X and 243Z (municipal parallels).
**Trap:** The CAG does not audit Panchayats under Part IX; the State law decides the auditing machinery. (✅ Art 243J names no CAG role; wording confirmed.)
**Back:** *"J for Journal: the State writes the rules for panchayat books and their audit."*

### Flashcard Art-243K — Article 243K
**Front:** Article 243K — Elections to the Panchayats
**Provision:** (1) The **superintendence, direction and control** of the preparation of electoral rolls and the conduct of all elections to Panchayats are vested in a **State Election Commission**. (2) The **Governor** appoints the State Election Commissioner; his conditions of service and tenure may not be varied to his disadvantage after appointment; he may be removed only in the manner and on the grounds of a High Court judge. (3) The Governor must make available the staff needed by the SEC.
**Learn:**
- The **SEC**, not the Election Commission of India, runs panchayat polls.
- The Commissioner has High-Court-judge protection of tenure and service conditions.
- Political decentralisation is secured by this body; functional decentralisation is not (owner).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 73rd Amendment 1992; Art 243ZA (municipal SEC); *State of Goa v Fouziya Imtiaz Shaikh* (2021) — SEC independence protected (⚖️ per owner; verify); Art 324 (ECI, not for panchayats).
**Trap:** The ECI is **not** the panchayat election authority. The SEC is appointed by the **Governor**. Do not say the SEC is appointed by the President.
**Back:** *"K for Commission: the SEC runs panchayat polls, not the ECI."*

### Flashcard Art-243L — Article 243L
**Front:** Article 243L — Application to Union territories
**Provision:** The provisions of Part IX shall apply to **Union territories**, provided that the President may direct that they apply to any UT with such exceptions and modifications as he may specify by public notification. (⚠️ a separate Parliament-law route is not stated in the Art 243L text; Parliament's general UT power is Art 246(4). The President's notification route is confirmed.)
**Learn:**
- Part IX is applied to UTs by **Presidential** notification, not by a separate parliamentary statute.
- Exceptions and modifications are permitted.
- The UT scheme is parallel to Part IXA (Art 243ZB).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 243ZB (municipal parallel); Art 239 (UT administration); Art 246(4) (Parliament's power over UTs).
**Trap:** The "application to UTs" route is Presidential notification, while Part IXA uses a parallel route. Do not say all UTs have panchayats — the notification decides.
**Back:** *"L for Lakshadweep-style UTs: the President notifies Part IX in."*

### Flashcard Art-243M — Article 243M
**Front:** Article 243M — Part not to apply to certain areas
**Provision:** (1) Part IX does **not** apply to the **Fifth Schedule Scheduled Areas** and the **Sixth Schedule tribal areas** (Art 244(1), (2)). (2)(a) Part IX does **not** apply to the **States of Nagaland, Meghalaya and Mizoram**; (2)(b) nor to the **hill areas of Manipur** for which a District Council exists. (3)(a) the district-level provisions do not apply to the **Darjeeling** hill areas with the Darjeeling Gorkha Hill Council (✅ corrected: the card placed Manipur and Darjeeling under cl (4)); (3A) Art 243D SC seat reservation does not apply to Arunachal Pradesh. (4)(a) a State in cl (2)(a) may extend Part IX by Assembly resolution supported by a majority of total membership and two-thirds of members present and voting (✅ owner note confirmed); (4)(b) Parliament may extend Part IX to the cl (1) areas with exceptions and modifications, and such a law is not a constitutional amendment under Art 368 (✅ corrected: the card placed Parliament's power at cl (3)). Clause numbering confirmed on Wikisource Part IX.
**Learn:**
- Nagaland, Meghalaya, Mizoram are excluded from Part IX entirely, though they have their own tribal governance.
- The Fifth Schedule (protective, Governor/President) and the Sixth Schedule (autonomous councils) are excluded from Part IX.
- PESA (1996) extends a *modified* framework to Fifth Schedule areas by a separate route.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 244 (Fifth/Sixth Schedules); PESA Act 1996; Art 243ZC (municipal parallel); Art 243ZB.
**Trap:** Only **Nagaland, Meghalaya and Mizoram** are excluded by name; **Tripura, Assam** are not excluded by name (their tribal areas are excluded by the Schedule route). PESA is **not** Part IX; it is a separate statute for Fifth Schedule areas only, not Sixth.
**Back:** *"M for Mizoram-Meghalaya-Nagaland: those three States sit outside Part IX."*

### Flashcard Art-243N — Article 243N
**Front:** Article 243N — Continuance of existing laws and Panchayats
**Provision:** Notwithstanding anything in Part IX, any provision of any law relating to Panchayats in force in a State immediately before the commencement of the 73rd Amendment that is **inconsistent** with Part IX shall continue in force **until amended or repealed** by a competent legislature, or **until the expiration of one year** from such commencement, whichever is earlier. Existing Panchayats continue until their term ends or they are lawfully dissolved (owner).
**Learn:**
- A transition rule: pre-1993 panchayat laws survive for at most **one year** after 24 April 1993 unless earlier amended.
- Existing bodies were not abolished by the amendment.
- It fixed the transition, not permanent old law.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 73rd Amendment 1992 (in force 24 April 1993); Art 243ZF (municipal parallel: old municipal laws continued for at most one year, existing municipalities to term).
**Trap:** The one-year rule is the **earlier** of amendment and expiry of one year; the law does not last indefinitely. Do not say old panchayats were dissolved automatically.
**Back:** *"N for Next year: old panchayat laws last at most one year, unless changed sooner."*

### Flashcard Art-243O — Article 243O
**Front:** Article 243O — Bar to interference by courts in electoral matters
**Provision:** Notwithstanding anything in Part IX, (a) the validity of any law relating to the **delimitation of constituencies** or the **allotment of seats** to such constituencies (made under Art 243K; ✅ Wikisource Part IX, 243O(a)) shall not be called in question in any court; and (b) no election to a Panchayat shall be called in question except by an **election petition** presented to such authority and in such manner as the State legislature may by law provide.
**Learn:**
- Courts are barred from interfering in delimitation and seat allotment for panchayats.
- Election disputes are channelled into a statutory **election petition** route under State law.
- Mirrors Art 329 for Parliament and State legislatures (owner).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 329 (bar on election challenge for legislatures); Art 243ZG (municipal parallel); *Kishansing Tomar* (2006) (timely elections a mandate, but no court interference with election calendar generally).
**Trap:** Art 243O bars courts from delimitation/seat-allotment challenges; it does **not** exclude all judicial review (a valid election petition still goes to the designated authority). Do not confuse with Art 329(b).
**Back:** *"O for Ouster: courts keep out of seats and election disputes, except by petition."*

### PART IXA — Municipalities (Articles 243P–243ZG)

### Flashcard Art-243P — Article 243P
**Front:** Article 243P — Definitions (Part IXA) (✅ heading "Definitions", Wikisource Part IXA; clause (c) Metropolitan area = ten lakh or more)
**Provision:** Defines the terms for Part IXA, including "Metropolitan area" (an area having a population of **ten lakh or more** comprised in one or more districts and consisting of two or more Municipalities or other contiguous areas, specified by the Governor by public notification), "Municipal area", "Municipality", "Committee", and "District" (owner).
**Learn:**
- Metropolitan area = **10 lakh or more** population (MPC applies there under Art 243ZE).
- "Municipality" is defined as an institution of self-government for urban areas (Part IXA's core unit).
- Part IXA covers **urban** local bodies; Part IX covers **rural** ones.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 74th Amendment 1992 (in force 1 June 1993 📰); Art 243ZE (Metropolitan Planning Committee); Art 243Q (types).
**Trap:** Metropolitan = **10 lakh or more**; do not confuse with the 3 lakh Wards Committee threshold (Art 243S). Definitions are not the same as the constitution of bodies (Art 243Q).
**Back:** *"P for Population ten lakh: that's a Metropolis."*

### Flashcard Art-243Q — Article 243Q
**Front:** Article 243Q — Constitution of Municipalities
**Provision:** There shall be three types of Municipality: (a) a **Nagar Panchayat** for a transitional area (rural to urban); (b) a **Municipal Council** for a smaller urban area; (c) a **Municipal Corporation** for a larger urban area. The Governor may, by public notification, specify an **industrial township** where no Municipality need be constituted (proviso) (✅ proviso confirmed, Wikisource Part IXA). The Governor classifies areas by population, density, revenue generated, non-agricultural employment and economic importance (✅ 243Q(2) factors confirmed).
**Learn:**
- Size ladder: **Nagar Panchayat < Municipal Council < Municipal Corporation** (transitional → smaller urban → larger urban).
- Classification is by the **Governor**, using population, density, revenue, employment and importance.
- An industrial township may be notified with no municipality.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 74th Amendment 1992; Twelfth Schedule (Art 243W); Art 243P (definitions).
**Trap:** A Nagar Panchayat is **urban** (transitional), not a village panchayat. Do not invert the Corporation/Council order. Industrial township is a Governor-specified exception, not a fourth type.
**Back:** *"Nagar, Council, Corporation: small to big, transitional to metro."*

### Flashcard Art-243R — Article 243R
**Front:** Article 243R — Composition of Municipalities
**Provision:** All seats in a Municipality are filled by **direct election** from territorial constituencies (wards). State law may provide representation for persons with **special knowledge or experience** in municipal administration (without voting rights), for **MPs and MLAs** of the area, for **Rajya Sabha and Legislative Council members** registered as electors in the municipal area, and for **chairpersons of committees**; the State law decides how the chairperson is elected (✅ cl (1) direct election and cl (2)(a)–(b) structure confirmed, Wikisource Part IXA; ⚠️ category-list wording unverified).
**Learn:**
- Territorial seats are direct; the Constitution permits limited, State-decided additional representation.
- Experts may be members but **cannot vote** in municipal meetings.
- The chairperson's election method is for State law.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 243D (panchayat parallel); Art 243S (Wards Committees); Art 243T (reservation).
**Trap:** The 74th Amendment permits **experts without vote**; the panchayat scheme (Art 243C) does not use that device. Do not say all municipal members are directly elected — the additional categories are not territorial.
**Back:** *"Wards vote, experts advise: direct seats, expert non-voters, State decides the rest."*

### Flashcard Art-243S — Article 243S
**Front:** Article 243S — Constitution and composition of Wards Committees
**Provision:** There shall be a **Wards Committee**, consisting of one or more wards, within every Municipality with a **population of three lakh or more**. The State law may make provision with respect to the composition and territorial area of such committees and other matters (✅ cl (1) three-lakh threshold and cl (2) State law, Wikisource Part IXA). The threshold is population-based.
**Learn:**
- Wards Committees are **mandatory only at ≥ 3 lakh population**; not in every municipality.
- They provide intra-city decentralisation (ward-level), not a separate tier of government.
- Composition is State-determined.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 74th Amendment 1992; Art 243R (composition); Art 243W (functions via Twelfth Schedule).
**Trap:** Do not say Wards Committees exist everywhere — threshold is **3 lakh**. Do not confuse them with Art 243ZD (DPC) or 243ZE (MPC).
**Back:** *"S for Size: three lakh and above, a Wards Committee stands."*

### Flashcard Art-243T — Article 243T
**Front:** Article 243T — Reservation of seats
**Provision:** (1) Seats are reserved for **SCs and STs** in proportion to their population in the municipal area, and may be rotated among constituencies (✅ Wikisource Part IXA). (2) At least one-third of those SC/ST-reserved seats go to **women** of those groups (✅). (3) **Not less than one-third** of the total number of seats (including seats reserved for SC/ST women) are reserved for **women** (✅ corrected: was clause (2)). (4) Offices of chairpersons are reserved for SC, ST and women as State law provides (✅ corrected: was clause (3)). (6) State law may provide reservation for **backward classes** (enabling) (✅ corrected: was clause (4)).
**Learn:**
- Same architecture as Art 243D (panchayats): population-linked SC/ST, one-third women floor.
- Chairperson reservation is State-law territory.
- Backward-class reservation is enabling.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 74th Amendment 1992; Art 243D (panchayat); *K. Krishna Murthy* (2010) and *Vikas Kishanrao Gawali* (2021) — OBC political reservation requires local-body-specific data and the triple test (⚖️ per owner).
**Trap:** The one-third is a **floor**; many States go higher. Do not treat backward-class reservation as mandatory — the Constitution makes it enabling.
**Back:** *"T for Total one-third: SC/ST by population, women at least a third, OBCs by choice."*

### Flashcard Art-243U — Article 243U
**Front:** Article 243U — Duration of Municipalities
**Provision:** Every Municipality continues for **five years** from the date of its first meeting unless dissolved earlier. Before dissolution, the body must be given a **reasonable opportunity** of being heard (✅ proviso to cl (1), Wikisource Part IXA). Fresh elections must be held **before expiry** or **within six months** of dissolution. A reconstituted Municipality serves only the **unexpired remainder**. No election is needed where the remainder is less than six months (owner).
**Learn:**
- Five-year term, same rule as Art 243E.
- The "reasonable opportunity" protects a municipality from arbitrary dissolution.
- Early reconstituted bodies serve only the balance.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 74th Amendment 1992; Art 243E (panchayat parallel); *Kishansing Tomar* (2006) (timely local elections).
**Trap:** Six months runs from **dissolution**; the reconstituted body does not get a new five-year term. Do not say the reasonable-opportunity rule applies to Panchayats (it is in the municipal text).
**Back:** *"U for Unexpired: five years, six months, and the successor gets only the rest."*

### Flashcard Art-243V — Article 243V
**Front:** Article 243V — Disqualifications for membership
**Provision:** A person is disqualified for being chosen as, or being, a member of a Municipality if he is disqualified under any law for the time being in force for elections to the Legislature of the State, or under any law made by the State legislature, and (owner) a candidate must be at least **21 years of age** (✅ proviso to cl (1)(a): no disqualification for being under 25 if 21 is attained; Wikisource Part IXA). Disputes are decided by the authority the State law prescribes.
**Learn:**
- Municipal candidate minimum age **21**, not 25.
- Disqualification follows State assembly law plus State-made grounds.
- Mirrors Art 243F for panchayats.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 74th Amendment 1992; Art 243F (panchayat parallel); Art 173 (legislature age 25).
**Trap:** Municipal age is **21** (Art 243V per owner), not the assembly age of 25. Do not confuse disqualification with the Art 102/191 legislative scheme.
**Back:** *"V for Varsity-age: 21 to contest a municipal seat, same as a panchayat."*

### Flashcard Art-243W — Article 243W
**Front:** Article 243W — Powers, authority and responsibilities of Municipalities
**Provision:** Subject to the Constitution, the State legislature may by law endow Municipalities with powers and authority necessary to enable them to function as **institutions of self-government**, and may provide for the devolution of powers and responsibilities upon them in relation to matters in the **Twelfth Schedule** (**18 subjects**), including urban planning (including town planning), regulation of land use and construction of buildings, water supply, public health, sanitation, fire services, urban poverty alleviation, slums and urban forestry, etc.
**Learn:**
- Twelfth Schedule = **18 subjects** (municipal); Eleventh Schedule = **29** (panchayats).
- Enabling "may" language: devolution is State-discretionary.
- Most States have not completed activity mapping; line departments and parastatals run many functions.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 74th Amendment 1992; Twelfth Schedule; Art 243G (panchayat parallel); Art 243ZD/ZE (planning committees).
**Trap:** Twelfth = **18** (municipal); do not swap with 29 (panchayat). The article is enabling; do not assert automatic transfer of 18 subjects.
**Back:** *"Twelve-eighteen: the Twelfth has 18 for cities; 243W says 'may'."*

### Flashcard Art-243X — Article 243X
**Front:** Article 243X — Power to impose taxes by, and Funds of, the Municipalities
**Provision:** The State legislature may by law (a) authorise a Municipality to levy, collect and appropriate taxes, duties, tolls and fees; (b) assign to a Municipality taxes, duties, tolls and fees levied and collected by the State; (c) provide for **grants-in-aid** from the Consolidated Fund of the State; (d) provide for constitution of **Funds** for Municipalities and payment of moneys into them.
**Learn:**
- Four channels (same structure as Art 243H): own taxes, assigned State taxes, grants, municipal funds.
- Property tax is the mainstay; user charges, tolls, licensing and market fees are other own-revenue heads.
- Municipal bonds are a capital-market route (SEBI framework; 📰).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 74th Amendment 1992; Art 243H (panchayat parallel); Art 243Y (municipal SFC); SEBI (Issue and Listing of Municipal Debt Securities) Regulations 2015 (📰 owner).
**Trap:** Municipal bonds are **debt**, not own tax. Finance Commission grants are grants, not own revenue. The power is enabling.
**Back:** *"X marks the money: own tax, assigned tax, grant, fund — as the State allows."*

### Flashcard Art-243Y — Article 243Y
**Front:** Article 243Y — Finance Commission
**Provision:** The **State Finance Commission** constituted under Art 243I for Panchayats shall, with necessary modifications, also review the financial position of Municipalities and make recommendations on the same subjects as Art 243I(3). The Governor lays recommendations and an action memorandum before the State legislature.
**Learn:**
- The **same** SFC serves both panchayats and municipalities.
- Recommendations cover tax distribution, assignments, grants and funds.
- SFC delays are a documented implementation gap.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 243I (SFC); Art 280 (Union Finance Commission); 16th Finance Commission (2026–31 award, 📰).
**Trap:** The municipal SFC is **not** a separate body under Art 243Y; it is the Art 243I SFC with modifications. Do not confuse with the Union Finance Commission.
**Back:** *"Y for Yoke: one State Finance Commission serves both villages and towns."*

### Flashcard Art-243Z — Article 243Z
**Front:** Article 243Z — Audit of accounts of Municipalities (✅ heading, Wikisource Part IXA)
**Provision:** The State legislature may by law make provision with respect to the **maintenance of accounts** by Municipalities and the **auditing** of such accounts (✅ wording confirmed on Wikisource Part IXA).
**Learn:**
- Municipal accounts are State-law territory.
- Parallel to Art 243J (panchayats).
- Accountability runs accounts → audit → legislature.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 74th Amendment 1992; Art 243J (panchayat parallel); Art 243X (municipal funds).
**Trap:** Do not confuse the municipal audit with the CAG's Union/State audit role under Art 149/151.
**Back:** *"Z for Zero-error books: the State writes the municipal audit rules."*

### Flashcard Art-243ZA — Article 243ZA
**Front:** Article 243ZA — Elections to the Municipalities
**Provision:** The superintendence, direction and control of the preparation of electoral rolls and the conduct of all elections to Municipalities is vested in the **State Election Commission** (the same body as Art 243K, appointed by the Governor), subject to State law. Owner: Art 243ZA is the municipal SEC route.
**Learn:**
- The **SEC**, not the ECI, runs municipal polls.
- Same independence protections as the panchayat SEC.
- *State of Goa v Fouziya Imtiaz Shaikh* (2021) protects SEC independence (⚖️ per owner; verify).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 243K (panchayat SEC); Art 324 (ECI); 74th Amendment 1992.
**Trap:** The ECI does not conduct municipal polls. Do not say the SEC is appointed by the President.
**Back:** *"ZA for Zonal Authority: the SEC runs city polls too."*

### Flashcard Art-243ZB — Article 243ZB
**Front:** Article 243ZB — Application to Union territories
**Provision:** The President may direct that the provisions of Part IXA shall apply to any Union territory with such exceptions and modifications as he may specify by public notification (owner: President may adapt Part IXA for UTs).
**Learn:**
- Presidential route for UT municipalities, parallel to Art 243L.
- Exceptions and modifications are allowed.
- Scheduled and tribal areas are excluded separately (Art 243ZC).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 74th Amendment 1992; Art 243L (panchayat parallel); Art 239 (UT administration).
**Trap:** The UT route is Presidential notification; a UT does not automatically get municipalities.
**Back:** *"ZB: the President brings the city law to the UTs by notice."*

### Flashcard Art-243ZC — Article 243ZC
**Front:** Article 243ZC — Application of this Part to certain areas
**Provision:** (1) Part IXA does **not** apply to **Scheduled Areas** and **tribal areas** under Art 244(2) (✅ Wikisource Part IXA; ✅ corrected: the card also named Manipur hill areas, which is Art 243M(2)(b) for Part IX only). (2) Nothing in the Part affects the powers of the Darjeeling Gorkha Hill Council (✅). (3) Parliament may extend the Part to those areas with exceptions and modifications, and such a law is not a constitutional amendment for Art 368 purposes (✅ clause numbering confirmed).
