**Learn:**
- Municipal Part IXA mirrors Art 243M for panchayats.
- Scheduled and tribal areas stay under Art 244 governance.
- Parliament's extension power is the only way to bring them in.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 243M (panchayat exclusion); Art 244 (Fifth/Sixth Schedules); Art 243ZB.
**Trap:** Art 243ZC exclusion is for Scheduled/tribal areas; municipal government in Sixth-Schedule towns is not automatically Part IXA. Do not confuse with Art 243M's list of three States.
**Back:** *"ZC: the city law stops at the tribal fence unless Parliament opens the gate."*

### Flashcard Art-243ZD — Article 243ZD
**Front:** Article 243ZD — Committee for district planning
**Provision:** Every State shall constitute a **District Planning Committee** at the district level to consolidate the plans prepared by Panchayats and Municipalities in the district and prepare a **draft development plan** for the district. The DPC shall have at least **four-fifths** of its members elected from the elected members of district-level Panchayats and municipalities in the district, in proportion to the rural and urban population of the district. The chairperson forwards the draft plan to the State Government (owner).
**Learn:**
- DPC = **four-fifths elected** (4/5), in rural:urban population ratio.
- It consolidates panchayat and municipal plans into one district plan.
- The planning committee is the constitutional heart of Part IXA but is often weak or unconstituted.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 74th Amendment 1992; Art 243ZE (MPC: two-thirds elected); Art 243G/243W (functions); 2023 Mains routing (planning-committee weak link).
**Trap:** **DPC = 4/5 elected** (Art 243ZD); **MPC = 2/3 elected** (Art 243ZE) — do not swap. DPC is at district level; MPC is for metropolitan areas.
**Back:** *"ZD: Four-fifths for the District, two-thirds for the Metro (ZE)."*

### Flashcard Art-243ZE — Article 243ZE
**Front:** Article 243ZE — Committee for metropolitan planning
**Provision:** Every Metropolitan area shall have a **Metropolitan Planning Committee** to prepare a **draft development plan** for the metropolitan area as a whole. The MPC must have at least **two-thirds** of its members elected from the elected members of the Municipalities and the chairpersons of Panchayats in the metropolitan area, in proportion to the population ratio. The chairperson forwards the draft plan to the State Government (owner).
**Learn:**
- MPC = **two-thirds elected**, for metropolitan areas (population **≥ 10 lakh**).
- It plans the metropolitan area as a whole across municipalities and panchayats.
- Many States have not constituted MPCs even in major metros, or left them powerless (owner).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 74th Amendment 1992; Art 243P(c) (metro = 10 lakh); Art 243ZD (DPC); development authorities and parastatals as capture (owner).
**Trap:** MPC = **2/3**; DPC = **4/5**. A metropolitan area is 10 lakh+. Planning committees do not control every parastatal or land power.
**Back:** *"ZE: two-thirds for the Metro, four-fifths for the District."*

### Flashcard Art-243ZF — Article 243ZF
**Front:** Article 243ZF — Continuance of existing laws and Municipalities
**Provision:** Notwithstanding anything in Part IXA, any law relating to Municipalities in force immediately before the commencement of the 74th Amendment which is inconsistent with Part IXA shall continue **until amended or repealed**, or **until one year** from commencement, whichever is earlier. Existing municipalities continue until their term ends or they are lawfully dissolved (owner).
**Learn:**
- Transition clause parallel to Art 243N (panchayats).
- Old municipal laws survive at most one year unless earlier amended.
- Existing municipalities were not abolished by the amendment.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 74th Amendment 1992 (in force 1 June 1993); Art 243N (panchayat parallel).
**Trap:** Same pattern as Art 243N: earlier of amendment or one year. Do not say existing municipalities had to be re-constituted automatically.
**Back:** *"ZF: the old city law gets one year, unless changed earlier."*

### Flashcard Art-243ZG — Article 243ZG
**Front:** Article 243ZG — Bar to interference by courts in electoral matters
**Provision:** Notwithstanding anything in Part IXA, (a) the validity of any law relating to the delimitation of wards or the allotment of seats to such wards shall not be called in question in any court; and (b) no election to a Municipality shall be called in question except by an **election petition** presented to such authority and in such manner as State law provides (owner: "bars court interference in electoral matters") (✅ subclauses (a)–(b) confirmed, Wikisource Part IXA; delimitation and seat allotment under Art 243ZA).
**Learn:**
- Municipal parallel to Art 243O: courts stay out of ward delimitation and seat allotment.
- Election disputes go by petition under State law.
- Protects the municipal election calendar.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 243O (panchayat); Art 329 (legislatures); *Kishansing Tomar* (2006).
**Trap:** Art 243ZG bars challenges to ward delimitation and seat allotment, and channels election disputes to State-law petitions; it is not an absolute ban on all judicial review.
**Back:** *"ZG for Gate-shut: the courts stay out of wards and seats."*

### PART IXB — Co-operative Societies (Articles 243ZH–243ZT)

### Flashcard Art-243ZH — Article 243ZH
**Front:** Article 243ZH — Definitions (Part IXB) (✅ heading "Definitions", Wikisource Part IXB)
**Provision:** Defines Part IXB terms, including "co-operative society" (a society registered or deemed registered under any law relating to co-operative societies) and "multi-State co-operative society" (a society with objects not confined to one State, registered under the Multi-State Co-operative Societies Act) (✅ defined terms confirmed on Wikisource Part IXB; exact definition wording ⚠️ unverified).
**Learn:**
- Part IXB is the co-operative part of the Constitution, inserted by the **97th Amendment (2011)**.
- Multi-State co-operatives fall under Union List Entry 44 (corporations with objects not confined to one State).
- Single-State co-operatives fall under State List Entry 32.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 97th Amendment 2011 (in force 15 February 2012 per owner 📰); Union List Entry 44; State List Entry 32; *Rajendra N. Shah* (2021).
**Trap:** Part **IX-B** = co-operatives; Part **IX-A** = municipalities. Part IXB Articles are **243ZH–243ZT**, not the 243-G range.
**Back:** *"ZH: the co-op definitions; IX-B is cooperatives, not municipalities."*

### Flashcard Art-243ZI — Article 243ZI
**Front:** Article 243ZI — Incorporation of co-operative societies (✅ heading, Wikisource Part IXB)
**Provision:** Part IXB provides for the **incorporation** of co-operative societies on the basis of voluntary membership, democratic member control, member economic participation and autonomy (✅ principles confirmed on Wikisource Part IXB: voluntary formation, democratic member control, member economic participation, autonomous functioning).
**Learn:**
- The Constitution's co-op principles mirror Art 43-B (voluntary, autonomous, democratic, professional).
- Incorporation and registration are governed by the applicable law (State law for single-State; MSCS Act for multi-State).
- Registration route is not an Art 368 amendment question.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 43-B (DPSP); Art 19(1)(c) (right to form co-ops); *Rajendra N. Shah* (2021).
**Trap:** Art 19(1)(c) gives the **right to form** a co-op; Art 243ZI sets principles for incorporation; neither makes co-operatives a Union subject for single-State bodies.
**Back:** *"ZI: co-ops start by free choice, run by members, under the law."*

### Flashcard Art-243ZJ — Article 243ZJ
**Front:** Article 243ZJ — Number and term of members of board and its office bearers (✅ corrected heading; was "Number of members and term of office of board")
**Provision:** The board of a co-operative society shall consist of **not more than 21 directors**, elected for a **five-year** term (✅ Wikisource Part IXB). **Functional directors** are not counted in the 21, and up to **two non-voting experts** may be co-opted (✅). Where individual membership includes them, one seat is reserved for an **SC/ST** member and two seats for **women** (✅ this reservation text moved here from the former 243ZM card). After *Rajendra N. Shah* the operative field is the multi-State one (✅ para 80; see 243ZR/ZS). Office-bearers elected from among the directors: ⚠️ not in the fetched summary.
**Learn:**
- Maximum board size **21** directors; term **five years**.
- Applies to the multi-State co-operative field that survived the 2021 decision.
- A uniform governance floor for the surviving field.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** *Rajendra N. Shah* (2021) (State-field invalidity); Multi-State Co-operative Societies Act 2002 (as amended 2023); 97th Amendment 2011.
**Trap:** The **21-member** and **five-year** rules apply to the **surviving multi-State** field; they do not govern single-State co-operatives, which fall under State law after the 2021 severance.
**Back:** *"ZJ: twenty-one directors, five years, for the multi-State field alone."*

### Flashcard Art-243ZK — Article 243ZK
**Front:** Article 243ZK — Election of members of board (✅ heading, Wikisource Part IXB)
**Provision:** Members of the board of a co-operative society shall be elected by the members in accordance with the Part and the law; elections shall be conducted under the supervision of such authority as the law provides (✅ Wikisource Part IXB summary: electoral rolls and conduct under a State-designated authority; elections held before the board's term ends so the new board takes over at once).
**Learn:**
- Board members are elected by the society's members, not appointed.
- The Election Authority created by the 2023 Multi-State Act (📰 owner; ⚠️ name and status unverified) supervises elections for multi-State bodies.
- Elections are scheduled before expiry of the term.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Multi-State Co-operative Societies (Amendment) Act 2023 (Co-operative Election Authority, 📰 owner; ⚠️ unverified: PRS and Wikipedia pages unreachable); Art 243ZJ.
**Trap:** The Co-operative Election Authority is a **statutory** body under the 2023 Act, not a constitutional Election Commission (⚠️ per owner, unverified). Do not say the ECI runs co-operative elections.
**Back:** *"ZK: members elect, a statutory authority supervises — not the ECI."*

### Flashcard Art-243ZL — Article 243ZL
**Front:** Article 243ZL — Supersession and suspension of board and interim management (✅ corrected heading; was "Co-option and related board matters", and co-option sits in 243ZJ)
**Provision:** A board may be **superseded or suspended** (with interim management) for **not more than six months** (one year for non-multi-State banking co-operatives), only on the grounds the Article lists: persistent default, negligence, acts prejudicial to the society, stalemate, or failure of the election authority (✅ Wikisource Part IXB summary; exact grounds wording ⚠️ unverified). Elections must be arranged within the period (✅).
**Learn:**
- Supersession and suspension are capped at six months, with a one-year exception for non-multi-State banking societies (✅).
- No supersession where the Government has no financial stake; the Banking Regulation Act, 1949 applies to banking societies (✅ summary).
- The administrator must arrange elections within the period; the State may set the administrator's service conditions (✅).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 97th Amendment 2011; Art 243ZJ (board size); Art 243ZK (elections).
**Trap:** Six months is the **maximum** for supersession, not a standard period; do not confuse it with the audit deadline (Art 243ZM). The co-option figure (two non-voting experts) belongs to Art 243ZJ.
**Back:** *"ZL: supersession lasts at most six months, for stated grounds only."*

### Flashcard Art-243ZM — Article 243ZM
**Front:** Article 243ZM — Audit of accounts of co-operative societies (✅ corrected heading; was "Reservation of seats", which is in 243ZJ)
**Provision:** States may regulate the accounts of co-operative societies and require audit at least **yearly**; the State sets auditors' qualifications; the general body appoints auditors from an approved panel; the audit must be completed **within six months** of the close of the financial year; apex-society audit reports go before the State Legislature (✅ Wikisource Part IXB summary; exact wording ⚠️ unverified).
**Learn:**
- Audit deadline: six months after the financial year closes (✅).
- Auditors come from a State-approved panel and are appointed by the general body (✅).
- Relevant to multi-State bodies under the MSCS Act and to the 2023 concurrent-audit controls (📰 owner; ⚠️ unverified).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 97th Amendment 2011; Multi-State Co-operative Societies (Amendment) Act 2023 (concurrent-audit controls, 📰 owner; ⚠️ unverified).
**Trap:** Six months is the **audit** deadline after year-end, not the supersession cap (Art 243ZL). Do not swap the six-month figures.
**Back:** *"ZM: the books are audited within six months of year-end."*

### Flashcard Art-243ZN — Article 243ZN
**Front:** Article 243ZN — Convening of general body meetings (✅ corrected heading; was "Term of office and supersession of board", which is 243ZL)
**Provision:** States may require the **annual general body meeting** to be held within **six months** of the close of the financial year, and may prescribe its procedure (✅ Wikisource Part IXB summary; exact wording ⚠️ unverified). The six-month supersession cap belongs to Art 243ZL.
**Learn:**
- The general body (members' assembly) is the sovereign body; the annual meeting is the recurring event (✅).
- The meeting falls within six months of year-end (✅).
- Do not invent a meeting count beyond what State law prescribes.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 97th Amendment 2011; Art 243ZL (supersession); Art 43-B (democratic control).
**Trap:** The six months here is the **meeting** deadline after year-end, not the supersession cap (Art 243ZL); do not swap the two six-month figures.
**Back:** *"ZN: the general body meets within six months of year-end."*

### Flashcard Art-243ZO — Article 243ZO
**Front:** Article 243ZO — Right of a member to get information (✅ corrected heading; was "Audit of accounts", which is 243ZM)
**Provision:** States may give a member access to the books and accounts relating to the member's dealings with the society, may set participation conditions (such as meeting attendance or minimum use of services), and may provide co-operative education and training (✅ Wikisource Part IXB summary; exact wording ⚠️ unverified).
**Learn:**
- Member access to the books of their own dealings is a member-level governance right within the Part IXB field (✅).
- The 2023 Act adds a member-facing **Co-operative Information Officer** (📰 owner; ⚠️ unverified).
- Participation conditions and education are State-law matters (✅).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Multi-State Co-operative Societies (Amendment) Act 2023 (Co-operative Information Officer, 📰 owner; ⚠️ unverified); *Thalappalam* (co-ops and RTI public-authority status — per owner, fact-sensitive); Art 43-B.
**Trap:** A co-op member's information right under Part IXB is not the same as the RTI Act; registration alone does not automatically make a society an RTI public authority (owner, Thalappalam; ⚠️ fact-sensitive).
**Back:** *"ZO: members see the books of their own dealings; State law sets the terms."*

### Flashcard Art-243ZP — Article 243ZP
**Front:** Article 243ZP — Returns (✅ corrected heading; was "General body meeting of co-operative societies", which is 243ZN)
**Provision:** Societies must file returns within **six months** of the close of the financial year: the annual report, audited accounts, surplus-disposal plan, bye-law amendments, dates of meetings and elections, and any other information the Registrar requires (✅ Wikisource Part IXB summary; exact wording ⚠️ unverified).
**Learn:**
- Returns are the statutory reporting duty to the Registrar; the six-month filing clock runs from year-end (✅).
- The list includes the audited accounts and the surplus-disposal plan (✅).
- The Registrar may require further information (✅).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 97th Amendment 2011; Art 243ZM (audit); Art 243ZN (general body meeting); MSCS Act 2002.
**Trap:** Do not confuse the return deadline (six months) with the AGM deadline (Art 243ZN) or the audit deadline (Art 243ZM); all three are six-month rules.
**Back:** *"ZP: file the returns within six months of year-end."*

### Flashcard Art-243ZQ — Article 243ZQ
**Front:** Article 243ZQ — Offences and penalties (✅ corrected heading; was "Right of member to information", which is 243ZO)
**Provision:** States may define **offences and penalties**: false returns or information; disobeying summonses or orders; employers failing to remit employee deductions within **14 days**; officers withholding records or property; corrupt election practices (✅ Wikisource Part IXB summary; exact wording ⚠️ unverified).
**Learn:**
- Offences are defined by State law within the Part's frame (✅).
- The 14-day remittance rule applies to employers' deductions, not as a general deadline (✅).
- Corrupt election practices are a listed offence category (✅).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 97th Amendment 2011; Art 243ZO (member information); Art 243ZP (returns).
**Trap:** Penalties are set by the Part and State law; do not present the 14-day figure as a general time limit.
**Back:** *"ZQ: the offence list — false returns, defiance, late deductions, corrupt polls."*

### Flashcard Art-243ZR — Article 243ZR
**Front:** Article 243ZR — Application to multi-State co-operative societies (✅ corrected heading, Wikisource Part IXB)
**Provision:** For multi-State co-operative societies, references in the Part to the State legislature, State Act and State Government are read as Parliament, the Central Act and the Central Government (✅ Wikisource Part IXB summary; exact wording ⚠️ unverified).
**Learn:**
- The surviving operative field of Part IXB is the **multi-State** co-operative field after *Rajendra N. Shah*.
- The application provisions decide how the Part reaches multi-State bodies and UT-based societies.
- After the 2021 severance the surviving field is multi-State only (✅ Rajendra N. Shah, para 80).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** *Rajendra N. Shah* (2021) (severability; surviving multi-State field); Multi-State Co-operative Societies Act 2002; Union List Entry 44.
**Trap:** Do not present 243ZR as a general application to all co-operatives; the 2021 severance limits it to the multi-State field.
**Back:** *"ZR: the scope article — the surviving field is multi-State only."*

### Flashcard Art-243ZS — Article 243ZS
**Front:** Article 243ZS — Application to Union territories (✅ corrected heading, Wikisource Part IXB)
**Provision:** Part IXB applies to Union territories, with the administrator or the territory's Legislative Assembly standing in for the State legislature; the President may exempt a territory by notification (✅ Wikisource Part IXB summary). Per *Rajendra N. Shah* para 78, a society with no ramifications beyond one UT is not covered (✅ para 78 read on indiankanoon).
**Learn:**
- Multi-State societies can operate in UTs; the Part reaches them through the multi-State field.
- A society confined within one UT is not blanket-covered (owner, *Rajendra N. Shah*).
- Para 78 treats Art 246(4) as the route for Parliament's power over UTs (✅).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** *Rajendra N. Shah* (2021) (para 78 ✅); Art 243ZR; Multi-State Co-operative Societies Act 2002.
**Trap:** Multi-State is not the same as "in a UT"; a UT-confined society is not automatically a multi-State society.
**Back:** *"ZS: multi-State reach, not a UT-confined blanket."*

### Flashcard Art-243ZT — Article 243ZT
**Front:** Article 243ZT — Continuance of existing laws (✅ corrected heading, Wikisource Part IXB)
**Provision:** The final Article of Part IXB. Inconsistent existing State co-operative laws continue until amended or repealed, or for **one year** from commencement of the 97th Amendment Act, 2011, whichever is earlier (✅ Wikisource Part IXB summary; exact wording ⚠️ unverified).
**Learn:**
- Part IXB's last Article closes the 97th Amendment's co-operative code.
- Continuance is capped at one year unless the law is earlier amended or repealed (✅).
- The heading is confirmed; the rule is transitional rather than a governance rule (✅).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 97th Amendment 2011 (in force 15 February 2012 ✅ Wikisource footnote); *Rajendra N. Shah* (2021) (State-law co-operatives return to State law).
**Trap:** After the 2021 severance, State-field co-operatives are governed by **State law**, not by Part IXB; do not treat 243ZT as reviving the State-field application.
**Back:** *"ZT: the last door; State-field co-ops return to State law."*

### Rajendra N. Shah verdict — Union of India v Rajendra N. Shah (2021)
**Front:** Union of India v Rajendra N. Shah (2021) — the Part IXB verdict
**Provision:** Decided **20 July 2021**; bench of Nariman J. (author), K.M. Joseph J. and B.R. Gavai J. (✅ indiankanoon listing and judgment header). The Court held Part IXB **operative only insofar as it concerns multi-State co-operative societies** (✅ para 80), upholding the High Court's invalidation of Part IXB as applied to co-operatives within a State, because the 97th Amendment touches the **State List (Entry 32)** and was **not ratified** as **Art 368(2)** requires (✅ paras 31–32 framing). The surviving field covers multi-State societies, including those in UTs; a society with no ramifications beyond one UT is not covered (✅ para 78). State-field co-operatives return to State law. The amendment was **not** struck down wholesale (✅ para 80). Art 19(1)(c) and Art 43-B stand (per the Gujarat HC 2013 judgment as recorded; ⚠️ not separately confirmed). ⚠️ The "2:1" split is not verified.
**Learn:**
- The ratification trap: a constitutional amendment touching a State-List subject needs State ratification.
- Severance saved the multi-State field; State-field Part IXB is void.
- Joseph J. agreed that 243ZI–243ZQ and 243ZT are unconstitutional but disagreed on severability of 243ZR and 243ZS (✅ corrected: the card said the dissent would strike the whole Part; indiankanoon para 2 onward and para 28).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 368(2); Entry 32 (State List); Entry 44 (Union List, multi-State corporations); Art 19(1)(c); Art 43-B; 97th Amendment 2011.
**Trap:** Only the **State-co-operative application** fell; do not say the whole 97th Amendment was struck. Do not place co-operatives in the Union List (only multi-State corporations go there under Entry 44).
**Back:** *"Ratify first: a State-list amendment without State consent falls for the State field alone."*

### PART X — The Scheduled and Tribal Areas (Articles 244–244A)

### Flashcard Art-244 — Article 244
**Front:** Article 244 — Administration of Scheduled Areas and Tribal Areas
**Provision:** (1) The provisions of the **Fifth Schedule** shall apply to the administration and control of the Scheduled Areas and Scheduled Tribes in any State **other than** Assam, Meghalaya, Tripura and Mizoram. (2) The provisions of the **Sixth Schedule** shall apply to the administration of the **tribal areas** in the States of Assam, Meghalaya, Tripura and Mizoram.
**Learn:**
- Two regimes: **Fifth** (protective; Governor/President; TAC) for Scheduled Areas in most States; **Sixth** (autonomous district/regional councils) for the four NE States.
- Scheduled Areas exist in **10 States** (Andhra Pradesh, Telangana, Chhattisgarh, Gujarat, Himachal Pradesh, Jharkhand, Madhya Pradesh, Maharashtra, Odisha, Rajasthan) — owner's list.
- Art 244A is a separate route for an autonomous State within Assam.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Fifth Schedule; Sixth Schedule; Art 244A; Art 342 (ST notification by the President); Art 243M (Part IX exclusion); Art 243ZC (Part IXA exclusion); PESA 1996; FRA 2006; *Samatha v State of Andhra Pradesh* (1997) (land/mining-lease transfer to non-tribals barred in Scheduled Areas, owner ⚠️ nationwide reach debated).
**Trap:** **Fifth Schedule = Scheduled Areas** (not Sixth); **Sixth Schedule = tribal areas** (only four NE States). The four NE States are Assam, Meghalaya, Tripura, Mizoram — do not include Nagaland or Sikkim here. The President declares Scheduled Areas; the Governor organises Sixth-Schedule districts.
**Back:** *"A-M-T-M for the Sixth; everyone else gets the Fifth."*

### Flashcard Art-244A — Article 244A
**Front:** Article 244A — Formation of an autonomous State comprising certain tribal areas in Assam and creation of local Legislature or Council of Ministers or both therefor
**Provision:** Parliament may by law form within the State of **Assam** an autonomous State comprising certain tribal areas, with a **local Legislature** or **Council of Ministers**, or both, with such constitution, powers and functions as the law provides; Parliament may make consequential amendments, and such a law is not treated as a constitutional amendment for Art 368 purposes (owner: "autonomous state within Assam with its own legislature/CoM") (✅ clause numbering (1)–(4) confirmed, Wikisource Part X; changes to the (2)(a)–(b) subjects need two-thirds of members present and voting in each House).
**Learn:**
- Assam-specific route: the only constitutional route to an autonomous State within a State short of statehood.
- The local legislature/CoM are created by Parliament's law, not by the Constitution directly.
- Inserted by the **22nd Amendment, 1969** (✅ Lok Sabha passed 15 April 1969; in force 25 Sept 1969, Wikipedia).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 22nd Amendment 1969; Sixth Schedule (Art 244(2)); Art 244 (administration); Art 371B (Assam tribal areas, different route — ⚠️ verify relation, owner Topic 22 owns Art 371).
**Trap:** Art 244A is **Assam-specific**; do not generalise it to Meghalaya or others. Do not confuse with the Sixth-Schedule council route (Art 244(2)). Art 244A autonomy is a **statute-created** legislature, not a Sixth-Schedule district council.
**Back:** *"244A: Assam's own tribal State, by law, not by Sixth Schedule."*

### PART XI — Centre-State Relations (Articles 245–263)

### Flashcard Art-245 — Article 245
**Front:** Article 245 — Extent of laws made by Parliament and by the Legislatures of States
**Provision:** 245(1): Subject to the Constitution, Parliament may make laws for the whole or any part of India; a State legislature may make laws for the whole or any part of the State. 245(2): No law made by Parliament is deemed invalid on the ground that it would have extra-territorial operation.
**Learn:**
- Two-tier territorial reach: Union law = whole of India; State law = State territory, but a State law may still reach outside the State.
- Extra-territorial State law is valid only if there is a real and not illusory connection between the State and the object/person, and the liability is pertinent to that connection.
- The territorial-nexus test concerns reach (competence over the object), not which List the subject falls in.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Territorial-nexus line: *State of Bombay v. R.M.D. Chamarbaugwala* (1957) ⚠️ (from memory; verify citation). Owner: `Centre-State-Relations.md` §8.4. Links: Art 246 (subject allocation), Art 248 (residue).
**Trap:** Art 245(2) protects Union laws only; a State law reaching outside the State is tested by territorial nexus, not by 245(2). Do not confuse territorial nexus with pith and substance.
**Back:** *"Union's map is India; the State's map is the State — and nexus must be real, not illusory."*

### Flashcard Art-246 — Article 246
**Front:** Article 246 — Subject-matter of laws made by Parliament and by the Legislatures of States
**Provision:** 246(1): Parliament has exclusive power over matters in List I (Union List). 246(2): Parliament and State legislatures both may legislate on List III (Concurrent List). 246(3): State legislatures have exclusive power over List II (State List). 246(4): Parliament may legislate for any area not part of a State, notwithstanding the State List.
**Learn:**
- Predominance order: Union List > Concurrent List > State List; the phrase "notwithstanding" in 246(1) and 246(2) signals Union predominance.
- Seventh Schedule standard examination counts: Union List 100 entries, State List 61, Concurrent List 52; residuary power goes to the Centre (Entry 97, List I).
- 42nd Amendment (1976) moved five subjects from State to Concurrent: education, forests, weights and measures, protection of wild animals and birds, administration of justice.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Pith and substance (the law's true nature decides its List) ⚠️ Prafulla Kumar Mukherjee v. Bank of Commerce (1947, Privy Council) — from memory; verify. Art 246A (GST, 101st Amendment); Art 248 (residuary). Owner: `Centre-State-Relations.md` §8.4.
**Trap:** The counts 100/61/52 are standard examination counts; omitted and inserted entries can produce different literal counts. Art 246 says "Union predominance", not "Union supremacy in all cases": harmonious construction comes first.
**Back:** *"Union first, Concurrent second, State last; 'notwithstanding' is the Centre's tiebreaker."*

### Flashcard Art-246A — Article 246A
**Front:** Article 246A — Special provision with respect to goods and services tax
**Provision:** Inserted by the 101st Amendment (2016). 246A(1): Notwithstanding Articles 246 and 254, Parliament and the Legislature of every State have power to make laws with respect to GST levied by the Union or by such State (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XI, Art 246A(1)). 246A(2): Parliament has exclusive power to make laws with respect to GST where the supply of goods or services takes place in the course of inter-State trade or commerce.
**Learn:**
- Creates simultaneous Union and State competence over one tax, breaking the exclusive List design.
- Parliament alone has exclusive power over inter-State supply (IGST field, linked to Art 269A).
- GST is not a Concurrent-List entry; 246A is the special source.
- GST rolled out from 1 July 2017.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** *Union of India v. Mohit Minerals* (2022): GST Council recommendations are persuasive, not binding, because both Parliament and State legislatures have simultaneous power under 246A. Owner: `GST-Council.md` §5.7. Links: Art 269A (IGST), Art 279A (GST Council), Art 366(12A) (definition).
**Trap:** Art 246A is a special provision, not an entry in the Concurrent List. The Council recommends; it does not legislate. Do not number it as "Art 246-A" in a way that confuses it with Art 246.
**Back:** *"Two legislatures, one tax: 246A gives simultaneous power, and 246A(2) keeps inter-State supply for the Union."*

### Flashcard Art-247 — Article 247
**Front:** Article 247 — Power of Parliament to provide for the establishment of certain additional courts
**Provision:** Parliament may by law provide for the establishment of additional courts for the better administration of laws made by Parliament or of existing laws with respect to a matter in the Union List.
**Learn:**
- Lets the Union create extra courts for Union-List laws without a constitutional amendment.
- It is a court-structure power, not a legislative-competence power.
- Contrast with Art 350A/High Court jurisdiction in State-subject matters, which remains with State courts.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Owner: no dedicated owner entry; cross-link to the judicial-structure owners (`Supreme-Court.md`, `High-Court.md`). Art 247 is the Union's court-creation power.
**Trap:** Art 247 applies to Union-List laws and existing laws on Union matters; it does not let Parliament create courts for State-List matters at will.
**Back:** *"Union laws, Union courts: 247 is the Centre's extra bench, not a general court power."*

### Flashcard Art-248 — Article 248
**Front:** Article 248 — Residuary powers of legislation
**Provision:** Subject to Art 246A (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XI), Parliament has exclusive power to make any law with respect to any matter not enumerated in the Concurrent List or the State List, including any tax not mentioned in either List (248(2)). Entry 97, List I, is the residuary entry.
**Learn:**
- Residuary power lies with the Centre, the opposite of the USA and Australia, where the residue stays with the States.
- It covers newly emerging subjects that no List anticipated in 1950.
- Residuary tax power is textual: Entry 97 extends to any tax not in Lists II/III.
- Limit: the residue is genuinely residual; a subject that falls in List II cannot be pulled into Entry 97 merely because the Union wants it.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 101st Amendment (2016) amended Art 248 (per amendment table). Owner: `Centre-State-Relations.md` §8.4 ("single sharpest textual proof of centralised federalism"); `Federal-System.md`. Links: Art 246, Entry 97 List I.
**Trap:** Residuary tax power exists under Art 248(2) and Entry 97, but it is not a general power to tax any State subject. Keep residuary power separate from the "emergency" routes (Arts 249–250).
**Back:** *"Whatever is unlisted falls to the Union: the sharpest textual proof of central design."*

### Flashcard Art-249 — Article 249
**Front:** Article 249 — Power of Parliament to legislate with respect to a matter in the State List in the national interest
**Provision:** If the Rajya Sabha passes a resolution, supported by not less than two-thirds of members present and voting, declaring that it is necessary in the national interest for Parliament to make laws on a State-List matter, Parliament may do so. The resolution remains in force for one year and may be renewed. Such a law ceases to have effect six months after the resolution ceases to be in force, save for things already done.
**Learn:**
- Needs no emergency: only a two-thirds Rajya Sabha resolution.
- Parliament can legislate on a State subject for the period of the resolution (one year, renewable).
- The Union law is not automatically repealed; it lapses six months after the resolution ends.
- Art 249 was amended by the 101st Amendment (2016) to refer to GST.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Owner: `Centre-State-Relations.md` §8.4 ("Art 249 needs only a 2/3 RS resolution, not an emergency"); `Federal-System.md` (unitary features). Compare Art 250 (emergency) and Art 252 (States' request).
**Trap:** Art 249 is the Rajya Sabha route, not Lok Sabha; the threshold is two-thirds of members present and voting, not total membership; no emergency is required.
**Back:** *"Two-thirds of the upper house, one year on, six months off."*

### Flashcard Art-250 — Article 250
**Front:** Article 250 — Power of Parliament to legislate with respect to any matter in the State List if a Proclamation of Emergency is in operation
**Provision:** While a Proclamation of Emergency under Art 352 is in operation, Parliament has power to make laws for the whole or any part of India with respect to any matter in the State List. Such a law ceases to have effect six months after the emergency ceases to operate, save for things already done.
**Learn:**
- Needs a national emergency (Art 352), not a Rajya Sabha resolution.
- Duration is tied to the emergency plus six months.
- Art 250 was amended by the 101st Amendment (2016).
- Emergency-era amendments changed emergency law (42nd Amendment, 1976; 44th Amendment, 1978) but Art 250's basic design remained: a Union power on the State List while the emergency lasts.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Owner: `Centre-State-Relations.md` §8.4 (Art 249 vs 250 distinction). Links: Art 352 (national emergency), Art 251 (inconsistency).
**Trap:** Art 250 operates during a national emergency only; Art 249 does not require one. Do not treat Art 250 as the President's Rule route (that is Art 356).
**Back:** *"Emergency unlocks the State List; six months after the emergency, the law goes quiet."*

### Flashcard Art-251 — Article 251
**Front:** Article 251 — Inconsistency between laws made under Articles 249 and 250 and laws made by the Legislatures of States
**Provision:** Where a law made by Parliament under Arts 249 or 250 is inconsistent with a law made by a State legislature, the Union law prevails and the State law is inoperative to the extent of the inconsistency, while the Union law is in force; the State law revives afterwards.
**Learn:**
- Union law prevails for the duration of the 249/250 power only.
- After the power lapses, the normal Art 254 repugnancy rules apply again.
- This is a time-limited override, not a permanent repeal of the State law.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Owner: no direct owner section; cross-links `Centre-State-Relations.md` §8.4 (repugnancy). Compare Art 254 (Concurrent-List repugnancy).
**Trap:** Art 251 covers 249/250 laws only, not the general Concurrent-List repugnancy rule of Art 254; do not conflate them.
**Back:** *"While the Union holds the State List, the Union wins; when it lets go, the State law returns."*

### Flashcard Art-252 — Article 252
**Front:** Article 252 — Power of Parliament to legislate for two or more States by consent and adoption of such legislation by any other State
**Provision:** If the Legislatures of two or more States resolve that it is desirable for Parliament to regulate a matter in the State List, Parliament may legislate for those States. Any other State may adopt the Act by resolution. Only Parliament may amend or repeal such an Act.
**Learn:**
- Consent route: the affected States' legislatures request, not the Rajya Sabha.
- Adoption route: non-consenting States can later adopt the Central Act by their own resolution.
- The amendment/repeal power stays with Parliament, so a State cannot amend a Central Act it has adopted.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Owner: `Centre-State-Relations.md` §8 (five Parliament State-List routes: R-N-S-I-P). Compare Art 249 (Rajya Sabha) and Art 253 (treaties).
**Trap:** The consent is of the State legislatures, not of the Governors or the Executive; the "States' request" route is distinct from the Rajya Sabha route and from emergency.
**Back:** *"Two or more ask, others may join; only Parliament can change it."*

### Flashcard Art-253 — Article 253
**Front:** Article 253 — Legislation for giving effect to international agreements
**Provision:** Notwithstanding anything in the preceding Articles, Parliament has power to make any law for the whole or any part of India for implementing any treaty, agreement or convention with any other country, or any decision made at any international conference, association or other body.
**Learn:**
- Treaty power extends to State-List subjects.
- Parliament's power on treaties is supported by Union List Entry 14 (entering into treaties and agreements).
- It is the fifth "R-N-S-I-P" route (I = international agreement).
- Use it for environment, trade and human-rights treaties where a State subject is engaged.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Owner: `Centre-State-Relations.md` §8 (R-N-S-I-P mnemonic). The Environment Protection Act, 1986 was passed under Art 253 to implement the decisions of the UN Conference on the Human Environment, Stockholm (June 1972) (✅ Wikipedia, Environment Protection Act, 1986). Compare Art 51 (international peace) and Art 253 in the treaty-implementation debate.
**Trap:** Art 253 gives Parliament the power; it does not make a treaty automatically binding domestic law. Implementation through legislation is needed. Do not confuse Art 253 with Art 260 (foreign territory jurisdiction).
**Back:** *"A treaty tunes the Centre's voice, even over the State List."*

### Flashcard Art-254 — Article 254
**Front:** Article 254 — Inconsistency between laws made by Parliament and laws made by the Legislatures of States
**Provision:** 254(1): If a State law on a Concurrent-List matter is repugnant to a Union law (whether earlier or later), the Union law prevails and the State law is void to the extent of the repugnancy, unless 254(2) applies. 254(2): A State law on a Concurrent-List matter that has been reserved for the President's consideration and has received assent prevails in that State. Parliament may later add to, amend, vary or repeal such a State law.
**Learn:**
- Repugnancy operates on Concurrent-List matters, not on the List allocation itself.
- Union prevails by default (254(1)).
- A President-assented State law (254(2)) prevails in that State only; Parliament can still override it.
- Test: the two laws must be in the same field and cannot be reconciled.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Repugnancy line: ⚠️ *Zaverbhai Amaidas v. State of Bombay* (1954) and *Deep Chand v. State of U.P.* (1959) — from memory; verify holdings. Owner: `Centre-State-Relations.md` §8.4 and §8.0 (2019 demand). Links: Art 246, Art 251, Art 200/201 (assent route for State bills).
**Trap:** 254(2) is the exception only for a President-assented State law; the Governor's assent alone does not bring 254(2) into play. Parliament always retains an override. Do not treat 254 as a rule about the State List.
**Back:** *"Concurrent conflict: the Centre wins, unless the President's nod keeps the State law alive in its own State."*

### Flashcard Art-255 — Article 255
**Front:** Article 255 — Requirements as to recommendations and previous sanctions to be regarded as matters of procedure only
**Provision:** No requirement as to a recommendation or previous sanction (under the Constitution) regarding the introduction or passing of any Act of Parliament or State legislature, or the making of any grant, is to be regarded as a matter of substance, so that its absence cannot invalidate an Act to which the President, the Governor or the Rajpramukh has given assent (✅ corrected: the assent condition in paras (a)–(c) of Art 255 was missing; Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XI).
**Learn:**
- Procedural formalities (recommendation of the Governor or President) can be cured or waived without invalidating the law.
- Protects the validity of legislation against technical lapses.
- Distinguishes procedural requirements from substantive constitutional limits.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Owner: no dedicated owner section; cross-link `Centre-State-Relations.md` §8.4. Links: Art 207 (money bills), Art 274 (taxation bills).
**Trap:** Art 255 does not excuse a lack of legislative competence or a breach of Part III; it saves only procedural formalities.
**Back:** *"Procedure bends, substance holds: a missing recommendation does not void the Act."*

### Flashcard Art-256 — Article 256
**Front:** Article 256 — Obligation of States and of the Union
**Provision:** The executive power of every State must be so exercised as to ensure compliance with the laws made by Parliament and any existing laws applicable in the State. The executive power of the Union extends to giving such directions to a State as may be necessary for that purpose.
**Learn:**
- Compliance duty runs from the State to Central laws.
- The Union may give directions to ensure that compliance.
- Directions are constitutionally bounded; they are not a general takeover.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Owner: `Centre-State-Relations.md` §8.5 (Bank B: "States must comply with Central laws"). Links: Art 257 (further Union directions), Art 365 (non-compliance with directions), Art 356 (President's Rule).
**Trap:** Art 256 imposes a compliance duty and enables directions; it does not itself permit President's Rule. Art 365 is the failure-to-comply route that can support a presidential conclusion under Art 356.
**Back:** *"Obey Parliament's law; the Union may direct, not take over."*

### Flashcard Art-257 — Article 257
**Front:** Article 257 — Control of the Union over States in certain cases
**Provision:** 257(1): The executive power of every State must not impede or prejudice the exercise of the Union's executive power, and the Union may give directions to the State for that purpose. 257(2)–(4): Further specific directions relate to the construction and maintenance of means of communication declared to be of national or military importance, the protection of railways, and related costs and conditions.
**Learn:**
- Non-impediment is the core test (257(1)).
- Specific Union directions cover communications of national or military importance and railways.
- Costs are addressed in the clauses that follow.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Owner: `Centre-State-Relations.md` §8.5 and §8.0 (2020 demand). Links: Art 256, Art 355 (Union's duty), Art 365.
**Trap:** Art 257 directions are limited to the stated subjects; they are not an unlimited Union override over State executive authority.
**Back:** *"Never impede the Union; the Union may direct rail, roads and communications."*

### Flashcard Art-257A — Article 257A
**Front:** Article 257A — Assistance to States by deployment of armed forces or other forces of the Union
**Provision:** **Omitted** by the 44th Amendment (1978; w.e.f. 20 June 1979 — ✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XI). It was inserted by the 42nd Amendment (1976; w.e.f. 3 Jan 1977 — ✅ same source) and allowed the Union, on a State's request or with the President's sanction, to deploy armed forces or other Union forces in a State to deal with a situation.
**Learn:**
- Inserted by the 42nd Amendment (1976) to enable Union deployment in aid of a State.
- Omitted by the 44th Amendment, which restored the position that deployment is governed by Art 355 and the normal executive route.
- Why it matters: the 42nd-44th swing is a classic 'emergency-era amendment reversed' point.
- Current law: deployment of forces in a State now rests on Art 355 (Union's duty) and the normal Union/State command structure, not on a standalone Art 257A.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Amendment table (Wikipedia list): inserted 42nd (1976), removed 44th. Owner: `Centre-State-Relations.md` (Art 355 as Union duty). Compare Art 355.
**Trap:** Do not cite Art 257A as operative law; a "Union deployment" question in the current text is answered by Art 355 and Art 257, not 257A.
**Back:** *"Added in 1976, struck in 1978: the deployment power that the emergency gave and the Constitution took back."*

### Flashcard Art-258 — Article 258
**Front:** Article 258 — Power of the Union to confer powers, etc., on States in certain cases
**Provision:** 258(1): With the consent of a State, the President may entrust functions in relation to any matter to which the executive power of the Union extends to that State or its officers. 258(2): Parliament may confer powers and impose duties on States or their officers, notwithstanding that the matter is not in the State's legislative field. Costs are addressed in 258(3).
**Learn:**
- Consent-based Union-to-State delegation of executive functions.
- Parliament may also confer powers on States directly under 258(2).
- Costs and conditions are dealt with in the following clause.
- Compare Art 258A (reverse route, with consent).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Owner: `Federal-System.md` ("reciprocal delegation", Arts 258–258A). Links: Art 258A, Art 163 (Governor's functions).
**Trap:** Delegation needs the State's consent (258(1)); Parliament's power under 258(2) is a legislative route and need not require the State's consent in the same way. Do not reverse the direction.
**Back:** *"Union hands tasks to a State only with the State's consent."*

### Flashcard Art-258A — Article 258A
**Front:** Article 258A — Power of the States to entrust functions to the Union
**Provision:** The Governor of a State may, with the consent of the Government of India, entrust either conditionally or unconditionally to the Government of India or its officers functions in relation to any matter to which the executive power of the State extends. Inserted by the 7th Amendment (1956).
**Learn:**
- Reverse of Art 258: the State delegates its executive functions to the Union, with Union consent.
- Executive delegation; legislative competence does not move automatically.
- Inserted by the 7th Amendment (1956), the same Amendment that reorganised States.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Amendment table (7th, 1956). Owner: `Federal-System.md` (Arts 258–258A reciprocal delegation). Compare Art 258.
**Trap:** Art 258A is entrustment by the Governor on behalf of the State; it does not transfer the State's legislative power to Parliament.
**Back:** *"The State hands the task to the Union with the Union's consent: 258A is the mirror of 258."*

### Flashcard Art-259 — Article 259
**Front:** Article 259 — Armed Forces in States in Part B of the First Schedule
**Provision:** **Omitted** by the 7th Amendment (1956). It provided for the Armed Forces of the Union to be in the Part B States (as then in the First Schedule), and for the Union's and the Part B State's arrangements on their charges.
**Learn:**
- Part B States were abolished as a category by the 7th Amendment (1956), which removed the rationale for the provision.
- Omitted in the 1956 reorganisation; no longer part of the operative text.
- Current equivalent: Union deployment in States under Art 355 and the normal Union/State structure.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Omission by the 7th Amendment (1956, s.29 and Sch.; w.e.f. 1 Nov 1956) confirmed (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XI). Compare Art 257A (later deployment provision).
**Trap:** Do not present Art 259 as current law; if asked about Union forces in a State, cite Art 355 and Art 257, not 259.
**Back:** *"Part B States gone, armed-force clause gone: 259 is a 1956 relic."*

### Flashcard Art-260 — Article 260
**Front:** Article 260 — Jurisdiction of the Union in relation to territories outside India
**Provision:** The Government of India may, by agreement with the government of any territory outside India, undertake any executive, legislative or judicial functions vested in that government by such agreement, subject to the terms of the agreement and any law of Parliament.
**Learn:**
- Enables the Union to exercise jurisdiction in foreign territories it administers under agreement.
- Based on consent of the foreign government.
- Subject to Parliament's law and the agreement's terms.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Owner: no dedicated owner section. Cross-link `Centre-State-Relations.md` (Part XI scope). Links: Art 253 (treaty implementation).
**Trap:** Art 260 concerns foreign territory jurisdiction; it is not a treaty-implementation power (that is Art 253) and not a Centre–State power.
**Back:** *"Beyond India's border, only by agreement: 260 is the Union's foreign-jurisdiction clause."*

### Flashcard Art-261 — Article 261
**Front:** Article 261 — Public acts, records and judicial proceedings
**Provision:** Full faith and credit shall be given throughout the territory of India to public acts, records and judicial proceedings of the Union and of every State. Parliament may by law prescribe the manner in which they are proved and the effect of judgments.
**Learn:**
- Full faith and credit: public acts, records and judgments are recognised across India.
- Civil judgments are executable across States; criminal and penal judgments are not covered in the same way.
- Parliament prescribes the manner of proof and the effect of judgments.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Owner: `Centre-State-Relations.md` §8.7 (Bank D: "full faith and credit"; "not criminal/penal laws"). Compare Art 262 (river disputes) and Art 263 (Inter-State Council).
**Trap:** Art 261 covers civil judgments and public records; it is not a general power to enforce criminal judgments across States.
**Back:** *"Each State's records and civil decrees travel: full faith and credit, not criminal reach."*

### Flashcard Art-262 — Article 262
**Front:** Article 262 — Adjudication of disputes relating to waters of inter-State rivers or river valleys
**Provision:** 262(1): Parliament may by law provide for the adjudication of any dispute or complaint with respect to the use, distribution or control of the waters of any inter-State river or river valley. 262(2): Parliament may by law provide that neither the Supreme Court nor any other court shall exercise jurisdiction over such a dispute.
**Learn:**
- Parliament can create tribunals for river disputes and exclude the Supreme Court and other courts.
- Statute: Inter-State River Water Disputes Act, 1956 (and the River Boards Act, 1956).
- Example tribunal: Cauvery Water Disputes Tribunal (1990), per owner.
- The 2019 Amendment Bill lapsed with the 17th Lok Sabha; a revised proposal is not current law (owner's status note).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Owner: `Centre-State-Relations.md` §8.7 (Bank D); §8.11 (water disputes & SC). Links: Art 136 (SC appeal), Art 131 (original suits between States).
**Trap:** Art 262 lets Parliament exclude the Supreme Court's jurisdiction over river disputes. Do not say the Constitution bars courts from every water dispute; the bar is by parliamentary law.
**Back:** *"River disputes: a tribunal, and Parliament may close the courthouse door."*

### Flashcard Art-263 — Article 263
**Front:** Article 263 — Provisions as to Inter-State Council
**Provision:** If at any time it appears to the President that the public interest would be served by establishing a council to (a) inquire into and advise on disputes between States; (b) investigate and discuss subjects of common interest between the Union and States; or (c) make recommendations for better coordination, the President may establish such a council by order, defining its duties, organisation and procedure.
**Learn:**
- Set up in 1990 by presidential order on the Sarkaria Commission's recommendation; chaired by the Prime Minister (owner).
- Advisory, not binding; a constitutional provision, but the body itself is created by order.
- Owner notes the council meets rarely; Punchhi Commission (2007–10) recommended a stronger, regularly convened council.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Owner: `Centre-State-Relations.md` §8.7 and §8.8 (Sarkaria, Punchhi); `Federal-System.md`. Compare Zonal Councils (statutory, States Reorganisation Act, 1956) and NITI Aayog (neither constitutional nor statutory).
**Trap:** Art 263 is a constitutional provision; the Inter-State Council body was created by a 1990 executive order. Zonal Councils are statutory (SRA 1956). NITI Aayog is neither. Do not mix the three.
**Back:** *"Council under 263 advises, the PM chairs, and it is the Constitution's offer, not a Constitution-made body."*

### PART XII — Finance, Property, Contracts and Suits (Articles 264–300A)

### Flashcard Art-264 — Article 264
**Front:** Article 264 — Interpretation
**Provision:** In Part XII, unless the context otherwise requires, "Finance Commission" means the Finance Commission constituted under Art 280. Part XII's definitional clause supports the financial and property articles that follow.
**Learn:**
- A short interpretive article; it fixes the meaning of "Finance Commission" for Part XII.
- Keeps the Finance Commission (Art 280) as the single body the financial articles refer to.
- Use it to show that Part XII is a self-contained financial code.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Owner: `Finance-Commission.md` (Art 280 constitution). Links: Art 280, Art 270.
**Trap:** Art 264 is not a power-conferring article; it only defines terms within Part XII.
**Back:** *"Part XII names its Finance Commission: the one that Art 280 creates."*

### Flashcard Art-265 — Article 265
**Front:** Article 265 — Taxes not to be imposed save by authority of law
**Provision:** No tax shall be levied or collected except by authority of law.
**Learn:**
- The core of tax legality: the taxing power must be expressly conferred by law.
- The Concurrent List contains no general tax entry; GST is governed by Art 246A, not by a Concurrent-List tax power.
- Applies to both Union and States.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Owner: `Centre-State-Relations.md` §8.6 (Bank C: "no tax without law"). Case: *Mathuram Agrawal v. State of M.P.* (1999) ⚠️ (from memory; verify holding on the tax-law requirement). Links: Art 246A, Art 276.
**Trap:** Art 265 says a tax needs a law; it does not say which level has the power. The power is found in Lists/Articles (e.g., Art 246A for GST).
**Back:** *"No levy without law: taxing power must be expressly conferred."*

### Flashcard Art-266 — Article 266
**Front:** Article 266 — Consolidated Funds and public accounts of India and of the States
**Provision:** 266(1): All revenues received by the Government of India, loans raised and receipts from loans form the Consolidated Fund of India. 266(2): The same applies to each State as the Consolidated Fund of the State. 266(3): Money withdrawn from the Consolidated Fund only as authorised by law and for purposes specified; other public moneys go to the public account.
**Learn:**
- Consolidated Fund of India (CFI) and Consolidated Fund of each State (CFS) are the single pool of government revenue.
- Withdrawals require appropriation by law (Parliament/State legislature).
- Public account holds other moneys (not appropriated from the CF).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Owner: `Public-Services.md` (UPSC expenses on CFI; SPSC on CFS) and `Finance-Commission.md`. Compare Art 267 (Contingency Fund).
**Trap:** The CFI/CFS is distinct from the Contingency Fund (Art 267) and the public account; money in the public account does not need appropriation in the same way.
**Back:** *"One Consolidated Fund per government; withdrawals only by law."*

### Flashcard Art-267 — Article 267
**Front:** Article 267 — Contingency Fund
**Provision:** 267(1): Parliament may by law establish a Contingency Fund of India into which amounts are paid, the imprest to be placed at the disposal of the executive to meet unforeseen expenditure pending authorisation by law. 267(2): A State legislature may similarly establish a Contingency Fund of the State.
**Learn:**
- The Contingency Fund is an imprest for unforeseen spending before Parliament/legislature authorises it.
- Parliament establishes the CFI contingency fund; the State legislature establishes its own.
- Money is later replenished and the expenditure is authorised by law.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Owner: no dedicated owner section; cross-link `Finance-Commission.md` (the fiscal process). Compare Art 266.
**Trap:** The Contingency Fund is not the Consolidated Fund; spending from it must later be regularised by appropriation.
**Back:** *"Unforeseen spending gets an imprest; the legislature authorises it later."*

### Flashcard Art-268 — Article 268
**Front:** Article 268 — Duties levied by the Union but collected and appropriated by the States
**Provision:** Stamp duties on bills of exchange, cheques, promissory notes and similar instruments are levied by the Union but, where leviable within a State, collected and appropriated by that State (subject to the Article's conditions, including Parliament's power to vary them). The excise-duty words on medicinal and toilet preparations were omitted by the 101st Amendment (2016, s.6; w.e.f. 16 Sept 2016) (✅ corrected: the earlier text listed those excise duties as well; Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XII, Art 268 footnote).
**Learn:**
- Union levies; State collects and keeps the proceeds.
- The leviable items and conditions are as set out in the Article's clauses.
- Parliament may by law provide for the duties to be levied but amounts go to the States.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Owner: `Centre-State-Relations.md` §8.6 (Art 268: "levied by Union, collected/appropriated by States"). Amendment: 101st Amendment (2016, s.6) omitted the medicinal/toilet excise words (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XII).
**Trap:** Art 268 is levy-by-Union but proceeds-to-State; it is not the shared pool (Art 270). Do not treat 268 proceeds as divisible-pool money.
**Back:** *"Union levies, State keeps: 268 is the Union's tax, the State's cash."*

### Flashcard Art-268A — Article 268A
**Front:** Article 268A — Service tax levied by the Union and collected and appropriated by the Union and the States
**Provision:** **Omitted** by the 101st Amendment (2016, s.7; w.e.f. 16 Sept 2016 — ✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XII). Inserted by the 88th Amendment (2003), but that Act was never brought into force (the Wikisource footnote says its date is "yet to be notified"; ✅ corrected: the card implied it operated). It would have authorised the Union to levy service tax, collected by the Union and appropriated by the Union and the States in a manner Parliament provided.
**Learn:**
- Inserted in 2003 (88th Amendment) to give a constitutional basis for a service tax, but never brought into force (✅ corrected: "created the service tax basis in 2003" was wrong).
- Omitted in 2016 when GST subsumed service tax and other indirect taxes.
- Why it matters: it shows how services were brought within a joint tax framework before GST.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Inserted 88th (2003; never enforced), omitted 101st (2016) (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XII, fn 5 and s.7). Owner: `GST-Council.md` §5.7 (101st Amendment). Links: Art 246A, Art 270.
**Trap:** Do not present Art 268A as current law; GST (Art 246A) replaced the service-tax basis.
**Back:** *"Services-tax clause written in 2003, never switched on, dropped in 2016 for GST."*

### Flashcard Art-269 — Article 269
**Front:** Article 269 — Taxes levied and collected by the Union but assigned to the States
**Provision:** Duties and taxes levied and collected by the Union but assigned to the States, including succession and estate duties on property (other than agricultural land), terminal taxes on goods or passengers carried by railway, sea or air, taxes on railway fares and freights, and taxes on newspapers and advertisements, as specified in the Article's clauses. Parliament determines the principles of distribution. The 101st Amendment (2016, s.8) amended the Article to insert the carve-out "except as provided in article 269A" (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XII). ⚠️ The claim that it also removed an inter-State sales-tax element is not confirmed by the footnotes checked.
**Learn:**
- Union collects; the proceeds are assigned to the States.
- Distinct from Art 268 (State collects) and Art 270 (divisible pool).
- Post-101st text: check the current sub-clause list before quoting specific items.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Owner: `Centre-State-Relations.md` §8.6 ("269 (assigned)"). Amendment: 101st (2016) amended 269 (per amendment table). Compare Art 269A (GST inter-State).
**Trap:** Art 269 assigns proceeds of Union-collected taxes; it is not the Art 270 shared pool and cesses under Art 271 are not assigned here.
**Back:** *"Union collects, States get it assigned: 269 is the Union's assignment list."*

### Flashcard Art-269A — Article 269A
**Front:** Article 269A — Goods and services tax on supplies in the course of inter-State trade or commerce
**Provision:** Inserted by the 101st Amendment (2016). GST on supplies in the course of inter-State trade or commerce is levied and collected by the Government of India and apportioned between the Union and the States in the manner Parliament prescribes on the recommendation of the GST Council. Inter-State supply is treated as a Union-controlled tax base.
**Learn:**
- IGST: inter-State GST, levied and collected by the Union, then apportioned.
- Prevents each State from taxing the same inter-State transaction.
- Apportionment principles are set by Parliament on GST Council recommendation.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** *Union of India v. Mohit Minerals* (2022): the IGST on ocean freight on reverse charge was struck down; Council recommendations are persuasive. Owner: `GST-Council.md` §5.4 and §5.7. Links: Art 246A(2), Art 279A.
**Trap:** Do not conflate Art 269A (IGST, inter-State) with Art 246A (GST power). Parliament's exclusive inter-State power in 246A(2) is the legal basis; 269A is the collection and apportionment mechanism.
**Back:** *"Inter-State GST: the Union collects, the States share, the Council recommends."*

### Flashcard Art-270 — Article 270
**Front:** Article 270 — Taxes levied and distributed between the Union and the States
**Provision:** All taxes and duties referred to in the Union List, except duties and taxes referred to in Arts 268 and 269 and the surcharge under Art 271, are levied and collected by the Union and distributed between the Union and the States in the manner provided by Parliament, on the recommendation of the Finance Commission (as substituted by the 80th Amendment, 2000, and amended by the 101st, 2016; the 88th Amendment, 2003, was not enforced — ✅ corrected: the card listed the 88th as operative; Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XII).
**Learn:**
- This is the divisible pool: the net proceeds of Union taxes shared vertically under the Finance Commission's recommendation.
- Vertical devolution (Centre to States) is the Art 270 share.
- Horizontal allocation among States is a further formula from the Finance Commission.
- Cess and surcharge (Art 271) sit outside the divisible pool.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 80th Amendment (2000) substituted Art 270 (tax-devolution scheme); the 88th (2003) change was not enforced; 101st (2016) changed the cross-references to Arts 268, 269 and 269A (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XII). Owner: `Finance-Commission.md` §5.6 and §5.11 (Art 270 pool); 16th FC (Arvind Panagariya; constituted 31 Dec 2023; award period 2026–31 — ✅ Wikipedia, Finance Commission of India). ⚠️ The owner's claim that the 16th FC "retained the 41% vertical share" is unverified: that page gives no share figure, so do not quote it without the Commission's report. Compare Art 275 and 282.
**Trap:** Art 270 taxes are shared; Art 271 surcharges are not. Devolution applies only to the net proceeds in the pool.
**Back:** *"The pool is shared by the Finance Commission: surcharges stay with the Union."*

### Flashcard Art-271 — Article 271
**Front:** Article 271 — Surcharge on certain duties and taxes for purposes of the Union
**Provision:** Parliament may at any time increase any of the duties or taxes referred to in Arts 269 and 270 by a surcharge, and the proceeds of such surcharge are for the purposes of the Union. Amended by the 101st Amendment (2016, s.11): the surcharge power excludes the goods and services tax under Art 246A (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XII).
**Learn:**
- Surcharge is a Union-only revenue: the proceeds go wholly to the Union.
- Not shared through the Finance Commission's divisible pool.
- The surcharge can be raised at any time by Parliament.
- Owner's warning: the rise of cesses and surcharges shrinking the divisible pool is the central fiscal grievance of States.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Owner: `Centre-State-Relations.md` §8.6 ("Art 271 surcharges/cesses stay outside the divisible pool"); `Finance-Commission.md` §5.6. Amendment: 101st (2016).
**Trap:** Surcharge (271) and the shareable tax (270) are different. A cess is not a surcharge in the Constitution, but both are kept outside the pool in practice; state the status carefully.
**Back:** *"Surcharge is the Union's own: it never enters the shared pool."*

### Flashcard Art-272 — Article 272
**Front:** Article 272 — Taxes which are levied and collected by the Union and may be distributed between the Union and the States (✅ corrected: was "Duties and taxes which are to form the Consolidated Fund of India"; Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XII)
**Provision:** **Omitted** by the 80th Amendment (2000, s.4; w.e.f. 9 June 2000 — ✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XII). The original Article addressed certain Union taxes that could be distributed between the Union and the States; its function was taken over by the revised Article 270 (tax-devolution scheme recommended by the Tenth Finance Commission).
**Learn:**
- The 80th Amendment (2000) implemented the Tenth Finance Commission's scheme of tax devolution.
- Art 272 was removed as the revised Art 270 took the field.
- Why it matters: a clean example of an old financial Article replaced by a rules-based FC scheme.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Amendment table: 80th (2000) removed Art 272. Owner: `Centre-State-Relations.md` §8.6 ("80th Amdt (2000): implemented the alternative tax-devolution scheme recommended by the Tenth Finance Commission"). Subject line confirmed (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XII).
**Trap:** Do not cite Art 272 as operative; Art 270 (as amended) governs tax sharing.
**Back:** *"The 2000 scheme replaced 272: Art 270 now shares the tax."*

### Flashcard Art-273 — Article 273
**Front:** Article 273 — Grants in lieu of export duty on jute and jute products
**Provision:** Provides for a grant from the Consolidated Fund of India to the States of Assam, Bihar, Odisha (then Orissa) and West Bengal in lieu of export duty on jute and jute products; the sums are payable until jute export duty ends or ten years from the Constitution's commencement, whichever is earlier (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XII, Art 273; "Orissa" became "Odisha" under the Orissa (Alteration of Name) Act, 2011).
**Learn:**
- A historical transfer that compensated jute-producing States for loss of export-duty share.
- Operates as a grant from the Union to specified States.
- Time-limited: payable until jute export duty ends or ten years from commencement, whichever is earlier (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XII).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Not covered in the owner files; description checked against Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XII (✅). Compare Art 275 (statutory grants).
**Trap:** Art 273 is a specific jute-export compensation, not a general grant power; do not generalise it to all States.
**Back:** *"Jute's export duty, compensated: a named-State grant, not a general one."*

### Flashcard Art-274 — Article 274
**Front:** Article 274 — Prior recommendation of the President required for Bills affecting taxation in which States are interested
**Provision:** A Bill or amendment which imposes or varies any tax or duty in which States are interested, or which varies the principles of distribution or allocation of taxes or grants to States, may not be introduced or moved in either House of Parliament except on the President's recommendation.
**Learn:**
- Protects State interests in tax and grant bills at introduction.
- Recommendation of the President is needed for bills affecting States' tax share or grants.
- A procedural safeguard, but it has substance: introduction is barred without the recommendation.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Owner: no dedicated owner section; cross-link `Finance-Commission.md` (distribution). Compare Art 255 (procedural) and Art 117 (money bills).
**Trap:** Art 274 is a recommendation requirement for introduction; it is not a veto over passage. Money bills (Art 110) have their own route.
**Back:** *"A tax bill touching States needs the President's nod before it is introduced."*

### Flashcard Art-275 — Article 275
**Front:** Article 275 — Grants from the Union to certain States
**Provision:** Parliament may by law make grants-in-aid from the Consolidated Fund of India to such States as are in need of assistance, in such sums as Parliament determines, which are charged on the Consolidated Fund. A proviso enables specific grants for the welfare of Scheduled Tribes and the administration of Scheduled Areas.
**Learn:**
- Statutory grants-in-aid: charged on the Consolidated Fund, on the Finance Commission's recommendation.
- Distinct from Art 282 (discretionary grants).
- Specific-purpose grants for Scheduled Tribes and Scheduled Areas are part of this route.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Owner: `Finance-Commission.md` §5.5 (statutory grants, Art 275) and §5.11 ("Art 275 = statutory grants on FC recommendation; 282 = discretionary"). Compare Art 282.
**Trap:** Art 275 (statutory, FC-based) ≠ Art 282 (discretionary, any public purpose). Do not mix them.
**Back:** *"275 is need-based and statutory; 282 is discretionary."*

### Flashcard Art-276 — Article 276
**Front:** Article 276 — Taxes on professions, trades, callings and employments
**Provision:** Notwithstanding Art 246, no law of a State is invalid on the ground that it imposes or authorises imposition of a tax on professions, trades, callings or employments. 276(2): The total amount payable by any one person in respect of such tax shall not exceed Rs 2,500 per annum (✅ cap raised from Rs 250 by the 60th Amendment, 1988, w.e.f. 20 Dec 1988 — Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XII).
**Learn:**
- A State power, exempt from the normal List allocation (Art 246).
- Annual cap: Rs 2,500 per person (276(2)).
- Used for professional tax in States.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Owner: no dedicated owner section; cross-link `Centre-State-Relations.md` (Art 246 and List II). Compare Art 277 (savings).
**Trap:** The Rs 2,500 cap is per person per annum; do not confuse with the State List entry for taxes on professions. The cap is in the Article itself.
**Back:** *"States may tax the job, up to Rs 2,500 a year."*

### Flashcard Art-277 — Article 277
**Front:** Article 277 — Savings
**Provision:** Any tax, duty, cess or fee which, immediately before the commencement of the Constitution, was being lawfully levied by the Government of a Province or a State, may continue to be levied until Parliament or the appropriate legislature provides otherwise.
**Learn:**
- Continuity saving: pre-Constitution taxes survive until Parliament/legislature changes them.
- Prevents a fiscal vacuum at commencement.
- A transitional provision with continuing practical relevance.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Owner: no dedicated owner section; cross-link `Centre-State-Relations.md` (fiscal continuity). Compare Art 276.
**Trap:** Art 277 saves taxes levied immediately before commencement; it does not create new taxing power for later taxes.
**Back:** *"Pre-1950 taxes carry on until the legislature changes them."*

### Flashcard Art-278 — Article 278
**Front:** Article 278 — Agreement with States (Part B States)
**Provision:** **Omitted** by the 7th Amendment (1956, s.29 and Sch.; w.e.f. 1 Nov 1956 — ✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XII). The Article was titled "Agreement with States in Part B of the First Schedule with regard to certain financial matters" (✅ corrected: the earlier description of it as taking over administration of functions was not its title).
**Learn:**
- Part B States existed in the original First Schedule and were reorganised by the 7th Amendment (1956).
- The Article dealt with agreements on certain financial matters with Part B States.
- Treat the Article as historical: check the official text before stating its status.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Omission and amending Act confirmed (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XII, Art 278 footnote). Compare Art 258 (current consent-based delegation).
**Trap:** Do not present Art 278 as operative; if a question asks about current Union–State functional agreements, the answer is Art 258/258A.
**Back:** *"Part B's old agreement clause: historical, not the live delegation route."*

### Flashcard Art-279 — Article 279
**Front:** Article 279 — Calculation of "net proceeds"
**Provision:** 279(1): "Net proceeds" of a tax means the proceeds less the cost of collection, as certified by the Comptroller and Auditor-General of India. 279(2): The certificate of the CAG is final.
**Learn:**
- Net proceeds is the base for the Art 270 divisible pool: gross collection minus collection cost.
- CAG certifies the figure, and the certificate is final.
- The CAG's role here makes the divisible-pool arithmetic independent of the Government.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Owner: `Finance-Commission.md` §5.12 and §5.11 ("net proceeds" = tax minus cost of collection, certified by the CAG). Links: Art 270, CAG (Art 148).
**Trap:** Net proceeds is not gross tax revenue; collection cost is deducted, and surcharge and cess are outside the pool.
**Back:** *"Net is gross minus collection cost, and the CAG certifies it."*

### Flashcard Art-279A — Article 279A
**Front:** Article 279A — Goods and Services Tax Council
**Provision:** Inserted by the 101st Amendment (2016). The President constitutes a GST Council, chaired by the Union Finance Minister, with the Union Minister of State in charge of Revenue or Finance and the Minister in charge of finance (or nominated Minister) of each State as members. The Council recommends on GST rates, exemptions, model laws, thresholds and related matters. Quorum is one-half of total members; decisions need at least three-fourths of the weighted votes of members present and voting; the Union has one-third of the votes and the States together two-thirds. The States' members choose a Vice-Chairperson.
**Learn:**
- Recommendatory body; its recommendations are persuasive, not binding (Mohit Minerals, 2022).
- Vote weights: Centre 1/3, States 2/3, decision threshold 3/4 of weighted votes present and voting, quorum 1/2 of members.
- The Centre alone can block (it holds 1/3 which exceeds one quarter) but cannot pass a decision without the States.
- Presidential order constituted the Council on 15 September 2016 (owner).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** *Union of India v. Mohit Minerals* (2022). Owner: `GST-Council.md` §5.5 (voting), §5.7 (case), §5.10 (Prelims pairs). Links: Art 246A, Art 269A.
**Trap:** Quorum (1/2) is not the Centre's weight (1/3); the decision threshold is 3/4 of weighted votes, not a simple majority; the Council is constitutional (Art 279A), chaired by the Union Finance Minister, not the PM. GST = 101st Amendment, not the 122nd Bill.
**Back:** *"Centre one-third, States two-thirds, three-quarters to decide: the Council recommends, never commands."*

### Flashcard Art-280 — Article 280
**Front:** Article 280 — Finance Commission
**Provision:** The President constitutes a Finance Commission within two years of the Constitution's commencement and thereafter at the expiration of every fifth year or earlier. It consists of a Chairman and four other members, qualified as Parliament prescribes. It recommends: (a) the distribution of net proceeds of taxes between the Union and the States (vertical); (b) allocation among the States (horizontal); (bb) measures to augment a State's Consolidated Fund for panchayats on the SFC's recommendations (Art 243-I); (c) the same for municipalities (Art 243-Y); (d) principles for grants-in-aid (Art 275); (e) any other matter referred in the interests of sound finance.
**Learn:**
- Constitutional body; "balancing wheel of fiscal federalism" (owner; Laxmikant describes it as quasi-judicial).
- Composition: Chairman + 4 members; members are eligible for reappointment (owner).
- Qualifications prescribed by Parliament (Finance Commission (Miscellaneous Provisions) Act, 1951, per owner).
- Advice is advisory, not binding; rarely rejected in practice.
- 16th Finance Commission (Chair Arvind Panagariya; constituted 31 Dec 2023; award period 2026–31 — ✅ Wikipedia, Finance Commission of India) recommendations cover 2026–31. The owner's statement that the States' vertical share was "retained at 41%" is ⚠️ unverified: no dated source reached gives that figure; verify against the Commission's report before quoting.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Owner: `Finance-Commission.md` §5.4, §5.5, §5.11 (Prelims), §5.12. Links: Art 270, 275, 281, 282, 243-I, 243-Y. Lettering of 280(3) checked (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XII: (bb) inserted by 73rd Amdt 1992; (c) by 74th Amdt 1992; former (c) re-lettered (d)).
**Trap:** FC = Art 280 (constitutional, Chairman + 4, every 5th year); SFC = Art 243-I/243-Y; GST Council = Art 279A; NITI Aayog is neither constitutional nor statutory. Do not say "chairman + 5" or "every year".
**Back:** *"Five-yearly, one chairman and four: the Commission balances, it does not command."*
