### Flashcard Art-281 — Article 281
**Front:** Article 281 — Recommendations of the Finance Commission
**Provision:** The President shall cause every recommendation of the Finance Commission, together with an explanatory memorandum as to the action taken on each recommendation, to be laid before each House of Parliament.
**Learn:**
- Transparency mechanism: the government must publicly report what it accepted and why it departed from the advice.
- Makes advisory recommendations consequential.
- Laid before both Houses, not one.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Owner: `Finance-Commission.md` §5.5 ("Art 281 closes the loop"). Compare Art 323 (UPSC memorandum on non-accepted advice).
**Trap:** Art 281 requires laying and an action-taken memorandum; it does not make the recommendation binding.
**Back:** *"Every FC report to both Houses, with the government's reasons."*

### Flashcard Art-282 — Article 282
**Front:** Article 282 — Expenditure defrayable by the Union or a State out of its revenues
**Provision:** The Union or a State may make any grants for any public purpose, notwithstanding that the purpose is not one with respect to which Parliament or the State legislature may make laws.
**Learn:**
- Discretionary grants for any public purpose: the route for centrally sponsored schemes (CSS).
- Not limited by legislative competence.
- Contested because the Union uses it to fund schemes in State-List subjects, bypassing the Finance Commission (owner).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Owner: `Finance-Commission.md` §5.5 and §5.9; `Centre-State-Relations.md` §8.6 (Art 282 discretion = central leverage). Compare Art 275. Planning Commission era (pre-2014) used discretionary plan transfers that bypassed the FC; NITI Aayog replaced it in 2015 with no fund-allocation role (owner).
**Trap:** Art 282 permits grants outside the legislative field; it does not let the Union legislate on that field. Keep it distinct from Art 275.
**Back:** *"282 is for any public purpose, which is why CSS conditions are contested."*

### Flashcard Art-283 — Article 283
**Front:** Article 283 — Custody, etc., of Consolidated Funds, Contingency Funds and moneys credited to the Public Account
**Provision:** The custody of the Consolidated Fund of India and the Contingency Fund, and the payment of moneys into and out of them, and the custody of public moneys credited to the Public Account, are governed by the law of Parliament; until such law, existing rules and forms apply.
**Learn:**
- Operational custody and payment rules.
- Existing rules continue until Parliament/legislature legislates.
- Mechanism for routine treasury operations.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Owner: no dedicated owner section; cross-link Art 266, Art 267. Links: Art 266(3), Art 267.
**Trap:** Art 283 is about custody and payment, not about the appropriation itself (that is Art 266(3)).
**Back:** *"Custody and payment rules hold until a law replaces them."*

### Flashcard Art-284 — Article 284
**Front:** Article 284 — Custody of suitors' deposits and other moneys received by public servants and courts
**Provision:** Moneys received by or deposited with a court or public servant in connection with judicial proceedings (suitors' deposits and similar moneys) are paid into the public account or such accounts as the law provides; their custody and payment are governed by the law made by Parliament or the State legislature.
**Learn:**
- Protects the money of litigants in court custody.
- Routed through the public accounts structure.
- Custody and payment are legislative matters.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Owner: no dedicated owner section; cross-link Art 283, Art 266.
**Trap:** Art 284 deals with suitors' money and court deposits, not the Consolidated Fund's ordinary revenues.
**Back:** *"Litigants' deposits are protected by the public account, not by the Consolidated Fund."*

### Flashcard Art-285 — Article 285
**Front:** Article 285 — Exemption of property and income of the Union from State taxation
**Provision:** The property of the Union is, save in so far as Parliament may by law otherwise provide, exempt from all taxes imposed by a State or by any authority within a State.
**Learn:**
- Union property is immune from State taxes unless Parliament provides otherwise.
- The exemption protects Union property, not private property used by the Union.
- Parliament may make exceptions by law.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Owner: no dedicated owner section; cross-link `Centre-State-Relations.md` §8.6 (taxing relations). Compare Art 289 (State property exempt from Union tax).
**Trap:** Art 285 is the Union's exemption from State taxes; Art 289 is the reverse (State exempt from Union tax). Keep them distinct.
**Back:** *"Union property: exempt from State taxes, unless Parliament says otherwise."*

### Flashcard Art-286 — Article 286
**Front:** Article 286 — Restrictions as to imposition of tax on the sale or purchase of goods
**Provision:** No State law may impose tax on the sale or purchase of goods where the sale or purchase takes place outside the State or in the course of import into or export out of India (286(1)). Parliament may by law formulate principles for determining when a sale or purchase takes place in the course of inter-State trade or commerce (286(2), as substituted by the 101st Amendment, 2016, s.13(ii); ✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XII fn). Former clause (3), on Parliament's regulation of tax on goods declared essential, was omitted by the 101st Amendment (s.13(iii); ✅ corrected: the card still described it as current).
**Learn:**
- Export and import sales are protected from State tax.
- Inter-State trade sales: after the 101st Amendment, Parliament formulates the principles for when a sale is "in the course of inter-State trade" (286(2)).
- The former clause (3), on goods declared essential, was omitted by the 101st Amendment (2016).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Owner: `Centre-State-Relations.md` (Art 301 bank); 101st Amendment (2016, s.13) substituted cl. (1)–(2) and omitted cl. (3) (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XII fn). Links: Art 301–304, 246A.
**Trap:** Art 286 is a tax-limit article; do not confuse it with the free-trade guarantee of Art 301 (which concerns non-tax restrictions too). After GST, many inter-State sale taxes are subsumed.
**Back:** *"Export and import sales: no State tax; inter-State sale principles are Parliament's to frame."*

### Flashcard Art-287 — Article 287
**Front:** Article 287 — Exemption from taxes on electricity
**Provision:** No law of a State may impose a tax on the consumption or sale of electricity (a) consumed by the Government of India or (b) sold to the Government of India, or consumed by the State in certain circumstances; the Article's clauses specify the exemptions, subject to Parliament's law.
**Learn:**
- Electricity consumed by the Union is exempt from State tax.
- Electricity sold to the Union is also exempt.
- Specific exceptions are set out in the Article.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Owner: no dedicated owner section; cross-link `GST-Council.md` (electricity kept outside GST). Compare Art 288.
**Trap:** Art 287 concerns Union consumption/sale of electricity; Art 288 concerns water and electricity under Union control. Do not merge them.
**Back:** *"Union consumes or buys power: the State cannot tax it."*

### Flashcard Art-288 — Article 288
**Front:** Article 288 — Exemption from taxation by States in respect of water or electricity in certain cases
**Provision:** No State tax may be levied on water or electricity stored, generated, consumed, distributed or sold by any authority established by Parliament for regulating or developing an inter-State river or river valley, or on water/electricity supplied by such authority. Parliament may, by law, make exceptions.
**Learn:**
- Protects Union-established inter-State river authorities from State taxes.
- Covers water and electricity from such projects.
- Parliament may provide exceptions.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Owner: no dedicated owner section; cross-link Art 262 (inter-State rivers). Compare Art 287.
**Trap:** Art 288 is about authorities created by Parliament for inter-State rivers; it is not a general electricity-tax exemption.
**Back:** *"River authorities of Parliament's making: their water and power are untaxed by States."*

### Flashcard Art-289 — Article 289
**Front:** Article 289 — Exemption of property and income of a State from Union taxation
**Provision:** The property and income of a State are exempt from Union taxation, save as Parliament may provide. Parliament may by law provide for Union taxation of a trade or business carried on by or on behalf of a State, or of property connected with it, as specified in the Article.
**Learn:**
- States are exempt from Union tax on their property and income.
- Exception: Union may tax a State's commercial or business enterprises by Parliament's law.
- The reverse of Art 285.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Owner: no dedicated owner section; cross-link Art 285. Compare Art 285 and 289 as a pair of reciprocal exemptions.
**Trap:** The State exemption is subject to the Union's power over commercial enterprises; do not state it as an absolute exemption.
**Back:** *"States are exempt from Union tax, except their commercial enterprises that Parliament chooses to tax."*

### Flashcard Art-290 — Article 290
**Front:** Article 290 — Adjustment in respect of certain expenses and pensions
**Provision:** Adjusts financial contributions between the Union and a State in respect of certain expenses or pensions: the contribution is "as may be agreed"; failing agreement, an arbitrator appointed by the Chief Justice of India decides (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XII; no amendment or sum stated).
**Learn:**
- Administrative accounting between Union and States for shared expenses.
- Concerns expenses of State officers and pensions, as set out in the Article.
- Operates through the Union's and State's financial accounts.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Not covered in the owner files; mechanism checked against Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XII (✅). Compare Art 290A.
**Trap:** Art 290 is an adjustment mechanism, not a shared-tax or grant route.
**Back:** *"Shared costs and pensions get settled: 290 is the bookkeeping clause."*

### Flashcard Art-290A — Article 290A
**Front:** Article 290A — Annual payment to certain Devaswom Funds (Kerala and Tamil Nadu)
**Provision:** Inserted by the 7th Amendment (1956, s.19; ✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XII). It provides fixed annual sums for Devaswom Funds: ₹46,50,000 (forty-six lakhs and fifty thousand rupees) to the Travancore Devaswom Fund (Kerala) and ₹13,50,000 (thirteen lakhs and fifty thousand rupees) to the Devaswom Fund of Tamil Nadu (formerly Madras). ✅ corrected: the card named only Kerala and said the Union pays; the Wikisource page records Kerala and Tamil Nadu as the paying States. ⚠️ Direction of payment to be confirmed against the clause text.
**Learn:**
- State-specific annual sums for temple (Devaswom) funds in Kerala and Tamil Nadu.
- Inserted in 1956 for the reorganised State of Kerala.
- A specific constitutional payment, not a general grant.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Inserted 7th (1956) (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XII). Sums ✅ per Wikisource; ⚠️ verify the payment direction and clause wording against the official text before quoting.
**Trap:** Art 290A names two States (Kerala and Tamil Nadu) for Devaswom Fund payments; do not generalise it to other States or to Art 275.
**Back:** *"Kerala and Tamil Nadu Devaswom sums: named State payments in the Constitution's money chapter."*

### Flashcard Art-291 — Article 291
**Front:** Article 291 — Privy purses
**Provision:** **Omitted** by the 26th Amendment (1971). The original Article provided for sums payable to Rulers of former Indian States (privy purses), which were abolished by the 26th Amendment; the 26th Amendment also omitted Art 362 and inserted Art 363A.
**Learn:**
- Privy purses paid to former princely rulers were abolished by the 26th Amendment (1971).
- Art 363A (inserted by the 26th Amendment) bars courts from enforcing privy-purse payments.
- Why it matters: the abolition shows constitutional change to remove a legacy of the princely order.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Amendment table: 26th (1971) removed Arts 291 and 362; inserted 363A. Compare Art 362 (omitted).
**Trap:** Do not present privy purses as current law; check the 26th Amendment for Art 363A.
**Back:** *"Privy purses gone in 1971: the princes' money clause, abolished."*

### Flashcard Art-292 — Article 292
**Front:** Article 292 — Borrowing by the Government of India
**Provision:** The executive power of the Union extends to borrowing upon the security of the Consolidated Fund of India within such limits as Parliament may from time to time by law fix, and to giving guarantees within such limits.
**Learn:**
- Union borrowing is within limits set by Parliament.
- Secured on the Consolidated Fund of India.
- Guarantees also require limits fixed by law.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Owner: `Centre-State-Relations.md` §8.6 (Art 292 Union borrowing). Compare Art 293 (State borrowing).
**Trap:** Art 292 limits are set by Parliament; they do not depend on the Finance Commission.
**Back:** *"Union borrows within Parliament's limits, secured on the Consolidated Fund."*

### Flashcard Art-293 — Article 293
**Front:** Article 293 — Borrowing by the States
**Provision:** A State may borrow within the territory of India upon the security of its Consolidated Fund within limits fixed by its legislature. The Union may give consent where a State owes a loan from the Union or a guaranteed loan, and such consent may be conditioned as specified in the Article. A State may not borrow outside India.
**Learn:**
- States borrow only within India, not abroad.
- Limits are fixed by the State legislature.
- Union consent is required if a Union loan is outstanding.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Owner: `Centre-State-Relations.md` §8.6 ("States borrow only within India; Union consent if a Central loan outstanding"); `Federal-System.md`. Compare Art 292.
**Trap:** The 'only within India' rule (293(3)) and the Union-consent condition (for an outstanding Union loan) are the two exact limits; do not say consent is always needed.
**Back:** *"States borrow at home, within their legislature's limit, and need the Union's leave while its loan is outstanding."*

### Flashcard Art-294 — Article 294
**Front:** Article 294 — Succession to property, assets, rights, liabilities and obligations in certain cases
**Provision:** As from the commencement of the Constitution, all property and assets of the Dominion of India and of each Governor's Province, and all rights, liabilities and obligations, vest in the Union or the corresponding State as specified in the Article.
**Learn:**
- Transitional continuity at 1950: government property and liabilities passed to the Union or State.
- Dominion of India and Governor's Provinces named.
- Enduring idea: the State is a juristic successor.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Owner: `Rights-and-Liabilities-of-the-Government.md` §3.1. Compare Art 295 (former Indian States).
**Trap:** Art 294 is a transitional continuity provision; it does not create present government liability.
**Back:** *"In 1950 the State inherited the Dominion's assets and debts."*

### Flashcard Art-295 — Article 295
**Front:** Article 295 — Succession to property, assets, rights, liabilities and obligations of former Indian States
**Provision:** Property, assets, rights, liabilities and obligations of former Indian States vest in the Union or the State in accordance with the purpose for which they were held, subject to the Union List connection and any agreement, as specified in the Article.
**Learn:**
- Former princely States' assets follow the purpose (Union or State) they served.
- The Union-List connection is the test for vesting in the Union.
- Transitional continuity, like Art 294.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Owner: `Rights-and-Liabilities-of-the-Government.md` §3.1 ("uses the governmental purpose/Union-List connection"). Compare Art 294.
**Trap:** Art 295 turns on purpose and the List connection, not on the Union or State's convenience.
**Back:** *"Princely property follows its purpose: Union if the Union's List claims it, State otherwise."*

### Flashcard Art-296 — Article 296
**Front:** Article 296 — Property accruing by escheat or lapse or as bona vacantia
**Provision:** Property in a State (within its territory) without a lawful heir (escheat), or accruing by lapse or as bona vacantia, vests in the State. Where the property is in a State and was held for the Union's purposes, or was in the Union's possession and control, the Article's proviso directs vesting in the Union.
**Learn:**
- Escheat: property returns to the State for want of a lawful heir.
- Lapse: accrual to the State when a legal right fails.
- Bona vacantia: ownerless property.
- Location is the general rule; the proviso uses the Union/State purpose.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Owner: `Rights-and-Liabilities-of-the-Government.md` §3.2 (escheat table). Compare Art 297 (maritime).
**Trap:** Escheat/lapse/bona vacantia are the ownerless-property route; do not confuse with the maritime vesting route of Art 297.
**Back:** *"No heir, no owner: the State where the property lies, unless the Union held it."*

### Flashcard Art-297 — Article 297
**Front:** Article 297 — Lands, minerals and other things of value underlying the ocean within territorial waters, etc., vesting in the Union
**Provision:** All lands, minerals and other things of value underlying the ocean within the territorial waters, or the continental shelf, or the exclusive economic zone of India vest in the Union, and all other resources of the EEZ vest in the Union, as specified in the Article.
**Learn:**
- Maritime resources vest in the Union, not the coastal State.
- Covers territorial waters, continental shelf and EEZ.
- Maritime-zone limits are set by parliamentary law, not by the Article's words.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Owner: `Rights-and-Liabilities-of-the-Government.md` §3.3 (maritime zone limits by statute). Art 297 substituted by the 40th Amendment (1976, s.2; w.e.f. 27 May 1976 — ✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XII). Links: Territorial Waters, Continental Shelf and Exclusive Economic Zone Act, 1976 ⚠️ (the statute name is from memory; verify).
**Trap:** The vesting is in the Union; a coastal State's territorial-sea claim does not carry the seabed minerals to the State.
**Back:** *"Under the sea, the Union owns it: territorial waters, shelf and EEZ."*

### Flashcard Art-298 — Article 298
**Front:** Article 298 — Power to carry on trade, etc.
**Provision:** The executive power of the Union and of each State extends to carrying on any trade or business, and to acquiring, holding and disposing of property and making contracts for any purpose. Provisos preserve the relevant legislature's competence where the executive activity falls outside its ordinary legislative field.
**Learn:**
- Executive capacity to trade, hold property and contract.
- Subject to legislative competence limits (the provisos).
- Does not exempt the activity from ordinary law, Fundamental Rights or competition rules.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Owner: `Rights-and-Liabilities-of-the-Government.md` §3.4 (trap: executive capacity is not an exemption). Amendment: 7th (1956) substituted Art 298 (✅ corrected from "inserted"; Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XII, s.20). Compare Art 299 (form of contracts).
**Trap:** Art 298 grants capacity; it does not dispense with law, Fundamental Rights or Art 299 contract form.
**Back:** *"The State may trade and contract, but still under law."*

### Flashcard Art-299 — Article 299
**Front:** Article 299 — Form and execution of contracts
**Provision:** 299(1): Contracts made in exercise of executive power of the Union or a State must be expressed to be made by the President or the Governor, and executed on behalf of the President or Governor, by persons and in the manner directed or authorised by them. 299(2): The President or Governor, and the person executing the contract on their behalf, are not personally liable in respect of contracts or assurances made or given in the exercise of such power.
**Learn:**
- Three formalities: expressed in the name of the President/Governor; executed on their behalf; executed by an authorised person in the authorised manner.
- Personal immunity of the President/Governor and of the official signatory in respect of valid official execution.
- Protects public funds by ensuring authority and form.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** *State of West Bengal v. B.K. Mondal & Sons* (1962): an unenforceable Art 299 contract does not bar an independent Contract Act s.70 restitution claim where a lawful non-gratuitous act was accepted and enjoyed (owner's use of the case). Owner: `Rights-and-Liabilities-of-the-Government.md` §4 and §10.
**Trap:** Personal immunity of the head does not immunise the Union or State from contract liability. Invalid form ≠ automatic restitution; keep the contract claim and restitution claim separate.
**Back:** *"Name, execute, authorise: three formalities, and the head is never personally bound."*

### Flashcard Art-300 — Article 300
**Front:** Article 300 — Suits and proceedings
**Provision:** The Government of India may sue or be sued by the name of the Union of India, and the Government of a State by the name of the State, in matters concerning their respective affairs. Legal proceedings are preserved as they were before the Constitution, subject to legislation.
**Learn:**
- Proper juristic names: "Union of India" and "State".
- Liability continues on the pre-Constitution baseline, subject to any competent parliamentary or State law.
- Procedural provisions (CPC s.79) name the proper party; CPC s.80 requires notice before specified suits.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Owner: `Rights-and-Liabilities-of-the-Government.md` §5 (sovereign-immunity trajectory: P&O Steam Navigation; Kasturi Lal; N. Nagendra Rao (1994); Common Cause (1999); State of A.P. v. Challa Ramkrishna Reddy (2000); Nilabati Behera (1993)). Owner's CPC/BNSS references are procedural, not immunity.
**Trap:** Art 300 names the juristic party and preserves the baseline; it does not give sovereign immunity. The welfare-State cases narrowed broad immunity; do not say sovereign immunity has been abolished without qualification.
**Back:** *"Sue the Union of India or the State by name; immunity has narrowed, not vanished."*

### Flashcard Art-300A — Article 300A
**Front:** Article 300A — Persons not to be deprived of property save by authority of law
**Provision:** No person shall be deprived of his property save by authority of law. Inserted by the 44th Amendment (1978, s.34; Chapter IV in force w.e.f. 20 June 1979 — ✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XII).
**Learn:**
- Protects every person (not only citizens) against deprivation of property without law.
- A constitutional right outside Part III: not a Fundamental Right, and not merely a statutory right.
- Requires authority of law (statute); unsupported executive action fails.
- Compensation is not expressly fixed; the courts require public purpose and non-illusory compensation.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** *K.T. Plantation Pvt. Ltd. v. State of Karnataka* (2011): public purpose; compensation cannot be illusory. *Vidya Devi v. State of Himachal Pradesh* (2020): executive occupation without acquisition and compensation violates 300A. *Kolkata Municipal Corporation v. Bimal Kumar Shah* (2024 INSC 435): seven procedural sub-rights — notice, hearing, reasoned decision, public purpose, restitution or fair compensation, efficient process, conclusion (owner). Owner: `Rights-and-Liabilities-of-the-Government.md` §7.1; `Fundamental-Rights.md` (cross-link). ⚠️ Verify case citations and the seven-sub-right list against the judgment text before quoting.
**Trap:** Art 300A is not a Fundamental Right and not part of the Art 294–300 government-liability sequence; do not cite it for contracts or suits.
**Back:** *"No property taken without law: outside Part III, but not without limits."*

### PART XIII — Trade, Commerce and Intercourse within the Territory of India (Articles 301–307)

### Flashcard Art-301 — Article 301
**Front:** Article 301 — Freedom of trade, commerce and intercourse
**Provision:** Subject to the other provisions of Part XIII, trade, commerce and intercourse throughout the territory of India shall be free.
**Learn:**
- A common-market guarantee: a limit on both Union and State legislative and executive power.
- Not a Fundamental Right and not enforceable under Art 32 as such (owner).
- Subject to the exceptions in Arts 302–305.
- Judicial construction: *Atiabari Tea Co.* (1961) and *Automobile Transport (Rajasthan)* (1962): only laws that directly and immediately restrict movement of trade attract Art 301; regulatory and compensatory measures do not (owner).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** *Jindal Stainless v. State of Haryana* (2016, nine-judge bench): rejected the 'compensatory tax' doctrine; a tax is tested under Art 304(a) non-discrimination (owner, ⚠️ verify holding and bench strength). Owner: `Centre-State-Relations.md` §8.8A (Bank F). Links: Art 302–304, 286, 246A (GST as single-market fix).
**Trap:** Art 301 is not a Fundamental Right and not an Art 32 remedy. Do not confuse it with Art 286 (tax limits) or Art 19(1)(g) (individual freedom to practise trade).
**Back:** *"Free trade across India: a limit on both levels, not a Fundamental Right."*

### Flashcard Art-302 — Article 302
**Front:** Article 302 — Power of Parliament to impose restrictions on trade, commerce and intercourse
**Provision:** Parliament may by law impose such restrictions on the freedom of trade, commerce or intercourse between one State and another, or within any part of India, as may be required in the public interest.
**Learn:**
- Parliament alone may restrict Art 301 freedom, and only in the public interest.
- Restrictions can be inter-State or within a part of India.
- A public-interest standard; it is not a general licence.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Owner: `Centre-State-Relations.md` §8.8A (Art 302). Compare Art 303(2) (scarcity) and Art 304(b) (State restrictions with President's sanction).
**Trap:** Art 302 is Parliament's power, not the States'. State restrictions need the President's previous sanction under Art 304(b).
**Back:** *"Only Parliament restricts free trade, and only in the public interest."*

### Flashcard Art-303 — Article 303
**Front:** Article 303 — Restrictions on the legislative powers of the Union and of the States with regard to trade and commerce
**Provision:** 303(1): Neither Parliament nor a State legislature shall make any law giving, or authorising the giving of, any preference to one State over another, or making, or authorising the making of, any discrimination between one State and another, by virtue of any entry relating to trade, commerce or production in the Lists. 303(2): Nothing in 303(1) prevents Parliament from making such a law as is necessary to deal with a specific scarcity of goods in any part of India.
**Learn:**
- Non-discrimination binds both Parliament and State legislatures (303(1)).
- Preference or discrimination is allowed only where Parliament, to deal with scarcity, enacts such a law (303(2)).
- The scarcity exception is Parliament's alone.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Owner: `Centre-State-Relations.md` §8.8A (Art 303(1) bars both; 303(2) lets Parliament alone deal with scarcity).
**Trap:** Art 303(1) binds State legislatures too; the scarcity exception in 303(2) is for Parliament only. Do not say State legislatures can use the scarcity exception.
**Back:** *"No State preferred or discriminated against, except Parliament in a scarcity."*

### Flashcard Art-304 — Article 304
**Front:** Article 304 — Restrictions on trade, commerce and intercourse among States
**Provision:** Notwithstanding Art 301 or 303(1), a State may by law: (a) impose on goods imported from other States or Union Territories any tax to which similar goods manufactured or produced in that State are subject, provided the tax does not discriminate between imported and domestic goods; (b) impose such reasonable restrictions on trade, commerce or intercourse within the State as may be required in the public interest, provided no such Bill or amendment is introduced or moved in a State legislature without the previous sanction of the President.
**Learn:**
- 304(a): non-discrimination test for State entry taxes on goods from other States.
- 304(b): reasonable public-interest restrictions need the President's previous sanction.
- After Jindal Stainless (2016), 304(a) is the operative test for entry taxes (owner).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Owner: `Centre-State-Relations.md` §8.8A (Art 304(a), 304(b)). Links: Art 301, Art 302, Jindal Stainless (2016).
**Trap:** The President's sanction is for 304(b) restrictions, not for 304(a) taxes; do not merge the two. Entry-tax challenges turn on non-discrimination, not compensation.
**Back:** *"States tax imports only without discrimination; restrictions need the President's nod."*

### Flashcard Art-305 — Article 305
**Front:** Article 305 — Saving for existing laws and laws providing for State monopolies
**Provision:** Nothing in Arts 301 and 303 affects the provisions of any existing law, except in so far as the President may by order otherwise direct (✅ corrected: the President's order power was missing; Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XIII, Art 305, substituted by the 4th Amendment, 1955). The Article also saves laws relating to matters in Art 19(6)(ii) (for example, State trade and monopoly laws), so State monopolies are preserved as the Article provides.
**Learn:**
- Saves existing laws from the free-trade guarantee.
- Preserves State monopolies created by law (for example, State trading monopolies), as the Article allows.
- Saving is for existing laws and monopolies; it is not a general exemption for new restrictions.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Owner: `Centre-State-Relations.md` §8.8A (Bank F), with Art 305 noted in the PART XIII line. Clause wording checked against Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XIII (✅).
**Trap:** Art 305 saves existing laws and State monopolies; new non-discriminatory restrictions still need Art 304 procedure.
**Back:** *"Existing laws and State monopolies are saved from the free-trade guarantee."*

### Flashcard Art-306 — Article 306
**Front:** Article 306 — Power of certain States in Part B of the First Schedule to impose restrictions on trade and commerce (✅ corrected: was "Provisions as to Part B States"; Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XIII)
**Provision:** **Omitted** by the 7th Amendment (1956, s.29 and Sch.; w.e.f. 1 Nov 1956 — ✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XIII). The original Article empowered certain Part B States to impose restrictions on trade and commerce.
**Learn:**
- Part B States were abolished as a category in 1956.
- The provision was tied to the original Part B arrangement and lost its function.
- Current law: Arts 301–305 and 307 govern free trade.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Omission and amending Act confirmed (✅ Wikisource Part XIII). Compare Art 278 and Art 259 (other Part B relics).
**Trap:** Do not present Art 306 as operative; for a current trade question, cite Arts 301–304.
**Back:** *"Part B's trade relic: gone with the 1956 reorganisation."*

### Flashcard Art-307 — Article 307
**Front:** Article 307 — Appointment of authority for carrying out the purposes of Arts 301 to 304
**Provision:** Parliament may by law appoint such authority as it considers appropriate for carrying out the purposes of Arts 301, 302, 303 and 304, and confer on it such powers and duties as necessary.
**Learn:**
- A backstop institution: Parliament may create an authority to enforce Part XIII.
- No such authority has been constituted (owner), so enforcement has been judicial.
- Treat the absence as a design gap, not a scandal.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Owner: `Centre-State-Relations.md` §8.8A (Bank F, Art 307 row). Links: Art 301–304.
**Trap:** Art 307 is permissive; the authority exists only if Parliament creates it.
**Back:** *"Parliament may build a trade authority under 307; none has been built, so the courts enforce."*

### PART XIV — Services under the Union and the States (Articles 308–323)

### Flashcard Art-308 — Article 308
**Front:** Article 308 — Interpretation
**Provision:** In Part XIV, unless the context otherwise requires, "State" and related expressions have the meanings given in the Article, in the 2020 text excluding the State of Jammu and Kashmir (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XIV; the 2019 J&K reorganisation postdates this text, so check the current position before quoting).
**Learn:**
- The definitional clause for Part XIV: it fixes which bodies and services count as "State" for the Part.
- Frames Arts 309–323 (services and PSCs).
- Use it to explain why the 'State' for Part XIV is defined within the Part.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Owner: `Public-Services.md` §5.4 (Part XIV, Arts 308–314). Exclusion wording checked against Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XIV (✅ J&K exclusion in the 2020 text).
**Trap:** Do not transfer the Part XIV definitional scope to other Parts without checking the Article.
**Back:** *"Part XIV's own dictionary: what 'State' means for services."*

### Flashcard Art-309 — Article 309
**Front:** Article 309 — Recruitment and conditions of service of persons serving the Union or a State
**Provision:** Subject to the provisions of the Constitution, Acts of the appropriate legislature may regulate the recruitment and conditions of service of persons appointed to public services and posts in connection with the affairs of the Union or of a State. Until provision is made by such Act, the President (for the Union) or the Governor (for a State) may make rules to regulate these matters.
**Learn:**
- Legislation first; executive rules fill the gap until legislation is made.
- Executive rules are subordinate to any Act and to the other Articles of Part XIV.
- Executive instructions cannot override the Constitution, a statute or valid statutory rules (owner).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Owner: `Public-Services.md` §5.4 (Art 309 hierarchy). Ministries file cross-link (`Ministries-and-Departments-of-Government.md` for Art 309 context).
**Trap:** Art 309 gives executive rule-making only as a stopgap until legislation is made; once an Act exists, rules are subordinate to it.
**Back:** *"Legislation first; the President or Governor makes rules only until the Act comes."*

### Flashcard Art-310 — Article 310
**Front:** Article 310 — Tenure of office of persons serving the Union or a State
**Provision:** Except as expressly provided by the Constitution, a person who is a member of a defence service or of a civil service of the Union, or who holds any civil post under the Union, holds office during the pleasure of the President; a person in a State service holds office during the pleasure of the Governor. Contracts of service may provide otherwise in specified cases.
**Learn:**
- Doctrine of pleasure, inherited from English law.
- The pleasure is not absolute in India: it is subject to Art 311, express constitutional exceptions and judicial review (owner).
- Express exceptions include the constitutional posts with fixed tenure (judges, CAG, CEC, UPSC members) (owner).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Owner: `Public-Services.md` §5.5 (Bank B). Compare Art 311 and Art 317 (PSC removal).
**Trap:** Art 310 is the pleasure rule; Art 311 is the limit. Never state the pleasure as absolute.
**Back:** *"Office at pleasure, but Article 311 fetters the pleasure."*

### Flashcard Art-311 — Article 311
**Front:** Article 311 — Dismissal, removal or reduction in rank of persons employed in civil capacities under the Union or a State
**Provision:** 311(1): No person who is a member of a civil service of the Union or an All-India Service, or holds a civil post, shall be dismissed or removed by an authority subordinate to that by which he was appointed. 311(2): No such person shall be dismissed, removed or reduced in rank except after an inquiry in which he has been informed of the charges and given a reasonable opportunity of being heard in respect of those charges. Second proviso: the inquiry is dispensed with where (a) the person is dismissed on conduct that led to conviction on a criminal charge; (b) the authority is satisfied, for reasons recorded in writing, that inquiry is not reasonably practicable; or (c) the President or Governor is satisfied that in the interest of the security of the State inquiry is not expedient.
**Learn:**
- Two shields: (1) no dismissal by a subordinate authority; (2) inquiry plus reasonable opportunity.
- Three exceptions to the inquiry: conviction; not reasonably practicable (recorded in writing); State security (President/Governor satisfied).
- The 42nd Amendment (1976) removed the second-stage hearing on the proposed penalty (owner; ✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XIV: the first proviso to 311(2) lets the penalty rest on evidence already recorded, with no further hearing on the penalty; 42nd Amendment footnote).
- Protects civil servants and holders of civil posts; defence personnel are excluded (owner).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** *Parshotam Lal Dhingra v. Union of India* (1958): the 'motive vs foundation' test; termination is punitive only where misconduct is the foundation. *Union of India v. Tulsiram Patel* (1985, Constitution Bench): upheld the three exceptions; natural justice excluded to that extent, subject to limited review. *Managing Director, ECIL v. B. Karunakar* (1993, Constitution Bench): adverse inquiry report must be furnished before penalty; relief only on proof of prejudice (owner). Owner: `Public-Services.md` §5.6. ⚠️ Verify citations and holdings against judgment text before quoting.
**Trap:** The 'not reasonably practicable' ground must be recorded in writing; the 42nd Amendment removed only the second-stage hearing, not the inquiry. Do not say Art 311 protects defence personnel.
**Back:** *"Two shields and three exits: no subordinate dismissal, a fair inquiry, unless conviction, impracticability or State security."*

### Flashcard Art-312 — Article 312
**Front:** Article 312 — All-India Services
**Provision:** 312(1): If the Rajya Sabha has declared by a resolution supported by not less than two-thirds of the members present and voting that it is necessary or expedient in the national interest, Parliament may by law create one or more All-India Services common to the Union and the States, including an All-India Judicial Service (not inferior to the post of District Judge, per 312(3)). 312(2): The Indian Administrative Service and the Indian Police Service are deemed to be services created under this Article. The Indian Forest Service was constituted later under the All-India Services framework (owner).
**Learn:**
- Rajya Sabha resolution, two-thirds of members present and voting, national interest.
- Three existing All-India Services: IAS, IPS and IFoS (Indian Forest Service, 1966; not the Indian Foreign Service).
- All-India Judicial Service: enabled, not created; no creating law as per owner's check.
- Recruited by the Centre, serve the States; ultimate control Centre, immediate control State (owner).
- The 42nd Amendment (1976) amended Art 312 (per amendment table).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Owner: `Public-Services.md` §5.7; `Centre-State-Relations.md` §8.5; `High-Court.md` (AIJS). Amendment: 42nd (1976) amended 312.
**Trap:** The resolution is by the Rajya Sabha (not Lok Sabha) and needs two-thirds of present and voting members, not total membership. IFoS is not the Indian Foreign Service. AIJS cannot include posts below District Judge.
**Back:** *"The States' House creates the All-India Service: two-thirds, national interest, Rajya Sabha."*

### Flashcard Art-312A — Article 312A
**Front:** Article 312A — Power of Parliament to vary or revoke conditions of service of officers of certain services
**Provision:** Inserted by the 28th Amendment (1972, w.e.f. 29 Aug 1972 — ✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XIV). Parliament may by law vary or revoke the conditions of service applicable to officers of certain services (the former Indian Civil Service and similar Secretary of State services) appointed before commencement, as set out in the Article. The Article remains in the Constitution.
**Learn:**
- Lets Parliament change service conditions of ex-ICS and similar pre-Independence service officers.
- Inserted in 1972 alongside the repeal of Art 314.
- Historical-completeness value: a 'Part XIV scheme' point rather than everyday law.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Amendment table: 28th (1972) inserted 312A and removed 314. Owner: `Public-Services.md` §5.4 and §5.13 (Art 312A vs 314).
**Trap:** Art 312A is an inserted provision that remains; Art 314 (the original protection) was repealed. Do not mix them.
**Back:** *"28th Amendment, 1972: 312A lets Parliament change the old ICS terms; 314 was struck."*

### Flashcard Art-313 — Article 313
**Front:** Article 313 — Transitional provisions
**Provision:** Until other provision is made under the Constitution, all laws in force immediately before its commencement that are applicable to any public service or post continuing to exist after commencement (as an all-India service or as a service or post under the Union or a State) continue in force so far as consistent with the Constitution (✅ corrected to the Art 313 text, Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XIV; the earlier "civil posts" and "until altered" wording was not the text).
**Learn:**
- A continuity clause: pre-Constitution service conditions continue until superseded.
- Transitional by design: it wound down as later service law replaced it.
- Mainly historical now; cite for a 'Part XIV scheme' question, not as everyday law (owner).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Owner: `Public-Services.md` §5.4 (Art 313 "transitional"). Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XIV shows no amendment to Art 313 (✅); operative status is as in the 2020 text.
**Trap:** Art 313 continues old service law until altered; it does not freeze all service law permanently.
**Back:** *"Old service rules continue until someone alters them."*

### Flashcard Art-314 — Article 314
**Front:** Article 314 — Provisions for protection of existing officers of certain services
**Provision:** **Omitted** by the 28th Amendment (1972). The original Article protected the conditions of service of officers of the former Indian Civil Service and similar services appointed before the Constitution; the 28th Amendment repealed it and inserted Art 312A, giving Parliament power to vary those conditions instead.
**Learn:**
- Original protection for ex-Crown-service officers: conditions could not be varied to their disadvantage.
- Replaced by the 28th Amendment (1972), which gave Parliament power under Art 312A.
- The change moved the position from fixed protection to a parliamentary power to vary.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Amendment table: 28th (1972) removed Art 314. Owner: `Public-Services.md` §5.4 and §5.12 (Art 312A vs 314).
**Trap:** Do not present Art 314 as current protection; the operative clause for ex-ICS terms is Art 312A (Parliament may vary or revoke).
**Back:** *"314 gave the old officers fixed protection; 1972 swapped it for Parliament's power to vary."*

### Flashcard Art-315 — Article 315
**Front:** Article 315 — Public Service Commissions for the Union and for the States
**Provision:** 315(1): There shall be a Union Public Service Commission for the Union and a State Public Service Commission for each State, subject to Art 315(2) on Joint Commissions. 315(2): Parliament may by law provide for the establishment of a Joint Public Service Commission for two or more States, on the requests of the State legislatures concerned. 315(4): The UPSC may, with the Governor's request and the President's approval, serve a State's needs.
**Learn:**
- UPSC for the Union; SPSC for each State; Joint PSC by Parliament's law on States' request (statutory, not constitutional).
- The UPSC may serve a State's needs under 315(4).
- Part XIV, Arts 315–323, is the constitutional home of the PSCs.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Owner: `UPSC-and-SPSC.md` §5.4 and §5.7 (Joint PSC is statutory). Links: Art 316–323.
**Trap:** The Joint PSC is created by Parliament's law at the States' request, not by the Constitution itself; do not call it a constitutional body.
**Back:** *"One UPSC, one SPSC per State, and a statutory Joint PSC if the States ask."*

### Flashcard Art-316 — Article 316
**Front:** Article 316 — Appointment and term of office of members
**Provision:** 316(1): Members of a Public Service Commission are appointed by the President (UPSC and Joint Commission) or the Governor (SPSC). Half of the members must have held office for at least ten years under the Government of India or of a State. The President or Governor may appoint an acting chairman. 316(2): A member holds office for six years from appointment or until age 65 (UPSC) or 62 (SPSC and Joint Commission), whichever is earlier.
**Learn:**
- Appointment: President (UPSC, Joint PSC); Governor (SPSC).
- Strength: not fixed by the Constitution (owner).
- Qualification: one-half must have ten years' government service; no other constitutional qualification.
- Term: six years or age limit (65 UPSC; 62 SPSC/Joint PSC), whichever earlier.
- Reappointment: members are not eligible for a second term in the same office (owner's post-office rule).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Owner: `UPSC-and-SPSC.md` §5.4 and §5.11 (Art 316 terms). Compare Art 317 and Art 319.
**Trap:** SPSC retirement age is 62, not 65; the strength is not fixed; the ten-year rule applies to half the members only.
**Back:** *"Six years, or 65 for the Union and 62 for the States, whichever comes first."*

### Flashcard Art-317 — Article 317
**Front:** Article 317 — Removal and suspension of a member of a Public Service Commission
**Provision:** 317(1): A member of a PSC may be removed by the President only on the ground of misbehaviour, after the Supreme Court, on a reference by the President, has inquired and reported that the member ought to be removed. 317(2): The President may suspend a member pending that inquiry (for a UPSC or Joint Commission member; the Governor for an SPSC member under the corresponding rule). 317(3): A member may be removed by the President on grounds of insolvency, paid outside employment, or infirmity of mind or body. 317(4): A member is deemed guilty of misbehaviour if interested in a government contract.
**Learn:**
- Removal is only by the President; a Governor cannot remove an SPSC member.
- Misbehaviour route: presidential reference; Supreme Court inquiry; SC advice is binding.
- Objective grounds (317(3)) and deemed misbehaviour (317(4)) are separate.
- No resolution of both Houses route for PSC members (owner's trap).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Owner: `UPSC-and-SPSC.md` §5.5 and §5.12. Compare Art 124 (SC judge removal by address).
**Trap:** The SPSC member is appointed by the Governor but removed only by the President; do not say the Governor removes or suspends an SPSC member under 317(1). The Governor's suspension route for an SPSC member is the owner's stated rule.
**Back:** *"Only the President removes a PSC member, and only after the Supreme Court says so for misbehaviour."*

### Flashcard Art-318 — Article 318
**Front:** Article 318 — Power to make regulations as to conditions of service of members and staff of the Commission
**Provision:** In the case of the UPSC, the President may by regulation determine the number of members and their conditions of service, and the number and conditions of service of the staff. In the case of an SPSC, the Governor may do the same. The conditions of service cannot be varied to the disadvantage of a member after appointment.
**Learn:**
- Regulation-making power over commission size, pay and staff: President for UPSC, Governor for SPSC.
- Protection: conditions cannot be altered to a member's disadvantage after appointment.
- Ties to the security of tenure of PSC members.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Owner: `UPSC-and-SPSC.md` §5.5 (Art 318 proviso) and the Composition bullet (President determines number under Art 318).
**Trap:** The number of members is set by regulation, not by the Constitution; Art 318 does not fix the strength.
**Back:** *"The President or Governor sets the commission's size and terms, but not to a member's loss."*

### Flashcard Art-319 — Article 319
**Front:** Article 319 — Prohibition as to the holding of offices by members of Commission on ceasing to be such members
**Provision:** On ceasing to hold office, the Chairman of the UPSC is ineligible for further employment under the Government of India or of a State. An SPSC Chairman is ineligible for further employment under the Government of India or a State, except as Chairman or member of the UPSC or as Chairman of another SPSC. A UPSC member is ineligible for any other employment except as Chairman of the UPSC or of an SPSC. An SPSC member is ineligible for further employment except as Chairman or member of the UPSC or as Chairman of that or another SPSC.
**Learn:**
- Office-specific post-tenure rules (owner's table).
- UPSC Chairman: no further government employment.
- SPSC Chairman: may become UPSC Chairman/member or another SPSC Chairman.
- No reappointment to the same office.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Owner: `UPSC-and-SPSC.md` §5.5 (post-office table). Case: *A.R. Kidwai* ⚠️ (owner: appointment of a retired UPSC Chairman as Governor upheld because a Governor holds a constitutional office, not employment under Art 319; verify the citation and holding before quoting).
**Trap:** The bar is office-specific, not a blanket ban; a Governor is a constitutional office and falls outside the employment bar as the owner records.
**Back:** *"Post-tenure bars depend on the office: the UPSC Chairman gets nothing further."*

### Flashcard Art-320 — Article 320
**Front:** Article 320 — Functions of Public Service Commissions
**Provision:** 320(1): The UPSC (and SPSC) conducts examinations for appointments to the services of the Union (or the State). 320(3): The UPSC (and SPSC) must be consulted on: (a) methods of recruitment; (b) principles for appointments, promotions and transfers; (c) disciplinary matters affecting civil servants and the award of pensions; (d) claims for legal expenses; (e) pension and injury claims. The proviso to 320(3) allows the President or Governor, by regulation, to exclude matters from consultation. 320(4): The UPSC is not consulted on reservations for backward classes, SC/ST claims and Art 335 matters.
**Learn:**
- Core function: examinations and recruitment advice.
- Consultation duty under 320(3) is directory, not mandatory: non-consultation does not by itself invalidate action (owner: *Manbodhan Lal Srivastava*, 1957).
- Exemption regulations under the proviso and 320(4) exclusions are the main limits.
- Advice confers no right to the post (owner).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** *State of U.P. v. Manbodhan Lal Srivastava* (1957): consultation under 320(3)(c) is directory (owner); ⚠️ holding not verified in any fetched source. Owner: `UPSC-and-SPSC.md` §5.6 and §5.12.
**Trap:** The UPSC is an advisory recruiting body in its personnel role; its advice is not binding. Do not say consultation is mandatory and non-consultation void.
**Back:** *"UPSC examines and advises; consultation is directory, so the advice is powerful but not binding."*

### Flashcard Art-321 — Article 321
**Front:** Article 321 — Power to extend functions of Public Service Commissions
**Provision:** An Act of Parliament or of a State legislature may confer additional functions on the UPSC or the SPSC in respect of the services of the Union, the State, or a local authority, a corporation or a public body, as specified in the Article.
**Learn:**
- Parliament (UPSC) or the State legislature (SPSC) may extend functions by law.
- Additional functions may cover local authorities and public bodies.
- A flexible route for expanding PSC roles without a constitutional amendment.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Owner: `UPSC-and-SPSC.md` §5.6 (Art 321 extension). Compare Art 320.
**Trap:** Art 321 extension is by law, not by executive order; the enabling legislature differs for UPSC and SPSC.
**Back:** *"A law can give the Commission more work: Parliament for the UPSC, the legislature for the SPSC."*

### Flashcard Art-322 — Article 322
**Front:** Article 322 — Expenses of Public Service Commissions
**Provision:** The expenses of the UPSC, including salaries, allowances and pensions of its members and staff, are charged on the Consolidated Fund of India. The expenses of an SPSC are charged on the Consolidated Fund of the State.
**Learn:**
- UPSC expenses: Consolidated Fund of India (non-votable).
- SPSC expenses: Consolidated Fund of the State (owner: Art 322).
- Charged expenditure insulates the Commission from annual budget pressure.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Owner: `UPSC-and-SPSC.md` §5.5 and §5.12 (Art 322 finance). Links: Art 266 (Consolidated Fund).
**Trap:** The charged-expenditure rule protects the expenses, not the outcome of the advice; do not confuse it with the Art 319 post-office bar.
**Back:** *"UPSC money comes from the Consolidated Fund of India; SPSC money from the State's fund."*

### Flashcard Art-323 — Article 323
**Front:** Article 323 — Reports of Public Service Commissions
**Provision:** The UPSC must present an annual report to the President on its work, and the President must lay it before each House of Parliament. Where the government has not accepted the Commission's advice, the report must include a memorandum explaining the reasons for non-acceptance. An SPSC reports to the Governor, and the Governor lays the report before the State legislature with a similar memorandum.
**Learn:**
- Annual reporting makes advice publicly accountable.
- The non-acceptance memorandum is the accountability tool: it forces public reasons for departure.
- SPSC: report to the Governor and the State legislature.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Owner: `UPSC-and-SPSC.md` §5.6 (Art 323 memorandum) and §5.10 (verdict: the Art 323 memorandum keeps the advice consequential). Compare Art 281 (FC report and memorandum).
**Trap:** Art 323 does not make the UPSC advice binding; it makes departures public.
**Back:** *"Annual report, and the government must say why it refused the Commission's advice."*

### PART XIVA — Tribunals (Articles 323A–323B)

### Flashcard Art-323A — Article 323A
**Front:** Article 323A — Administrative Tribunals
**Provision:** Inserted by the 42nd Amendment (1976, w.e.f. 3 Jan 1977 — ✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XIVA). Parliament may by law provide for the adjudication or trial by administrative tribunals of disputes and complaints about recruitment and conditions of service of persons appointed to public services and posts under the Union, a State, a local authority or a public corporation. Parliament alone may make such a law; the Article's exclusion-of-jurisdiction clause (323A(2)(d)) is the one that L. Chandra Kumar read down.
**Learn:**
- Subject: recruitment and service conditions of public servants.
- Law-maker: Parliament only, not State legislatures.
- Pattern: one administrative-tribunal field.
- Implementing law: Administrative Tribunals Act, 1985 (creates the Central Administrative Tribunal; enables State and Joint tribunals) (owner).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** *L. Chandra Kumar v. Union of India* (1997, seven-judge bench): judicial review under Arts 226/227 and 32 is part of the basic structure; the exclusion clause in 323A(2)(d) is unconstitutional to that extent; tribunal decisions lie to a High Court Division Bench (owner). *S.P. Sampath Kumar* (1987): tribunals accepted as an effective alternative mechanism (owner). Madras Bar Association (2014): National Tax Tribunal Act struck down (owner). Tribunal Reforms Act, 2021 (owner). Owner: `Administrative-Tribunals.md` (Arts 323A/323B, Bank A). `Public-Services.md` §5.4 (CAT, L. Chandra Kumar).
**Trap:** 323A is Parliament-only. Tribunals supplement, not supplant, the High Courts (L. Chandra Kumar). Do not say tribunals oust the constitutional courts.
**Back:** *"323A: Parliament makes the service tribunal; the High Court keeps its review, as L. Chandra Kumar held."*

### Flashcard Art-323B — Article 323B
**Front:** Article 323B — Tribunals for other matters
**Provision:** Inserted by the 42nd Amendment (1976). Parliament or a State legislature (within its competence) may by law provide for the adjudication or trial by tribunals of disputes on specified matters, including taxation, foreign exchange, import and export, industrial and labour matters, land reforms and ceilings, and elections to legislatures (as specified in the Article). The exclusion-of-jurisdiction clause (323B(3)(d)) is read down in the same way as 323A(2)(d) (owner: L. Chandra Kumar).
**Learn:**
- Subject: specified fields such as tax, foreign exchange, labour, land reform and elections.
- Law-maker: Parliament or a State legislature within its competence.
- Pattern: multiple specified subject fields.
- State tribunals under Art 323B vary in workload and independence (owner).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** *L. Chandra Kumar* (1997) as above. Owner: `Administrative-Tribunals.md` (Snapshot table: 323A vs 323B; Bank A). Compare Art 323A.
**Trap:** 323B is not identical to 323A: it is multi-field and can be made by a State legislature. The 'elections' field is a specified subject, not a general power.
**Back:** *"323B: many fields, Parliament or the State; the same review floor as 323A."*

### PART XV — Elections (Articles 324–329A)

### Flashcard Art-324 — Article 324
**Front:** Article 324 — Superintendence, direction and control of elections to be vested in an Election Commission
**Provision:** The superintendence, direction and control of the preparation of electoral rolls and the conduct of all elections to Parliament, to the State Legislatures, and of the offices of President and Vice-President shall be vested in an Election Commission (EC). The EC consists of the Chief Election Commissioner (CEC) and such number of other Election Commissioners as the President may fix from time to time.
**Learn:**
- The EC is a permanent, independent constitutional body; the CEC and the two ECs have equal powers and decide differences by majority.
- Composition has been three-member (CEC + 2 ECs) since October 1993; the Constitution fixes neither the number nor the term.
- Term is six years or age sixty-five, whichever is earlier (fixed by law, not by the Constitution). CEC is removable like a Supreme Court judge; other ECs and Regional Commissioners only on the CEC's recommendation.
- Powers are administrative (rolls, schedule, symbols, Model Code, cancelling rigged polls), advisory (disqualification of MPs/MLAs on the President's/Governor's reference) and quasi-judicial (party/symbol disputes).
- Art 324 is a reservoir of residual power where the law is silent, subject to natural justice (*Mohinder Singh Gill v. CEC*, 1978).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** *Mohinder Singh Gill v. CEC* (1978): Art 324 as a reservoir of power. *Anoop Baranwal* (2023): interim selection panel of PM, LoP and CJI "until a law is made by Parliament". The *CEC and other ECs (Appointment, Conditions of Service and Term of Office) Act, 2023* replaced the CJI with a Union Cabinet Minister; the constitutional challenge to section 7 remains pending (no later stay or merits verdict located as at 5 Sep 2026 per owner — ⚠️ re-verify). State Election Commissions run panchayat/municipal polls under Arts 243K and 243ZA. Links: Art 325–329; RPA 1950 and 1951.
**Trap:** The ECI does NOT conduct local-body elections (SEC does). The CEC's "like a SC judge" removal protection is stronger than that of the other ECs. The ECI cannot itself disqualify an MP for corrupt practice; that runs through the election petition and Art 103/192.
**Back:** *"Superintend, Direct, Control — Art 324 gives the ECI a plenary field, not a list of powers."*

### Flashcard Art-325 — Article 325
**Front:** Article 325 — No person to be ineligible for inclusion in, or to claim separate electorate for, electoral rolls on grounds of religion, race, caste or sex
**Provision:** There shall be one general electoral roll for every territorial constituency for election to either House of Parliament or to the House/Houses of the State Legislature. No person shall be ineligible for inclusion in any such roll, or claim to be included in a special electoral roll for any constituency, on grounds only of religion, race, caste, sex or any of them.
**Learn:**
- Establishes a single common roll and territorial constituencies — no communal or separate electorates.
- Reservation of seats (Arts 330, 332) identifies constituencies; the whole constituency votes in one common electorate.
- Art 325 is the anti-communal electoral counterpart of Art 15 and the one-roll principle.
- Electoral rolls are prepared under the Representation of the People Act, 1950 (RPA 1950); the ECI supervises under Art 324.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Owner (Election Commission basic file) cross-links Art 325 with Art 326 and the RPA 1950 (seat allotment and rolls). Separate electorates were the 1909/1919/1935 colonial model that Art 325 displaced — ⚠️ historical framing only; not verified in this session. Reserved-seat map: Art 330/332 and the Delimitation Order, 2008 (owner, Parliament/Special Provisions).
**Trap:** Art 325 does not itself confer voting rights (that is Art 326); and reservation of seats (Art 330/332) is NOT the same as separate electorates — the owner stresses the ordinary territorial electorate votes.
**Back:** *"One roll, no caste, no creed, no sex — 325 bans the separate roll."*

### Flashcard Art-326 — Article 326
**Front:** Article 326 — Elections to the House of the People and to the Legislative Assemblies to be on the basis of adult suffrage
**Provision:** The elections to the House of the People and to the Legislative Assembly of every State shall be on the basis of adult suffrage; that is, every person who is a citizen of India and who is not less than eighteen years of age (as on the qualifying date) and is not otherwise disqualified under the Constitution or any law (on non-residence, unsoundness of mind, crime, corrupt or illegal practice) shall be entitled to be registered as a voter.
**Learn:**
- Universal adult franchise without property, literacy or sex qualification.
- The voting age was lowered from 21 to 18 by the 61st Amendment (1988) — not from 1950.
- Disqualifications listed in Art 326 include non-citizenship, unsound mind, crime or corrupt/illegal practice, and non-residence — (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XV: non-residence, unsound mind, crime, corrupt or illegal practice).
- The Election Laws (Amendment) Act, 2021 added four qualifying dates (1 January, 1 April, 1 July, 1 October) in the RPA framework (owner).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 61st Amendment (1988): age 21→18 (owner, Election Commission file). Art 326 (universal adult suffrage) and Art 329 (judicial bar) are the owner's two key anchors. Council of States (RS) and State Councils are indirectly elected and not covered by Art 326's adult-suffrage rule. Election Laws (Amendment) Act, 2021 (owner).
**Trap:** Voting age 18 comes from the 61st Amendment (1988), not from Art 326's original text. Adult suffrage is not the same as "all persons" — citizenship and disqualifications still apply. Art 326 governs LS and State Assemblies, not Rajya Sabha.
**Back:** *"Eighteen is the gate — 326 opens the vote to all adult citizens, and 61st (1988) lowered it."*

### Flashcard Art-327 — Article 327
**Front:** Article 327 — Power of Parliament to make provision with respect to elections to legislatures
**Provision:** Subject to the provisions of the Constitution, Parliament may by law make provision with respect to all matters relating to, or in connection with, the election of members to either House of Parliament or to the House/Houses of the Legislature of a State, including the preparation of electoral rolls, delimitation of constituencies and all other matters necessary for securing the due constitution of such Houses.
**Learn:**
- The Parliament-made law for these elections is the Representation of the People Act, 1950 (rolls, seat allotment, delimitation machinery) and the RPA 1951 (conduct, qualifications/disqualifications, corrupt practices, disputes).
- Art 327 is the source of Parliament's power; Art 328 is the parallel State power for State legislatures in the absence of a Union law.
- The Delimitation Commission's orders have the force of law and are not questioned in any court (Art 329(a)).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 327 → RPA 1950/1951 (owner). Art 328 (State Legislature's power). Art 329 (courts' bar). Art 82 and 170 for the delimitation framework (owner, Special Provisions file: the 1971 Census freeze on allocation).
**Trap:** Do not attribute disqualification or election-petition detail to the RPA 1950; those are in the RPA 1951. Do not confuse Art 327 (Parliament's power) with Art 328 (State power) or with Art 324 (ECI's superintendence).
**Back:** *"327 = Parliament's election law (RPA 1950/1951); 328 = the State's own."*

### Flashcard Art-328 — Article 328
**Front:** Article 328 — Power of the Legislature of a State to make provision with respect to elections to such Legislature
**Provision:** Subject to the provisions of the Constitution, the Legislature of a State may, by law, make provision with respect to all matters relating to, or in connection with, the election of members to the State Legislature, including the preparation of electoral rolls and all other matters necessary for securing the due constitution of that House, in so far as no provision has been made by Parliament under Art 327 (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XV, Art 328).
**Learn:**
- Secondary power: operates only in the gap left by Parliament's law under Art 327.
- Parliamentary law under Art 327 prevails where it covers the field.
- Local-body elections are governed by separate State laws under Part IX/IXA, not by Art 328.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 327 (Parliament), Art 324 (ECI superintends), Art 243K/243ZA (SEC for panchayats/municipalities; owner). State Election Commissions conduct local-body polls.
**Trap:** Art 328 does not give a State power over Parliament's elections. Art 328 is the State's legislative power over election to its own House only, and only where Parliament has not legislated.
**Back:** *"328 = the State writes its own election law only where Parliament is silent."*

### Flashcard Art-329 — Article 329
**Front:** Article 329 — Bar to interference by courts in electoral matters
**Provision:** (a) Notwithstanding anything in the Constitution, the validity of any law relating to the delimitation of constituencies or the allotment of seats to such constituencies, made or purporting to be made under Art 327 or 328, shall not be called in question in any court. (b) No election to either House of Parliament or to the House/Houses of a State Legislature shall be called in question except by an election petition presented to such authority and in such manner as may be provided for by or under any law made by the appropriate Legislature.
**Learn:**
- Clause (a): delimitation laws and orders (including the Delimitation Commission's orders, which have the force of law) cannot be questioned in any court.
- Clause (b): election disputes go only by election petition under the RPA 1951 to the High Court (Sections 80, 80A), with appeal to the Supreme Court under Section 116A.
- *N.P. Ponnuswami v. Returning Officer* (1952): Art 329(b) is an exclusive bar on judicial interference midway through the poll — no writ can stop or correct the process during the election.
- *Indira Nehru Gandhi v. Raj Narain* (1975): free and fair elections and judicial review are basic features; Art 329A as struck down.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Ponnuswami (1952); Indira Nehru Gandhi v. Raj Narain (1975); Art 329A (omitted); RPA 1951 ss. 80–100, 116A (owner's Election Commission file, Bank D).
**Trap:** Art 329(a) bars challenges to delimitation; it does not bar challenges to election results — those go by petition under 329(b). Do not say courts can never intervene in elections: post-poll petitions and the basic-structure review remain open.
**Back:** *"329(a): don't question the map. 329(b): don't stop the poll — petition afterwards."*

### Flashcard Art-329A — Article 329A (Omitted)
**Front:** Article 329A — Special provision as to elections to Parliament in the case of Prime Minister and Speaker (omitted)
**Provision:** **Omitted** by the 44th Amendment (1978, s.36, w.e.f. 20 Jun 1979 ✅ Wikisource Part XV). Inserted by the 39th Amendment (1975; insertion footnote ⚠️ not checked), it sought to place the election of the Prime Minister and the Speaker beyond the reach of ordinary election-petition scrutiny by providing that such elections could not be challenged in the courts except before a body set up by Parliament.
**Learn:**
- Inserted during the 1975 Emergency so that the then Prime Minister's election could not be voided by the Allahabad High Court's ruling in the Raj Narain case.
- Struck down by the Supreme Court in *Indira Nehru Gandhi v. Raj Narain* (1975) as violating free and fair elections and judicial review (basic structure).
- The 44th Amendment removed Art 329A, and the Article now survives only as a historical lesson — the number is kept as an omitted slot.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 39th Amendment (1975) inserted; Indira Nehru Gandhi v. Raj Narain (1975) struck it down; 44th Amendment (1978) omitted it (brief). Art 329(b) and Art 329 generally (owner).
**Trap:** Do not say 329A was "struck down by the 44th Amendment" — the Supreme Court struck it down in 1975; the 44th Amendment formally omitted it. The Court case rested on basic structure, not on the 44th Amendment.
**Back:** *"39th tried to shield the PM's seat, Raj Narain struck it, the 44th deleted it."*

### PART XVI — Special Provisions Relating to Certain Classes (Articles 330–342A)

### Flashcard Art-330 — Article 330
**Front:** Article 330 — Reservation of seats for Scheduled Castes and Scheduled Tribes in the House of the People
**Provision:** Seats shall be reserved in the House of the People (Lok Sabha) for (a) the Scheduled Castes and (b) the Scheduled Tribes, except the STs of the autonomous districts of Assam. The number reserved shall bear, as nearly as may be, the same proportion to the total number of seats as the population of such castes or tribes in the State or UT bears to the total population.
**Learn:**
- Reservation identifies constituencies/seats; it does not create a separate electorate. The whole territorial constituency votes in the ordinary election.
- The proportion is population-linked; seat numbers are reset at each delimitation (Art 82 and 170 framework).
- Current delimitation boundary: allocation/total seats still rests on the 1971 Census; constituencies may be readjusted on 2001 Census basis; the Delimitation Order, 2008 supplies the reserved-seat map in the covered States/UTs (owner).
- Seat reservation in LS for SC/ST is time-limited by Art 334 (80 years, ending 25 January 2030 per owner, subject to the existing-House proviso).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 334 (time limit); Art 330A (women within SC/ST seats, 106th Amendment); Art 332 (State Assemblies); Art 341–342 (lists); Delimitation Order, 2008 (owner).
**Trap:** SC/ST reservation in LS (Art 330) is not the same as reservation in Rajya Sabha (none exists) or in State Councils (none). Art 330 is a seat-reservation clause, not a service quota (that is Art 335/16).
**Back:** *"330: population-proportioned LS seats for SC/ST — seats, not separate voter-rolls."*

### Flashcard Art-330A — Article 330A
**Front:** Article 330A — Reservation of seats for women in the House of the People
**Provision:** Inserted by the 106th Amendment (2023): seats shall be reserved for women in the House of the People (Lok Sabha) — one-third of total seats, including one-third within the seats reserved for Scheduled Castes and Scheduled Tribes. The reservation applies on the commencement of the amendment, but its electoral operation is gated by Art 334A.
**Learn:**
- Women's reservation is in LS, State Assemblies and the Delhi Legislative Assembly (Art 332A for States/NCT). It does NOT extend to Rajya Sabha or State Legislative Councils (owner).
- Commencement (owner: S.O. 1922(E), dated 16 April 2026, under s.1(2) of the Amendment Act; date ✅ Wikipedia "Nari Shakti Vandan Adhiniyam" citing Deccan Herald, 16 Apr 2026; S.O. number ⚠️ unverified) is distinct from operation: Art 334A requires publication of the first post-commencement census figures and a purpose-specific delimitation before the seats operate.
- Census 2027 notified 16 June 2025, reference date 1 March 2027 (owner). As at owner's check date (5 Sep 2026), the figures have not been published.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 106th Amendment (2023); Art 332A; Art 334A; Art 82/170 delimitation; Parliament.md owner (women's quota dates).
**Trap:** "Enacted", "commenced" and "operational" are three different legal events. Do not compute a fifteen-year end-date; quote "fifteen years from such commencement" per Art 334A. Do not give an election year for the quota.
**Back:** *"330A: one-third of LS seats for women — passed in 2023, operates only after the census and delimitation."*

### Flashcard Art-331 — Article 331
**Front:** Article 331 — Representation of the Anglo-Indian community in the House of the People
**Provision:** If the President is of the opinion that the Anglo-Indian community is not adequately represented in the House of the People, he may nominate not more than two members of that community to the House of the People (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XVI, Art 331).
**Learn:**
- Nomination, not election; the nominees are members of the House.
- Art 331 remains printed in the Constitution, but the special nomination period was governed by Art 334 (time limit) and was not extended beyond seventy years (owner).
- The owner records that Anglo-Indian nomination ceased from 25 January 2020.
- Parliament's Anglo-Indian nomination was never a reservation of seats in the elective sense.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 334 (time limit; 104th Amendment extended SC/ST seats to eighty years but not Anglo-Indian nomination — owner). Art 333 (State Assembly equivalent). Art 366(2) defines Anglo-Indian (owner: ⚠️ not checked this session).
**Trap:** "Article 331 omitted" is wrong. The Article still stands in the text; the nomination power has lapsed through the Art 334 time clause. Do not say the Anglo-Indian nomination was extended in 2019.
**Back:** *"331: two Anglo-Indian nominees to LS — the text remains, the time clause ended it in 2020."*

### Flashcard Art-332 — Article 332
**Front:** Article 332 — Reservation of seats for Scheduled Castes and Scheduled Tribes in the Legislative Assemblies of the States
**Provision:** Seats shall be reserved in the Legislative Assembly of every State for (a) SCs and (b) STs (except STs of autonomous districts of Assam), in proportion to the population of such castes/tribes in the State relative to the total population. Special provisions apply to the Assam autonomous districts and the North-East (owner: "special Assam/NE provisions").
**Learn:**
- Same architecture as Art 330 for the State level: population-linked, constituency-based, not a separate electorate.
- Art 334 time limit applies (80 years, to 25 January 2030 per owner, subject to the existing-House proviso).
- Art 332(2) reserves seats for the Assam autonomous districts; 332(3A) and (3B) give interim rules for Arunachal Pradesh, Meghalaya, Mizoram, Nagaland and Tripura; 332(4)–(6) set the Assam district minimum and constituency rules (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XVI; corrected: was "332(3) and (4)").
- The 104th Amendment (2019) extended the seat-reservation period.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 104th Amendment (2019); Art 332A (women in State Assemblies); Art 330 (LS); Art 334; Fifth/Sixth Schedules (owner's Special Provisions file: Art 244).
**Trap:** Art 332 does not reserve seats in State Legislative Councils. Assam's autonomous-district arrangements are in Art 332 but the Sixth Schedule is separate (Art 244(2)).
**Back:** *"332: SC/ST seats in State Assemblies — the State version of 330, with Assam's districts carved out."*

### Flashcard Art-332A — Article 332A
**Front:** Article 332A — Reservation of seats for women in the Legislative Assemblies of the States
**Provision:** Inserted by the 106th Amendment (2023): one-third of seats in every State Legislative Assembly and in the Legislative Assembly of the National Capital Territory of Delhi shall be reserved for women, including one-third within the seats reserved for SCs and STs. Electoral operation awaits Art 334A.
**Learn:**
- Coverage: State Legislative Assemblies and the Delhi Assembly; NOT the Rajya Sabha or State Legislative Councils.
- Same commencement-versus-operation gate as Art 330A.
- The Delhi reference is an NCT Assembly clause (owner).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 106th Amendment (2023); Art 330A; Art 334A; Art 332; Parliament.md (quota timeline — owner).
**Trap:** Do not say the women's reservation applies to the Rajya Sabha. Do not say it is operational because the amendment has commenced.
**Back:** *"332A: one-third of State and Delhi Assembly seats for women — same gate as 330A."*

### Flashcard Art-333 — Article 333
**Front:** Article 333 — Representation of the Anglo-Indian community in the Legislative Assemblies of the States
**Provision:** If the Governor is of the opinion that the Anglo-Indian community is not adequately represented in the State's Legislative Assembly, he may nominate one member of that community to the Assembly (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XVI, Art 333; corrected: was "not more than one").
**Learn:**
- The State-level counterpart of Art 331; nomination by the Governor on the Governor's own opinion.
- Same time-limit issue under Art 334 — owner: nomination ceased from 25 January 2020 (seventy-year period).
- The text remains printed; its operation ended through the time clause.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 331; Art 334; 104th Amendment (2019) did not extend Anglo-Indian nomination (owner).
**Trap:** "Article 333 omitted" is wrong; Anglo-Indian nomination has lapsed by time limit, not by repeal.
**Back:** *"333: the Governor's one Anglo-Indian nominee — the State mirror of 331, lapsed in 2020."*

### Flashcard Art-334 — Article 334
**Front:** Article 334 — Reservation of seats and special representation to cease after a fixed period
**Provision:** The provisions of Arts 330 and 332 (SC/ST seats in LS and State Assemblies) and Art 331/333 (Anglo-Indian nomination) shall cease to have effect on the expiration of the period specified (as amended). Under the 104th Amendment (2019), the SC/ST seat-reservation period was extended to eighty years from commencement, while Anglo-Indian nomination was not extended beyond seventy years. Proviso: existing Houses are unaffected until dissolution.
**Learn:**
- SC/ST seat reservation now runs to 25 January 2030 (owner), subject to the existing-House proviso.
- Anglo-Indian nomination ceased on 25 January 2020 (owner).
