- Art 334 is the time-limit clause; it is separate from Art 334A (women).
- Art 334 expiry does not repeal the text of Arts 330–333; it terminates their operation.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 104th Amendment (2019; effective 2020 per owner); 106th Amendment inserted Art 334A; Parliament.md and the owner's "Article 334 clocks". Do not compute a new end-date beyond what the owner states.
**Trap:** Art 334's expiry clock is different for SC/ST (80 years) and Anglo-Indians (70 years). The 2030 date is an owner-verified statement; "subject to the existing-House proviso" matters.
**Back:** *"334: SC/ST seats to 80 years (2030), Anglo-Indian nominees to 70 years (2020)."*

### Flashcard Art-334A — Article 334A
**Front:** Article 334A — Reservation of seats for women: commencement and duration (inserted by the 106th Amendment)
**Provision:** Inserted by the 106th Amendment (2023): the provisions of Arts 330A, 332A and this Article shall come into effect on the date notified (commencement) — but the reservation shall take effect only after (a) the first census after commencement, (b) publication of the relevant figures, and (c) delimitation undertaken for this purpose. Parliament may by law determine continuation of the reservation beyond fifteen years from commencement and may determine rotation of seats at each subsequent delimitation. Existing Houses are not affected until dissolution.
**Learn:**
- Five distinct legal events: enactment → commencement notification → census publication → delimitation → electoral operation.
- Owner's check (5 Sep 2026): commencement notification S.O. 1922(E), 16 April 2026 under s.1(2) — gazette date 16 Apr 2026 ✅ (Wikipedia, citing Deccan Herald); S.O. 1922(E) number ⚠️ unverified; re-check the Gazette before quoting.
- Census 2027: general reference date 1 March 2027; snow-bound areas 1 October 2026 (owner). Population figures not yet published at owner's check date.
- Census publication alone does not draw or rotate constituencies.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 106th Amendment (2023); Arts 330A, 332A; Arts 82/170 (delimitation); Census Act 1948 (⚠️ statutory basis not re-verified here); Parliament.md (quota timeline).
**Trap:** Never say "women's reservation applies from the 2024 Lok Sabha election" or any specific election year. Do not compute "fifteen years" as a calendar end-date.
**Back:** *"334A: enacted, commenced, counted, delimited, operated — five gates, not one."*

### Flashcard Art-335 — Article 335
**Front:** Article 335 — Claims of Scheduled Castes and Scheduled Tribes to services and posts
**Provision:** The claims of members of the SCs and STs shall be taken into consideration, consistently with the maintenance of efficiency of administration, in the making of appointments to services and posts in connection with the affairs of the Union or of a State. Proviso (inserted by the 82nd Amendment, 2000, w.e.f. 6 Sep 2000 — ✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XVI): nothing shall prevent provision for relaxation of qualifying marks in any examination or lowering of evaluation standards for reservation in promotion.
**Learn:**
- A consideration-and-efficiency clause, not a fixed percentage quota; the source of service reservation is Arts 16(4), 16(4A), 16(4B), and case law.
- Efficiency is integrated into substantive equality; it does not automatically defeat reservation.
- Promotion reservation doctrine (*M. Nagaraj*, 2006; *Jarnail Singh*, 2018) belongs to Fundamental Rights.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 82nd Amendment (proviso for relaxation); Arts 15(4), 16(4), 16(4A), 16(4B); *M. Nagaraj v. Union of India* (2006); *Jarnail Singh v. Lachhmi Narain Gupta* (2018) (owner, Special Provisions file §6.3).
**Trap:** "Art 335 gives a 15% or 7.5% quota" is wrong; it gives an efficiency consideration. Art 335 is SC/ST only — it is not the source of OBC or EWS reservation (EWS: Arts 15(6)/16(6)).
**Back:** *"335: consider SC/ST claims without breaking efficiency — a consideration clause, not a quota."*

### Flashcard Art-336 — Article 336
**Front:** Article 336 — Special provision for Anglo-Indian community in certain services (transitional)
**Provision:** Transitional provision (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XVI, Art 336: first two years on the pre-15 Aug 1947 basis, then about a 10% reduction each two-year period; all reservations end at ten years from commencement): for a limited initial period after commencement, a fixed proportion of appointments in the Railways, Customs, Postal and Telegraph services and similar Union services were reserved for the Anglo-Indian community. The clause is time-limited and has expired.
**Learn:**
- A temporary, transitional guarantee tied to a specific period; it has no operative force now.
- Ended at ten years from commencement (✅ Art 336 proviso, Wikisource Part XVI); the ten-year window and the reduction schedule are confirmed.
- Art 336 is classified in the owner as a time-bound/transitional provision, alongside Art 337.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 337 (educational grants); Art 334 (time limit for Anglo-Indian nomination); owner Special Provisions §7 (time-bound axis).
**Trap:** "Art 336 is still in force" is wrong. Do not confuse it with the Art 331/333 nomination power, which is a different Anglo-Indian mechanism.
**Back:** *"336: a ten-year Anglo-Indian services bridge — spent, not in force."*

### Flashcard Art-337 — Article 337
**Front:** Article 337 — Special provision with respect to educational grants for the Anglo-Indian community (transitional)
**Provision:** During the first three financial years after commencement, and thereafter on a progressively reducing scale for the period specified, grants in aid of education of the Anglo-Indian community shall continue to be made by the State as they were made in 1947–48 (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XVI, Art 337: first three financial years on the FY-1947–48 basis; later three-year periods may be ten per cent lower; grants cease at ten years from commencement; a 40 per cent open-admissions proviso applies to institutions).
**Learn:**
- A financial-transition guarantee for Anglo-Indian educational institutions, not a permanent right.
- Expired by its own time clause.
- Pairs thematically with Art 336 (services) as one of the two Anglo-Indian transitional guarantees.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 336; Art 30(1) (minority educational institutions) — ⚠️ general cross-reference only; owner Special Provisions §7 (time-bound axis).
**Trap:** Do not treat Art 337 as an ongoing grant right; do not conflate it with minority educational rights under Art 30(1).
**Back:** *"337: a dwindling education-grant bridge for Anglo-Indians — spent."*

### Flashcard Art-338 — Article 338
**Front:** Article 338 — National Commission for Scheduled Castes
**Provision:** There shall be a National Commission for the Scheduled Castes consisting of a Chairperson, a Vice-Chairperson and three other members, appointed by the President by warrant under hand and seal. The Commission investigates and monitors all matters relating to the safeguards for SCs under the Constitution or other laws; inquires into specific complaints; advises on socio-economic development and participates in planning; reports to the President annually and on other occasions; and performs other functions the President may specify by law.
**Learn:**
- Composition: Chairperson + Vice-Chairperson + 3 members (owner; not "chairman + 5").
- Civil-court powers while investigating (summon, enforce attendance, require documents, receive affidavits).
- Reports go to the President → laid before Parliament with action-taken memorandum; State-related matter through the Governor to the State legislature.
- Recommendations are advisory, not binding decrees.
- The NCSC was multi-member since the 65th Amendment (1990) and was split from the NCST by the 89th Amendment (2003), with the NCST existing from 2004.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 65th Amendment (1990); 89th Amendment (2003); Art 338A (NCST); Art 338B (NCBC); Art 15(5) and Art 30(1) (minority institutions limit; owner's *T.M.A. Pai* (2002), *P.A. Inamdar* (2005)). Owner: NCSC cannot mandate reservation in minority institutions. NCSC 2018 and 2020 demands (owner).
**Trap:** The NCSC has advisory, not adjudicatory, power; "civil-court powers" do not make its recommendations binding. Do not say the NCSC was created by the 89th Amendment without its split (split effect 2004).
**Back:** *"338: the SC watchdog — investigate, inquire, advise, report; never adjudicate."*

### Flashcard Art-338A — Article 338A
**Front:** Article 338A — National Commission for Scheduled Tribes
**Provision:** There shall be a National Commission for the Scheduled Tribes with the same structure as the NCSC: a Chairperson, Vice-Chairperson and three members appointed by the President. It investigates and monitors safeguards for STs, inquires into complaints, advises on development and planning, and reports to the President, with the tribal-specific mandate that the NCSC lacks.
**Learn:**
- Inserted by the 89th Amendment (2003); NCST came into existence in 2004 (owner).
- Tribal-specific duties (owner): forest and mining issues, minor forest produce, PESA/FRA, displacement and tribal land (owner §5.8 and §8).
- Same constitutional-commission design as Art 338: civil-court powers, President's report route, advisory force.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 89th Amendment (2003); Art 338; Art 339 (Union control over Scheduled Areas and ST welfare); Fifth and Sixth Schedules (Art 244; owner's Scheduled Areas file).
**Trap:** Do not claim the NCST is "statutory" like the NCW. Do not say the NCST's recommendations bind the State government — they are advisory.
**Back:** *"338A: the ST twin of 338 — same powers, tribal duties added in 2004."*

### Flashcard Art-338B — Article 338B
**Front:** Article 338B — National Commission for Backward Classes
**Provision:** Inserted by the 102nd Amendment (2018): a National Commission for the Socially and Educationally Backward Classes, with a Chairperson, Vice-Chairperson and three members appointed by the President. It investigates and monitors safeguards for SEBCs, advises the Union and States on SEBC development, consultation on major SEBC policy is mandatory for the Centre and States (owner: advice not binding), and reports to the President.
**Learn:**
- Statutory from 1993 (NCBC Act 1993) until the 102nd Amendment (2018) constitutionalised it.
- Same design as 338/338A: civil-court powers, President's report route, advisory force.
- Works with Art 342A (President specifies SEBCs; 105th Amendment restored State/UT own-purpose lists).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 102nd Amendment (2018); Art 342A; 105th Amendment (2021); *Jaishri Laxmanrao Patil v. CM Maharashtra* (2021) read Art 342A as centralising SEBC identification (owner); *Indra Sawhney* (1992) origin (owner).
**Trap:** NCBC is constitutional since 2018 — not since 1993. Art 340 commissions are temporary; the NCBC (338B) is standing. Do not say the 102nd Amendment created an EWS quota (it did not).
**Back:** *"338B: the backward-classes watchdog — constitutional since 2018, advisory still."*

### Flashcard Art-339 — Article 339
**Front:** Article 339 — Control of the Union over the administration of Scheduled Areas and the welfare of Scheduled Tribes
**Provision:** The President may at any time appoint a commission to report on the administration of Scheduled Areas and the welfare of Scheduled Tribes in a State (and must do so once ten years have passed from the commencement of the Constitution — ✅ Art 339(1) per Wikisource Part XVI; wording otherwise paraphrased). The executive power of the Union extends to giving directions to a State as to the drawing up and execution of schemes essential for the welfare of STs in the State.
**Learn:**
- A Union supervisory device for Scheduled Areas (outside the Fifth/Sixth Schedule machinery of Art 244).
- Union directions to States on ST welfare schemes are an executive power.
- Distinct from the Fifth/Sixth Schedule governance architecture and the PESA framework (owner: Scheduled-and-Tribal-Areas file).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 244(1) (Fifth Schedule), Art 244(2) (Sixth Schedule); Art 275 (grants-in-aid); Art 338A (NCST); owner Special Provisions §5.
**Trap:** Art 339 is not the Fifth/Sixth Schedule machinery; it is Union supervision. Art 275(1) is a fiscal (grant) provision, not a power to alter Art 342 lists.
**Back:** *"339: Union eyes on Scheduled Areas — a commission and directions, not the Fifth Schedule."*

### Flashcard Art-340 — Article 340
**Front:** Article 340 — Appointment of a Commission to investigate the conditions of backward classes
**Provision:** The President may by order appoint a commission consisting of such persons as he thinks fit, to investigate the conditions of socially and educationally backward classes within the territory of India and the difficulties under which they labour, and to make recommendations on the steps to be taken for the Union or a State to remove such difficulties and improve their conditions, including grants for such purposes.
**Learn:**
- Temporary, investigative commission appointed by order of the President — the basis for the Kaka Kalelkar Commission (First BC Commission, 1953) and the Mandal Commission (Second BC Commission, 1979).
- Its report and an action memorandum are laid before Parliament (owner).
- Distinct from the standing NCBC (Art 338B, 2018).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Kalelkar (1953); Mandal (1979); *Indra Sawhney* (1992) (50% ceiling and permanent body) (owner); 338B; 342A.
**Trap:** An Art 340 commission is temporary and investigative; the NCBC under Art 338B is the standing constitutional body. Do not say Art 340 creates reservation.
**Back:** *"340: the President's inquiry commission on backwardness — Kalelkar 1953, Mandal 1979."*

### Flashcard Art-341 — Article 341
**Front:** Article 341 — Scheduled Castes
**Provision:** The President may, after consultation with the Governor of a State (where it concerns a State), by public notification specify the castes/races/tribes/groups which shall for the purposes of the Constitution be deemed Scheduled Castes in relation to that State or UT. Parliament may by law include in or exclude from the list any caste, race or tribe, but no subsequent notification may alter the list.
**Learn:**
- Two-step: President specifies (by notification, after consulting the Governor); Parliament alone includes or excludes by law.
- States cannot alter the Presidential SC list by their own law or executive order.
- Lists are State/UT-specific, not uniform across India.
- *State of Punjab v. Davinder Singh* (2024) (seven-judge Bench, owner): evidence-based sub-classification of SCs for fair distribution of benefits is permitted, but it does not transfer Art 341 list-alteration power to a State, does not compel sub-classification, and does not permit complete exclusion of a listed caste (owner's firewall).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** *E.V. Chinnaiah* (2004) (indivisible class), overruled on this point by *Davinder Singh* (2024) (owner). *Indra Sawhney* (1992).
**Trap:** "Which community is on the SC list" and "what benefit does it receive" are different questions. A State can sub-classify for distribution; it cannot add or delete from the list. Do not say Davinder Singh made the SC list alterable by States.
**Back:** *"341: the President lists, Parliament alters — a State may sub-divide the benefit, never the list."*

### Flashcard Art-342 — Article 342
**Front:** Article 342 — Scheduled Tribes
**Provision:** The President may, after consultation with the Governor (where it concerns a State), by public notification specify the tribes or tribal communities or parts of or groups within them deemed Scheduled Tribes for the purposes of the Constitution in relation to that State or UT. Parliament may by law include in or exclude from the list any tribe, and no subsequent notification may alter it.
**Learn:**
- Same architecture as Art 341: President specifies, Parliament alters.
- A tribe is specified in relation to a State or UT; status is not automatically identical across India.
- ST list interacts with Art 244 (Fifth/Sixth Schedules) and Art 339 (Union supervision), but the lists are separate constitutional instruments.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 341; Art 244; Art 338A (NCST); owner's Special Provisions §6.1.
**Trap:** Do not confuse the ST list (Art 342) with the Scheduled Area declaration under the Fifth Schedule (Art 244). A tribe on the Art 342 list is not automatically in a Scheduled Area.
**Back:** *"342: the ST twin of 341 — President lists, Parliament alone adds or removes."*

### Flashcard Art-342A — Article 342A
**Front:** Article 342A — Socially and educationally backward classes (inserted by the 102nd Amendment; amended by the 105th Amendment)
**Provision:** Inserted by the 102nd Amendment (2018): (1) the President, after consultation with the Governor of a State, may by public notification specify the SEBCs for the purposes of the Constitution in relation to that State or UT; (2) Parliament may by law include or exclude SEBCs from the Central list. The 105th Amendment (2021) inserted clause (3) restoring the power of each State or UT to prepare and maintain its own list of SEBCs for its own purposes by law (owner).
**Learn:**
- Central SEBC list (President specifies; Parliament includes/excludes) and State/UT list (own-purpose, by law) are distinct after the 105th Amendment.
- Art 342A(3) was inserted after *Jaishri Laxmanrao Patil* (2021) read the 102nd Amendment as centralising SEBC identification (owner).
- Identification of a class (list) is distinct from the benefit (reservation under Art 15(4)/16(4) or policy).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 102nd (2018); 105th (2021); *Jaishri Laxmanrao Patil* (2021); Art 338B; Art 366(26C) (definition, inserted by 102nd, w.e.f. 11 Aug 2018 — ✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XIX).
**Trap:** The 105th Amendment did not abolish the Central SEBC List; it restored State/UT list power alongside it. The EWS category is NOT an SEBC list; it arises under Arts 15(6)/16(6) (103rd Amendment, upheld 3:2 in *Janhit Abhiyan* (2022)).
**Back:** *"342A: Centre keeps its SEBC list, States keep their own — the 105th restored the second list."*


### PART XVII — Official Language (Articles 343–351)

### Flashcard Art-343 — Article 343
**Front:** Article 343 — Official language of the Union
**Provision:** (1) The official language of the Union shall be Hindi in Devanagari script; the form of numerals to be used for the official purposes of the Union shall be the international form of Indian numerals. (2) Notwithstanding clause (1), English shall continue to be used for all official purposes of the Union for which it was being used immediately before commencement, for a period of fifteen years (1950–1965). (3) Parliament may by law provide for the use, after that period, of English for specified purposes.
**Learn:**
- Hindi (Devanagari) is the official language of the Union; the Constitution declares NO national language.
- The numerals caveat: "international form of Indian numerals", not Devanagari numerals.
- The 15-year Hindi-only transition window expired in 1965; English's continuance since then rests on the Official Languages Act, 1963 (as amended in 1967), a statute — NOT on Art 343 itself.
- Owner: Munshi–Ayyangar formula was the Constituent Assembly's compromise (descriptive label, not constitutional text).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Official Languages Act, 1963 (s.3, substituted in 1967 with a State-consent lock); Art 344 (Commissions); Art 351 (directive to develop Hindi); Art 348 (courts). Owner: Official Language file §5.4.
**Trap:** "Hindi is the national language" is always wrong. "Art 343 guarantees English" is wrong; English rests on the 1963 Act. Numerals are the international form, not Devanagari.
**Back:** *"343: Hindi is the official tongue of the Union, not the national one; English stayed by statute."*

### Flashcard Art-344 — Article 344
**Front:** Article 344 — Commission and Committee on official language
**Provision:** (1) The President shall, at the expiration of five years from the commencement and thereafter at the expiration of ten years, constitute a Commission consisting of a Chairman and members representing the languages in the Eighth Schedule, to make recommendations on the progressive use of Hindi for official purposes and on restrictions on English. (4) A Committee of thirty members (20 from the House of the People, 10 from the Council of States) shall be constituted to examine the Commission's recommendations and report its opinion to the President; (5) is the Committee's examining duty, and (6) lets the President issue directions on the report (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XVII; corrected: the Committee is cl (4), not cl (2)).
**Learn:**
- First Official Language Commission: B.G. Kher (1955); examined by a G.B. Pant committee (owner).
- The Committee of Parliament on Official Language is a 30-member committee (20 LS + 10 RS), chaired by convention by the Union Home Minister (owner). It is a statutory committee under section 4 of the Official Languages Act, 1963 (owner), not a permanent Art 344 commission.
- Recommendations are advisory; they cannot override section 3 of the OLA 1963.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Official Languages Act, 1963 s.4; Art 343; Art 351; Eighth Schedule (22 languages).
**Trap:** Do not treat the Committee on Official Language as a permanent Art 344 body; it is statutory. Art 344 is machinery; Art 351 is the directive to develop Hindi — not the same.
**Back:** *"344: a Commission at 5 and 10 years, a 30-member Committee to check its homework."*

### Flashcard Art-345 — Article 345
**Front:** Article 345 — Official language or languages of a State
**Provision:** Subject to the other provisions of Part XVII, the Legislature of a State may by law adopt any one or more of the languages in use in the State, or Hindi, as the language or languages to be used for all or any of the official purposes of that State. Until the Legislature so provides, the English language shall continue to be used for those official purposes for which it was being used immediately before commencement.
**Learn:**
- A State's choice is not restricted to the Eighth Schedule: any language in use in the State may be adopted.
- English continues in State official use until the State legislature decides otherwise.
- Central-State language matters go to Art 346, minority recognition to Art 347.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 346 (inter-State and Union–State communication); Art 347 (recognition for substantial linguistic population); Art 345 is a State-legislature power.
**Trap:** "States must pick from the Eighth Schedule" is wrong. "The Governor chooses the State language" is wrong — it is the State legislature.
**Back:** *"345: a State may adopt any language in use there, or Hindi — the Schedule is not a limit."*

### Flashcard Art-346 — Article 346
**Front:** Article 346 — Official language for communication between States and between a State and the Union
**Provision:** The language for the time being authorised for use for the purposes of the Union shall be the language for communication between one State and another State, and between a State and the Union. Where two or more States agree that Hindi shall be the language for communication between them, that language may be used (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XVII, gist: States may agree to use Hindi). Without such agreement, English continues for these purposes under the Union's authorised language arrangement (⚠️ verify).
**Learn:**
- The Union's official language governs inter-governmental communication between States and between a State and the Union.
- States may agree among themselves to use Hindi for their communication.
- The owner describes it as "Hindi or the authorised language" for inter-governmental communication.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 343 (Union official language); Art 345 (State language); Official Languages Act, 1963 (section 3 consent lock).
**Trap:** Art 346 is about communication between governments, not about the language of citizens or courts (those are Arts 348 and 350).
**Back:** *"346: governments talk in the Union's language, or in the one they agree between themselves."*

### Flashcard Art-347 — Article 347
**Front:** Article 347 — Special provision relating to language spoken by a section of the population of a State
**Provision:** On a demand made by a substantial proportion of the population of a State that a language spoken by them be recognised, the President may direct that such language shall also be officially recognised throughout the State or any part of it, for such purpose as he may specify.
**Learn:**
- A minority-language safeguard: Presidential direction on demand by a substantial proportion of the population.
- Discretionary and executive; protection varies with the political will of the Centre and State.
- Owner: a State's choice is not confined to the Eighth Schedule; Art 347 is the recognition route for a language below the State-legislature power.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 350 (grievance representation); Art 350A (mother-tongue primary schooling); Art 350B (Linguistic Minorities Commissioner).
**Trap:** Art 347 is a Presidential direction on demand, not a constitutional right to official use. "Substantial proportion" is a standard, not a fixed percentage.
**Back:** *"347: a substantial demand lets the President recognise a minority tongue in a State."*

### Flashcard Art-348 — Article 348
**Front:** Article 348 — Language to be used in the Supreme Court, High Courts and for Acts, Bills and orders
**Provision:** (1) Until Parliament provides otherwise, all proceedings in the Supreme Court and in every High Court, and the authoritative texts of Bills, Acts, Ordinances, orders, rules and regulations, shall be in English. (2) Notwithstanding (1), the Governor of a State may, with the previous consent of the President, authorise Hindi or the official language of the State to be used in the proceedings of the High Court; but judgments, decrees and orders shall remain in English unless the OLA 1963 s.7 route is used with an authorised English translation (owner). (3) Art 348(3) allows an English translation of State Acts authorised by the Governor to be treated as authoritative (owner).
**Learn:**
- English remains the default language of the higher judiciary and of authoritative legal texts.
- High Court proceedings in Hindi/State language need Governor + President's prior consent.
- Judgments/orders need an authorised English translation (OLA 1963 s.7).
- Authoritative Texts (Central Laws) Act, 1973 enables authoritative Central-law translations in Eighth Schedule languages other than Hindi (owner).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** OLA 1963 ss.5–7; Authoritative Texts (Central Laws) Act, 1973; Art 349 (President's sanction for certain Bills); owner Official Language §5.6.
**Trap:** "HC judgments may be given in Hindi" is wrong without the s.7 route and translation. Art 348 covers only the Supreme Court and High Courts (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XVII, Art 348(1)); the Supreme Court's own proceedings are English unless Parliament provides otherwise.
**Back:** *"348: English is the court's default; a Governor and the President may open the HC's proceedings, not its judgments."*

### Flashcard Art-349 — Article 349
**Front:** Article 349 — Special procedure for enactment of certain laws relating to language
**Provision:** During the period of fifteen years from commencement, no Bill or amendment making provision for the language to be used for the purposes of Art 348(1) (proceedings of the Supreme Court and High Courts and authoritative legal texts) shall be introduced or moved in Parliament without the previous sanction of the President, who may give it only after considering the Art 344(1) Commission's recommendations and the Art 344(4) Committee's report (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XVII; corrected: was "any language specified in the Eighth Schedule").
**Learn:**
- A transitional, 15-year safeguard that expired in 1965.
- Time-bound; it no longer restricts Parliament, but it shows the framers' design of gradual change.
- Owner: "the President's prior sanction for certain language-related Bills during the first fifteen years".
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 344 (Commission); Art 343(2) (15-year window); owner Official Language §5.6.
**Trap:** Art 349 is spent; do not describe it as a current requirement for Bills.
**Back:** *"349: a fifteen-year brake on language Bills — spent by 1965."*

### Flashcard Art-350 — Article 350
**Front:** Article 350 — Right to submit representation for redress of grievances in any language
**Provision:** Every person shall be entitled to submit a representation for the redress of any grievance to any officer or authority of the Union or a State in any of the languages used in the Union or in the State, as the case may be.
**Learn:**
- An individual right of representation in any language used in the Union or the State.
- Pairs with Art 350A (mother-tongue primary education) and Art 350B (Special Officer).
- Owner: this is the constitutional guarantee of citizen access to officials in their own language.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 350A; Art 350B; Art 347; owner Official Language §5.7.
**Trap:** Art 350 is a right to submit a representation, not a right to be answered in one's language in all official correspondence.
**Back:** *"350: a grievance may be lodged in any language the Union or the State uses."*

### Flashcard Art-350A — Article 350A
**Front:** Article 350A — Facilities for instruction in mother-tongue at primary stage
**Provision:** It shall be the endeavour of every State and of every local authority within the State to provide adequate facilities for instruction in the mother-tongue at the primary stage of education to children belonging to linguistic minority groups; and the President may issue directions to any State for securing such facilities.
**Learn:**
- Inserted by the 7th Amendment (1956), s.21, w.e.f. 1 Nov 1956 (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XVII fn 3).
- Primary stage only; "endeavour" language makes it a directive-like duty, not an enforceable entitlement, though the President can direct.
- Linked to Art 350B (Special Officer for Linguistic Minorities) who monitors safeguards.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 7th Amendment (1956); Art 350B; Art 30(1) (minority educational institutions) — ⚠️ cross-reference only.
**Trap:** 350A is about primary mother-tongue instruction; 350B is about the Special Officer. The "endeavour" phrasing is a weaker duty than a mandatory command.
**Back:** *"350A: mother-tongue at primary school, an endeavour the President can press."*

### Flashcard Art-350B — Article 350B
**Front:** Article 350B — Special Officer for Linguistic Minorities
**Provision:** There shall be a Special Officer for Linguistic Minorities to be appointed by the President. It is his duty to investigate all matters relating to the safeguards provided for linguistic minorities under the Constitution and to report to the President at such intervals as the President may direct, with the reports laid before each House of Parliament and sent to the Governments of the States concerned.
**Learn:**
- Inserted by the 7th Amendment (1956), s.21, w.e.f. 1 Nov 1956 (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XVII fn 3).
- An investigative, non-enforcing office; it reports, it does not adjudicate.
- Administratively under the Ministry of Minority Affairs; headquarters New Delhi since 1 June 2015 (owner, National Commissions file, checked 5 Sep 2026).
- The Constitution is silent on tenure and removal (owner).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 7th Amendment (1956); Art 350A; Art 347; National Commissions owner (§5.5).
**Trap:** 350B is not a commission; it is a single Special Officer. Do not say the officer has civil-court powers — the owner treats it as investigative, and the Constitution does not confer those powers in terms (⚠️).
**Back:** *"350B: one Special Officer who investigates linguistic safeguards and reports, never enforces."*

### Flashcard Art-351 — Article 351
**Front:** Article 351 — Directive for development of the Hindi language
**Provision:** It shall be the duty of the Union to promote the spread of the Hindi language, to develop it so that it may serve as a medium of expression for all the elements of the composite culture of India, and to secure its enrichment by assimilating, without interfering with its genius, the forms, style and expressions used in Hindustani and in the other languages of India specified in the Eighth Schedule, drawing mainly on Sanskrit and secondarily on other languages.
**Learn:**
- A directive within Part XVII; not a Fundamental Right and not a Part IV DPSP — it guides policy but is not enforceable in court.
- "Composite culture" is the textual anchor; the directive protects against a sectarian or Sanskritised Hindi.
- Owner: the Union's duty is to promote and enrich, not to make Hindi the sole medium.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 343 (official language); Art 344 (Commission); Eighth Schedule (22 languages).
**Trap:** Art 351 is a Union duty; it is not an obligation on States, and it does not make Hindi a national language. Do not confuse it with Art 344 (machinery).
**Back:** *"351: the Union promotes Hindi as a medium for all India's composite culture — a duty, not a decree."*

### PART XVIII — Emergency Provisions (Articles 352–360)

### Flashcard Art-352 — Article 352
**Front:** Article 352 — Proclamation of Emergency (National Emergency)
**Provision:** If the President is satisfied that a grave emergency exists whereby the security of India or of any part of its territory is threatened, whether by war or external aggression or armed rebellion, he may make a Proclamation of Emergency. It may be made before the actual occurrence of war or aggression if there is imminent danger. As amended by the 44th Amendment (1978): the President may act only on the written recommendation of the Union Cabinet; the Proclamation must be approved by both Houses within one month by a special majority; it ceases after six months unless renewed by a fresh resolution; it may be revoked at any time by the President; and the Lok Sabha may disapprove it by simple majority, forcing revocation.
**Learn:**
- Three grounds: war, external aggression, armed rebellion (44th replaced "internal disturbance").
- Approval: one month, special majority (total membership + 2/3 present and voting per Art 368 logic in both Houses); renewal every six months, indefinitely.
- The Lok Sabha (not the Rajya Sabha) holds the simple-majority revocation power; one-tenth of LS members can requisition a special sitting within 14 days (owner).
- Declared three times: 1962 (China), 1971 (Pakistan), 1975 (internal disturbance) (owner).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 44th Amendment (1978); 38th Amendment (1975) (judicial review immunity, later removed by 44th); 42nd Amendment (1976) (part-India emergency, extended legislature terms) (owner); Art 358 and 359; *ADM Jabalpur* (1976) overruled by *Puttaswamy* (2017).
**Trap:** Pre-44th: "internal disturbance" and 2-month/simple majority; post-44th: "armed rebellion", written Cabinet advice, special majority. Do not say only the PM can recommend. Do not say the Rajya Sabha can revoke.
**Back:** *"352: war, aggression or armed revolt — Cabinet writes, both Houses approve, the Lok Sabha can undo."*

### Flashcard Art-353 — Article 353
**Front:** Article 353 — Effect of Proclamation of Emergency
**Provision:** While a Proclamation of Emergency is in operation: (a) the executive power of the Union extends to giving directions to any State on the manner of its executive power; (b) Parliament may make laws on any matter in the State List (for the Union as a whole) for the duration of the Emergency; the law made ceases to have effect six months after the emergency ceases (⚠️ paraphrased; verify exact sub-clause wording).
**Learn:**
- The emergency makes the Union's legislative reach extend to the State List for the duration of the Proclamation.
- State governments and legislatures continue to function under a National Emergency; the Union's power is additive.
- Owner: a State is not dissolved under Art 352 — that is Art 356.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 352; Art 354 (revenue distribution modified); Art 356 (State emergency, owner's contrast).
**Trap:** Under 352 the State government is NOT dismissed; that is a President's Rule (Art 356) effect. Art 353 is "unitary" in effect but not a dissolution.
**Back:** *"353: in a national emergency, the Union directs States and may legislate on State List subjects."*

### Flashcard Art-354 — Article 354
**Front:** Article 354 — Application of provisions relating to distribution of revenues while a Proclamation of Emergency is in operation
**Provision:** The President may by order direct that all or any of the provisions of Chapter I of Part XII (distribution of revenues between Union and States) shall, during the emergency, have effect subject to such exceptions and modifications as he thinks fit, not extending in any case beyond the expiration of the financial year in which the Proclamation ceases to operate (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XVIII, Art 354; orders must be laid before Parliament).
**Learn:**
- The President can modify the Union–State financial-transfer provisions during an emergency.
- The modification may not extend beyond the financial year in which the emergency ceases.
- Pairs with Art 360 (financial emergency) as a separate fiscal device.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 268–279 (the articles Art 354 names; ✅ Wikisource Part XVIII; corrected: was "268–281"); Art 360.
**Trap:** Art 354 is a revenue-distribution modification, not a Financial Emergency under Art 360. Do not conflate them.
**Back:** *"354: in a national emergency the President may retune the Union–State revenue split."*

### Flashcard Art-355 — Article 355
**Front:** Article 355 — Duty of the Union to protect States against external aggression and internal disturbance
**Provision:** It shall be the duty of the Union to protect every State against external aggression and internal disturbance, and to ensure that the government of every State is carried on in accordance with the provisions of the Constitution.
**Learn:**
- The Union's duty clause; it is the constitutional basis for the Union's intervention power.
- Ambedkar anticipated Art 356 as a "dead letter" if Art 355 worked; owner records Ambedkar's hope.
- Art 355 creates a duty, but it does not itself trigger a President's Rule; that requires Art 356.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 356 (State emergency); Art 365 (failure to comply with Union directions); *S.R. Bommai* (1994) (owner): Art 356 is judicially reviewable; Art 355 and Art 365 and a Governor's report do not create automatic failure.
**Trap:** Art 355 is a duty of the Union, not a power to dismiss a State government; the dismissal path is Art 356.
**Back:** *"355: the Union's duty to guard each State — the duty is not yet the dismissal."*

### Flashcard Art-356 — Article 356
**Front:** Article 356 — Provisions in case of failure of constitutional machinery in States (President's Rule)
**Provision:** If the President, on the report of the Governor of a State or otherwise, is satisfied that a situation has arisen in which the government of the State cannot be carried on in accordance with the provisions of the Constitution, he may issue a Proclamation: he may assume to himself the functions of the State government and powers of the Governor, and declare that the powers of the Legislature shall be exercisable by or under the authority of Parliament. The Proclamation must be approved by both Houses within two months (simple majority). It ceases after six months unless renewed. Under the 44th Amendment (1978), beyond one year it can continue only if a National Emergency is in operation in the whole or part of the State, and the ECI certifies that the election cannot be held. Maximum duration: three years.
**Learn:**
- Triggers: failure of constitutional machinery; the Governor's report is one route, "or otherwise" another.
- The President dismisses the Council of Ministers/CM and may suspend or dissolve the Assembly; the State High Court's powers cannot be taken over.
- *S.R. Bommai* (1994): the proclamation is judicially reviewable; a disputed majority is tested on the floor of the House; the President's satisfaction must rest on relevant material with the burden on the Centre; the Assembly may be dissolved only after Parliament approves; a wrongly dismissed government can be restored; secularism and federalism are basic features.
- *Rameshwar Prasad* (2006): the 2005 Bihar Assembly dissolution was unconstitutional and mala fide (owner).
- Art 356 has been invoked over 125 times, most before the mid-1990s (owner).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** *S.R. Bommai* (1994); *Rameshwar Prasad* (2006); 38th (1975); 42nd (1976); 44th (1978) (one-year and ECI-certificate limit); Sarkaria Commission (1988) and Punchhi Commission (2007–10) on last-resort use (owner); Manipur President's Rule (imposed 13 Feb 2025; revoked 4 Feb 2026 per owner, 📰 re-verify).
**Trap:** 356 = simple majority, max three years; 352 = special majority, indefinite renewal. Do not say the Governor can declare a majority lost by a subjective report — the floor test is the usual mechanism. Art 356 does not take over the High Court.
**Back:** *"356: the State's machinery fails — two months to approve, three years at most, floor-tested in court."*

### Flashcard Art-357 — Article 357
**Front:** Article 357 — Exercise of legislative powers under Proclamation issued under Article 356
**Provision:** Where the President has issued a Proclamation under Art 356 (a) Parliament may confer on the President the power of the State Legislature to make laws, or delegate this to another authority; (b) the President may authorise expenditure from the State Consolidated Fund, while the House of the People is not in session, pending Parliament's sanction (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XVIII, Art 357(1); corrected: was "Parliament or the President"); and the President may make any laws the Parliament (or delegate) makes, to be operative until revoked by the Legislature.
**Learn:**
- The mechanism for State lawmaking under President's Rule: Parliament makes laws, or delegates to the President.
- Laws made under Art 357 are temporary; they continue in force until altered, repealed or amended by a competent Legislature or other authority (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XVIII, Art 357(2)).
- The State High Court's jurisdiction is not transferred to the Union.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 356; Art 357(1)(a) (Parliament's law); *S.R. Bommai* (1994).
**Trap:** Under 356 the State High Court remains; only the legislative and executive functions shift. Do not say Parliament dissolves the State High Court.
**Back:** *"357: under President's Rule, Parliament writes the State's laws until the State is restored."*

### Flashcard Art-358 — Article 358
**Front:** Article 358 — Suspension of provisions of Article 19 during emergencies
**Provision:** While a Proclamation of Emergency under Art 352 is in operation, nothing in Art 19 shall restrict the power of the State to make any law or take any executive action, provided the law or action is related to the emergency. Post-44th Amendment (1978): Art 358 operates only when the emergency is on grounds of war or external aggression, not armed rebellion; the protection applies automatically, but only for laws and actions carrying the constitutional recital and related to the emergency (owner).
**Learn:**
- Automatic suspension of Art 19 (six freedoms), not of Art 20 or 21.
- Only for a War/External-aggression Emergency (owner); does not operate in an armed-rebellion emergency.
- Rights firewall: Art 358 concerns Art 19 only; Art 359 concerns enforcement of other rights by order.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 44th Amendment (1978); Art 359; *ADM Jabalpur* (1976) (overruled by *Puttaswamy* (2017)).
**Trap:** Art 358 is automatic; Art 359 needs a Presidential order. Art 358 does not suspend Art 21 or Art 20.
**Back:** *"358: in external war, Article 19's freedoms pause automatically — only for emergency-related laws."*

### Flashcard Art-359 — Article 359
**Front:** Article 359 — Suspension of the enforcement of rights conferred by Part III during emergencies
**Provision:** Where a Proclamation of Emergency is in operation, the President may by order declare that the right to move any court for the enforcement of such rights conferred by Part III (except Arts 20 and 21, as amended by the 44th Amendment in 1978) shall remain suspended for the period specified in the order. Such an order must be laid before each House of Parliament.
**Learn:**
- Requires a Presidential order; it suspends enforcement (the right to move court), not the substantive right.
- Articles 20 and 21 can NEVER be suspended under Art 359 (44th Amendment).
- Applies to both National Emergency types (war/external aggression or armed rebellion), unlike Art 358 (owner).
- Owner's routing: the rights-in-emergency debate runs *ADM Jabalpur* (1976, majority held no locus for Art 21 habeas corpus; Justice H.R. Khanna dissented) → *Puttaswamy* (2017, overruled ADM Jabalpur).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 44th Amendment (1978); *ADM Jabalpur* (1976); *K.S. Puttaswamy* (2017) (nine-judge bench); Art 22 (preventive detention) — owner: sits in Fundamental Rights, not emergency articles.
**Trap:** Art 359 suspends enforcement, not the rights themselves; Articles 20 and 21 cannot be suspended. Do not cite ADM Jabalpur as good law.
**Back:** *"359: the President may mute the right to go to court — never for Articles 20 and 21."*

### Flashcard Art-359A — Article 359A (omitted)
**Front:** Article 359A — Application of this Part to the State of Punjab (inserted by the 59th Amendment, 1988; omitted by the 63rd Amendment, 1989; corrected: was "special provision as to Punjab", "1970s")
**Provision:** Inserted by the 59th Amendment (1988) and to lapse after two years; omitted by the Constitution (Sixty-third Amendment) Act, 1989, s.3, w.e.f. 6 Jan 1990 (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XVIII). It has no present operation. Its clause wording is not reproduced in the Wikisource extract (⚠️ unverified).
**Learn:**
- Insertion (59th, 1988) and omission (63rd, 1989, w.e.f. 6-1-1990) are confirmed; the clause text is not.
- Its subject matter (Punjab-specific emergency provisions) concerns a State-specific emergency arrangement, which resembles the State-specific design of Art 371.
- Use the Art 356 and 352 cards for the general emergency architecture.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Verify against the official Constitution text (Legislative Department) before citing; cross-check Part XVIII listing in the Ministry of Law's PDF.
**Trap:** Do not assume 359A is a current, operative Punjab emergency power without checking the official text.
**Back:** *"359A: a Punjab-specific emergency clause, inserted 1988 and omitted from 1990."*

### Flashcard Art-360 — Article 360
**Front:** Article 360 — Provisions as to financial emergency
**Provision:** If the President is satisfied that a situation has arisen whereby the financial stability or credit of India or of any part of the territory is threatened, he may by Proclamation make a declaration to that effect. The Proclamation must be approved by both Houses within two months by simple majority (per the 44th Amendment's text as the owner records). It continues in force indefinitely until revoked (no six-month renewal, no maximum). During it, the Union may direct States to observe canons of financial propriety, reduce the salaries of persons serving the State (including the judges of the Supreme Court and High Courts) and require State Money Bills to be reserved for the President's consideration.
**Learn:**
- Trigger: threat to financial stability or credit of India or any part.
- Approval: two months, simple majority; then indefinite until revoked.
- Consequences: canons of financial propriety; salary cuts for State and Union staff, including SC/HC judges; State Money Bills reserved for the President.
- Modelled on the US National Industrial Recovery Act, 1933 (Ambedkar, per owner).
- Never proclaimed (closest: the 1991 balance-of-payments crisis). H.N. Kunzru warned about State financial autonomy (owner).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 44th Amendment (1978) safeguards; Art 352/356 contrast (owner).
**Trap:** Art 360 is not renewable like Art 352; 360's approval is two months, not one month. Art 360 does not suspend Fundamental Rights, unlike Art 358/359 mechanics. Do not confuse the salary cut for SC/HC judges with an ordinary pay order.
**Back:** *"360: two months to approve, forever to revoke — and even the judges' pay can be cut. Never used."*

### PART XIX — Miscellaneous (Articles 361–367)

### Flashcard Art-361 — Article 361
**Front:** Article 361 — Protection of President and Governors and Rajpramukhs
**Provision:** (1) The President or Governor shall not be answerable to any court for the exercise and performance of the powers and duties of his office; provisos preserve a Parliament-appointed inquiry into an Art 61 impeachment charge and suits against the Union or State governments (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XIX). (2) No criminal proceedings shall be instituted or continued against the President or a Governor during the term of office. (3) No process for the arrest or imprisonment of the President or a Governor may issue from any court during the term. (4) No civil proceedings for relief in respect of acts done in personal capacity may be instituted during the term unless two months' prior written notice has been given (corrected: the civil bar is cl (4), not cl (3)).
**Learn:**
- Immunity for the exercise of official powers; criminal immunity during the term; civil proceedings for personal acts need two months' prior notice.
- The impeachment route (Art 61 for the President) is the constitutional accountability route.
- "Rajpramukhs" in the text is a historical reference to princely-State heads — ⚠️ wording; verify.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 61 (impeachment of President); Art 356 (Governor's report, owner); Art 361(4) (two months' notice).
**Trap:** Immunity is not a bar to all proceedings: criminal immunity is only during the term; the Governor's official acts can still be subject to the Art 356 floor-test scrutiny of the State government.
**Back:** *"361: no court answers for the President's or Governor's office — no criminal case during the term."*

### Flashcard Art-361A — Article 361A
**Front:** Article 361A — Protection of publication of proceedings of Parliament and State Legislatures
**Provision:** Inserted by the 44th Amendment (1978): no person shall be liable to any proceedings, civil or criminal, in respect of the publication in a newspaper (or by radio or television) of a substantially true report of the proceedings of either House of Parliament or of a House of a State Legislature, unless it is proved that the publication was made with malice (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XIX: "substantially true report", "unless the publication is proved to have been made with malice"; inserted by the 44th Amendment, s.42, w.e.f. 20 Jun 1979).
**Learn:**
- A press-protection clause tied to legislative proceedings; the protection is for a substantially true, fair report.
- The 44th Amendment (1978) added it alongside its other pro-liberty changes.
- Only the "substantially true" report protection is conferred; a malicious publication is not protected.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 44th Amendment (1978); Art 105 and 194 (privilege of legislatures, owner-consistent, ⚠️ cross-reference only); Art 19(1)(a) (free speech).
**Trap:** Art 361A protects reporting of proceedings, not all speech; malice removes the shield. Do not confuse it with Art 105(2) (MPs' immunity inside the House).
**Back:** *"361A: a fair, true report of the House is safe — malice is not."*

### Flashcard Art-361B — Article 361B
**Front:** Article 361B — Disqualification for appointment to a remunerative political post
**Provision:** Inserted by the 91st Amendment (2003), s.4, w.e.f. 1 Jan 2004 (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XIX): a member of either House of Parliament or of a State Legislature who is disqualified under paragraph 2 of the Tenth Schedule (defection) is also disqualified from holding any remunerative political post until his term would end or he is elected again to a House, whichever is earlier (corrected: the ground is defection only, not Art 102(1)(a); the insertion year is 2003, not 1985).
**Learn:**
- Inserted by the 91st Amendment (2003), w.e.f. 1 Jan 2004 (✅ Wikisource); the 52nd Amendment (1985) is the origin of the Tenth Schedule it refers to.
- Targets appointment to political posts of profit, closing the route from a seat to a paid political post.
- Owner link: Art 102 and 191 (disqualification grounds); Art 361B is an anti-defection companion.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 91st Amendment (2003); 52nd Amendment (1985) (Tenth Schedule); Art 102(1)(a); Art 191(1)(a).
**Trap:** Art 361B is about remunerative political posts for a disqualified legislator; it is not a general office-of-profit rule (that is Art 102/191).
**Back:** *"361B: a legislator disqualified for defection cannot be paid for a political post."*

### Flashcard Art-362 — Article 362 (Omitted)
**Front:** Article 362 — Rights and privileges of Rulers of Indian States (omitted)
**Provision:** **Omitted** by the 26th Amendment (1971). The article had protected the privileges, rights and dignities of Rulers of former princely States (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XIX: heading "Rights and privileges of Rulers of Indian States"; omitted by the 26th Amendment, s.2, w.e.f. 28 Dec 1971).
**Learn:**
- Part of the princely-State settlement; the Rulers' privy purses were abolished by the 26th Amendment (1971), which also omitted Art 291 (privy purses) (owner-consistent; ⚠️ verify).
- Art 363A (recognition ceasing) was inserted by the same amendment.
- Omission marks the end of princely privilege under the Constitution; it does not repeal the related state-succession story.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 26th Amendment (1971); Art 363 and 363A; Art 291 (privy purses) (⚠️ cross-reference).
**Trap:** Do not state that the Constitution still protects Rulers. Background: the Supreme Court ruled in favour of the rulers against the President's 6 Sep 1970 derecognition order in *H.H. Maharajadhiraja Madhav Rao Scindia v. Union of India* (15 Dec 1970) (✅ Wikipedia "Privy Purse in India"; "struck down" wording not used there); the 26th Amendment (1971) then inserted Art 363A and omitted Art 362 (✅ Wikisource); the omission of Art 291 is ⚠️ unverified.
**Back:** *"362: the princes' privileges — omitted in 1971 when the purse was abolished."*

### Flashcard Art-363 — Article 363
**Front:** Article 363 — Bar to interference by courts in disputes arising out of certain treaties, agreements, etc.
**Provision:** Notwithstanding anything in the Constitution, no court shall have jurisdiction in any dispute arising out of any treaty, agreement, covenant, engagement, sanad or other similar instrument which was entered into or executed before the commencement of the Constitution by any Ruler of an Indian State, and to which the Government of India or its predecessor was a party (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XIX, cl (1): "Notwithstanding anything in this Constitution, but subject to article 143"; the 26th Amendment attribution ⚠️ unverified).
**Learn:**
- A jurisdiction bar on princely-State instruments; the 26th Amendment (1971) is cited as reducing its reach (⚠️ effect not verified in the Wikisource extract).
- Owner-consistent: this is a Part XIX legacy of the princely-State merger.
- Courts have been reluctant to hear claims on such instruments (⚠️ general).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 26th Amendment (1971); Art 363A (recognition); Art 362 (omitted).
**Trap:** The 26th Amendment changed the Art 363 scope; do not describe it as a current, unmodified bar on all treaty disputes.
**Back:** *"363: pre-1950 princely treaties — the courts stayed out, mostly."*

### Flashcard Art-363A — Article 363A
**Front:** Article 363A — Recognition granted to Rulers of Indian States to cease, and the privy purses to be abolished
**Provision:** Inserted by the 26th Amendment (1971): notwithstanding anything in the Constitution or any law, the recognition granted to any Ruler of a former Indian State (including the right to privy purses and privileges, and the rights and duties attaching to them) shall cease and be derecognised, with the privy purses abolished (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XIX: recognition ceases from commencement of the 26th Amendment; privy purse abolished and its rights and obligations extinguished; inserted by the 26th Amendment, s.3, w.e.f. 28 Dec 1971).
**Learn:**
- Inserted by the 26th Amendment (1971), the amendment that ended the princely privy purse.
- Derecognition of Rulers; this removed the privileged status constitutionally recognised after 1947.
- Pairs with the omission of Art 291 (privy purses) and Art 362.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 26th Amendment (1971); Art 291 (omitted); Art 362 (omitted); Madhav Rao Scindia case (✅ name; decided 15 Dec 1970 per Wikipedia "Privy Purse in India"; holding ⚠️ see Trap).
**Trap:** Art 363A is an abolition-of-privilege clause, not an article protecting Rulers. Do not confuse 363A with Art 363 (court bar).
**Back:** *"363A: derecognise the Rulers, end the purse — 26th Amendment, 1971."*

### Flashcard Art-364 — Article 364
**Front:** Article 364 — Special provisions as to major ports and aerodromes
**Provision:** Where the President is of the opinion that a Union law or the federal coordination of major ports and aerodromes is necessary, by public notification the President may state that laws made by Parliament or by a State Legislature shall not apply to major ports or aerodromes, or shall apply only with exceptions or modifications; the same power covers existing laws (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XIX, Art 364 gist; corrected: mechanism is a public notification, not a general Union-control order).
**Learn:**
- Union jurisdiction over major ports and aerodromes (declared by Parliament) is secured against State law.
- The rule is that the Union's law prevails over State law in these matters.
- Pairs with the Union List entries on ports and aerodromes (⚠️ cross-reference to Schedule VII).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Schedule VII (Union List); Art 246; Art 254 (repugnancy) (⚠️ cross-reference).
**Trap:** Art 364 concerns major ports and aerodromes (declared by Parliament/Union), not all ports; do not treat it as a general emergency power.
**Back:** *"364: major ports and airports answer to the Union, not the State."*

### Flashcard Art-365 — Article 365
**Front:** Article 365 — Effect of failure to comply with, or to give effect to, directions given by the Union
**Provision:** Where any State has failed to comply with or to give effect to any directions given in the exercise of the executive power of the Union under any of the provisions of the Constitution, it shall be lawful for the President to hold that a situation has arisen in which the government of the State cannot be carried on in accordance with the provisions of the Constitution, which may trigger Art 356.
**Learn:**
- An alternative trigger for President's Rule: non-compliance with Union directions.
- Pairs with Art 355 (duty) and Art 356 (proclamation); the trigger is not automatic failure.
- *S.R. Bommai* (1994): Art 365, Art 355 and a Governor's report do not create automatic failure; the President's satisfaction must rest on relevant material (owner).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 355; Art 356; Art 256/257 (Union directions; ⚠️ cross-reference); *S.R. Bommai* (1994).
**Trap:** Art 365 is a trigger, not a power to dismiss a government by itself; non-compliance must be shown on material. Do not say "non-compliance automatically ends State government".
**Back:** *"365: disobey the Union and the President may find the State's machinery broken."*

### Flashcard Art-366 — Article 366
**Front:** Article 366 — Definitions
**Provision:** In the Constitution, unless the context otherwise requires, the expressions defined in Art 366 have the meanings given: e.g., "Anglo-Indian" (cl. 2), "Scheduled Castes" (cl. 24) and "Scheduled Tribes" (cl. 25) (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XIX: cl (2) Anglo-Indian, (24) SC, (25) ST, (26C) SEBC), and "socially and educationally backward classes" (cl. 26C, inserted by the 102nd Amendment, owner).
**Learn:**
- Definitional clause for the whole Constitution; it fixes what SC, ST and Anglo-Indian mean in the text.
- The 102nd Amendment inserted 366(26C) (owner) to define SEBCs.
- Definitions interact with Arts 341–342A (lists) and Art 331/333 (nomination).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 341, 342, 342A; Art 331, 333; 102nd Amendment (2018).
**Trap:** The definition of Anglo-Indian and SC/ST does not itself list communities; the lists are under Arts 341–342. Art 366(26C) defines SEBC by reference to 342A, not by census.
**Back:** *"366: the Constitution's dictionary — Anglo-Indian, SC, ST, SEBC are defined here, not listed."*

### Flashcard Art-367 — Article 367
**Front:** Article 367 — Interpretation
**Provision:** Unless the context otherwise requires, the General Clauses Act, 1897 applies to the interpretation of the Constitution as it applies to a Central Act. The clause also contains interpretive substitutions used in the 2019 J&K orders, including reading "Constituent Assembly" as "Legislative Assembly" in the Article 370 context (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XIX: cl (1) GCA 1897 applies, subject to Art 372 adaptations; cl (3) defines "Foreign State". The 2019 substitution sits in a new cl (4) added by C.O. 272 (✅ Wikipedia, Art 370 page); its exact wording ⚠️ unverified).
**Learn:**
- A rule of construction; General Clauses Act, 1897 applies to the Constitution.
- The 2019 substitution (new cl (4), not cl (3) which defines "Foreign State") was used in the 2019 J&K orders to read "Constituent Assembly" as "Legislative Assembly" of J&K; in re Article 370 (2023) held that Art 367 substitution invalid to the extent stated and unnecessary to the outcome (owner; ⚠️ holding not confirmed on Wikipedia).
- Interpretive devices must not be used to achieve a substantive amendment without Art 368 (judicial reasoning in 2023 — owner).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 370; C.O. 272 (5 Aug 2019); C.O. 273 (6 Aug 2019) (owner); In re Article 370 (11 Dec 2023) (✅ date and five-judge bench per Wikipedia).
**Trap:** Art 367 is a clause of interpretation, not a clause of substance. Do not say the 2019 orders validly amended Art 370 through Art 367 — the 2023 judgment held the substitution invalid to that extent (⚠️ holding unverified).
**Back:** *"367: the rule-book for reading the Constitution — it cannot amend the Constitution by a side door."*

### PART XX — Amendment of the Constitution (Article 368)

### Flashcard Art-368 — Article 368
**Front:** Article 368 — Power of Parliament to amend the Constitution and procedure therefor
**Provision:** Parliament may in exercise of its constituent power amend by way of addition, variation or repeal any provision of the Constitution in accordance with the procedure. An amendment Bill may originate in either House. Each House must pass it by a majority of its total membership and two-thirds of the members present and voting. For amendments to federal provisions, ratification by not less than half of the State legislatures by simple majority is required before the President assents. The President must give assent (24th Amendment, 1971). (Owner: the federal provisions are Arts 54–55, 73/162, 241, 279A, Ch IV Pt V, Ch V Pt VI, Ch I Pt XI, Seventh Schedule lists, representation of States in Parliament, and Art 368 itself.)
**Learn:**
- Three routes: simple majority (outside Art 368); special majority; special majority plus ratification by half the States.
- No joint sitting to break a deadlock for amendment Bills.
- Basic structure: *Kesavananda Bharati* (1973, 13-judge bench): Parliament can amend FR but not destroy the basic structure. *Golak Nath* (1967) had made FR unamendable; *Shankari Prasad* (1951) and *Sajjan Singh* (1965) had upheld amendments; the 24th Amendment (1971) restored the power; *Minerva Mills* (1980) struck the 42nd's unlimited power and held limited amending power a basic feature; *I.R. Coelho* (2007) subjects Ninth Schedule laws post-24 April 1973 to basic-structure review; *NJAC* (2015) struck the 99th Amendment (owner).
- Worked example (owner): the Constitution (131st Amendment) Bill, 2026 was defeated in the first House (298 : 230 of 528 present and voting), showing the two limbs of Art 368(2); it is a defeated Bill, not law or precedent.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 24th (1971); 42nd (1976); 44th; 99th/NJAC (2015); Anjum Kadari (2024 INSC 831) (ordinary-statute boundary) (owner); Comparative-Constitutional-Schemes.md (owner).
**Trap:** Amendments cannot be initiated in State legislatures (only Parliament). State ratification is by simple majority of not less than half the States (not three-fourths, not special majority). The President cannot withhold assent to an amendment Bill. No joint sitting for amendment Bills.
**Back:** *"368: Parliament amends, two-thirds vote, half the States for the federal core — never the basic structure."*

### PART XXI — Temporary, Transitional and Special Provisions (Articles 369–392)

### Flashcard Art-369 — Article 369
**Front:** Article 369 — Temporary power to Parliament to make laws with respect to certain matters in the State List
**Provision:** Notwithstanding anything in the Constitution, for a period of five years from commencement, Parliament shall have power to make laws with respect to any of the matters enumerated in the State List (limited to the enumerated items: cotton and woollen textiles, raw cotton, cotton seed, paper, food-stuffs, cattle fodder, coal, iron, steel and mica, with offences, court jurisdiction (not the Supreme Court) and fees as ancillary matters; "land" is not in the list; corrected: was "except land") as if they were in the Concurrent List, for the whole or any part of India (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XXI; laws beyond Parliament's competence lapse when the period ends, except for things already done).
**Learn:**
- A five-year transitional power (1950–1955) to legislate on State List subjects for the enumerated items (trade in and production, supply and distribution of the listed goods such as textiles, paper, food-stuffs, coal, iron, steel and mica; ✅ Wikisource).
- Spent: its period expired in 1955; it has no operative force now.
- Demonstrates the framers' design of an interim Union-led framework during the transition.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 249 (Parliament's power over State List in national interest) and Art 250 (emergency) are the live counterparts — ⚠️ cross-reference only.
**Trap:** Art 369 is spent, not current. Do not confuse it with Art 249 (Rajya Sabha resolution) or Art 352 (emergency).
**Back:** *"369: five years of Union power over State subjects — spent by 1955."*

### Flashcard Art-370 — Article 370
**Front:** Article 370 — Temporary provisions with respect to the State of Jammu and Kashmir (⚠️ 2019 status and 2023 judgment)
**Provision:** Originally, Art 370 gave Jammu and Kashmir a special status: Parliament could make laws on Union-list and Concurrent-list matters for J&K only with the concurrence of the State Government (370(1)(b)); other provisions could be extended with the concurrence of the State Government; the President could declare Art 370 inoperative (370(3)) on the recommendation of the Constituent Assembly of the State. The 2019 Presidential orders — C.O. 272 (5 August 2019) and C.O. 273 (dated 5 August 2019, operative from 6 August 2019; ✅ Wikisource Part XXI footnote; corrected: was "6 Aug 2019") — applied the Constitution to J&K and rendered Art 370 inoperative; the Supreme Court (*In re Article 370*, 11 December 2023; 2023 INSC 1058 citation ⚠️ unverified) upheld the constitutional result (✅ Wikipedia: unanimous five-judge bench, 11 Dec 2023); the reasoning on temporariness and internal sovereignty is ⚠️ unverified.
**Learn:**
- Popularly called the "abrogation" (2019); legally, the 2019 orders rendered Art 370 inoperative through C.O. 272 and C.O. 273, read with the Jammu and Kashmir Reorganisation Act, 2019.
- The Supreme Court in 2023 upheld the constitutional result, but is recorded as treating the Art 367 substitution as invalid to the stated extent (⚠️ not confirmed on Wikipedia) and did not finally adjudicate the conversion of J&K from State to Union Territory after recording the Union's restoration assurance (owner).
- Current position (owner, checked 5 Sep 2026): J&K remains a UT with a legislature; Ladakh a UT without one. No statehood change had been enacted.
- Art 35A (inserted by the 1954 Presidential Order) ceased to operate with the 2019 reconfiguration (owner).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** *In re Article 370* (11 Dec 2023); C.O. 272 and 273 (2019); J&K Reorganisation Act, 2019; Art 35A (1954 Presidential Order); Art 367 (2019 substitution); Art 3 (formation of States — ⚠️ cross-reference).
**Trap:** Art 370 is still printed in Part XXI but inoperative; "Article 370 is abolished by amendment" is wrong — it was made inoperative by Presidential orders, and the 2023 judgment is on the validity of that process. The 2023 judgment did not order statehood restoration as a fact; it recorded the Union's assurance.
**Back:** *"370: printed but inoperative — two 2019 orders, and the 2023 Court upheld the result."*

### Flashcard Art-371 — Article 371
**Front:** Article 371 — Special provisions with respect to Maharashtra and Gujarat
**Provision:** The President may by order made with respect to the State of Maharashtra or Gujarat, provide for the establishment of separate development boards for different parts of the State (for Vidarbha, Marathwada and the rest of Maharashtra; Saurashtra and Kutch in Gujarat — (✅ Wikisource Part XXI, cl (2): Vidarbha, Marathwada, Saurashtra, Kutch and the rest; present board status ⚠️ unverified)), equitable allocation of funds for development expenditure, equitable opportunities in technical education, vocational training and employment in State services, and annual reports to the Assembly on the working of the boards. The President may assign the Governor special responsibility for these matters.
**Learn:**
- Regional-balance device under which the Governor is assigned special responsibility for equitable development in the defined regions.
- Annual Assembly reporting and a development-funds mechanism are the enforcement tools.
- Owner: the current Maharashtra–Gujarat clause is the State-specific form that evolved from the reorganisation era (✅ Wikisource Part XXI: "Bombay" replaced by Maharashtra and Gujarat via the Bombay Reorganisation Act, 1960, w.e.f. 1 May 1960; cl (1) omitted by the 32nd Amendment, 1973, w.e.f. 1 Jul 1974).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 371 family; Art 371 (Maharashtra/Gujarat) interacts with the Governor's special responsibility in Art 371A/371C/371H (owner's Special Provisions file §2).
**Trap:** The Governor's special responsibility under 371 is by Presidential order, not automatic; do not present the boards as Sixth Schedule councils.
**Back:** *"371: Maharashtra and Gujarat's regional boards — the Governor watches, the Assembly hears annually."*

### Flashcard Art-371A — Article 371A
**Front:** Article 371A — Special provision with respect to the State of Nagaland
**Provision:** Inserted by the 13th Amendment (1962; w.e.f. 1 Dec 1963 ✅ Wikisource Part XXI). No Act of Parliament in respect of (a) religious or social practices of the Nagas, (b) Naga customary law and procedure, (c) administration of civil and criminal justice involving decisions according to Naga customary law, and (d) ownership and transfer of land and its resources shall apply to Nagaland unless the Legislative Assembly of Nagaland, by resolution, so decides. The Governor has a bounded transitional law-and-order responsibility, and the Tuensang district has special arrangements (owner).
**Learn:**
- Consent shield for Naga customary law, land and resources; the Assembly must resolve before a central law applies.
- Wording on resources is broader than religion/customs; a tax-and-resource shield.
- The Governor's responsibility for law and order is bounded and transitional (owner).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 13th Amendment (1962) (owner); Art 371G (Mizoram, parallel shield, owner); Sixth Schedule is not applicable to Nagaland (owner's Special Provisions §5).
**Trap:** 371A's consent shield is subject-specific; Art 371G (Mizoram) is textually distinct. Do not confuse the Nagaland Assembly resolution with the Assam committee (371B).
**Back:** *"371A: Nagaland's customs, land and resources — the Assembly decides, not Parliament."*

### Flashcard Art-371B — Article 371B
**Front:** Article 371B — Special provision with respect to Assam
**Provision:** Inserted by the 22nd Amendment (1969; w.e.f. 25 Sep 1969 ✅ Wikisource Part XXI). The President may by order provide for the constitution and functions of a committee of the Legislative Assembly of Assam, consisting of members of the Assembly elected from the tribal areas of Assam and such other members as the President may specify, and make adaptations and modifications to the Assembly's procedure for those purposes.
**Learn:**
- An Assembly committee model for tribal areas of Assam, created by Presidential order.
- Not a Sixth Schedule council (owner): the Sixth Schedule (Art 244(2)) governs autonomous district councils.
- Owner: Assam committee, distinct from Manipur's Hill Areas Committee (371C).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 22nd Amendment (1969); Art 371C; Art 244(2) (Sixth Schedule, owner's Special Provisions §5).
**Trap:** 371B is an Assembly committee, not an autonomous council; do not confuse it with the Sixth Schedule's district/regional councils.
**Back:** *"371B: Assam's tribal-area committee of the Assembly — not a Sixth Schedule council."*

### Flashcard Art-371C — Article 371C
**Front:** Article 371C — Special provision with respect to the State of Manipur
**Provision:** Inserted by the 27th Amendment (1971; w.e.f. 15 Feb 1972 ✅ Wikisource Part XXI). The President may by order provide for the constitution and functions of a committee of the Legislative Assembly of Manipur for the hill areas of the State, and may make the Governor responsible for the peace and equitable advancement of the hill areas, with an annual report to the President and Union directions possible (owner: "Governor responsibility, annual report to President; Union directions possible").
**Learn:**
- Hill Areas Committee plus Governor responsibility, a combined model.
- The Governor's annual report goes to the President, and the Union may give directions (owner).
- Not a Sixth Schedule council.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 27th Amendment (1971); Art 371B; Art 244(2) (owner's Special Provisions §5).
**Trap:** Do not say Manipur hill areas have autonomous Sixth Schedule councils. 371C combines a committee with Governor reporting and Union directions.
**Back:** *"371C: Manipur's hill committee and a Governor who reports to the President."*

### Flashcard Art-371D — Article 371D
**Front:** Article 371D — Special provisions with respect to the State of Andhra Pradesh (adapted for Telangana)
**Provision:** Inserted by the 32nd Amendment (1973; w.e.f. 1 Jul 1974 ✅ Wikisource Part XXI). The President may by order provide for equitable opportunities and facilities for people belonging to different parts of the State in public employment and in educational matters, and may create local cadres and local areas for the purpose. Section 97 of the Andhra Pradesh Reorganisation Act, 2014 adapted Art 371D for Telangana (owner).
**Learn:**
- Regional equal opportunity in jobs and education via Presidential order; local cadres and local areas.
- The Andhra Pradesh Administrative Tribunal was abolished by G.S.R. 30(E), 14 January 2020 (owner); do not present it as operating (⚠️ G.S.R. date not verified). Wikisource Part XXI fn: the Supreme Court in *P. Sambamurthy* (AIR 1987 SC 663) declared cl (5) and its proviso void (✅).
- The Presidential orders operationalise it; the constitutional power survives.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 32nd Amendment (1973); Andhra Pradesh Reorganisation Act, 2014 s.97 (owner); Art 371E (Central University).
**Trap:** The power survives but the Tribunal does not. Do not confuse 371D with 371E. Do not say 371D is a Sixth Schedule provision.
**Back:** *"371D: equal chances in jobs and schools by region — the 32nd Amendment, adapted for Telangana in 2014."*

### Flashcard Art-371E — Article 371E
**Front:** Article 371E — Establishment of Central University in Andhra Pradesh
**Provision:** Inserted by the 32nd Amendment (1973; presumed from the same Wikisource block, no separate footnote ⚠️). Parliament may by law provide for the establishment of a Central University in the State of Andhra Pradesh (owner: "Parliament may establish a Central University").
**Learn:**
- A specific legislative power for a Central University in Andhra Pradesh.
- Parliament acts by law; the State does not establish it.
- Shows the Art 371 family's mix of regional tools: education, employment, and development.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 32nd Amendment (1973); Art 371D (opportunity); Central Universities Act (⚠️ statute not verified here).
**Trap:** Art 371E is a power for Parliament to establish a Central University; it is not a State university power.
**Back:** *"371E: Parliament may found a Central University in Andhra Pradesh."*

### Flashcard Art-371F — Article 371F
**Front:** Article 371F — Special provisions with respect to the State of Sikkim
**Provision:** Inserted by the 36th Amendment (1975; w.e.f. 26 Apr 1975 ✅ Wikisource Part XXI). A detailed accession and integration settlement for Sikkim: representation in Parliament and the State Assembly, continuance of existing laws and courts, and Governor responsibility for peace and equitable advancement of the State's communities (owner: "integration, representation, existing-law/court continuity, and Governor responsibility"); the clause specifies which of Parliament, the President and the Governor acts in each respect.
**Learn:**
- The 36th Amendment (1975) followed Sikkim's merger with India (1975).
- More than a minimum Assembly clause: a settlement of accession (owner).
- Continuity of laws and courts through the transition.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 36th Amendment (1975); Art 2/3 (⚠️ cross-reference); Sikkim's Assembly (⚠️ minimum-member figure not verified).
**Trap:** 371F is not just an Assembly-size clause; do not reduce it to one number.
**Back:** *"371F: Sikkim's accession settlement — representation, laws and courts kept, the Governor watches."*

### Flashcard Art-371G — Article 371G
**Front:** Article 371G — Special provision with respect to the State of Mizoram
**Provision:** Inserted by the 53rd Amendment (1986; w.e.f. 20 Feb 1987 ✅ Wikisource Part XXI).
