No Act of Parliament in respect of (a) religious or social practices of the Mizos, (b) Mizo customary law and procedure, (c) administration of civil and criminal justice involving decisions according to Mizo customary law, and (d) ownership and transfer of land, shall apply to Mizoram unless the Legislative Assembly of Mizoram, by resolution, so decides. The Assembly must have at least forty members (owner).
**Learn:**
- A Mizoram consent shield parallel to 371A but textually distinct (owner's firewall).
- Mizo customary law and land: Assembly-resolution gate.
- The 40-member minimum (owner).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 53rd Amendment (1986); Art 371A (Nagaland); Art 371H (Arunachal); Sixth Schedule (Art 244(2)) covers autonomous districts in four North-Eastern States, including Mizoram (owner's Special Provisions §5) — the Sixth Schedule is a separate device from the 371G shield.
**Trap:** 371G and 371A both shield customs; do not treat them as identical. The Mizoram shield includes customary justice and land; the Mizoram Assembly minimum is 40.
**Back:** *"371G: Mizo customs, justice and land — the Mizo Assembly, forty strong, decides."*

### Flashcard Art-371H — Article 371H
**Front:** Article 371H — Special provision with respect to the State of Arunachal Pradesh
**Provision:** Inserted by the 55th Amendment (1986; w.e.f. 20 Feb 1987 ✅ Wikisource Part XXI). The Governor of Arunachal Pradesh shall have special responsibility with respect to law and order in the State, to be exercised after consultation with the Council of Ministers but in his individual judgment; the President may terminate this responsibility by order. The Assembly must have at least thirty members (owner).
**Learn:**
- Governor's bounded law-and-order responsibility, after ministerial consultation and in individual judgment.
- Presidential termination power.
- Assembly minimum: thirty (owner).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 55th Amendment (1986); Art 371A; Art 371I; owner's Special Provisions §2 and §3.
**Trap:** The Governor here uses individual judgment on law and order — but only in the 371H responsibility, and subject to termination; do not say the Governor overrides an elected government generally.
**Back:** *"371H: Arunachal's Governor guards law and order in his own judgment — the President can end it."*

### Flashcard Art-371I — Article 371I
**Front:** Article 371I — Special provision with respect to the State of Goa
**Provision:** Inserted by the 56th Amendment (1987; w.e.f. 30 May 1987 ✅ Wikisource Part XXI). It fixes the minimum size of the Goa Legislative Assembly at thirty members (owner: "Assembly at least 30").
**Learn:**
- A narrow clause: it only fixes the minimum Assembly size.
- Goa became a State in 1987 (✅ consistent with the 371I effective date of 30 May 1987 in Wikisource Part XXI).
- Not a regional development or consent shield.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 56th Amendment (1987); Art 371H (similar 30-member minimum for Arunachal).
**Trap:** Do not present 371I as a broad special-status provision; it is a size clause.
**Back:** *"371I: Goa's Assembly — thirty members at least, nothing more."*

### Flashcard Art-371J — Article 371J
**Front:** Article 371J — Special provisions with respect to Hyderabad-Karnataka (now Kalyana Karnataka)
**Provision:** Inserted by the 98th Amendment (2012), effective 1 Oct 2013 (✅ Wikisource Part XXI). The President may by order provide for a separate development board for the Hyderabad-Karnataka region (now officially Kalyana Karnataka), equitable allocation of funds, equitable opportunity in education and employment, and proportionate reservation of seats/posts in educational institutions and public employment for local-area candidates. The President may also assign the Governor special responsibility for these matters (owner).
**Learn:**
- A regional development board and proportional local-area reservation device for Karnataka's northern region.
- The text still says "Hyderabad-Karnataka"; the official name is now Kalyana Karnataka (owner).
- Assigns Governor responsibility by Presidential order.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 98th Amendment (2012) (owner); Art 371 (Maharashtra–Gujarat model); Art 371D (regional equal opportunity).
**Trap:** The constitutional text says Hyderabad-Karnataka; the regional name is Kalyana Karnataka. Do not say Karnataka's 371J is a Sixth Schedule council.
**Back:** *"371J: Karnataka's northern board — the text says Hyderabad-Karnataka, the State calls it Kalyana Karnataka."*

### Flashcard Art-372 — Article 372
**Front:** Article 372 — Continuance in force of existing laws and their adaptation
**Provision:** (1) Notwithstanding the repeal of the enactments by Art 395, all the laws in force in the territory of India immediately before commencement shall continue in force until altered or repealed or amended by a competent legislature or other competent authority. (2) For bringing the laws into harmony with the Constitution, the President may by order make such adaptations and modifications (✅ Wikisource Part XXI: the time limit sits in cl (3)(a), three years from commencement, i.e. to 26 Jan 1953 by computation; substituted for two years by the 1st Amendment, 1951, w.e.f. 18 Jun 1951). (3) Cl (3)(a) fixes that limit; clause (2) adaptations cannot be questioned in court (corrected: cl (3) is not a "deemed in force" clause).
**Learn:**
- Continuity clause: pre-Constitution laws survive until replaced by a competent legislature.
- The President's adaptation power under 372(2) was time-limited (✅ three years from commencement under cl (3)(a)); a separate adaptation article, Art 372A, is carded below.
- The principle: laws survive but are read subject to the Constitution (Part III).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 395 (repeal of 1935/1947 Acts); Art 13 (pre-Constitution laws void to the extent of FR inconsistency); Art 372A; Art 373.
**Trap:** Art 372 keeps old laws alive but not against Part III: pre-Constitution laws inconsistent with FRs are void under Art 13(1). Do not say all colonial laws are valid forever.
**Back:** *"372: old laws survive until a competent legislature changes them — but Part III still bites."*

### Flashcard Art-372A — Article 372A
**Front:** Article 372A — Power of the President to adapt laws (inserted by the 7th Amendment, 1956, s.23, w.e.f. 1 Nov 1956 ✅)
**Provision:** Inserted by the 7th Amendment, 1956, s.23 (w.e.f. 1 Nov 1956) (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XXI). The President may, by order made before 1 Nov 1957, adapt laws to conform to the Constitution as amended; cl (2) preserves the legislature's power to amend or repeal adapted laws (✅ paraphrase; exact clause wording ⚠️ unverified).
**Learn:**
- Insertion (1956) and the 1 Nov 1957 order deadline are confirmed; the operative clause wording still needs the official text before quoting.
- Distinct from the 1950-era adaptation power in Art 372(2).
- Owner files do not cover it; no owner-verified case or amendment is attached.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 372; Art 372(2); verify against official Legislative Department text.
**Trap:** Do not assert it is a current power: its order deadline was 1 Nov 1957. Do not assume 372A is the same as 372(2).
**Back:** *"372A: the President could tidy old laws by order until 1 Nov 1957."*

### Flashcard Art-373 — Article 373
**Front:** Article 373 — Power of President to make orders in respect of preventive detention pending Parliament's law
**Provision:** Until Parliament makes provision under Art 22(7) (which empowers Parliament to prescribe the circumstances and classes of cases for preventive detention beyond the usual periods), or one year from commencement, whichever is earlier, references to Parliament in Art 22(4) and (7) are read as references to the President, who may by order provide for preventive detention in the cases the order specifies (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XXI, Art 373 paraphrase; heading "Power of President to make order in respect of persons under preventive detention in certain cases"; corrected: was "pending Parliament's law" with no one-year limit).
**Learn:**
- A pre-Parliament preventive-detention holding power, linked to Art 22(7).
- Once Parliament legislates under Art 22(7), the transitional power yields. Its time-limited nature makes it spent (✅ expiry: the earlier of Parliament's Art 22(7) law or one year from commencement, i.e. 26 Jan 1951 at the latest).
- Preventive-detention safeguards (Art 22(4)–(7)) sit in Fundamental Rights, not the emergency Articles (owner: Emergency file §9.10).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 22(4)–(7); Art 359 (suspension of Art 22 rights is not permitted by 20/21 but 22 may be suspended in emergencies by order — ⚠️ check); Preventive Detention Acts (historical); owner Emergency file §9.10 (POTA routing).
**Trap:** Art 373 is a transitional power of 1950–51, not an emergency power. Do not confuse it with Art 359 or with the (repealed) POTA-era anti-terror law.
**Back:** *"373: a holding order for preventive detention until Parliament legislates under Art 22(7), or one year passes."*

### Flashcard Art-374 — Article 374
**Front:** Article 374 — Provisions as to judges of the Federal Court and proceedings pending in the Federal Court
**Provision:** Judges of the Federal Court holding office immediately before commencement became judges of the Supreme Court unless they opted otherwise (cl (1), ✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XXI); suits, appeals and proceedings pending in the Federal Court move to the Supreme Court (cl (2), ✅); cl (4) ends the Privy Council's jurisdiction over States "specified in Part B of the First Schedule" (✅). The Federal Court Chief Justice's succession to CJI is not stated in the clause text (⚠️ unverified).
**Learn:**
- A transitional succession clause from the Federal Court (1937–1950) to the Supreme Court.
- Spent: the Federal Court's functions passed to the SC on 26 January 1950.
- The Chief Justice of the Federal Court's succession to CJI is the key exam fact (⚠️ general knowledge; not stated in the Art 374 text).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 124 (SC composition); Art 125; Art 374 (1950 transition).
**Trap:** Art 374 is about the Federal Court's succession, not about the SC's current composition; the Supreme Court's composition is in Art 124.
**Back:** *"374: the Federal Court hands its bench to the Supreme Court in 1950."*

### Flashcard Art-375 — Article 375
**Front:** Article 375 — Courts, authorities and officers to continue to function subject to the provisions of the Constitution
**Provision:** All courts of civil, criminal and revenue jurisdiction, and all authorities and officers, existing in the territory of India immediately before commencement, shall continue to exercise their respective functions subject to the provisions of the Constitution, (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XXI: "to continue to exercise their respective functions subject to the provisions of the Constitution"; corrected: the tail "until altered or abolished by competent authority" is not in the 2020 text).
**Learn:**
- Continuity of the judicial and administrative machinery at commencement.
- Works with Art 372 (laws continue) to avoid a legal vacuum on 26 January 1950.
- Subject to Part III and to the Constitution's allocation of jurisdiction.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 372; Art 226 and 227 (High Court jurisdiction, owner's basic-structure list); Art 225 (existing jurisdiction of High Courts).
**Trap:** Art 375 continues existing courts subject to the Constitution — not an endless grant; do not say the Constitution fixes all court jurisdictions here.
**Back:** *"375: courts and officers carry on at the switch-over — subject to the new Constitution."*

### Flashcard Art-376 — Article 376
**Front:** Article 376 — Provisions as to Judges of High Courts (corrected: was "in Part A States")
**Provision:** Cl (1): judges of a Province's High Court in office at commencement become judges of the corresponding State's High Court unless they opted otherwise, notwithstanding Art 217(2) (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XXI). Cl (2): judges of a High Court in a State "specified in Part B of the First Schedule" become judges of that State's High Court and continue for a period the President fixes by order (✅). Corrected: the heading is "Provisions as to Judges of High Courts", and the text has no "Part A State" clause.
**Learn:**
- A transitional clause for High Court judges after the 1950 scheme; the 7th Amendment (1956) removed "Part A/B" wording from several provisions, but cl (2) of Art 376 still refers to "Part B" (✅ Wikisource footnotes; the full abolition of the Part A/B/C/D categories is ⚠️ general knowledge).
- Now spent: the judges' position is governed by Arts 214–217.
- Pair with Art 375 (courts continue).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 214–217 (High Court judges); Art 375; 7th Amendment (1956) (✅ Art 376 cl (1) footnote for the 1951 First Amendment; the 1956 reorganisation point ⚠️ verify).
**Trap:** Art 376 is transitional, not a current appointment clause; High Court appointments are in Art 217.
**Back:** *"376: the Part A High Court judges carry on in 1950 — appointments now sit in Art 217."*

### Flashcard Art-377 — Article 377
**Front:** Article 377 — Provisions as to the Comptroller and Auditor-General of India
**Provision:** The Auditor-General of India holding office immediately before commencement shall become the Comptroller and Auditor-General of India (CAG) for the purposes of the Constitution and shall hold office on salary, leave and pension rights under Art 148(3) and serves until his term expires under the rules applicable to him before commencement (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XXI; corrected: "terms and conditions specified in Art 148" was loose).
**Learn:**
- A succession clause for the audit office at commencement.
- The CAG's present tenure and removal rules are in Art 148 (removal like a Supreme Court judge), not in Art 377.
- The CAG audits Union and State accounts (Art 149–151) — owner-not-covered; ⚠️ cross-reference only (Art 148(3) confirmed by Wikisource Part XXI).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 148 (CAG appointment and removal); Art 149 (duties); Art 151 (audit reports); Art 377 (transitional).
**Trap:** Art 377 is transitional succession; the CAG's present tenure/removal is Art 148. Do not confuse the CAG with the ECI or the UPSC.
**Back:** *"377: the Auditor-General becomes the CAG on 26 January 1950 — today's rules are in Art 148."*

### Flashcard Art-378 — Article 378 (status ✅ present, not omitted)
**Front:** Article 378 — Provisions as to Public Service Commissions for the Dominion of India (✅ present in the 2020 text, not omitted — Wikisource Part XXI)
**Provision:** Clause wording is not reproduced in the Wikisource extract (⚠️ unverified). As a transitional article, it concerned the continuance of the Federal Public Service Commission and the Provincial arrangements (⚠️ paraphrase). The brief lists its status as to be verified; treat its present operative force as unconfirmed.
**Learn:**
- Status confirmed (present, not omitted); its wording still needs the official text before quoting.
- For the operative rules on public service commissions use Arts 315–323 (UPSC and State PSCs).
- Transitional succession articles in Part XXI are generally spent.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Arts 315–323 (UPSC/State PSCs); Art 378A (below); verify against the Legislative Department text.
**Trap:** Art 378 is not the operative source for the UPSC or State PSC; Arts 315–323 are.
**Back:** *"378: a transitional PSC clause — check the text; the living rules are Arts 315–323."*

### Flashcard Art-378A — Article 378A (status ✅ present, not omitted)
**Front:** Article 378A — Special provision as to duration of Andhra Pradesh Legislative Assembly (✅ inserted by the 7th Amendment, 1956, s.24, w.e.f. 1 Nov 1956; present, not omitted — Wikisource Part XXI)
**Provision:** Clause wording is not reproduced in the Wikisource extract (⚠️ unverified). As understood, it concerns the term (duration) of the Andhra Pradesh Legislative Assembly, a special rule departing from the general five-year term of Art 172 for a particular Assembly (⚠️ verify the clause, the Assembly concerned and the amending Act before citing).
**Learn:**
- A State-specific term clause (a rare example of a Part XXI State-specific term provision).
- Read with the Art 371D family (Andhra Pradesh and Telangana) as a State-specific device; do not merge them.
- Current operative effect: verify before use.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 172 (duration of State legislatures); Art 371D (AP–Telangana); Andhra Pradesh Reorganisation Act, 2014 (owner: s.97 adapted 371D).
**Trap:** Do not say Art 378A governs the current Telangana or AP Assembly term without verifying the clause; Art 172 is the general duration rule.
**Back:** *"378A: a special term clause for the Andhra Assembly — verify before you cite it."*

### Flashcard Art-379 — Article 379 (Omitted)
**Front:** Article 379 — Provisional transitional provision of the 1950 settlement (exact original title ⚠️ unverified)
**Provision:** **Omitted** (✅ by the Constitution (Seventh Amendment) Act, 1956, s.29 and Sch., w.e.f. 1 Nov 1956 — Wikisource Part XXI block entry). Note: the commencement clause (Art 394) brought Arts 379, 380, 388 and 391 into force at once (✅ Art 394 list, Wikisource Part XXII; the 26 Nov 1949 date ⚠️ not stated in the text) — so they existed at commencement and were removed later.
**Learn:**
- A transitional block for the Provisional Parliament, its presiding officers and the transition to the new Parliament (⚠️ general description; clause content not verified).
- Spent by time and later omission; no present operation.
- Use the Art 394 commencement structure as a way to remember the transitional block.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 394 (commencement, verified); Arts 380–391 (below); verify the omitting Act in the official Constitution text.
**Trap:** An omitted article has no present force; do not cite Art 379 as a live power.
**Back:** *"379: part of the 1949 transition block — spent, then struck out."*

### Flashcard Art-380 — Article 380 (Omitted)
**Front:** Article 380 — Provisional transitional provision of the 1950 settlement (exact original title ⚠️ unverified)
**Provision:** **Omitted** (✅ omitted by the Constitution (Seventh Amendment) Act, 1956, s.29 and Sch., w.e.f. 1 Nov 1956 — Wikisource Part XXI block entry). Brought into force on 26 November 1949 by Art 394 (✅ Art 394 list, Wikisource Part XXII; the 26 Nov 1949 date ⚠️ not stated in the text), as part of the transitional block for the Provisional Parliament and its institutions.
**Learn:**
- Transitional, spent provision from the 1949–1950 switch-over.
- Shares the commencement group with Arts 379, 388 and 391.
- No present operation.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 394; Arts 379, 388, 391.
**Trap:** Do not cite Art 380 as a current source of Presidential or Parliamentary power.
**Back:** *"380: the 1949 switch-over — spent and omitted."*

### Flashcard Art-392 — Article 392
**Front:** Article 392 — Power of the President to remove difficulties
**Provision:** (1) If any difficulty arises in bringing into operation the Constitution, the President may by order make such provisions, not being inconsistent with the Constitution, as appear to him to be necessary or expedient for removing the difficulty — a power available for a limited transitional period, which ends at the first meeting of Parliament constituted under Chapter II of Part V (✅ Wikisource Part XXI proviso; corrected: was "ending on 26 January 1950"; clause wording is a paraphrase). The Article is listed among the provisions brought into force on 26 November 1949 by Art 394 (✅ Art 394 list, Wikisource Part XXII; the 26 Nov 1949 date ⚠️ not stated in the text).
**Learn:**
- The transitional "difficulty-removal" power of the President, exhausted after the commencement period.
- Unlike the omitted articles, Art 392 remains in the text, but its time-limited power is spent.
- Do not treat it as a general power to amend the Constitution (that is Art 368).
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 394 (commencement); Art 368 (amendment).
**Trap:** Art 392 is a transitional power of the President, not a substitute for amendment; a current "removal of difficulties" order is not available under Art 392 in the same way. The period is the first meeting of Parliament under Part V, Ch II (✅ proviso).
**Back:** *"392: the President's 1950 'removal of difficulties' power — spent, and not a route to amend."*

### PART XXII — Miscellaneous Final Provisions (Articles 393–395)

### Flashcard Art-393 — Article 393
**Front:** Article 393 — Short title
**Provision:** "This Constitution may be called the Constitution of India." (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XXII: "the Constitution of India").
**Learn:**
- The Constitution's short title is "the Constitution of India".
- The Article is one of the provisions brought into force on 26 November 1949 by Art 394 (✅ Art 394 list, Wikisource Part XXII; the 26 Nov 1949 date ⚠️ not stated in the text).
- Part XXII is the Constitution's closing miscellaneous Part.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 394 (commencement); Art 395 (repeal).
**Trap:** Do not say the short title is "Republic of India" — that is the country's name in Art 1 ("India, that is Bharat, shall be a Union of States").
**Back:** *"393: 'the Constitution of India' — its short title, in one line."*

### Flashcard Art-394 — Article 394
**Front:** Article 394 — Commencement
**Provision:** Arts 5, 6, 7, 8, 9, 60, 324, 366, 367, 379, 380, 388, 391, 392, 393 and 394 came into force at once (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XXII, Art 394). The rest of the Constitution came into force on 26 January 1950 (✅ same source). The 26 November 1949 date is ⚠️ not stated in the Art 394 text (corrected: the text gives no date for the immediate-effect group). Art 394 is the commencement clause.
**Learn:**
- 26 November 1949 is the date of the Constitution's adoption/commencement group; 26 January 1950 is the date the Constitution as a whole came into force.
- Articles 379, 380, 388 and 391 entered into force on 26 November 1949 — though now omitted — which explains their transitional function.
- Art 324 (Election Commission) is in the 26 November group, showing the ECI's constitution came into being before the General Elections.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 379–392 (transitional group); Art 393; Art 324; Art 366–367 (definitions, interpretation).
**Trap:** Do not mix the two commencement dates (26 Nov 1949 for the group; 26 Jan 1950 for the rest). Art 394 is the commencement clause, not an amendment clause.
**Back:** *"394: some articles on 26 November 1949; the rest on 26 January 1950."*

### Flashcard Art-394A — Article 394A
**Front:** Article 394A — Authoritative text in the Hindi language (corrected heading; was "Authoritative text of the Constitution in Hindi")
**Provision:** Inserted by the 58th Amendment (1987), s.3, w.e.f. 9 Dec 1987 (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XXII): the President shall cause a Hindi translation of the Constitution, updated for amendments, to be published under his authority; each later amendment is also to be translated into Hindi; the translation is deemed the authoritative text in the Hindi language for all purposes (cl (3)).
**Learn:**
- A Hindi authoritative text of the Constitution, prepared under the President's authority.
- The Hindi text is the authoritative text in Hindi (cl (3)) (✅ corrected: was "same authority as the English text"; the clause does not say it is equal to English).
- Use Art 348 (English as default for authoritative legal texts) as the counterpart for courts.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** 58th Amendment (1987) (✅ Wikisource Part XXII); Art 348; Official Language Part XVII (owner).
**Trap:** Art 394A gives authority to the Hindi text of the Constitution, but Art 348 keeps English as default for other legal texts (Acts, Bills). Do not say all Acts are authoritative in Hindi.
**Back:** *"394A: the Constitution in Hindi, deemed authoritative — Acts stay English under Art 348."*

### Flashcard Art-395 — Article 395
**Front:** Article 395 — Repeal of the Indian Independence Act, 1947 and the Government of India Act, 1935
**Provision:** The Indian Independence Act, 1947 and the Government of India Act, 1935, together with all enactments amending or supplementing the latter Act, are repealed (✅ Wikisource Const. 2020, Part XXII: repealed, except the Abolition of Privy Council Jurisdiction Act, 1949, which is excluded from the repeal).
**Learn:**
- The Constitution repealed the colonial constitutional statutes at commencement.
- The 1935 Act's federal and administrative architecture was carried into the Constitution in part (owner-consistent general point).
- Art 395 coexists with Art 372 (continuing laws) — the general laws survive, the constitutional statutes go.
**Cases / Amendments / Links:** Art 372 (continuance of laws); Art 394; Art 395 (repeal).
**Trap:** Art 395 repeals the 1935 and 1947 constitutional statutes; it does not repeal all colonial laws (those survive under Art 372).
**Back:** *"395: the 1935 and 1947 Acts are repealed; the ordinary colonial laws continue under Art 372."*
